More than three hundred and fifty years ago, in the midst of May, 1492, the angel of the dawn, traveling with his broad, slow wings from the Levant to the Pillars of Hercules, and from the snow-capped summits of the Caucasus across the Alpine ridges to the dark, bare islands of the West, beheld nearly the same outline of firm land and unstable sea. He saw the same great mountain shadows cast upon the same valleys; the same olive groves, pine forests, and broad plains verdant with young corn or refreshed by rain. He saw the domes and spires of cities rising by riversides or mingled with the sedge-like masts along the curved coast, in precisely the same spots where they rise today. As the faint light of his passage pierced into human dwellings, it fell, as it does now, upon the rosy warmth of nesting infants; upon the haggard awakening of sorrow and sickness; upon the hurried rising of the hard-handed laborer; and upon the late slumber of the night-student, who had been questioning the stars, the sages, or his own soul for that hidden knowledge capable of breaking through the barrier of man's brief life, revealing its dark path as an arc within an immeasurable circle of light and glory.

The great river courses that have shaped the lives of mankind have scarcely altered; nor have those other streams, the currents of life flowing within human hearts, ceased to pulse toward the same great needs, the same profound loves and terrors. As our thought follows closely in the slow wake of the dawn, we are struck by the broad sameness of the human condition, which remains unchanged in the main headings of its history: hunger and labor, seed-time and harvest, love and death. Even if, instead of tracking the dim daybreak, our imagination pauses at a specific historical point to await the fuller morning, we may behold a world-renowned city that has hardly altered its silhouette since the days of Columbus. It stands seemingly as an almost unviolated symbol amidst the flux of human affairs, reminding us that we resemble the men of the past more than we differ from them, just as the great mechanical principles governing the construction of those domes and towers ensure a likeness in human architecture that transcends all possible change.

Suppose, then, that the spirit of a Florentine citizen, whose eyes were closed forever while Columbus waited and argued for his three humble vessels at the port of Palos, were permitted to return from the shades and pause where our thoughts now pause. This Spirit, clothed in the habit of his life, wears his well-lined black silk *lucco* in grave, unbroken lines from neck to ankle. Upon his head rests a plain cloth cap with its *becchetto*, a long hanging strip of drapery serving as a scarf in need, surmounting a penetrating face—not perhaps very handsome, yet distinguished by a firm, well-cut mouth, kept distinctly human by a close-shaven lip and chin. It is a face charged with the memories of a keen and varied life spent on the banks of the gleaming river below. As he gazes upon the scene, the sense of familiarity overwhelms the perception of change, leading him to think it possible to descend once more into the streets and resume that busy existence where he left it.

For it is not merely the mountains and the westward-bending river that he recognizes; not only the dark flanks of Mount Morello opposite him, nor the long valley of the Arno stretching its grey, low-tufted luxuriance toward the distant ridges of Carrara. He sees the steep height of Fiesole, crowned with monastic walls and cypresses; and all the green and grey slopes sprinkled with villas, naming them as he looks. Yet, though he misses the seventy or more towers that once surmounted the city walls like a regal diadem, his eyes refuse to dwell on that blank. They are drawn irresistibly to the unique tower springing, like a tall flower-stem reaching for the sun, from the square, turreted mass of the Old Palace in the very heart of the city—the tower that looks no worse for the four centuries passed since he walked beneath it.

The great dome, the greatest in the world, which in his early boyhood had been nothing more than a daring thought in the mind of a small, quick-eyed man—there it raises its vast curves still, eclipsing the hills. The well-known bell-towers appear: Giotto's, with its distant hint of rich color; the gracefully spired Badia; and the rest, all viewed once from the shoulder of his nurse. "Surely," he thinks, "Florence can still ring her bells with the solemn hammer-sound that used to beat upon the hearts of her citizens and strike out the fire within them. And here, on the right, stands the long dark mass of Santa Croce, where we buried our famous dead, laying laurels on their cold brows and fanning them with the breath of praise and banners. But Santa Croce had no spire then; we Florentines were too consumed with great building projects to complete them all in stone and marble. We had our frescoes and shrines to fund, not to mention rapacious condottieri, bribed royalty, and purchased territories. Our façades and spires had to wait. But what architect could the Frati Minori (the Franciscans) have employed to build that spire for them? If it had been constructed in my day, Filippo Brunelleschi or Michelozzo would have devised something of another fashion—something worthy to crown the church of Arnolfo."

At this, the Spirit sighs and turns his gaze to the city walls, dwelling now on the changes there with wonder at these modern times. Why have five of the eleven convenient gates been closed? And why, above all, should the towers that were once a glory and defense have been leveled? Has the world become so peaceful, and do Florentines dwell in such harmony, that there are no longer conspiracies to bring ambitious exiles home with armed bands at their backs? These are difficult questions; it is easier and pleasanter to recognize the old than to account for the new.

And flows the Arno, with its bridges exactly where they used to be—the Ponte Vecchio, least like any other bridge in the world, laden with the same quaint shops where our Spirit remembers perhaps lingering to watch the progress of that great palace Messer Luca Pitti was setting afoot with huge stones from the Hill of Bogoli (now Boboli), or perhaps transacting business with the cloth-dressers in Oltrarno. The exorbitant line of the Pitti roof is hidden from San Miniato, but the yearning of the old Florentine is not to see Messer Luca's overly ambitious palace, built unto himself; it is to be down among those narrow streets and busy, humming piazzas where he inherited the eager life of his fathers. Is not the anxious voting with black and white beans still going on down there? Who are the Priori in these months, eating soberly regulated official dinners in the Palazzo Vecchio, with removals of tripe and boiled partridges, seasoned by practical jokes against the ill-fated butt among those potent signors? Are not the significant banners still hung from the windows—still distributed with decent pomp under Orcagna's Loggia every two months? Life had its zest for the old Florentine when he, too, trod the marble steps and shared in those dignities.

His politics possessed an area as wide as his trade, stretching from Syria to Britain, yet they retained the passionate intensity and detailed practical interest belonging only to a narrow scene of corporate action; only to the members of a community shut in close by hills and walls of six miles' circuit, where men knew one another as they passed in the street, set their eyes daily on the memorials of their commonwealth, and were conscious of having not simply the right to vote, but the chance of being voted for. He loved his honors and his gains—the business of his counting-house, his guild, his public council chamber. He loved his enmities too, fingering the white bean destined to keep a hated name out of the *borsa* with more complacency than if it had been a golden florin. He loved to strengthen his family through a good alliance, returning home with a triumphant light in his eyes after concluding a satisfactory marriage for his son or daughter under his favorite loggia in the evening cool. He loved his games of chess under that same loggia, his biting jests, and even his coarse jokes, considering them beneath no dignity of a man eligible for the highest magistracy.

He had gained insight into all sorts of affairs at home and abroad: he had been of the "Ten" managing the war department, of the "Eight" attending to home discipline, of the Priori or Signori heading the executive government; he had even risen to the supreme office of Gonfaloniere. He had served in embassies to the Pope and the Venetians, and as commissary to the Republic's hired army, directing the inglorious, bloodless battles in which no man died of brave breast wounds—*virtuosi colpi*—but only of casual falls and trampling. In this way, he learned to distrust men without bitterness, viewing life mainly as a game of skill, yet not dead to traditions of heroism and clean-handed honor. For the human soul is hospitable, entertaining conflicting sentiments and contradictory opinions with much impartiality.

It was his pride, moreover, that he was duly tinctured with the learning of his age, judging not altogether with the vulgar but in

The Loggia de' Cerchi sat nestled in the beating heart of ancient Florence, hidden away in a maze of narrow lanes behind the Badia. Today, few strangers ventured this way unless driven by a doubtful quest for a specific, austere doorway marked with the inscription: *Qui Nacque Il Divino Poeta* ("Here was Born the Divine Poet"). To Dante's ear, these same streets had once echoed with the clamor of bloody feuds between rival clans; but in the fifteenth century, they resounded only with the raucous, ahistorical squabbles and boisterous jokes of wool-carders working in the districts of San Martino and Garbo.

Beneath this loggia, on the early morning of April 9, 1492, two men locked gazes. One stood slightly stooped, peering down with the keen scrutiny of curiosity; the other lay sprawled on the cobblestones, gazing upward with the startled eyes of someone jolted awake from a vivid dream.

The standing figure spoke first. He was a gray-haired man of broad shoulders, built by nature "with the fist and polished with the pickaxe," as a Tuscan might say. Yet, the self-important gravity etched into the deep lines of his brow and mouth served to counteract any assumptions of coarseness suggested by his rugged appearance. Beside him lay a large, well-filled sack of leather, and before him hung a peddler's basket filled with trinkets: spools of thread, sewing pins, and shards of glass, likely acquired in trade for his wares.

"Young man," he said, tapping the ring on the finger of the reclining figure, "once your chin grows a stiffer beard, you'll learn better than to sleep in street corners with such a gem on your forefinger. By the holy Evangelists! If anyone else had stood over you two minutes ago, I'd have taken it. But Bratti Ferravecchi is not a thief. The cat cannot eat her mouse unless she catches it alive, and Bratti cannot enjoy a gain unless he tastes the bargain first. Why, young man, one San Giovanni, three years past, the Saint sent a corpse my way—a blind beggar with a cap lined with coins. My stomach revolted at the idea of taking money I hadn't earned, until I realized San Giovanni owed me the value of what I spent annually at the Feast. So I buried the body, paid for a Mass, and called it a fair deal. But how does a youth with the face of Messer San Michele sleep on a stone slab with the wind for a blanket?"

His deep, guttural words were barely intelligible to the newly awakened listener, yet he understood the gesture toward his ring. With a half-automatic compliance, he removed the band and shoved it inside his doublet, then rose and stretched.

"Your tunic and hose clash dreadfully with that jewel, young man," Bratti remarked deliberately. "One might say the Saints sent *you* a corpse; but if you took the jewels, I trust you buried him—and you can surely afford a couple of Masses for him to boot."

A sharp, painful thrill seemed to shoot through the stranger's frame, halting his stretch. For an instant, he turned on Bratti with a sharp frown, but quickly masked it with indifference. He pulled off the red Levantine cap that dangled like a pouch over his left ear, tossed back his long, dark-brown curls, glanced at his attire, and smiled.

"You speak truth, friend: my garments are weather-stained like an old sail, and they are not old either. Like an old sail, however, they have borne the brunt of both sea spray and rain. The fact is, I am a stranger in Florence. When I arrived footsore last night, I preferred casting myself into a corner of this hospitable porch to continuing my hunt for a hostel, which might prove to be a nest of bloodsuckers of every stripe."

"A stranger, truly," Bratti agreed. "For your words melt from your throat so perfectly that neither a Christian nor a Florentine can distinguish a hook from a hanger. Are you not from Genoa? By the cut of your clothes, you seem more likely from Venice."

"At this precise moment," the stranger replied with a smile, "where I come from matters less than where I can find breakfast. Your city casts a grim shadow here: can you direct me to a livelier quarter where I might secure a meal and lodging?"

"I certainly can," Bratti said. "And it is your good fortune, young man, that I happened to walk in from Rovezzano this morning and detoured to Mercato Vecchio to say an Ave at the Badia. That is your luck. But let us see what my profit shall be. Nothing for nothing, young man. If I guide you to Mercato Vecchio, you shall swear by your patron saint to offer me the first bid for that stained suit of yours when you acquire finer raiment—which, doubtless, you will."

"Agreed, by San Niccolò," the other laughed. "But let us depart for this said Mercato, for I feel the urgent need to line this doublet you covet."

"Coveting? Nay," Bratti grunted, hoisting his bag onto his back and starting off. Suddenly, he broke into loud, harsh tones resembling the grinding of a cart wheel: "*Chi abbaratta—baratta—b'ratta—chi abbaratta cenci e vetri—b'ratta ferri vecchi?*" ("Who wants to exchange rags, broken glass, or old iron?")

"It is worth little," he continued, returning to a conversational tone. "Hose and all, your clothes fetch a low price. Still, if you wish to equip yourself with a lute surpassing any new instrument, a sword wielded by a Ridolfi, or a paternoster of the finest fashion, I could offer a grand bargain by allowing for the cost of your current attire. Simple as I stand here, I possess the best-furnished shop in the Ferravecchi district, situated close to the Mercato. Blessed be the Virgin! It is not a pumpkin I carry on my shoulders. But I do not remain caged in my shop all day; I have a wife and a raven to mind the stock at home. *Chi abbaratta...?* Now, young man, whence do you come, and what brings you to Florence?"

"I thought you delighted in nothing without a bargain," the stranger observed somewhat scornfully. "You have offered me nothing yet in exchange for that information."

"Well, well; a Florentine does not mind paying a fair price for news, even if he gains no hose thereby. If I take you to the fairest maiden in the Mercato for a cup of milk—that shall count as a fair bargain."

"Nay; I can locate her myself if she truly resides in the Mercato, for pretty heads tend to peer out of doors and windows. No, no. Moreover, a sharp trader like you should know that he who bids for nuts and news may discover them hollow."

"Ah! Young man," Bratti said, glancing sideways with a hint of admiration, "you were not born on a Sunday—the salt shops were open when you drew breath. Are you not a Hebrew? From Spain or Naples? Let me tell you, the Minor Friars are trying to heat Florence as hot as Spain regarding those dogs of hell who seek to monopolize usury profits for themselves, leaving none for Christians. And when you stroll the Calimala with a scrap of yellow cloth in your cap, it will ruin your beauty more than a sword-cut across that smooth olive cheek. *Abbaratta, baratta...* I tell you, young man, grey cloth stands against yellow cloth. There is as much grey cloth in Florence as needed to clothe the Duomo, yet not enough yellow cloth to make hose for Saint Christopher—blessed be his name, and grant me a vision of him today! *Abbaratta, baratta, b'ratta—chi abbaratta?*"

"All that is delightful information you part with gratis," the stranger retorted, "but it concerns me not. I am no Hebrew."

"See!" Bratti exclaimed triumphantly. "I've secured a fine bargain with mere words. I've extracted something from you, young man, though you are as slippery as a lamprey. Praised be San Giovanni! A blind Florentine matches two one-eyed men. But here we are in the Mercato."

Emerging from the narrow alleys, they entered a vast square known to earlier chroniclers as the Mercato Vecchio, or Old Market. Though this plaza had served as a provisioning hub since time immemorial—and fond imagination suggested it might be the very spot where the Fiesolan ancestors descended from their high fastnesses to trade with valley villagers—it had not been entirely avoided by Florentine wealth. In the early decades of the fifteenth century, nearing its end, the Medici and other powerful families of the *popolani grassi*, or commercial nobility, maintained residences there. They apparently found the roar of mixed dialects and the butcher's stalls, which the old poet Antonio Pucci hailed as a chief glory of a market surpassing all others, insufficient to offend their ears or shock their eyes. However, the pride of mutton and veal was currently absent; Lent was not yet fully concluded. The proud guild of butchers was in abeyance, replaced by the great harvest season of gardeners, cheesemongers, and vendors of macaroni, corn, eggs

After joining the cluster of chatterers, the young stranger grew impatient. Hopeless of discovering the cause of the local uproar—and indifferent to matters that concerned only those born of Florence—he decided to wander the piazza alone. He sought a vendor of food who might possess less curiosity about public affairs than the others. Suddenly, struck by a thought, he reached into the pouch hanging at his waist, rummaging through it with a look of growing frustration.

"Not a single coin!" he muttered in a language neither Tuscan nor Italian. "I thought I had one poor piece left. Very well, then—I must buy my breakfast for love!"

He hadn't walked far before locating a quiet corner of the market where such currency would surely be accepted. There stood two mules, decorated with red tassels and collars. One bore wooden vessels for milk; the other carried panniers overflowing with herbs and salads. Leaning her elbow against the milk-mule's neck was a young girl, no older than sixteen, her face framed by a red hood that concealed her hair entirely. Her features were so infantile in their prettiness that the total hiding of her hair enhanced the effect. Perhaps weary from her morning labor preparing for the trek from a castle several miles away, the child appeared to have fallen asleep in that half-standing, half-leaning posture.

Yet, the stranger felt no hesitation in waking her. His method, however, was so gentle that to the sleeping damsel, it seemed merely as if a sprig of thyme had brushed her lips as she bent to gather herbs. The dream shattered instantly; she opened wide, baby-blue eyes and sat up in astonishment and confusion, seeing the young man standing close before her. She heard his voice, strange yet soft, and even if she had been composed, she assumed he spoke words utterly unintelligible to her. Her first instinct was to turn her head slightly away and lift a corner of her green serge mantle as a shield.

He repeated his words gently: "Forgive me, pretty one, for awakening you. I am dying of hunger, and the scent of milk makes breakfast seem more desirable than ever."

He had chosen the phrase *"muoio di fame"* (I die of hunger) knowing it would be familiar to her ears, delivering it with the playful intonation of a beggar. This time, she understood. The corner of the mantle dropped, and within moments, a large cup of fragrant milk was offered to him. He took no further compliments before lifting it to his lips. As he drank, the little maiden gathered the courage to look up at the long, dark curls of this singular stranger. Though his clothes were ragged, he bore no resemblance to any beggar she had ever known.

While this observation occurred, another current of feeling moved her hand into a bag hanging beside the mule. When the stranger set down his empty cup, he saw a large piece of bread held out toward him, accompanied by a glance of encouraging blue eyes urging him to accept this extra gift.

"But perhaps that is your own breakfast," he said. "No, I have had enough without payment. A thousand thanks, my gentle one."

There was no verbal reply, but the bread was pushed closer, as if impatient at his refusal. As the stranger's long dark eyes rested on the baby-like face, it seemed the girl was gathering more courage to meet his gaze.

"Ah, then, if I must take the bread," he said, placing his hand upon it, "I shall grow bolder still, and beg for another kiss to make the bread sweeter."

His speech was becoming wonderfully clear despite the strange timbre of his voice, which had initially seemed enough to make her cross herself. She blushed deeply and lifted the corner of her mantle to cover her mouth once more. Just as the presumptuous stranger leaned forward, his fingers brushing the arm holding the shielding mantle, he was startled by a harsh voice close behind his ear.

"Who are *you*—and murrain to you! No honest buyer, I'll warrant, but a hanger-on of the dice-gamblers—or something worse. Go! Dance off and find fitter company, or I'll give you a tune at a tempo you won't like!"

The young stranger drew back, meeting the speaker's glare with a look provocatively free of alarm or apology. A slight expression of saucy amusement broke into a broad, beaming smile as he surveyed the figure of his threatener. She was a stout, brawny woman wearing a man's jerkin over her green serge gown, with the peaked hood of a discarded mantle fastened around her sunburnt face. Beneath all coarseness and premature wrinkles, her face displayed a half-sad, half-ludicrous maternal resemblance to the tender baby-face of her daughter—a kind of likeness that often serves as a more terrifying prophecy than the grim skull of Death.

There was something irresistibly charming in that bright young smile, but Monna Ghita was not a woman to show weakness. Speaking with heightened exasperation, she continued:

"Yes, yes, you can grin as well as other monkeys in caps and jerkins. You're a minstrel or a mountebank, I'll be sworn; you look as silly as a tumbler who has been upside down and managed to land on his feet. What fool's tricks have you been playing, Tessa?" she added, turning to her daughter, whose frightened countenance seemed even more inviting to abuse. "Giving away milk and victuals, it seems? Ay, ay, you'd carry water in your ears for any idle vagabond who wouldn't stoop to pick it up, you silly staring rabbit! Turn your back and lift the herbs from the panniers, or I'll make you recite a few Hail Marys without counting!"

"Nay, Madonna," said the stranger with a pleading smile, "do not be angry with your pretty Tessa for showing pity to a hungry traveler who found himself unexpectedly without a copper coin. Your handsome face looks so striking when it frowns that I long to see it illuminated by a smile."

"_Va via!_" (_Go away!) "I know what paste you are made of. You may tickle me with that straw for a long while before I laugh, mark my words. Get along with a bad Easter, or I'll put a beauty spot or two on that face of yours that will ruin your kissing prospects before Advent."

As Monna Ghita raised her formidable hands to enforce her command, Bratti, who had arrived a moment earlier, remarked to his companion, "What do you think of this pretty parrot, Nello? Does his tongue not smell of Venice?"

"Nay, Bratti," whispered the barber, "your wisdom contains much of the ass, as I told you just now; especially regarding the ears. This stranger is a Greek, or else I am not the barber who has exclusively shaved the excellent Demetrio and extracted more than one sore tooth from his learned jaw. And this youth could easily be taken as having come straight from Olympus—at least since he received a touch of my razor."

"_Orsù!_ Monna Ghita!" continued Nello, delighted to see some sport, "what storm has caused this commotion? Has this young stranger behaved badly?"

"By San Giovanni!" said the cautious Bratti, still harboring his original suspicions about the shabbily-dressed man possessing fine jewelry, "he was right to flee from *me*, if he intended to get into trouble. I can swear I found him beneath the Loggia de' Cerchi, wearing a ring on his finger such as I have seen on Bernardo Rucellai himself. Not another rusty nail's worth do I know about him."

"The truth is," said Nello, eyeing the stranger good-naturedly, "this *bello giovane* has been a little too presumptuous in admiring the charms of Monna Ghita and attempted to kiss her while her daughter's back was turned. Observe how busy the pretty Tessa is; she cannot look this way at present. Was it not so, Messer?" Nello concluded in a tone of courtesy.

"You have divined the offense like a soothsayer," laughed the stranger. "Only that I had not the good fortune to encounter Monna Ghita immediately. I begged a cup of milk from her daughter and accepted this gift of bread, for which I was making a humble offering of gratitude, before enjoying the higher pleasure of facing these riper charms—which I confess I may have admired too boldly."

"_Va, va!_ Be off, every one of you, and stay in purgatory until I pay to extract you, will you?" shrieked Monna Ghita fiercely, elbowing Nello and driving her mule forward to force the stranger aside. "Tessa, thou simpleton, bring the mule forward; the cart is coming upon us."

As Tessa turned to take the mule's bridle, she cast one timid glance at the stranger, who was now moving with Nello out of the path of an approaching market cart. Her glance lasted just long enough to catch the beckoning movement of his hand, indicating he had been waiting for this chance to bid farewell.

"Ebbene," said Bratti, raising his voice to speak over the cart, "I leave you with Nello, young man, for I cannot push my bag and basket any farther and have business at home. Remember our bargain, for if you found Tessa without me, it was not my fault. Nello will show you my shop in the Ferravecchi, and I shall not turn my back on you."

"A thousand thanks,

"To tell you the truth," said the young stranger to Nello, as they moved slightly apart from the tangled mass of carts and mules, "I am not sorry to be handed over by that patron of mine to one who speaks with a less barbarous accent and conducts a less enigmatic trade. Is it common among you Florentines for a wandering dealer in shattered glass and rags to boast of a shop where he sells lutes and swords?"

"Common? By no means. Our Bratti is no ordinary man. He possesses a theory and lives strictly by it—a feat I cannot claim for any philosopher I have the honor of shaving," replied Nello. His talkativeness was like an overfull bottle; it could never dispense merely a small draught. "Bratti intends to extract the maximum possible pleasure—that is to say, the hardest possible bargaining—from this life, concluding it with a deal for the smoothest passage through purgatory by handing his winnings over to the Holy Church once the game ends. He has drawn up his will to this precise effect on the cheapest terms a notary could be found. Yet I have often told him, 'Bratti, your bargain is a limp one, and you stand on the lame side of it. Does it not sadden you to gaze upon the paintings of Paradise? You will never be able there to haggle over rags and rusty nails; the saints and angels require neither pins nor tinder. Save perhaps with Saint Bartholomew, who carries his skin about in an inconvenient manner, I see no chance of your striking a deal for second-hand clothing.' But God forgive me," Nello added, shifting his tone and crossing himself, "such light chatter ill becomes a morning when Lorenzo lies dead and the Muses tear out their hair—a thought always painful to a barber. And you yourself, Messere, are likely under a cloud; when a man of your speech and bearing settles for such poor lodging, it argues some misfortune has befallen him."

"What Lorenzo is the one whose death you speak of?" asked the stranger, appearing to dwell with too anxious an interest on this point to have noticed the indirect inquiry that followed.

"What Lorenzo? There is but one Lorenzo, I imagine, whose passing could throw the Mercato into an uproar, make the lantern of the Duomo leap in desperation, and force the lions of the Republic to devour one another immediately. I mean Lorenzo de' Medici, the Pericles of our Athens—if I may draw such a comparison in the hearing of a Greek."

"Why not?" said the other, laughing. "For I doubt whether Athens, even in the days of Pericles, could have produced so learned a barber."

"Yes, yes; I thought I could not be mistaken," said the rapid Nello. "Otherwise, I have shaved the venerable Demetrio Calcondila to little purpose. But pardon me, I am lost in wonder: your Italian is better than his, though he has lived in Italy for forty years—even better than that of the accomplished Marullo, who may be said to have wed the Italic Muse in more senses than one, having married our learned and lovely Alessandra Scala."

"It will lighten your wonder to know that I come of Greek stock planted in Italian soil far longer than the mulberry trees which have taken so kindly to it. I was born at Bari, and my—I mean, I was raised by an Italian. In fact, I am a Greek, very much as your peaches are Persian. The Greek dye was subdued in me, I suppose, until I was dipped anew by long residence and much travel in the land of gods and heroes. And to confess something of my private affairs to you, this same Greek dye, along with a few ancient gems I carry about me, is the only fortune shipwreck has left me. But—when the towers fall, you know it is an ill business for the small nest-builders—the death of your Pericles makes me wish I had turned my steps toward Rome, as I should have done but for a fallacious Minerva in the shape of an Augustinian monk. 'At Rome,' he said, 'you will be lost in a crowd of hungry scholars; but at Florence, every corner is penetrated by the sunshine of Lorenzo's patronage: Florence is the best market in Italy for such commodities as yours.'"

"_Gnaffè_, and so it will remain, I hope," said Nello. "Lorenzo was not the sole patron and judge of learning in our city—heaven forbid! Because he was a large melon, every other Florentine is not a pumpkin, I suppose. Have we not Bernardo Rucellai, and Alamanno Rinuccini, and plenty more? And if you wish to be informed on such matters, I, Nello, am your man. It seems a thousand years until I can serve a *bel erudito* like yourself. And first, in the matter of your hair. That beard, my fine young man, must be parted with, were it as dear to you as the nymph of your dreams. Here at Florence, we dislike to see a man with his nose projecting over a cascade of hair. But remember, you will have crossed the Rubicon once you have been shaven: if you repent and allow your beard to grow after it has acquired stoutness through a struggle with the razor, your mouth will by-and-by show no longer what Messer Angelo calls the divine prerogative of lips, but will appear like a dark cavern fringed with horrent brambles."

"That is a terrible prophecy," said the Greek, "especially if your Florentine maidens are many of them as pretty as the little Tessa I stole a kiss from this morning."

"Tessa? She is a rough-handed peasant girl: you will rise into the favor of ladies who bring no scent of the mule-stables with them. But to that end, you must not have the air of a *sgherro*, or a man of evil repute: you must look like a courtier, and a scholar of the more polished sort, such as our Pietro Crinito—like one who sins among well-bred, well-fed people, and not one who sucks down vile *vino di sotto* in a chance tavern."

"With all my heart," said the stranger. "If the Florentine Graces demand it, I am willing to give up this small matter of my beard, but—"

"Yes, yes," interrupted Nello. "I know what you would say. It is the *bella zazzera*—the hyacinthine locks, you do not choose to part with; and there is no need. Just a little pruning—ecco!—and you will look not unlike the illustrious Prince Pico di Mirandola in his prime. And here we are in good time in the Piazza San Giovanni, and at the door of my shop. But you are pausing, I see: naturally, you want to look at our wonder of the world, our Duomo, our Santa Maria del Fiore. Well, well, a mere glance; but I beseech you to leave a closer survey until you have been shaved: I am quivering with the inspiration of my art even to the very edge of my razor. Ah, then, come round this way."

The mercurial barber seized the arm of the stranger and led him to a point on the south side of the piazza from which he could see at once the huge dark shell of the cupola, the slender soaring grace of Giotto's campanile, and the quaint octagon of San Giovanni in front of them, displaying its unique gates of storied bronze, which still bore the somewhat dimmed glory of their original gilding. The inlaid marbles were then fresher in their pink, white, and purple than they are now, when the winters of four centuries have turned their white to the rich ochre of well-mellowed meerschaum; the façade of the cathedral did not stand ignominious in faded stucco, but bore upon it the magnificent promise of the half-completed marble inlaying and statued niches, which Giotto had devised a hundred and fifty years before; and as the campanile, in all its harmonious variety of color and form, led the eyes upward, high into the clear air of this April morning, it seemed a prophetic symbol, telling that human life must somehow and sometime shape itself into accord with that pure aspiring beauty.

But this was not the impression it appeared to produce on the Greek. His eyes were irresistibly led upward, yet as he stood with his arms folded and his curls falling backward, there was a slight touch of scorn on his lip, and when his eyes fell again they glanced around with a scanning coolness that was rather piquing to Nello's Florentine spirit.

"Well, my fine young man," he said, with some impatience, "you seem to make as little of our Cathedral as if you were the Angel Gabriel come straight from Paradise. I should like to know if you have ever seen finer work than our Giotto's tower, or any cupola that would not look a mere mushroom by the side of Brunelleschi's there, or any marbles finer or more cunningly wrought than these that our Signoria obtained from far-off quarries at a price that would buy a dukedom. Come, now, have you ever seen anything to equal them?"

"If you asked me that question with a scimitar at my throat, after the Turkish fashion, or even your own razor," said the young Greek, smiling gaily and moving on toward the gates of the Baptistery, "I daresay you might get a confession of the true faith from me. But with my

"Good day, Messer Domenico," Nello greeted the first of the two men entering the shop, nodding respectfully to the second. "You arrive at the perfect moment, like cheese destined for macaroni. Ah, you are in a hurry—eager to be shaved without delay—*ecco!* Indeed, it is a morning when everyone carries heavy burdens upon their minds. Florence lies orphaned; the very pivot of Italy has been snatched away, leaving heaven itself unsure of its next move. *Oimè!* Very well then; yet the sun continues its journey toward dinner-time. As I mentioned, you arrive precisely like freshly grated cheese.

For this young stranger seeks an honorable merchant willing to advance funds against a ring of considerable value. If I were to count every goldsmith and moneylender in Florence on my fingers, I could find no finer name than Menico Cennini. Moreover, he deals in other wares relevant to your trade: Greek learning and youthful eyes—a dual commodity that printers are perpetually in need of."

The grave elderly man, son of Bernardo Cennini—who, twenty years prior, had heard of the German printing innovations and cast his own typefaces in Florence—remained silent and passive, covered in shaving lather, while Nello poured forth this torrent of conversation. However, he did cast a slow, sideways glance at the stranger.

"This fine youth offers unlimited services in Greek, Latin, or Italian," Nello continued, delighting in expansive interpretation. "He is a marvel of juvenile learning, comparable to Francesco Filelfo or our own incomparable Poliziano. He is, in fact, a second Guarino, though fate dealt him a cruel blow in the form of a shipwreck, likely causing him to lose a vast collection of precious manuscripts that could have corrected even your most accurate editions, Messer Domenico. Fortunately, he salvaged several gems of rare worth. His name is... you stated your name, sir, was—"

"Tito Melema," the stranger replied smoothly. He slipped the ring from his finger and presented it to Cennini, whose shave Nello had completed with equal speed and dexterity.

Meanwhile, the companion who had entered with the goldsmith—a tall figure around fifty years of age, sporting a neatly trimmed beard, an old felt hat, and a threadbare mantle—had kept his gaze fixed intently on the Greek youth. Now, he spoke abruptly.

"Young man, I am currently painting a depiction of Sinon deceiving old Priam. I would be most grateful if you would serve as my model for Sinon, provided you are willing to sit."

Tito Melema startled, turning his head with a pale expression of astonishment, as if struck by a sudden accusation. Yet, Nello offered him no respite to formulate a defense.

"Piero," the barber declared, "you are the most extraordinary mixture of moods and caprices ever confined within human skin. What trickery do you intend to employ with this distinguished scholar's visage to suit your traitor? Instead, ask him to lift his eyes upward; you might transform him into a Saint Sebastian capable of drawing crowds of pious women. Or, if you prefer a classical tone, drape myrtle around his curls and fashion him into a young Bacchus—or rather, a Phoebus Apollo, for his face shines as warm and bright as a summer morning. It won me as a friend within the span of a single 'Credo.'"

"Ay, Nello," the painter responded, speaking in abrupt pauses. "If your tongue could cease its endless chirping long enough for your understanding to reflect, you would realize you have just revealed the very reason why this gentleman's face suits my traitor perfectly. A true traitor must possess a face untouched by the marks of vice—lips capable of lying with a dimpled smile, eyes of such agate-like brilliance and depth that no infamy can cloud them, cheeks resilient enough to rise after murder without appearing haggard. I do not claim this young man is a traitor; I merely suggest he possesses the features of a perfect one should he harbor the corresponding heart. To state otherwise is simply to acknowledge that he has a beautiful face, filled with rich, vibrant blood, sustained sufficiently by nourishment to maintain its color with minimal reliance on virtue. He may possess the heart of a hero alongside it; I assert nothing to the contrary. Ask Domenico here if lapidaries can always identify a gem solely by sight. And now, I must stuff tow into my ears, for your chatter combined with the church bells exceeds my endurance. Say no further, but trim my beard."

With these final words, Piero (known as "di Cosimo" after his master, Cosimo Rosselli) extracted two pieces of tow, stuffed them firmly into his ears, and settled into the chair before Nello. The barber shrugged, casting a grimace of shared intelligence at the Greek, signaling, *"As you can see, a whimsical fellow! Everyone treats his words as mere jests."*

Tito, standing transfixed with his long dark eyes fixed on the enigmatic man who had addressed him so ambiguously, seemed to regain his composure only when Piero shifted positions. Satisfied with the explanation, he redirected his full attention to Cennini, who remarked shortly thereafter:

"This is a curious and valuable ring, young man. This intaglio of the fish bearing the crested serpent above it, set within the black stratum of the onyx—or rather, the niello—is beautifully highlighted by the surrounding blue of the upper layer. Does this ring, undoubtedly, carry a history?" Cennini asked, regarding the stranger with keen intensity.

"Yes, indeed," Tito replied, meeting the scrutiny openly. "The ring was discovered in Sicily. From those who specialize in gems and seals, I understand that both the stone and the carving possess virtues to bring fortune to the wearer, particularly at sea, and to restore whatever one may have lost. However," he continued, smiling faintly, "although I have worn it constantly since departing Greece, it has not rendered me entirely fortunate at sea, as you observe, unless escaping drowning counts as sufficient proof of its efficacy. Whether my lost chests will surface remains to be seen. Nevertheless, to ensure no opportunity is missed, Messer, I beg you to accept the ring as a pledge for a modest sum far below its true value. I shall redeem it as soon as I can liquidate certain other gems secured within my doublet, or indeed as soon as I can earn something through scholarly employment, should fortune allow me to secure such work."

"That can be arranged, young man, if you will accompany me," Cennini said. "My brother Pietro, who judges scholarship more competently than I, may be able to provide a task testing your abilities. In the meantime, take back your ring until I can deliver the necessary florins. If you wish, follow me."

"Yes, yes," Nello interjected eagerly. "Go with Messer Domenico; you could not be in better company. He was born under the constellation that bestows skill, wealth, and integrity upon its subjects—whatever that constellation may be, which matters little since infants cannot choose their own horoscopes. Indeed, if they could, there might be an inconvenient surge of babies during specific epochs. Furthermore, our Phoenix, the incomparable Pico, has demonstrated that horoscopes are nothing but nonsensical dreams—a view that causes fewer troubles. *Addio, bel giovane!* Do not fail to return to me."

"No fear of that," Tito replied, offering a wave of farewell as he turned his radiant face toward the door. "You are to render me a significant service; that serves as the most absolute guarantee of our future meetings."

"Say what you will, Piero," Nello muttered as the young stranger vanished. "I shall never behold such an exterior without assuming it signifies a lovable nature. You will surely argue next that Leonardo, whom you rave about incessantly, ought to have painted his Judas as beautiful as Saint John! But you are as deaf as the summit of Mount Morello with that cursed tow in your ears. Very well; I shall gather a bit more of this young man's history before escorting him to Bardo Bardi."

The Via de' Bardi, a thoroughfare immortalized in the chronicles of Florence, winds through the Oltrarno district, hugging the southern bank of the Arno. Stretching from the Ponte Vecchio to the Piazza de' Mozzi, where the Ponte alle Grazie rises, the street presents a striking duality. To the right, the row of houses climbs a steep incline known in the fifteenth century as the Hill of Bogoli—a notorious stone quarry that supplied the city's paving stones, often becoming treacherous slurry during rains. To the left, the architecture turns toward the river, displaying a facade of quaint, irregular elegance that seems to soften under the water's loving reflection as the sun dips toward the western horizon. Yet, to the historian's eye, these structures appear as a somewhat modern replacement for the legendary palaces of the Bardi family, razed by popular fury in the mid-fourteenth century.

Those Bardi were a lineage of formidable energy and pride. They were among the first to grasp the sword in Florence's internecine wars, when noble towers darkened the narrow lanes and the old war-god Mars might have smiled at the centuries of hollow worship paid to his rival, the Baptist. But the Bardi spirit was not limited to violence; their hands knew the delicate thrill of handling minted coin as well as gripping steel. Possessing the quintessential Florentine gaze, they understood that power could be seized through intellect and commerce just as effectively as through force. By the mid-1300s, they had ascended from commoners (*popolani*) to feudal Counts of Vernio, a title that bred resentment among their republican peers. Their ambition manifested in vast commercial ventures; they became the Christian era's equivalent of the Rothschilds, financing King Edward III of England and receiving revenues in wool, the crown jewel of Florentine trade. However, their financial hubris led to disaster. An unpaid debt to an "august" debtor triggered a panic among Sicilian creditors, resulting in a catastrophic collapse of their credit across the Mediterranean.

Yet, unlike modern bankrupts hiding in shame, the Bardi stood taller than ever. They became paragons of arrogance, drawing the ire of the armed populace in 1343. Fortified in their street between the two bridges, they held off the people's banners like panthers until an assault from the hill forced their surrender. Twenty-two of their great houses were sacked and burned, and many leading Bardi fled the city. But the family tree was deeply rooted. Though they lost their specific holdings on the riverbank—which by 1492 belonged largely to the Neri faction—the Bardi name persisted, resurfacing repeatedly in Florentine affairs, carrying a legacy of alternating glory and disgrace richer than most extended families could boast.

Among the Neri houses on the hillside stood the residence of a Bardi descendant, inheritor of the very branch that had once been Counts of Vernio. This heir possessed the old family fire and the drive to leave a permanent mark on the swirling earth, yet his passions had evolved. He was no warrior, nor a bold merchant-general. Instead, he was Bardo de' Bardi, a blind, impoverished scholar. His hands, once fit for command, were now cramped from endless hours of manuscript copying. Dressed in threadbare garments, first by choice, then by necessity, he dwelt amidst his books and shattered marble relics, illuminated only by the fading light of his memory. He was the man to whom Nello the barber had recently introduced the young Greek, Tito Melema.

Bardo's home was a somber mass of stone, punctuated by small windows and topped by a roofed terrace typical of the venerable city. Grim doors with scrolled hinges opened onto a vaulted courtyard, dominated by a massive iron lamp hanging from the center. A secondary door led to a stone staircase and the ground-floor rooms, which served as warehouses. Both floors were crammed with treasures destined for distant markets—from the Scheldt to the shores of Africa, the Aegean, or the Black Sea. Maso, the elderly servant, had to climb two flights to reach his master's chamber, entering only moments after Nello's conversation with the Greek.

Maso crossed the antechamber and pushed open the door to the left. He paused, nodding respectfully, before stepping aside. From the far end of a long, cavernous room came a clear, youthful voice: "Ah, you are back, Maso. All is well; we need nothing."

The speaker stood before a carved reading desk, reminiscent of those found in Italian church choirs. The room itself was a sanctuary of silence and order. Shelves lined the walls, meticulously stocked with books and antiquities. Between them stood isolated displays: a beautiful female torso, a headless statue brandishing a bladeless sword, severed infantile limbs inviting a kiss, preserved Roman busts, and vases from Magna Grecia. A central table held antique bronze lamps and dark pottery vessels. The palette of the room was predominantly pale and shadowy; the vellum bindings offered little contrast to the marble, which had grown livid with age. Even the once-splendid rug at the far end had faded to dimness. The dark bronzes waited for sunlight to reveal their verdigris, but the sun had not yet climbed high enough to pierce the narrow windows facing the Via de' Bardi.

The sole splash of vibrant color in the gloom belonged to a tall maiden of seventeen or eighteen. She stood before the reading desk, her hair a rich, reddish gold, rippling like sunset clouds against the twilight sky. Confined by a black fillet above her ears, the hair cascaded forward, framing a square-cut gown of black *rascia* serge. Her hazel eyes were fixed on a large volume resting on the desk. One long, white hand rested on the wood, while the other gently clasped the back of her father's chair.

Her blind father sat with his head lifted slightly, turned toward her as if visually engaging her. His delicate pallor, framed by a black velvet cap atop his drooping white hair, highlighted the uncanny resemblance between his aged features and hers. Both lacked the flush of health, sharing refined brows and noses, balanced by full, firm mouths and powerful chins that spoke of proud tenacity and latent impulsiveness. This trait was evident in the girl's posture: the backward tilt of her head, the grand line of her neck and shoulders. Her face was a puzzle—one that inspired not quite love, but a mixture of admiration and apprehension, dependent entirely on the eyes. Her father's eyes were silent forever; hers were currently tracing the Latin lines of Poliziano's *Miscellanea*, reading aloud at the eighty-first chapter.

"There was a certain nymph of Thebes named Chariclo, especially dear to Pallas..." Romola recited, her voice steady. "...and this nymph was the mother of Teiresias. But once, in the heat of summer, Pallas, accompanied by Chariclo, bathed her disrobed limbs in the Heliconian Hippocrene. It happened that Teiresias, coming as a hunter to quench his thirst, inadvertently beheld Minerva unveiled, and immediately became blind. For the Saturnian laws declare that he who sees the gods against their will shall pay a heavy penalty..."

She paused briefly, her hand slipping from the chair to meet her father's, which he had instinctively raised. She did not look up, continuing despite a subtle shift in her tone, moving into the Greek quotation from Nonnus.

"Stop, Romola," Bardo interrupted softly. "Bring me my own copy of Nonnus. It is superior to any in Poliziano's possession, for I made emendations to it that have not yet been shared. I completed it in 1477, when my sight was already failing."

Romola walked to the far end of the room with a queenly stride, effortless and uncalculated. "Is it in the proper place, Father?" Bardo asked, constantly verifying that reality matched the precise mental map he maintained.

"Yes, Father," she replied. "At the west end, on the third shelf from the bottom, behind the bust of Hadrian, above Apollonius Rhodius and Callimachus, and below Lucan and Silius Italicus."

A keen ear might have caught a hint of weary resignation in her otherwise patient delivery. As she approached, seeing his arms stretch out nervously to seize the volume, her hazel eyes filled with pity. She knelt beside him, placing the book on his lap and looking up as if believing her affection could penetrate the darkness blocking his vision. In that moment, the complex tension of pride and passion in her features transformed into pure, lovable tenderness. The deepest wells of her emotion had not yet surfaced in her expression, finding release only through her eyes.

Unaware of this radiant shift, Bardo appeared flushed and agitated as his fingers traced the spine of the book. "The vellum has yellowed in these thirteen years, Romola."

"Yes, Father," she said gently. "But your letters at the back remain dark and legible—fine Roman script. And the Greek characters," she added, opening the book on his knees, "are more beautiful than in any purchased manuscript."

"Indeed, child," Bardo murmured, running his finger across the page as if trying to distinguish margins by touch alone. "No hired scribe can match the writer who loves the words growing beneath his hand, for whom an error is more painful than sudden darkness. Even these mechanical printers, threatening to degrade learning, must rely on manuscripts crafted

When Maso reopened the door and admitted the two visitors, Nello performed a profound reverence toward Romola before gently pushing Tito ahead of him. Together, they advanced toward her father.

"Messer Bardo," Nello announced in a tone of measured respect, uncharacteristic of his usual manner, "I have the honor of presenting to you a Greek scholar. He has been eager to seek your counsel, driven not only by the report I gave him regarding your immense learning and priceless collections, but also by the hope that your patronage might assist him during the transient misfortune of a shipwreck. His name is Tito Melema, at your service."

Romola's astonishment was profound. If the stranger had appeared clad in a panther-skin and carrying a thyrsus, she could scarcely have been more surprised. The cunning barber had omitted any mention of the Greek's age or appearance. Among her father's scholarly guests, she had rarely encountered anyone but middle-aged or gray-haired men. Yet, there stood one masculine face, simultaneously youthful and beautiful, an image etched deeply in her mind: that of her brother, who had once lifted her onto his knee, kissed her, and vanished years ago. It was a fair face with sunny hair, like her own. However, her habitual mental posture toward strangers—a proud self-reliance and a resolve to ask for nothing, even a smile, confirmed by her father's laments against worldly injustice—stood like a snowy embankment holding back the rushing tide of her admiring surprise.

Tito's bright face displayed a richness of color that rivaled none, save perhaps for the deep black of his *sajo* or tunic reaching to his knees. He seemed like a wreath of spring dropped suddenly into Romola's young but wintry life, a season inherited only from memories: of a dead mother, a lost brother, and a blind father's happier past. These were memories of distant light, love, and beauty, buried in the dark mines of books, waiting to be kindled anew by the torch of some known joy. Nevertheless, she returned Tito's initial bow with the same pale, proud expression she usually wore. But as he drew nearer, the snow melted. When he dared to look at her again while Nello spoke, a pink flush spread across her cheeks, vanishing almost instantly as if her imperious will had summoned it back. In contrast, Tito's gaze held a gentle, beseeching admiration, the most appeasing appeal to a proud, shy woman, and perhaps the only atonement a man can offer for being too handsome. The complete fascination of his demeanor stemmed from an absolute lack of demand or assumption. It was the charm of a sleek, dark-eyed animal that delights you not by bounding away in indifference, but by unexpectedly resting its chin on your palm and looking up, desiring to be stroken—as if it loved you.

"Sir, I give you welcome," said Bardo with a touch of condescension. "Misfortune joined with learning, and especially Greek learning, serves as a letter of credit that ought to command the attention of every educated Florentine. As you surely know, since the time nearly a century ago when your countryman, Manuelo Crisolora, spread the light of his teaching throughout Italy's chief cities, no man is deemed worthy of the title 'scholar' unless he possesses more than the transplanted, derivative literature of the Latins. Rather, such inert students are branded as *opici*, or barbarians, using the very phrase of the Romans, who frankly replenished their urns at the fountainhead. As you perceive, and as Nello has undoubtedly warned you, I am entirely blind. This is a calamity to which we Florentines are particularly prone, whether due to the cold winds sweeping down from the Apennine passes in spring, the jarring transition from our indoor gloom to the blinding brilliance of our summer sun—which the *Lippi* attribute to the ancient Romans—or to some occult cause defying our shallow guesses. Nevertheless, please be seated. Nello, my friend, sit as well."

Bardo waited until his keen hearing confirmed that the visitors were settling, noting that Romola took her customary place at his right hand. Then he asked, "From which region of Greece do you hail, Sir? I had presumed your unfortunate homeland had been nearly drained of those sons capable of cherishing any image of her original glory, although indeed the barbarous Sultans have recently shown themselves unwilling to graft the precious vine upon their wild stock, despite having hewn it down and trampled it beneath their feet. From whence do you come?"

"I sailed last from Nauplia," Tito replied. "However, I have resided in both Constantinople and Thessalonica and traveled through various regions little visited by Western Christians since the triumph of Turkish arms. I must inform you, Sir, that I was not born in Greece, but at Bari. I spent the first sixteen years of my life in Southern Italy and Sicily."

As Tito spoke, a subtle emotion, like a breath across water, crossed Bardo's delicate features. He leaned forward, extended his right hand toward Romola, and turned his head as if intending to address her; but then, correcting himself, he turned away and spoke in a subdued voice, "Pardon me; is it not true—you are young?"

"I am twenty-three," Tito answered.

"Ah," Bardo murmured, his tone still tinged with subdued excitement, "and you doubtless had a father who cherished your early education—one who, perhaps, was a scholar himself?"

A slight pause preceded Tito's reply. To Bardo, it arrived clearly; to Romola and Nello, it began with a faint shock that seemed to ripple through the room, causing a momentary tremor of the lips, undoubtedly triggered by the resurgence of a supremely painful memory.

"Yes," Tito responded, "at least a father by adoption. He was a Neapolitan, accomplished in both Latin and Greek scholarship. But," he added after another brief hesitation, "he is lost to me—he perished on a voyage he too rashly undertook to Delos."

Bardo sank back, too refined to pose another question that might probe a sorrow he sensed was fresh. Romola, acutely aware of the specific fibers Tito's voice had touched in her father, realized with startling suddenness that this new acquaintance had breached the barrier separating them from the outside world. Nello, sensing that the apparent check on the conversation offered a graceful chance to relieve the silence, interjected, "In truth, it is as clear as Venetian glass that this fine young gentleman received the finest training. The two Cennini have already set him to work on their Greek texts, and they are not men to begin cutting before testing the edge of their tools. We may be sure they vetted him thoroughly beforehand. If there are two things that cannot be concealed—love and a cough—I posit a third: ignorance, once a man is compelled to act beyond mere nodding. The uneven barber betrays himself inevitably when he takes up the shears; is that not so, Messer Bardo? I speak as a barber, but as Luigi Pulci says:

*'Forgive me if I err: whoever listens
Let him understand my vernacular with his Latin.'*"

"Nay, my dear Nello," Bardo replied with an air of friendly severity, "you are not wholly illiterate and could doubtless achieve more respectable progress in learning if you had refrained somewhat from the *chatter* and gossip of the street corners, to which our Florentines are excessively prone; and even more so if you had not cluttered your memory with those frivolous productions for which Luigi Pulci provides the most egregious examples—a compendium of extravagances and contradictions furthest removed from the models of a pure age, resembling rather the *grylli* or conceits of a period when mystical meaning served as a warrant for monstrous forms. With this difference, however: while the monstrosity is retained, the mystical meaning is absent, creating a contemptible contrast with the great poem of Virgil. Long before Landino assumed the task of expounding the same opinion, I held with Filelfo that Virgil embodied the deepest lessons of philosophy within a graceful and well-knit fable. And I cannot help viewing the proliferation of these babbling, lawless works, despite being countenanced by patronage and to some extent emulated by Lorenzo himself—otherwise a friend of true learning—as a sign that the glorious hopes of this century are destined to be extinguished in gloom. Nay, they appear to be the delusive prologue to an age worse than the Iron Age—an age of tinsel and gossamer, where no thought possesses the substance required to be molded into consistent and enduring form."

"Once more, pardon," Nello said, spreading his palms outward and shrugging his shoulders. "I find myself knowing so many things in good Tuscan before I have time to summon the Latin for them; and Messer Luigi's rhymes invariably slip off the lips of my customers—that is what corrupts me. Indeed, speaking of customers, I have left my shop and my reputation too long in the custody of my slow Sandro, who does not deserve to be called an *uneven barber*, but rather to be pronounced simply a bungler in the vulgar tongue. So, with your permission, Messer Bardo, I shall take my leave—fully understanding that I remain at your service whenever Maso visits me. It seems a thousand years until I dress and perfume the damigella's hair, which deserves to shine in the heavens as a constellation, though indeed it would be a pity for it

Bartolommeo Scala, the secretary of the Florentine Republic, resided in a stately palace near the Porta Pinti, a building now recognized as the Casa Gherardesca. Its heraldic shield displayed an azure ladder across a golden field, bearing the motto *Gradatim* ("Step by Step") above the gate. This emblem declared to all passersby that the son of a miller had climbed to his high station solely through his own exertions, a truth he felt compelled to proclaim without shame.

Scala was a man of vanity and pomposity, yet fundamentally honest; he possessed a sincere conviction in his own worth and saw no necessity for pretense. By this time, he had ascended to the highest rung of his metaphorical ladder. Having served as secretary for twenty years, he had frequently delivered orations from the *ringhiera*, the balcony of the Old Palace, in the presence of royal guests, while the Marzocco, the symbol of the republic, donned its golden crown and the populace shouted, "Long live Messer Bartolommeo!" He had served as an ambassador to Rome, where he was appointed Titular Senator and Apostolic Secretary, knighted with the Order of the Golden Spur, and, eight years prior, elected Gonfaloniere—the ultimate prize of a Florentine citizen's ambition.

Amidst these triumphs, Scala had grown wealthier and more afflicted by gout, a common fate for successful mortals. The Knight of the Golden Spur often sat beneath the magnificent loggia he had constructed, his heel propped on a cushioned support, overlooking the expansive gardens behind his home. It was on such a day, suffering from this ailment, that he received the requested audience with Tito Melema.

The mid-May sun warmed the flowers and grass beyond the shaded loggia. Scala had discarded his heavy silk *lucco* coat in favor of a light mantle draped over his tunic. Seated beside him were his beautiful daughter, Alessandra, and her husband, Marullo, a Greek soldier-poet. Opposite them sat two friends, distinguished enough to offer counsel but not so illustrious as to dominate the conversation.

Yet, despite his physical discomfort and social standing, Scala's happiness was marred by a specific grievance: a collection of papers lying before him. These documents comprised a lengthy correspondence between himself and Angelo Poliziano (Politian). It was a peculiar habit of the era—and one difficult to comprehend today—to publicly air personal disputes and share entire exchanges of letters with scholarly acquaintances. This was not the first instance where Scala had solicited his friends' opinions on the moral weight of roughly thirty Latin letters exchanged with Poliziano, all stemming from a series of playful epigrams regarding a common insect.

It was the tale of a quintessential Renaissance squabble, significant here because it provided the necessary spark to ignite friendship's otherwise sluggish flame. Poliziano, having been spurned in his pursuit of Scala's daughter, maintained a keen, intellectual edge against the ambitious secretary who had refused the greatest scholar of the age as a son-in-law. Scala was a diligent public servant and a fortunate man—traits naturally irritating to an insulted academic. However, Scala suffered from a compulsion to write, albeit poorly. Like many such individuals, he composed poetic trifles purely for private amusement, sending them to friends who acted as the literary critics of the fifteenth century.

Scala had many admirers, including Ficino and Landino, who found his Latin prose elegant and robust. The formidable Joseph Scaliger, who would later declare Scala ignorant of Latin, belonged to the following century. Yet, the inherent coquetry of amateur authors rarely accepts flattery from friends alone. The critical, haughty Poliziano—a companion who lacked amiability—needed to know that the busy secretary possessed a fertile mind for verse, hinting at potential greatness if he were not consumed by worldly affairs.

The moment arrived when the man one secretly despises sends a Latin epigram containing a grammatical error: a false gender assignment, a dubious elision in hendecasyllables, or a poetic figure that is a glaring solecism. This moment had come for Poliziano. The secretary had poked his head out of his official duties, and the lurking critic descended upon him.

Poliziano, exercising the liberties of a friend, had penned a Latin epigram correcting Scala's mistake in assigning the feminine gender to the *culex* (gnat), an insect ubiquitous along the Arno. Scala retaliated with a witty Latin verse referencing Poliziano's failed romantic suit. Poliziano found the verses charming and amusing, unaware that they were technically flawed. Since Scala claimed to have written them imitating a Greek epigram, Poliziano, wishing to instruct rather than humiliate, enclosed a Greek poem of his own on the same subject. His piece featured a clever conceit comparing the gnat to Venus and Cupid, based on the biological fact that gnats, like Venus, were believed to arise from water—a popular theme at the time.

In response, Scala politely noted that his verses were never meant for a scholar with such refined sensibilities as Poliziano, who stood closest to ancient perfection and whose palate was so fastidious that even sturgeon would seem insipid to him. He defended his hasty, uncorrected verses as merely a summer diversion for himself and those content with mediocrity, unlike others who sought fame through publishers. Admitting he barely understood the Greek epigram Poliziano had sent, save for its basic meaning, he added a postscript: the comparison of the gnat to Venus was problematic. Venus might take offense; furthermore, unless one adhered to the doctrine of Thales regarding cold origins, the idea of a warm-loving creature emerging from water was doubtful. Perhaps a fish, an eagle, or even a bat or owl would be a more apt parallel given the gnat's flight capabilities and nocturnal habits.

Here lay a fresh opportunity for Poliziano. Unaware that Scala had raised questions about sturgeon, he clarified that his original intent involved frogs and gudgeons. He swiftly dismantled Scala's defenses of his Latin and savagely criticized his pedantic objections to the Greek epigram. "Wretched cavils," Poliziano wrote. Regarding the gnat's watery origin, Virgil himself called it the "offspring of waters"; as for Scala's comparisons to fish, eagles, and bats, they were irrelevant (*nihil ad rem*). Just because an eagle flies higher does not prove a gnat cannot fly.

Shamed by dwelling on such trifles and inflating a gnat into an elephant, Poliziano concluded by asserting his integrity. He offered no deceit, nor would he be swayed by the false praises of slanderers. Quoting Homer, he suggested that true character reveals itself regardless of absent criticism.

The dispute escalated. Logic deteriorated, Scala became increasingly pompous in his self-assurance, and Poliziano's temper grew venomous. Poliziano, generous in spirit, hoped to hold up a mirror to the inflated secretary, hoping Scala would recognize his ignorance and stop defending bad Latin against ancient authorities. Unless, perhaps, Scala intended to sink in literature as he rose in political rank, thereby balancing the scales for men of letters. In exchange, Poliziano invited Scala to examine his own writings, promising to find devout admiration for antiquity within them. Scala expressed shame for his contemporary age, claiming he could not elevate his own works to match divine antiquity, yet insisted his honors were well-earned, citing his rise from poverty in Florence.

Poliziano retorted that he was unsurprised Scala disliked the age, noting that he himself was equally disliked. He accurately recalled calling Scala a "branniest monster," formed from the refuse of mills, perfectly suited for the dusty task of grinding grain (*in pistrini sordibus natus et quidem pistrino dignissimus*).

These papers were produced specifically due to Tito Melema's impending arrival. Scala wished to test the new Greek scholar's judgment on Poliziano's epigram. After discussing Tito's travels, examining gemstones, and transitioning from Lorenzo de' Medici's art collections to broader classical tastes, the topic inevitably turned to Poliziano. Described as a man of immense talent but excessive arrogance, Poliziano assumed the role of a Hercules destined to destroy the literary monstrosities of the age. He wrote unsigned letters to his elders as if they were divine revelations. Were not his own criticisms sometimes questionable and his tastes perverse? Fond of mocking those who mimicked Cicero by ending sentences with *esse videtur*, did he not fall into the opposite trap of chasing obscure words and affected phrasing? Was every point in his celebrated *Miscellanea* truly defensible?

Tito, eager to please, examined the *Miscellanea* and found much to admire. Consequently, he proved himself worthy to adjudicate the famous correspondence regarding the *culex*. Scala presented the Greek epigram, which Poliziano undoubtedly considered the finest in the world, though he had feigned belief that Greeks abroad would dismiss it. Had not Poliziano spoken the truth in his false modesty?

Tito, acting as the wise young judge, analyzed the epigram to Scala's satisfaction. He appreciated satire even in the vernacular. Scala, a good host, offered a few unpublished sonnets targeting Poliziano's foibles, intending them as light refreshment for the company. Satisfied, Tito departed under an urgent invitation to return.

His interest lingered on the gems Scala had shown him, particularly an agate featuring a natural formation resembling Cupid riding a lion, and a "Jew's Stone" depicting a lion-headed serpent. Scala agreed to purchase both; the latter was intended as a talisman against his debilitating gout

For a Northerner, rising on Midsummer morning is a simple affair: one sees the dew clinging to the grassy verge of a dusty path, notices the tender shoots pushing through the dark green of oaks and firs in the coppice, and looks over the gate at the freshly shorn meadow, all without recalling that it is the Nativity of Saint John the Baptist.

Not so for the Florentine—and certainly not for the Florentine of the fifteenth century. To him, that specific morning held a distinct brilliance; the eastern sun striking the Arno possessed a special quality, and the ringing of the bells was articulate, proclaiming the great summer festival of Florence, the Day of San Giovanni.

San Giovanni had served as Florence's patron saint for at least eight centuries, ever since Lombard Queen Theodolinda commanded her subjects to honor him peculiarly. Indeed, according to the old chronicler Villani, this devotion stretched back to the days of Constantine the Great and Pope Sylvester, when the Florentines deposed their idol, Mars. They did so carefully, however, avoiding any insult; while they consecrated their beautiful temple to God and "Blessed Master Saint John," they placed the old god of war respectfully upon a high tower near the River Arno. Ancient memorials claimed Mars had been elected their tutelary deity under specific astral influences, warning that if he were broken or treated with indignity, the city would suffer great damage and upheaval. But by the fifteenth century, this discreet regard for the feelings of the Man-destroyer had long vanished. The god of spear and shield ceased to frown beside the Arno, and the defenses of the Republic were believed to lie in its craft and its coffers. For spears and shields could be hired with gold florins, and on those very gold florins, there had always been the image of San Giovanni.

Much good had come to Florence since the dim times of struggle between the old patron and the new: undoubtedly, there had been quarrels and bloodshed between Guelf and Ghibelline, Black and White, between orthodox sons of the Church and heretical Paterini; there had been floods, famines, and pestilences. Yet, amidst this, there had been immense wealth and glory. Florence had achieved conquests over walled cities once mightier than itself, particularly over hated Pisa, whose marble buildings were too high and beautiful, and whose masts were too honored on the Greek and Italian coasts. The name of Florence grew prouder in every court of Europe, even in Africa, bolstered by the purest gold coinage, finest dyes and textiles, pre-eminent scholarship, poetic genius, and wits of the most serviceable sort for statesmanship and banking. It was a name so omnipresent that a Pope with a turn for epigrams called Florentines "the fifth element." And for this high destiny, though it might partly depend on the stars and the Madonna dell'Impruneta, and certainly on other higher powers less often named, the praise was largely due to San Giovanni, whose image graced the fair gold florins.

Therefore, it was fitting that the Day of San Giovanni—an ancient Church festival already venerable in the days of Saint Augustine—should be a day of peculiar rejoicing for Florence. It was ushered in by a vigil kept in strict old Florentine fashion, filled with dancing, street jesting, and perhaps not a little stone-throwing and window-breaking, but emphatically with sights unique to a city employing a clever engineer and architect named Cecca. With Cecca's help, the very saints, surrounded by almond-shaped glory and floating on clouds with their joyful retinue of winged cherubs (as seen today in the works of Perugino), seemed to bring a piece of heaven down into the narrow streets, passing slowly through them. More wonderfully still, saints of gigantic size, accompanied by attendant angels, moved in a slow, mysterious manner along the thoroughfares, like a procession of colossal figures descended from the high domes and tribunes of the churches. The clouds were made of fine woven stuff; the saints and cherubs were unglorified mortals supported by firm bars; and those mysterious giants were actually men of steady brains, balancing themselves on stilts and enlarged, like Greek tragedians, by huge masks and stuffed shoulders. One would be a miserably unimaginative Florentine to think only of this mechanical trick—indeed, somewhat impious, for in the images of sacred things lay some of the virtue of the sacred things themselves. Following this came a company of merry black demons, well-armed with claws and thongs and other implements of sport, ready to perform impromptu farces of bastinadoing and clothes-tearing. Why, that was simply the demons' way of keeping vigil, and they, too, might have descended from the domes. The Tuscan mind slipped from the devout to the burlesque as readily as water rounds an angle. The saints had already done their turn, paused before the gates of San Giovanni to honor him on the eve of his *festa*, and departed. On the morrow, the great day thus inaugurated, it was fitting that the tributary symbols paid by Florence's dependent cities, districts, and villages—whether conquered, protected, or held by immemorial possession—should be offered at the shrine of San Giovanni in the old octagonal church, once the cathedral and now the baptistery, where every Florentine had received the sign of the Cross with anointing chrism on his brow. From the white-haired elder to the stripling, from the matron to the lisping child, all were clothed in their best to honor the great day and witness the great sight. When the sun began to slope and the streets cooled, there would follow the glorious race or *Corso*: unsaddled horses, clad in rich trappings, running right across the city from the Porta al Prato on the northwest, through the Mercato Vecchio, to the Porta Santa Croce on the southeast, where the richest *Palii*—velvet and brocade banners with silk linings and fringes of gold, suitable for a city that half-clothed the well-dressed world—awaited the winner on a triumphal car.

Thereafter followed more dancing. Nay, throughout the entire day, as an old chronicler noted at the beginning of that century, there were weddings, grandest gatherings, piping, music, song, balls, feasts, gladness, and ornament, such that this earth might have been mistaken for Paradise!

Yet in this year of 1492, it was perhaps a little less easy to make that mistake. Lorenzo the Magnificent and Subtle was dead, replaced by an arrogant and incautious Piero, an evil change for Florence unless, indeed, the wise horse prefers the bad rider, as he is more easily thrown from the saddle. Already, regrets for Lorenzo were yielding to murmured desires for government on a broader basis, where corruption might be arrested and there might exist that free play for everyone's jealousy and ambition which characterized the ideal liberty of the good old quarrelsome, struggling times when Florence raised her great buildings, reared her own soldiers, drove out would-be tyrants at the sword's point, and was proud to keep faith at her own loss. Lorenzo was dead; Pope Innocent was dying; and a troublesome Neapolitan succession, coupled with an intriguing, ambitious Milan, threatened to set Italy by the ears shortly.
