The times were likely to be difficult. Still, there was all the more reason for the Republic to maintain its religious festivals.

Midsummer morning in this year of 1492 was no less bright than usual. Early in the morning, the symbolic offerings destined for the grand procession were assembled at their starting point in the Piazza della Signoria—that famous square where stood the massive, turreted Palace of the People, the Palazzo Vecchio, and the spacious Loggia built by Orcagna, the scene of all grand state ceremony. The sky formed the fairest blue tent, beneath which the bells swung so vigorously that every evil spirit with sense enough to be formidable must long since have fled. Windows and terraced roofs were alive with human faces; sombre stone houses glowed with hanging draperies. The boldly soaring palace tower, the yet older square tower of the Bargello, and the spire of the neighboring Badia seemed to keep watch above. Below, on the broad polygonal flags of the piazza, unfolded the glorious display of banners, horses with rich trappings, and gigantic *ceri*, or tapers—fitly called towers—strangely aggrandized descendants of those torches by whose faint light the Church worshipped in the Catacombs. Early in the morning, all processions needed to move under the Midsummer sky of Florence, where the shelter of narrow streets had to be periodically exchanged for the glare of wide spaces; the sun would be high in the heavens before the long pomp completed its pilgrimage in the Piazza di San Giovanni.

But here, where the procession was to pause, the magnificent city, with its ingenious Cecca, had provided another tent than the sky. The entire Piazza del Duomo, from the octagonal baptistery in the center to the façade of the cathedral and the walls of the houses lining the quadrangle, was covered, at a height of forty feet or more, with blue drapery adorned with well-stitched yellow lilies and familiar coats of arms. Sheaves of many-colored banners drooped at fit angles beneath this superincumbent blue, creating a gorgeous, rainbow-lit shelter for the waiting spectators leaning from windows, forming a narrow border on the pavement, and wishing for the arrival of the

Tito plunged quickly into the throng. Though he offered Nello a casual shrug regarding his recent encounter, a fleeting longing stirred within him as he navigated toward the Corso degli Adimari. He wished, against reason, to catch sight of the pair of blue eyes that had gazed upward from beneath the white linen hood of the countrywoman at the festival. He knew well that face belonged to Tessa, yet he had carefully avoided naming her. What concern was Nello's business? Tito possessed an innate gift for silence—a talent that operated without conscious intent. Like many who find concealment effortless, he occasionally hid things that required no secrecy whatsoever, such as the mere fact that he had once spotted a flock of crows. However, this wistful thought for Tessa was swiftly overshadowed by the persistent memory of the friar whose gaze had held an unsettling significance for him. Why had that pale, fanatical figure stared specifically at *him*? Where else in his travels could he have seen such a face? "Folly!" he chided himself mentally. Vague memories cling to the mind like irritating cobwebs, demanding attention best dispelled by a sharp dismissal. With a mental shake-off, Tito turned his focus elsewhere.

By the time he emerged from the Corso into a side street, his sole concern was the height of the sun and the delay caused by the procession. He had missed his appointment with the room in the Via de' Bardi, where his arrival was anxiously anticipated. In his imagination, he rehearsed the scene: the blind man's face glowing with diffuse joy, like light filtering through translucent glass; the faint, rosy flush on Romola's cheeks and neck, adding a delicate femininity to her inherent nobility, enhanced further by a boyish openness in her expression and smile. Together, they were perfect companions around the invalid's chair. She appealed to him constantly, and he informed her, yet he felt strangely subject to Romola's simplicity. For the first time, he experienced a profound, undefined reverence in the presence of noble womanhood—a sentiment akin to the ancient worship of a goddess whose power was deeper and more primal than mere omniscience.

They had never been alone, leaving him unable to construct any plausible scenario of romantic intimacy. All he could conjure was a wild fantasy: that someday Romola would confess her love. He recalled a day in Greece, leaning against a sun-drenched wall, when a small, dark-eyed peasant girl had timidly approached, resting her water pot nearby. Shyly, she had asked for a kiss, pressing her round, olive-toned cheek against his in innocent trust. Tito was accustomed to love arriving in such unsolicited bursts. But Romola's love would never come this way. Would it ever come at all?—yet it remained the highest prize on which he had set his mind.

He was in the prime of his youth—not consumed by passion, but deeply impressionable. It was as natural for him to feel drawn to Romola as it was for white iris flowers to reflect in a sunlit stream. Lacking any pretense of superiority, he nonetheless sensed that Romola stood far above him. Many men have felt this distance before a wide-eyed, simple child. Still, Tito had achieved rapid success that might have emboldened others to presume too much. He had convinced fair judges that his talents matched his good fortune, wearing his status with such ease that no offense had been taken. He was destined for the finest Florentine society, a circle where fine plate abounded and the Signory permitted the wearing of rich brocades. Where else would a handsome young scholar be welcomed, capable of playing the lute and singing joyful songs? His bright countenance, easy smile, and liquid voice seemed to bring a holiday spirit to the weary and the sad. He was a professor likely to make the Greek classics beloved by the sons of great families.

But this was not Tito's entire fortune. He had sold all his jewels save for one ring he refused to part with, bringing him into possession of five hundred gold florins. The moment he first held this sum marked the climax of the first serious internal struggle his cheerful nature had faced. An importunate thought, previously ignored as a shadow trailing his steps, suddenly seized him. He was forced to pause and decide: would he submit to duty, or offer a refusal carrying irreversible consequences?

It was in the room above Nello's shop, which Tito now rented, that the elder Cennini delivered the final portion of the funds on behalf of Bernardo Rucellai, the buyer of the two precious gems.

"Ecco, giovane mio!" the respected printer and goldsmith declared. "You now possess a modest fortune. If you will take my advice, deposit these florins in a secure place where they may grow, rather than letting them slip through your fingers on banquets and youthful follies. It has become too fashionable among scholars, particularly those like our Pietro Crinito who believe their studies require scent and embroidery, to squander wealth with one hand until they must beg with the other. I have brought you the money; you are free to make a wise or unwise choice. I shall observe which way the scales tip. We Florentines consider a man a member of an Art only after he has demonstrated his skill and been matriculated; similarly, no one is truly initiated into the Art of Life until they have been thoroughly tempted. If you decide to invest your florins in usury, let me know tomorrow. Scholars may marry and should have provisions ready for the *morgen-cap*. Addio."

As Cennini closed the door, Tito turned, his smile fading, fixing his gaze on the table where the coins lay. He made no other move, standing with his thumbs hooked in his belt, staring downward in a trance-like state of intense concentration.

"A man's ransom!" Who had spoken those words? Wasn't five hundred florins precisely the price of a man's freedom? Suppose, under this midday sun on some distant, hot coast, a man of advanced years—one possessing lofty thoughts and a passionate heart, who decades prior had rescued a boy from beggary, filth, and cruelty, raising him tenderly as a father—was now toiling as a slave? Perhaps he was hacking wood or hauling water, beaten and bruised for lacking agility. Did he whisper to himself, "Tito will find me"? Could he not simply transport the manuscripts and gems to Venice, raise funds, and pursue his rescuer relentlessly? If this were certain, could Tito, seeing the gem-money before him, choose to remain in Florence, basking in the soft airs of promised love and prosperity, refusing to risk everything for his benefactor? No, certainly not, *if it were certain*.

Yet, nothing was certain. The galley had indeed been intercepted by a Turkish vessel en route to Delos, according to reports from the escaping companion ship. But there had been resistance, likely bloodshed, and a man had been seen plunging into the sea. Who survived amidst the chaos? What befell the multitude of possibilities? Had not Tito himself suffered shipwreck and narrowly escaped drowning? He had ample reason to fear the omnipresent danger of accidents that could render all plans futile. Rumors of pirate settlements on Delos were unreliable, possibly irrelevant. What would happen if he abandoned Florence for Venice, secured official letters, and sailed for the Archipelago? Most likely, he would be captured himself, spending all his florins on preliminary expenses before becoming another destitute wanderer, with no gems left to sell.

Tito saw this outcome with greater clarity than the moment he might locate his father and grant him deliverance. It would surely be unjust for him, in his full maturity, having endured the restrictions of schooling, to turn his back on promised love and distinction, only to lose both forever.

"And yet," he murmured to himself, "if I were certain Baldassarre Calvo was alive, and that I could free him through any effort or peril, I would leave now—I have the money. It was pointless to debate before. I would go to Bardo and Bartolommeo Scala and reveal the whole truth."

Tito did not explicitly acknowledge to himself that if those two men knew the full story, he would have no choice but to seek his benefactor, the rightful owner of the gems, whom he had ambiguously referred to as "lost." Nor did he admit what he already knew: that distinguished Greeks frequently made sacrifices, undertook repeated voyages, and petitioned crowned heads to liberate relatives from Turkish slavery. Public opinion did not view this as exceptional virtue.

This was his first genuine dialogue with his own conscience. Until now, he had followed the impulses of the moment, concealing half the truth without considering his motives long enough to confront them. What was the point of revealing everything? True, the thought had crossed his mind several times since leaving Nauplia that it was a relief to be rid of Baldassarre, and he would have preferred to know exactly who had fallen overboard. But such thoughts arise inevitably from relationships that have become burdensome. Baldassarre was demanding and grew stranger with age. Constantly scrutinizing Tito's mind to match his own inflated expectations, the elderly man—thick-set, heavy-browed, and bald, past sixty—had lost the attractiveness of his intellectual intensity, which had devolved into monotonous repetition. Stranded among strangers, unless he possessed the philosopher's stone, Baldassarre would find few admirers offering rank, youth, and beauty. Feelings fueled by novelty cannot build a home for dimmed spirits; if any love remains, it must be that rooted in memory, distilling the sweet balsam of fidelity and patient tenderness.

Were such

Three weeks had passed since Tito delivered his florins to Cennini. When he set out toward the Via de' Bardi, he carried himself with the visible ease of a mind entirely at peace. How could it be otherwise? His nature was too buoyant, too unburdened by fear, for hidden dangers or distant troubles to weigh upon him. Stepping from the blinding noonday sun into the cool shadow of a narrow alley, he removed his black *berretta*—a simple cap with an upturned flap—that framed his brown curls. He tossed his head back to invite the breeze, revealing a forehead that was neither marked by deceit nor stamped with naive innocence; it was simply a finely formed, smooth, square surface of youth. His slow, wandering glance at the upper windows of the houses possessed neither cunning nor guile, merely the innocent, wide-open gaze of a child's eye, clear and unclouded.

Was Tito's face a cipher, readable only by those who knew the specific key of the observer's heart? The dominant expression in his entire demeanor was a profound negativity—not of malice, but of emptiness. It was a truthful declaration of freedom from anxiety, restless vanity, or any uneasy claim upon his spirit. Consequently, the admiration that trailed him through the throngs of holidaymakers was offered willingly and without reservation.

By this time, the excitement of the Feast of St. John had seeped even into the smallest side streets. The massive crowds that had once lined the procession route now streamed outward in every direction, chasing new distractions. These interludes of a festival are precisely when the latent energy of a multitude becomes petulant, ready to sacrifice a single individual for the collective amusement. As Tito approached the neighborhood of San Martino, the crowd thickened. Near the inn known as the *Bertucce*, or Baboons, a cluster of people had gathered around some unseen attraction.

Tito prepared to turn aside when, cutting through the noise of laughter, his ears caught a distressed, childish cry: "Loose me! Holy Virgin, help me!" This sound compelled him to push his way into the knot of onlookers. He realized instantly that the voice belonged to a young peasant girl (*contadina*) whose white hood had slipped during her struggle to escape the grip of a man dressed in the motley garb of a *cerretano*, or conjurer. The magician was attempting to soothe and cajole her, earning the amused sympathy of the spectators. Through a chorus of mocking words calling her a simpleton—the Florentine tongue being rich in such epithets—they argued with the girl against her stubborn resistance.

At first, the girl's face was turned away; Tito saw only her light-brown hair, plaited and pinned with a long silver needle. But the struggle soon forced her face toward him. There, through the mist of swelling tears, he recognized the baby features of Tessa. Her blue eyes were filled with moisture, her lower lip trembling. Yet, Tessa also saw *him*. A sudden hope, akin to that which lights a child's face when held captive by a stranger and spotting a familiar hand reaching out, beamed across her tear-stained visage.

In an instant, Tito broke through the barrier of curious bystanders. Grasping Tessa's waist, he demanded, "Loose this child! What right have you to hold her against her will?"

The conjurer—a man with a face where the angles of the eyes, brows, nose, mouth, and jaw all tilted upward—displayed a row of small, regular teeth in an impish yet harmless grin. He released Tessa's hands, stretching his own backward, shrugging his shoulders in a half-apologetic, half-protesting gesture.

"I mean the ragazza no evil in the world, Messere," the conjurer replied. "Ask this respectable company. I was merely demonstrating a few tricks, in which this little damsel might have assisted due to her kitten-like face, promising her a handful of confetti as a reward. But what then? Messer surely has better confetti at hand, and she knows it."

A wave of laughter rippled through the crowd, likely provoked by the look of relief and confidence with which Tessa clung to Tito's arm as he drew her close and placed her hand within his. To Tessa, the laughter was as terrifying as the roar of wild beasts from which she was fleeing; she attached no meaning to it. Tito, however, foreseeing the awkwardness of having interrupted an entertainment, hastened to move away from observers who, deprived of their amusement, would inevitably seek to restore the balance with jests.

"See, see, little one! Here is your hood," the conjurer called out, flinging the piece of white drapery over Tessa's head. "*Orsù*, bear me no malice; come back to me when Messere can spare you."

"Ah! Maestro Vaiano, she'll return presently, as the toad said to the harrow," shouted a spectator, noting how Tessa shrank back at the conjurer's dismissal.

Vexed by this delay in his journey to the Via de' Bardi, Tito pushed vigorously toward the corner of a side street. Before they had fully entered, he addressed her in a kind but hurried tone, "Now, little one, where were you going? Did you come to the Festa alone?"

"No!" Tessa answered, her face regaining its look of fright and distress. "I have lost my mother in the crowd—my mother and my father-in-law. They will be angry; he will beat me. It happened in the crowd at San Pulinari; someone pushed me, and I couldn't stop myself, so I drifted away from them. Oh, I don't know where they've gone! Please, don't leave me!"

Her eyes swelled anew with tears, ending in a sob.

Tito hurried onward; the Church of the Badia was nearby. They could enter via the cloister at the rear, where he might speak with her—or perhaps leave her there. But it was past the hour when the church was open. Pausing under the shelter of the cloister, he asked, "Do you have no cousin or friend in Florence whose house you could reach? Or are you afraid to walk alone after being frightened by the conjurer? I am in a rush to get to Oltrarno, but if I could take you somewhere near—"

"Oh, I *am* frightened," Tessa whispered. "He was the devil—I know he was. I don't know where to go. I have nobody. My mother meant to have her dinner somewhere, and I don't know where. Holy Madonna! I shall be beaten."

The corners of her pouting mouth drooped pitifully, and the poor little bosom, adorned with prayer beads above her green serge gown, heaved violently. A loud sob was inevitable, and the great tears fell as if trying to make up for lost time. Here was a dilemma! To leave her would be brutal, and Tito's nature was all gentleness. Wishing he hadn't been summoned to the Via de' Bardi, he watched her lift her holiday apron to catch the rushing tears. Laying his own hand upon the fabric, he gently wiped one of her cheeks and kissed the baby-like roundness.

"My poor little Tessa! Leave off crying. Let us see what can be done. Where is your home—where do you live?"

There was no immediate answer, only subsiding sobs and slower-falling tears.

"Come! I'll take you a little way, if you'll tell me where you want to go."

The apron fell, and Tessa's face transformed, becoming as contented as a cherub emerging from a cloud. The conjurer, the anger, and the threat of beating seemed worlds away.

"I think I'll go home, if you'll take me," she said in a half-whisper, looking up at Tito with wide blue eyes and a sweetness surpassing a smile—a childlike calm.

"Come, then, little one," Tito replied in a caressing tone, tucking her arm inside his again. "Which way is it?"

"Beyond Peretola—where the large pear-tree is."

"Peretola? Which gate, *pazzarella*? Remember, I am a stranger."

"Out at the Por del Prato," Tessa said, gripping his arm tightly as they moved forward.

Unfamiliar with every twist and turn, and realizing that the busiest streets offered the greatest chance of encountering Monna Ghita to conclude his quest, Tito steered them straight for Porta Rossa and then toward Ognissanti. He presented his usual bright, propitiatory face to the mixed crowd, who threw their jests at him and his heavy-shod maiden with generous abandon. Alongside the decent holiday-goers were frolicking apprentices, envious of his good fortune; bold-eyed women wearing the yellow veil of penance; beggars thrusting forward their caps in mockery of his haste; gamblers, sharpers, and loafers of the worst sort; and boys trained to chant insults at brutal street games. For the streets of Florence were not always a moral spectacle in those days, and Tessa's terror of being lost in the crush was entirely justified.

Upon reaching the Piazza d'Ognissanti, Tito slowed his pace. Both were heated from their hurried walk, and here was a wider space to breathe. They sat on one of the stone benches lining the walls of the old houses.

"Holy Virgin!" Tessa exclaimed. "I am glad we have escaped those women and boys;

When Fra Luca finished speaking, Tito remained standing beside him, paralyzed by indecision. Only after the crowd of pilgrims began to thin and the friar rose to walk slowly toward the church of Santa Felicita did Tito follow, turning down the Via de' Bardi.

"If this monk is truly Florentine," he muttered to himself, "and if he intends to stay in Florence, then everything must come out." He sensed a new crisis looming, yet he was not so visibly shaken that he couldn't proceed with his scheduled visit to Bardo to apologize for his recent absence. Tito's skill at concealment was rapidly evolving from a passive trait into something more calculated. While it was certainly possible—even perhaps inevitable—that he should openly accept the changed circumstances and confess Baldassarre's existence, doing so would cast an uncomfortable light backward on his earlier silence. It would appear as a deliberate evasion designed to dodge a recognized obligation, compounded by the fact that he had already quietly settled himself and invested his florins before his benefactor's fate was confirmed.

For now, prudence dictated acting as though nothing had occurred. There was the entire night ahead for reflection, and no one would know the precise hour he had received the letter. Stepping into the room on the second floor—where Romola and her father sat surrounded by parchment and marble, isolated from the street life whether on holy days or ordinary ones—he wore a face only slightly brighter than usual, masking his regret over arriving late. He had sacrificed the spectacle of the Corso specifically to hide this regret, but he quickly masked it again, injecting extra animation into the evening conversation. Throughout the interaction, however, his mind operated like a complex machine, depositing thoughts entirely separate from the flow of dialogue. By the time he descended the stone stairs and emerged from the grim doorway into the starlight, his mind had genuinely advanced to a new stage in forming a purpose.

The following day, once freed from his professorial duties, he turned up the Via del Cocomero toward the convent of San Marco, his plan fully crystallized. He intended to interrogate Fra Luca directly about how much he suspected the truth and on what grounds; furthermore, he needed to know how long the friar planned to remain at San Marco. With that fuller knowledge, he hoped to craft a statement that would allow him to remain in Florence without having to flee.

Tito had never before needed to construct an ingenious falsehood, but the opportunity had finally arrived—a chance that circumstance invariably creates for those living in silent deception. His ingenuity was instantly ready. He had convinced himself that he was under no obligation to hunt down Baldassarre. He had previously stated that upon receiving fair assurance of his father's existence and whereabouts, he would immediately depart to find him. But why, exactly, was he bound to go? If examined closely, what was the ultimate goal of life but to extract the maximum sum of pleasure? Was not his own vibrant, blooming existence a promise of incomparably greater joy—not just for himself, but for others—as compared to the withered, wintry life of a man past the age of keen enjoyment, whose ideas had hardened into barren rigidity? Those rigid ideas had been planted in the fresh soil of Tito's mind; they were lively seeds there. That was the natural order: nature treated maturity merely as a nesting ground for youth. Baldassarre had done his work; he had drawn his fill of life. Now, it was Tito's turn.

The prospect was vague—"I think they are taking me to Antioch"—yet it offered a dazzling vista. After a long voyage, spending months or years searching for a quest that might ultimately prove futile, he would leave behind a life of distinction and love. And if he did find anything, he would return to the old, exacting companionship he already knew by heart. Certainly, the gems—and thus the florins—belonged to Baldassarre in a narrow, legalistic sense, the kind used in ordinary affairs. But in a broader, more radically natural view, where the world belongs to youth and strength, they belonged to whoever could derive the most pleasure from them.

He was acutely aware that this sentiment contradicted societal expectations. The men around him would demand he immediately apply those florins to his benefactor's rescue. But what was the sentiment of society anyway? A mere tangled web of anomalous traditions and opinions, unworthy of a wise man's guidance unless it served his own comfort. Did he care for the florins? Perhaps only for Romola's sake; he would gladly give them up. It was the joy that was rightfully his, hovering close to his lips, that he felt compelled to keep rather than thrust aside to travel onward, parched and empty. Any maxim requiring a man to discard the goods necessary to make existence sweet was merely the lining of human selfishness turned outward, crafted by those who wished others to sacrifice themselves for their benefit.

He preferred that Baldassarre not suffer; he disliked seeing anyone suffer. Yet, could any philosophy prove he was obligated to care for another's suffering more than his own? To believe so, he would need to love Baldassarre devotedly, and he did not. Was that his fault? Gratitude, when scrutinized closely, made no valid claim. His father's life would have been dreary without him; were we truly indebted to men for the pleasures they grant themselves? Once he began dismantling Baldassarre's claim, Tito's thoughts moved with the speed of a virulent acid, eating rapidly through every tissue of sentiment.

His mind lacked the dread often dismissed as mere animal self-preservation—the awe of Divine Nemesis felt by religious pagans, or the Christian equivalent, a vague terror of anything deemed "wrongdoing." Such fear of the unseen transcended simple cowardice; it was the initial recognition of a moral law restraining desire, checking the harsh scrutiny of imperfect thought regarding obligations that possess no sanctity without feeling.

"It is good," sang the old Eumenides in Aeschylus, "that fear should sit as the guardian of the soul, forcing it into wisdom... good that men should carry a threatening shadow in their hearts under the full sunshine; else, how should they learn to revere the right?" That guardianship might eventually become unnecessary, but only when all outward law becomes superfluous—only when duty and love unite into a single stream of force.

As Tito entered the outer cloister of San Marco and asked for Fra Luca, there was no shadowy presentiment in his mind. He considered himself too cultured and skeptical for such superstition. He had been raised in contempt for the scandalous lives of priests, a proverbial topic, and possessed an erudite familiarity with debates on the Chief Good, which he ultimately viewed as a matter of personal taste. Still, fear was a potent element within Tito's nature: the fear of losing pleasure. He harbored a definite anxiety that Fra Luca might be the instrument that drove him from Florence.

"Fra Luca? Ah, he has gone to Fiesole—to the Dominican monastery there. He was carried off on a litter early this morning. The poor Brother is very ill. Could you leave a message for him?"

The reply came from a *fra converso*, a lay brother whose accent revealed him to be a rough countryman (*contadino*), and whose dull gaze suggested no curiosity whatsoever.

"Thank you; my business can wait."

Tito turned away with a profound sense of relief. "This friar is unlikely to survive," he told himself. "I saw he was worn down to a shadow. And at Fiesole, there will be nothing to remind him of me. Besides, if he returns, my explanation will serve just as well then as now. Though I wonder what it was about his face that struck me."

Tito walked with a light, buoyant step. The paralyzing fear that had gripped him earlier had dissolved, allowing his natural disposition to reclaim its cheerful dominance. His destination was clear: he was going to see Romola. Yet, as he approached, the image of her life struck him—a portrait of loving, pitying devotion and patient endurance of tedious duties, qualities from which he had previously shrank and excused himself. However, Tito was not devoid of love for goodness, nor was he prepared to plunge fully into vice. He was still in the flush of youth, his pulses soft to all charm and loveliness, retaining a healthy appetite for ordinary human joys. The poison worked slowly, by degrees. Though he had sold himself to evil, life currently felt so unchanged to him that he remained unconscious of the bonds he had forged. He intended for everything to proceed exactly as before, both within and without him. He planned to win golden reputations through meritorious exertion, ingenious learning, and amiable compliance. He had no intention of doing anything that would disrupt his harmony with the beings he cherished. Above all, he cared for Romola; he desired to have her as his beautiful, loving wife. While a wealthier alliance might theoretically await someone of his eventual success, there was no woman in all Florence comparable to her. When she stood near him, regarding him with her sincere hazel eyes, he was subdued by a delicious influence as powerful and inevitable as the rhythmic vibrations of music that seize the soul, demanding to begin anew the moment they cease.

Even as he climbed the stone stairs, still outside the door with only Maso beside him, this influence seemed to activate merely through the anticipation of her presence.

"Welcome, *Tito mio*," the old man's voice greeted him before Tito could speak. There was a renewed vigor in the voice, a fresh cheerfulness in the blind face, since their first meeting more than two months prior. "You have brought fresh manuscripts, doubtless; but since we spoke last night, I have conceived new ideas. We must broaden our scope—we must return upon our footsteps."

Tito paid his customary homage to Romola as he advanced, then went straight to Bardo's chair, placing his hand in the palm extended to receive it. He settled onto the cross-legged leather seat with scrolled ends, positioning himself close to Bardo's elbow.

"Yes," Tito replied gently. "I have brought the new manuscript, but that can wait until your pleasure dictates. I have young limbs, you know, and can walk back up the hill without any difficulty."

He did not look at Romola while speaking, yet he knew perfectly well that her eyes were fixed upon him with delight.

"That is well said, my son," Bardo remarked, having recently adopted this form of address. "I perceive with gladness that you do not shrink from labor, without which, as the poet wisely noted, life gives nothing to mortals. Too often, young ambition seeks the *palma sine pulvere*, the prize of glory without the dust of the race. But what does the Greek say? 'In the morning of life, work; in the mid-day, give counsel; in the evening, pray.' It is true, I might be thought to have reached that helpless evening; but not so, while I possess counsel within me that remains unspoken. For my mind, as I have often stated, was shut up as by a dam; the plentiful waters lay dark and motionless. But you, my Tito, have opened a duct for them, and they rush forward with a force that surprises even myself. Now, what I require is that we review our preliminary ground again, adopting a wider scheme of commentary and illustration. Otherwise, I may lose opportunities which I now see retrospectively, chances that may never recur. Do you mark what I am saying, Tito?"

Just then, Bardo stooped to retrieve his manuscript, which had rolled down, and his jealous ear caught the slightest movement. Tito might have been excused for shrugging at the prospect ahead, but he was not naturally impatient. Moreover, he had been bred in that laborious erudition, both minute and copious, which defined the chief intellectual pursuit of the age. With Romola nearby, he was floated along by waves of agreeable sensation that made every task appear effortless.

"Assuredly," Tito agreed. "You wish to enlarge your comments on certain passages we have cited."

"Not only so," Bardo insisted. "I wish to introduce occasional *excursuses* where we have studied authors to whom I have given special attention, for I may die too soon to complete a separate work. Furthermore, this is not a time for scholarly integrity and well-sifted learning to remain idle. We must fear not only rash ignorance, but men like Calderino, who, as Poliziano has demonstrated, resort to impudent falsifications of citation to serve their vanity and secure triumphs for their own errors. Therefore, my Tito, I deem it ill-advised to let slip the opportunity beneath our hands. Let us turn back to the point where we cited the passage from Thucydides. First, I wish you to accompany me through all my notes on the Latin translation by Lorenzo Valla. The incomparable Pope Nicholas the Fifth—who honored me with his personal notice when I was young, and when he was still Thomas of Sarzana—paid him the sum of five hundred gold scudi (I say not unduly). Yet, since Valla, though otherwise of dubious fame, is held in high honor for his severe scholarship, whence the epigrammatist jocosely declared that since he descended to the shades, Pluto himself dares not speak in the ancient languages, it is all the more necessary that his name should not serve as a stamp warranting false goods. Thus, I propose introducing an *excursus* on Thucydides, wherein my castigation of Valla's text may find a fitting place. My Romola, you will fetch the needed volumes—you know them—from the fifth shelf of the cabinet."

Tito rose simultaneously with Romola, declaring, "I will fetch them, if you will point them out," and hurriedly followed her into the adjoining small room, where the walls were lined with ranges of books arranged in perfect order.

"There they are," Romola said, pointing upward. "Every book rests exactly where it was when my father ceased to see them."

Tito stood beside her, delaying his approach to the books. They had never shared this room together before.

"I hope," she continued, turning her gaze fully upon Tito with a look of grave confidence, "that he will not weary you; this work brings him such happiness."

"And me too, Romola—if you will only allow me to say I love you—if you will only consider me worthy of a little love."

His words were the softest murmur. The dark, beautiful face, drawn closer to hers than ever before, regarded her with beseeching tenderness.

"I do love you," Romola whispered. She looked at him with the same simple majesty as always, yet her voice had never before sunk to such a murmur. To both of them, it seemed they stared into each other's eyes for a long duration before her lips moved again; yet it was but a moment until she said, "I know *now* what it is to be happy."

Their faces met, and the dark curls mingled for an instant with the rippling gold. Swift as lightning thereafter, Tito stepped onto a projecting ledge of the bookshelves and retrieved the required volumes. Both were content to remain silent and separate, for that first blissful experience of mutual consciousness was all the more exquisite for being unperturbed by immediate physical sensation.

Everything had proceeded as rapidly as the irreversible mingling of waters; even the eager and jealous Bardo had not grown impatient.

"You have the volumes, my Romola?" the old man asked as they returned near him. "Now you must prepare your pen. As Tito marks off the scholia we decide to extract, it is well for you to copy them without delay—mind numbering them carefully to correspond with the numbers in the text which he will write."

Romola always possessed some task that granted her a share in this joint endeavor. Tito took his stand at the *leggio*, where he both wrote and read, while she positioned herself at a table directly in front of him, ready to hand anything he might request to her father's hands or relieve him of a volume he had finished. They had always occupied these positions since the work began, yet today it felt new. It was so different now for them to be opposite one another; so different for Tito to take a book from her as she lifted it from her father's knee. Yet, there was no finesse employed to secure an additional glance or touch. Each woman creates in her own likeness the tokens of love offered to her; and Romola's deep, calm happiness encompassed Tito like the rich, quiet light of an evening that dissipates all unrest.

Two hours had passed in their work, and they were pausing only because of the fading light, when the door opened and a figure entered, strangely incongruous with the current of their thoughts and the suggestions of every object around them. It was the figure of a short, stout, black-eyed woman, about fifty years old, wearing a black velvet *berretta* or close cap embroidered with pearls. Underneath, surprisingly massive black braids surmounted a little bulging forehead and fell in rich, plaited curves over her ears, contrasting with a startling carmine tint on the upper region of her fat cheeks, which stood out against the surrounding sallowness. It was a languid afternoon on the seventh of September, more than two months since Romola and Tito had dared to confess their love to one another. Tito had just stepped into Nello's shop, finding the barber sprawled on the bench, his cap pulled low over his eyes. One leg was drawn up, while the other had slipped toward the floor, dragging behind it a manuscript volume of verse whose pages were now crushed beneath the weight. In a corner, Sandro played *mora* alone, watching his left fingers respond to the arithmetic demands of his right with solemn fascination.

Tito moved with the lightest tread, snatching up the lute. He bent over the sleeping barber and plucked the strings softly, singing:

(Beauteous is life in blossom! And it fleeteth—fleeteth ever; Whoso would be joyful—let him! There's no surety for the morrow.)

Nello woke as easily as a startled bird. The cap flew from his eyes instantly, and he sprang upright. "Ah, my Apollino! I seem to be somewhat late with my siesta on this scorching day. Blame lies in not sleeping naturally, but drinking a potion of potent poetry. Listen, how I begin to match my words by their initial letters, like a *Trovatore*? That is one of my bad symptoms: I fear the fine wine of my understanding is running off at the spigot of authorship, leaving me an empty cask smelling of dregs, like many another genius I know. Well, what is it, my Orpheus?"

He stretched his arms fully, then curled them around to grasp Tito's curls, pulling them out playfully. "What do you want of your well-tamed Nello? I hear a coaxing sound in that soft strain of yours. Let me see the very needle's eye of your desire, as the sublime poet says, so I may thread it."

"That is merely a tailor's metaphor for your sublime poet," Tito replied, letting his fingers drift lightly across the strings. "But you have guessed the cause of my affectionate impatience. I need an extra touch of your art—not on my chin, certainly, but on the *zazzera*, which is as tangled as Florentine politics itself. You have an adroit way of inserting your comb that flatters the skin and stirs the animal spirits agreeably in that region. Also, a hint of your delicate orange scent would not go amiss, for I am bound for the Scala Palace. The young Cardinal Giovanni de' Medici will be there, bringing with him a certain young Bernardo Dovizi of Bibbiena, whose wit is so rapid that I see no way to outshine it save with the fragrance of orange blossoms."

Nello had already seized his comb and flourished it, pushing Tito gently backward into the chair and draping a cloth around him. "Never speak of rivalry, *bel giovane mio*. Bernardo Dovizi is a keen youngster who will never cast a net to catch the wind; yet he bears something of the same sharp-muzzled look as his brother, Ser Piero—the weasel that Piero de' Medici keeps at his beck to slip through small holes for him. No! You distance all rivals, and may soon touch the sky with your forefinger. They say you have even carried enough honey to sweeten the sour Messer Angelo, for he has pronounced you less of an ass than might be expected, considering the good understanding between you and the Secretary."

"And between ourselves, Nello mio, Messer Angelo possesses more genius and erudition than I can find in all the other Florentine scholars combined. It suits them well to praise me now that Poliziano is bowed down by grief or illness. I might try a flight with a sparrow-hawk like Pietro Crinito, but Poliziano is a large-beaked eagle who would swallow me, feathers and all, without noticing the difference."

"I will not contradict your modesty if you prefer it; but do not expect us clever Florentines to repeat the same phrases daily, as we must if we always told the truth. We cry down Dante and cry up Francesco Cei simply for variety. If we hail you as a new Poliziano, Heaven has ensured it is not quite such a great lie as it might have been. Besides, are you not a pattern of virtue in this wicked city? With your ears double-waxed against all siren invitations that would lure you from the Via de' Bardi and the great work destined to astonish posterity?"

"Posterity, in good truth, whom it will probably astonish as the universe does, by the impossibility of seeing what the plan actually was."

"Yes, something akin to that was prophesied here the other day. Cristoforo Landino remarked that the excellent Bardo was one of those scholars who lie overthrown in their learning, like cavaliers in heavy armor, and then become angry because they are over-ruled—a pithy remark that seems to me not to be a herb from his own garden. For of all men, for feeding one with an empty spoon and gagging one with vain expectation through long discourse, Messer Cristoforo is the pearl. *Ecco!* You are perfect now."

Here Nello withdrew the cloth. "Impossible to add further grace! But love is not always fed on learning, is it? I shall have to dress the *zazzera* for the betrothal soon, shall I not?"

"Perhaps," Tito smiled, "unless Messer Bernardo recommends that Bardo require me to yoke a lion and a wild boar to the car of the Zecca before I can win my Alcestis. But I confess he is right in holding me unworthy of Romola. She is a Pleiad that may grow dim by marrying any mortal."

"*Gnaffè*," Nello laughed. "Your modesty is in the right place there. Yet fate seems to have measured and chiselled you for the niche left empty by the old man's son. By the way, *Cronaca* told me he is now at San Marco. Did you know?"

A slight electric shock passed through Tito as he rose from the chair, though it was not outwardly visible. He immediately stooped to pick up the fallen book, busy flattening the leaves while he asked, "No; I thought he was at Fiesole. Are you sure he has returned to San Marco?"

"Cronaca is my authority," Nello shrugged. "I do not frequent that sanctuary, but he does. Ah," he added, taking the book from Tito's hands, "my poor Nencia da Barberino! It jars your scholarly feelings to see the pages dog-eared. I was lulled to sleep by the well-rhymed charms of that rustic maiden—'prettier than the turnip-flower,' 'with a cheek more savoury than cheese.' But to gain such a well-scented notion of the *contadina*, one must lie on velvet cushions in the Via Larga—not go to watch the *Fierucoloni* stomping into the Piazza della Nunziata this evening after sundown."

"And pray, who are the *Fierucoloni*?" Tito asked indifferently, settling his cap.

"The country women who come from the mountains of Pistoia and the Casentino, and heaven knows where else, to keep vigil in the church of the Nunziata and sell their yarn and dried mushrooms at the *Fierucola*, as we call it. They make a queer show with their paper lanterns, howling their hymns to the Virgin on this eve of her nativity. If you had the leisure to see them... No? Well, I have had enough of it myself, for there is wild work in the Piazza. One may get stones thrown at one's ears or shins without asking for it, and I was never fond of such pressing attention. *Addio*."

Tito departed carrying a little uneasiness, his visit ending sooner than anticipated. The boy-cardinal Giovanni de' Medici, the youngest of the red-hatted fathers—who would later present his broad, dark cheek to posterity as Pope Leo X—had been detained at his favorite pastime, the hunt, and failed to appear.

It was nearly half an hour before sunset when Tito left the Scala Palace, intending to proceed immediately to the Via de' Bardi. However, he had not gone far when, to his astonishment, he saw Romola advancing toward him along the Borgo Pinti. She wore a thick black veil and a black mantle, yet it was impossible to mistake her figure or her walk. Beside her was a short, stout form, which he recognized as Monna Brigida, despite the unusual plainness of her attire.

Romola had not been bred for devotional observances, and the occasions on which she took the air outside the loggia on the roof of her house were so rare—and so heavily discussed beforehand due to Bardo's dislike of being left without her—that Tito felt certain there must be some sudden, urgent reason for her absence, of which he had heard nothing the previous day.

She saw him through her veil and hastened her steps. "Romola, has anything happened?" Tito asked, turning to walk beside her.

She did not answer at first, and Monna Brigida interjected. "Ah, Messer Tito, you do well to turn round, for we are in

The roar of the crowd and the chaotic symphony of sounds that greeted Tito as he entered the piazza brought to mind Nello's warnings about the *Fierucoloni*. With a strange sense of delight, he thrust himself into the throng, enjoying the jostling elbows and the raucous hoots that filled the void. These noises served to dull the bleak calculations of his future, the terrifying prospect of wandering alone in search of an unknown fortune, and momentarily erased the thought of seeking out Baldassarre.

From the opposite entrances, streams of people surged into the square, carrying aloft paper lanterns on sticks that waved uncertainly in the wind. Above the din of a crude, monotonous chant, a tapestry of noise unfolded: screams, whistles, mocking songs sung in piping boyish voices, the beat of drums, and the tinkling of small bells, all colliding in a confused uproar. Periodically, one of the drifting lights vanished with a crash, struck by a stone hurled in a vague attempt at mischief, followed by shrieks and renewed shouting. Yet, on the fringes of this whirlwind, groups observed the Vigil of the Nativity of the Virgin with greater order than the riotous stone-throwers. Ragged men darted sharp glances around while their tongues rattled merrily, inviting country folk to games on generous terms. Two masqueraders on stilts, having snatched lanterns from the crowd, swung the lights in meteoric arcs as they paraded back and forth. A shrewd merchant conducted a profitable trade at a small covered stall, selling hot *berlingozzi*, a favorite flour-based delicacy. Another man stood atop a barrel, backed against a pillar of the loggia before the Foundling Hospital (*Spedale degli Innocenti*), peddling miracle pills invented by a doctor of Salerno, guaranteed to cure toothaches and prevent drowning. Nearby, a juggler performed tricks on a small platform, while a handful of apprentices, bored by the guerrilla-style stone-throwing, engaged in a concentrated match of their favorite Florentine sport at the narrow entrance of the Via de' Febbrai.

Forced through random gaps in the crowd, Tito found himself beside the trotting procession of barefoot peasant women. Their sun-dried, bronzed faces and strange, fragmented garments—dimmed by generations of dust and fashioned from obsolete materials—made them appear to the city dwellers as a weary ancestry returning from a pilgrimage begun a century prior. At that moment, it was the sturdy, sparingly fed women from the mountains of Pistoia entering with a year's labor in a modest bundle of yarn strapped to their backs. In their hearts lay that meager hope of goodness and that vast, dim fear of harm, both entrusted to the protection of the Blessed Virgin. On this Eve of her Nativity, her miraculous image, painted by angels, would have its veil lifted, allowing her divine potency to flow unobstructed.

Elsewhere, near the boundaries of the square, vendors had stationed themselves safely in their rear. Among them, Tito recognized Bratti, leaning against a pillar with a pursed lip of disdainful silence. He eyed every approacher with a cold look of superiority, clutching tightly to a serge cloth that hid the contents of his basket. Surprised by such unusual behavior for an anxious trader, Tito stepped closer. Two women approached the basket with curious glances, prompting Bratti to pull the covering tighter and look away. Provoked, one woman asked what was inside.

"Before I answer, Monna," Bratti replied, "I must know if you intend to buy. I cannot display such wares in this fair for every fly to land on and pay nothing. My goods are too choice for that. Besides, I have only two left, and I have no mind to soil them. Given the rumors of pestilence circulating among the wise, they might be worth their weight in gold. No, no: *andate con Dio*."

The women exchanged glances. "And what is the price?" asked the second.

"Not within what you are likely to carry, *buona donna*," Bratti said in a tone of compassionate condescension. "I advise you to trust in Messer Domeneddio and the saints; poor people can do no better for themselves."

"Not so poor!" the second woman retorted indignantly, pulling out her money-bag. "Come, now! What do you say to a *grosso*?"

"I say you may get twenty-one *quattrini* for it," Bratti answered coolly, "but not from me, for I lack such small change."

"Then two?" the woman demanded, growing exasperated, while her companion watched with envy. "It won't be more than two, I believe."

After further bidding and mercantile coquetry, Bratti feigned concession. "Since you are determined," he said slowly, lifting the cover, "I would be loath to cause you harm. Maestro Gabbadeo used to say that when a woman sets her mind on a thing and fails to obtain it, she falls into greater danger of the pestilence than before. *Ecco!* I have but two left. Let me tell you, the charm lies on the finger of Maestro Gabbadeo, who has gone to Bologna—he is as wise a doctor as sits at any door."

The treasures were two clumsy iron rings, hammered into the style of ancient Roman rings occasionally unearthed. The rust upon them and the completely hidden nature of their magic were so convincing that the *grossi* were paid without complaint. The first woman, lacking such handsome coins, managed after much feigned reluctance to drive a bargain using some of her yarn, eventually carrying off the remaining ring in triumph. Bratti covered his basket, now filled with miscellaneous items likely acquired under similar circumstances, and moved from his pillar directly toward Tito, who would have preferred to avoid recognition.

"By the head of San Giovanni," Bratti exclaimed, pulling Tito back to the pillar, "what a stroke of luck! For I was speaking of you this morning, Messer Greco. I remarked that he has risen among the gentlemen—and I am glad, for I was at the bottom of his fortune—but I rarely catch him, for he is not to be found lying on the stones anymore. But it is your luck, not mine, Messer Greco, save for a small trifle to compensate me for my trouble in this transaction."

"You speak in riddles, Bratti," Tito said. "Remember, I do not sharpen my wits as you do by haggling over iron rings; you must be plain."

"By the Holy Gospel! It was an easy bargain I gave them. If a Hebrew gains thirty-two percent, I hope a Christian may gain a little more. Had I not borne a conscience, I would have secured twice the money and twice the yarn. But speaking of rings, it is your ring—that very ring on your finger—for which I can find a buyer. Yes, a buyer with a deep purse."

"Really?" Tito asked, examining his ring.

"A Genoese heading straight to Hungary, as I understand. He came and inspected my entire shop searching for old items whose prices I did not know; I wager he thought I had a pumpkin on my shoulders. He had rummaged every shop in Florence. He wore a ring—not exactly like yours, but of the same fashion. As we discussed rings, I mentioned knowing a fine young man, a particular friend of mine, who possessed such a ring. He asked, 'Who is he? Tell him I'll give him his price.' Then I thought to follow you to Nello's tomorrow. In my opinion, Messer Greco, you are not the type to watch the Arno turn to broth and stand idle without dipping a finger in."

Tito had listened intently, though his mind remained busy. Why keep the ring? It had been merely a sentimental whim preventing him from selling it with the other gems. Had he been wiser and sold it, he might have avoided the identification by Fra Luca. True, it had been taken from Baldassarre's finger and placed on his own as his hand grew, yet there was no real virtue in such superstitious scruples regarding inanimate objects. The ring had facilitated his recognition, and Tito increasingly disliked recognition—it was a claim from the past. This foreigner's offer, if genuine, presented an opportunity to dispose of the ring without the hassle of hunting a buyer.

"You speak with your usual wisdom, Bratti," Tito said. "I have no objection to hearing what your Genoese offers. But when and where shall I meet him?"

"Tomorrow, at three hours after sunrise, he will be at my shop. If your wit possesses the sharpness I have always attributed to you, Messer Greco, you will demand a heavy price; for he cares little for money. I believe he buys for someone else, perhaps a great lord."

"It is well," Tito agreed. "I will be at your shop, if nothing hinders."

"And you will undoubtedly deal nobly with me for old acquaintances' sake, Messer Greco, so I shall not linger to fix the small sum you will give me as a token of my service. It seems a thousand years since I left the piazza; a fair is dull, indeed wicked, when one has no more goods to sell."

Tito made a hasty gesture of assent and farewell, stepping away from the pillar only to find himself once again swept toward the center of the square and back again, powerless to determine his own path. In this zigzag motion, he was carried to

When Romola reached the entrance of San Marco, a friar waited there, expecting her arrival. Monna Brigida withdrew into the adjacent church, and Romola was escorted to the door of the chapter-house in the outer cloister, the limit beyond which no woman was permitted to enter.

As the door swung open, the soft external light merged with the glow of two tapers set behind a wheeled bed. This illumination revealed the emaciated face of Fra Luca. Above it sat the tonsured crown of golden hair, and deep-sunken hazel eyes were fixed upon a small crucifix held before him. He was propped up in a near-seating position. As Romola threw aside her veil, she became acutely aware of another monk standing beside the bed, his black cowl pulled tight over his head; he moved toward the door as she entered. In the background, a crucified form rose high and pale against the frescoed wall, its lower half framed by pale, sorrowful faces.

In the next instant, her eyes met Fra Luca's as they lifted from the crucifix. She was seized by a pang of recognition that fused this monastic, wasted frame with the memory of her fair young brother.

"Dino!" she cried, her voice sounding like a low wail of pain.

Yet she did not lean toward him. She remained upright, pausing at a distance of two yards. An unconquerable repulsion gripped her regarding his monkish appearance. To her, it seemed the mark of cowardly ingratitude that had left her father desolate—a sign of groveling superstition capable of branding such disloyalty as "piety." Her father, a man of proud sincerity and simple living, had been one of the few frank pagans of his age. He had raised her with a silent disregard for any claim the Church might have on regulating the beliefs and actions of those endowed with cultivated reason. In her mind, the Church belonged to the chaotic mass of people from whom they had always separated themselves. Consequently, she possessed no framework of thought that could view her brother's path anything other than with incurious, indignant contempt.

Still, the loving nature of Romola's soul had clung to that image of her brother in the past. While she stood rigidly aloof, a yearning search burned in her eyes for something too faint to discern clearly. But there was no corresponding emotion in the face of the monk. He gazed at the younger sister, returned to him in her full womanly beauty, with the distant look of a spirit revisiting a former life.

"My sister!" he said, his voice feeble, interrupted, yet distinct. "It is well that you have not delayed your coming further, for I have a message to deliver to you, and my time is short."

Romola took a step closer. She assumed the message would be one of affectionate penitence addressed to her father, and her heart began to soften. Nothing could erase the long years of abandonment; yet, seeing the culprit reflect on those years with a sense of irreparable wrong, would surely evoke pity. At this final hour, there would be understanding and forgiveness. Dino would pour out natural filial feelings; he would inquire about his father's blindness—how swiftly it had descended, how the long dark days had been endured, what life existed now in the home where he himself had been nurtured. The final words from the dying lips would surely be ones of tenderness and regret.

"Romola," Fra Luca began, "I have had a vision concerning you. Three times in the last two months I have experienced it, and each time it grew clearer. Thus, I journeyed from Fiesole, convinced it was a mandate from heaven that I must deliver. And I draw a promise of mercy from this fact, that my breath is preserved specifically to—"

The difficult breathing that constantly broke his speech prevented him from finishing. Romola felt her heart chill anew. It was a vision, then—this message. One of those visions her father had often alluded to with bitter resentment. Indignation surged to her lips.

"Dino, I thought you had some words to send to my father! You deserted him when his sight failed; you made his life utterly desolate. Did you never care? Never repent? What is this religion of yours that places visions ahead of natural duties?"

The deep-sunken hazel eyes turned slowly toward her, resting upon her in silence for several moments, as if weighing whether to answer.

"No," he said finally, speaking in the same low, passionless tone, as though some non-human spirit were using dying human organs. "No; I have never repented for fleeing from the stifling, poisonous air of sin that was hot and thick around me, threatening to overwhelm my senses like intoxicating wine. My father could not hear the voice calling me night and day; he knew nothing of the demonic tempters trying to drag me back from following it. My father lived amidst human sin and misery without believing in them. He was like one busily picking shining stones in a mine, unaware that a world was dying of plague above him. I spoke, but he listened with scorn. I told him the studies he wished me to pursue were either childish trifles—dead toys—or else they must be enlivened by pulses beating to worldly ambitions and fleshly lusts, for these worldly ambitions and fleshly lusts constituted the entire substance of the poetry and history he demanded I contemplate continually."

"Has not my father led a pure and noble life, then?" Romola burst out, unable to remain silent while hearing this implied accusation against her father.

"He has sought no worldly honors; he has been truthful; he has denied himself all luxuries; he has lived like one of the ancient sages. He never wished you to live for worldly ambitions and fleshly lusts. He wished you to live as he himself has done, according to the purest maxims of philosophy, in which he raised you."

Romola spoke partly from rote, as ardent and sympathetic young creatures often do, but she spoke with intense conviction. A pink flush colored her face, and she quivered from head to toe. Her brother was slow to reply, regarding her passionate countenance with strangely impassive eyes.

"What were the maxims of philosophy to me? They commanded me to be strong when I felt weak; when I was ready, like the blessed Saint Benedict, to roll myself among thorns and court smarting wounds as a deliverance from temptation. For the Divine Love had sought me, penetrated me, and created a great void within me, like a seed needing room to grow. I had been raised in careless indifference to the true faith; I had not studied the doctrines of our religion. Yet it seemed to seize me like a rising flood. I felt there existed a life of perfect love and purity for the soul—a existence free from uneasy hunger for pleasure, free from tormenting questions, free from fear of suffering. Even before knowing the history of the saints, I had a foretaste of their ecstasy. For the same truth permeated pagan philosophy: that it is a bliss within man's reach to die to mortal needs and live in the life of God as the Unseen Perfection. But to attain this, I must forsake the world. I must have no affection, no hope binding me to that which passes away. I must relate to my fellow-beings only as human souls connected to the eternal, unseen life.

"That need urged me continually. It overwhelmed me in visions when my mind grew weary from the vain words recording the passions of dead men. It seized me after I had been tempted into sin and turned away with loathing from the scent of the emptied cup. And in visions, I perceived the meaning of the Crucifix."

He paused, breathing heavily for a minute or two, but Romola felt no impulse to speak again. It was futile for her mind to attempt contact with the mind of this unearthly brother; as useless as trying to grasp a shadow. When he spoke again, his heaving chest was calmer.

"I felt whom I must follow. But I saw that even among the servants of the Cross who professed to have renounced the world, my soul would be stifled by the fumes of hypocrisy, lust, and pride. God had not chosen me, as He chose Saint Dominic and Saint Francis, to wrestle with evil within the Church and the world. He called upon me to flee. I took the sacred vows, and I fled—I fled to lands where danger, scorn, and want bore me continually, like angels, to repose on the bosom of God. I have lived the life of a hermit, ministering to pilgrims. But my task has been brief: the veil dividing me from my everlasting rest has grown exceedingly thin. I returned to Florence that—"

"Dino, you *did* want to know if my father was alive," Romola interrupted, the picture of that suffering life stirring in her again the desire for union and forgiveness.

"—That before I die, I might urge other brothers of our order to study Eastern tongues, as I failed to do, and to pursue greater ends than I did. And I find them already bent on this work. Since I came, Romola, I have felt I was sent partly to you—not to renew the bonds of earthly affection, but to deliver the heavenly warning conveyed in a vision. For I have had that vision thrice. Through all the years since first the Divine Voice called me while I was still in the world, I have been taught and guided by visions. For in the painful linking of our waking thoughts, we can never be certain we have not mingled our own errors with the light we have prayed for; but in visions

Early the next morning, Tito returned from Bratti's shop along the narrow thoroughfare of the Ferravecchi. The Genoese stranger had departed with the onyx ring, leaving Tito in possession of fifty florins. It briefly crossed his mind that if Fortune, in one of her cunning devices, had spared him the necessity of fleeing Florence, it would have been wiser not to have parted with his ring. After all, he had worn it for specific memories and attachments. Yet, it was a trivial matter, not worth dwelling upon with emphasis; in those moments, his faith in Fortune had evaporated. The feverish excitement of the initial alarm, which had driven his mind toward the future, had settled into a dull, regretful lethargy. He cherished the pleasures that could only arise from the good opinion of his fellow-men so deeply that he now wished he had never risked ignominy by shrinking from what society termed "obligations."

But our actions are like children born to us; they live and act apart from our own will. Children may be strangled, but deeds cannot: they possess an indestructible life, both within and outside our consciousness. That dreadful vitality of deeds was pressing hard upon Tito for the first time.

He was heading back to his lodging in the Piazza di San Giovanni, yet he carefully avoided the Mercato Vecchio, his shortest route, lest he encounter Tessa. He was not in the mood to seek anything; he could only await the first sign of his altered destiny.

The square, with its sights of beauty, was bathed in the warm morning sunlight under which the autumn dew still lingered, inviting an idleness undulled by fatigue. It was a festival morning, too, when the soft warmth seemed to steal over one with a special invitation to lounge and gaze. Here, the signs of the fair were already visible; in the spaces around the octagonal Baptistery, stalls were being spread with fruit and flowers. Now and then, laden mules stood quietly absorbed in their nose-bags, while their drivers had perhaps gone through the hospitable sacred doors to kneel before the Blessed Virgin on this morning of her Nativity. On the broad marble steps of the Duomo, scattered groups of beggars and gossipers mingled. There was an old crone with white hair and a hard, sunburnt face encouraging a round-capped baby to try its tiny bare feet on the warmed marble, while a dog sitting nearby sniffed at the performance suspiciously. Nearby, a pair of shaggy-headed boys leaned forward to watch a small, pale cripple carving a face onto a cherry-stone. Above them, on the wide platform, men were tying changing knots in laughing, desultory chatter, or standing in close couples, gesticulating eagerly.

However, the largest and most important assembly of loungers lay in the direction Tito had to turn. It was the busiest time of the day for Nello, and in this warm season, at an hour when clients were numerous, most men preferred being shaved under the pretty red-and-white awning in front of the shop rather than within the narrow confines of the interior. It is not a sublime posture for a man to sit with a lathered chin thrown backward and have his nose used as a handle; yet, to be shaved was a fashion of Florentine respectability, and it is astonishing how gravely men look at one another when they are all adhering to the custom. It was also the hour when yesterday's crop of gossip was freshest, and the barber's tongue was always in its glory when his razor was busy. The deft activity of those two instruments seemed to be set in motion by a common spring. Tito foresaw that it would be impossible for him to escape being drawn into the circle; he would have to smile, retort, and appear perfectly at ease. Well! It was merely the ordeal of swallowing bread-and-cheese pills after all. The man who lets the mere anticipation of discovery choke him is simply a man of weak nerves.

Just then, Tito felt a hand laid upon his shoulder, and no amount of prior resolution could prevent the very unpleasant sensation with which that sudden touch jarred him. His face, as he turned, betrayed the inward shock; but the owner of the hand, which seemed to wield such evil magic, broke into a light laugh. He was a young man about Tito's own age, with keen features, a small, closely clipped head, and a close-shaven lip and chin, giving the impression of a mind as little encumbered as possible with non-nervous material. Those keen eyes were bright with hope and friendliness, as so many other youthful eyes have been that later closed upon the world in bitterness and disappointment; for at that time, there were nothing but pleasant predictions regarding Niccolò Machiavelli, a young man of promise expected to mend the broken fortunes of his ancient family.

"Why, Melema, what evil dream did you have last night that you took my light grasp for that of an *sbirro* or something worse?"

"Ah, Messer Niccolò!" said Tito, recovering himself immediately. "It must have been an extra amount of dulness in my veins this morning that shuddered at the approach of your wit. But the fact is, I have had a bad night."

"That is unlucky, because you will be expected to shine without any obstructing fog today in the Rucellai Gardens. I take it for granted you are to be there."

"Messer Bernardo did me the honor to invite me," said Tito. "But I shall be engaged elsewhere."

"Ah! I remember, you are in love," said Machiavelli, with a shrug. "Else you would never have such inconvenient engagements. Why, we are to eat a peacock and ortolans under the loggia among Bernardo Rucellai's rare trees; there are to be the choicest spirits in Florence and the choicest wines. Only, as Piero de' Medici is to be there, the choice spirits may happen to be swamped in the capping of impromptu verses. I hate that game; it is a device for the triumph of small wits, who are always inspired the most by the smallest occasions."

"What is that you are saying about Piero de' Medici and small wits, Messer Niccolò?" asked Nello, whose light figure was at that moment dominating the Herculean frame of Niccolò Caparra.

That famous worker in iron, whom we had last seen with bared muscular arms and a leathern apron in the Mercato Vecchio, was this morning dressed in holiday attire. As he sat submissively while Nello skipped round him, lathering him, seizing him by the nose, and scraping him with magical quickness, he looked much as a lion might if it had donned linen and tunic and was preparing to enter society.

"A private secretary will never rise in the world if he couples great and small in that way," continued Nello. "When great men are not allowed to marry their sons and daughters as they please, small men must not expect to marry their words as they please. Have you heard the news Domenico Cennini, here, has been telling us?—that Pagolantonio Soderini has given Ser Piero da Bibbiena a box on the ear for setting Piero de' Medici to interfere with the marriage between young Tommaso Soderini and Fiammetta Strozzi, and is to be sent ambassador to Venice as a punishment?"

"I don't know which I envy him most," said Machiavelli, "the offense or the punishment. The offense will make him the most popular man in all Florence, and the punishment will take him among the only people in Italy who have known how to manage their own affairs."

"Yes, if Soderini stays long enough at Venice," said Cennini, "he may chance to learn the Venetian fashion and bring it home with him. The Soderinis have been fast friends of the Medici, but what has happened is likely to open Pagolantonio's eyes to the good of our old Florentine trick of choosing a new harness when the old one galls us; if we have not quite lost the trick in these last fifty years."

"Not we," said Niccolò Caparra, rejoicing in the free use of his lips again. "Eat eggs in Lent and the snow will melt. That's what I say to our people when they get noisy over their cups at San Gallo and talk of raising a *romor* (insurrection). I say, never do you plan a *romor*; you may as well try to fill the Arno with buckets. When there's water enough, the Arno will be full, and that will not be until the torrent is ready."

"Caparra, that oracular speech of yours is due to my excellent shaving," said Nello. "You could never have made it with that dark rust on your chin. *Ecco*, Messer Domenico, I am ready for you now. By the way, my beloved erudite," continued Nello, as he saw Tito moving toward the door, "here has been old Maso seeking for you, but your nest was empty. He will come again presently. The old man looked mournful and seemed in haste. I hope there is nothing wrong in the Via de' Bardi."

"Doubtless Messer Tito knows that Bardo's son is dead," said Cronaca, who had just come up.

Tito's heart gave a leap—had the death occurred before Romola saw him?

"No, I had not heard it," he said, with no more dis

The loggia crowning Bardo's house soared above the flanking structures, forming a secluded gallery around a quadrangular courtyard. While the street-facing side was supported by sturdy columns, the rear opened onto a series of arched windows piercing the wall. Through these arches, one could gaze past a jumble of haphazard dwellings toward the hill of Bogoli, offering Romola a private promenade shielded from prying eyes.

Near one of these arches, close to the entrance, Tito waited. Sunlight slanted harshly before him, mingling with the wreckage of his hopes. He had never truly believed that Romola's affection could withstand the repulsion born of his secret; he lacked the arrogance to imagine her love stemmed solely from faith in him. Yet, as he pictured her approaching in her radiant beauty, softened by her hazel eyes, a selfish wish arose: that she were merely human, lower in station, so he might clutch her and kiss her freely before their inevitable separation. He had rarely received genuine tenderness from her—only fleeting glances, occasional kisses, and pressed hands—and he had longed endlessly for solitude with her. Now, they were finally alone, yet she stood rigidly distant.

His heart hammered as the door shifted. Romola appeared. It struck him like lightning: he felt rather than saw the halo around her head, the tearful appeal in her eyes; he sensed rather than heard the cry of "Tito!" In that instant, she was in his arms, sobbing against his chest.

How desperately Romola had craved to see that bright face during the long nights! The terrifying vision of death, the bewildering doubts stirred by tales of a life alien to her understanding, the haunting specter she seemed to inhabit as her own dream—it all sharpened her realization that Tito was the first to bring hope and gladness into her existence, the first to blunt the sharp edges of grief regarding her brother. She needed to confide in him; no one else could hold her burden. Though she had restrained herself before her father all morning, the dam broke now. To the world, she was proud and controlled; in her love for Tito, she was as simple and unguarded as a child. Content with days spent merely gazing at one another, she now felt the urgent need to cling, with no hesitation holding her back.

"My Romola! My goddess!" Tito whispered, embracing her passionately, kissing the thick golden waves of her neck. He felt himself in paradise; disgrace, shame, and parting dissolved into insignificance. This happiness was too potent to be tainted by the knowledge that she loved a deceived man; indeed, he rejoiced in her delusion. After all, concealment had been wisdom. His only regret was his unnecessary dread, though perhaps that fear had been worth enduring for this sudden rapture.

The sobbing ceased, and Romola lifted her head. Silence stretched between them, filled with the sweet wonder of youthful love. Her long white hands rested on his dark-brown curls; his dark fingers were bathed in her streaming gold. Each was the most beautiful object in the other's world, experiencing an undisturbed mutual consciousness for the first time. A fresh wave of sadness pressed on Romola's heart, making her linger in this silent, soothing proximity. Tito dared not press his lips to hers, for that would alter the sacred stillness.

"Tito," she finally said, "it has been altogether painful, but I must tell you everything. Your strength will help me resist impressions that reason cannot shake."

"I know, Romola—I know he is dead," Tito replied. His long, lustrous eyes revealed none of the myriad wishes that would have summoned that death long ago had mere desire possessed such power. Romola read her own pure thoughts in their dark depths as clearly as one reads letters in a happy dream.

"So changed, Tito! It pierced me to think it was Dino. And so strangely hard: not a word to my father, nothing but a vision he wanted to tell me. Yet it was pitiful—the struggling breath, the eyes that seemed to look toward the crucifix and yet not see it. I shall never forget it; it feels as if it stands between me and everything I shall ever behold."

Her heart swelled anew, forcing her to pause, but the compulsion to unload her mind to Tito urged her to suppress the rising anguish. When she resumed, her thoughts had drifted slightly.

"It was strange, Tito. The vision concerned our marriage, yet he knew nothing of you."

"What was it, my Romola? Sit down and tell me," Tito said, guiding her to a nearby bench. A fear crossed him that the vision might somehow implicate Baldassarre; this sudden shift prompted him to change positions.

Romola recounted every detail from her entry into San Marco, omitting not a single word of her brother's speech, which burned itself into her memory. But as she reached the climax of the vision, she faltered. The remainder was too vivid to utter; she sat staring into the distance, momentarily unaware of Tito's presence.

*His* mind remained at ease; the vague vision had swept over him like white mist, leaving no trace. He waited silently for her to continue.

"I took it," she continued, as if he had read her thoughts. "I took the crucifix; it is down below in my bedroom."

"And now, my Romola," Tito entreated, "banish these ghastly thoughts. Such visions are bred of fasting and fanatical ideas in monks. Surely they carry no weight for you."

"No, Tito; no. But poor Dino... *he* believed it was a divine message. It is strange," she mused, "this life of men possessed by fervid beliefs that seem like madness to others. Dino was not a vulgar fanatic; and that Fra Girolamo—his very voice seems to penetrate me with a sense that there is some truth in what moves them: some truth of which I know nothing."

"It was only because your feelings were highly wrought, my Romola. Your brother's state of mind was no more than a form of theosophy, the common ailment of excitable, dreamy minds throughout history—the same ideas your father's old adversary, Marsilio Ficino, pores over in the New Platonists.
