Only your brother's passionate nature drove him to act out what others merely discuss. As for Fra Girolamo, he is simply a narrow-minded monk gifted with instilling terror in the multitude. Any words or voice would have shaken you in that moment. Once your mind rests, you will judge such matters as you always have."

"Not about poor Dino," Romola insisted. "I was angry with him; my heart closed against him while he spoke. But since then, I have thought less of my own mind and more of his. Oh, Tito! It was terribly pitiful to watch his young life end thus. That yearning look at the crucifix as he gasped for breath—I can never forget it. Last night, I stared at the crucifix for a long time, trying to see how it could help him, until, by the lamplight, the suffering face seemed to shed pity."

"My Romola, promise me to resist such thoughts. They are fit for sickly nuns, not for my golden-tressed Aurora, who looks made to scatter all such twilight fantasies. Try not to think of them now; we shall not long be alone together."

His final words were delivered in a tone of tender pleading. He turned her face toward him with a gentle touch of his right hand. Romola had been gazing absently at the arched opening, missing the distant hill. She had been inside the chapter house, contemplating pale images of sorrow and death. Tito's touch and voice recalled her. Now, in the warm sunlight, she beheld a rich, dark beauty that seemed to gather images of joy around it: purple vines festooned between elms, strong corn ripening under the vibrating heat, bright-winged creatures darting among flowers, round limbs beating the earth in gladness with cymbals aloft, light melodies sung to the thrilling rhythm of strings—all signs of Nature reveling in her force.

A strange, bewildering transition occurred within her—from those pale specters of sorrow to this vibrant youthfulness, like a sun-god ignorant of night. What thought could reconcile the worn anguish on her brother's face, that straining reach for the invisible, with this satisfied strength and beauty, proving they belonged to the same world? Was there no reconciliation, only a blind worship of clashing deities, first in mad joy, then in wailing? For the first time, Romola felt this questioning need as a sudden, dizzying lack, a hunger for something to grasp. It was an experience shorter than a sigh, for the eager theorizing of ages compresses into the momentary void of a single mind. No answer met the need, and it vanished before the rushing tide of young sympathy for the glad, loving beauty beaming upon her in new radiance, like dawn after turning from the grey west.

"Your mind lingers apart from our love, my Romola," Tito murmured softly, reproachfully. "It seems a forgotten thing to you."

She looked at his beseeching eyes in silence until the sadness melted from her own countenance.

"My joy!" she exclaimed in her full, clear voice.

"Do you really care for me enough, then, to banish those chill fancies?" Tito asked, smiling brightly. "Or shall you always suspect me as the Great Tempter?"

"How could I not care for you more than for everything else?" Tito answered. "Everything I felt before in my

When Tito departed the Via de' Bardi that afternoon, he carried with him a profound sense of relief, convinced that the looming danger had finally passed. With Romola and her father no longer pressing upon his senses, his mind drifted back to those agonizing hours of terror. He realized with a jolt that during those moments of panic, he had acted in ways he would never have chosen had he possessed true foresight. Specifically, he regretted selling his ring. To Romola, and indeed to anyone who knew him, the act of parting with a cherished gem would appear sordid unless accompanied by a plausible excuse—perhaps a claim that he intended to buy a special gift. At that critical juncture, Tito had felt a sickening weariness toward the necessity of pretense. Fortunately, fate had intervened; the potential fallout of that initial deception had evaporated, leaving him unburdened. He reasoned that since luck had granted him immunity, it was only fair to accept it. Who truly suffered? The consequences for Baldassarre remained too murky to weigh heavily on his conscience.

Yet, despite this freedom, Tito felt a gnawing desire to shed every invisible shackle that might chafe against his newfound liberty, particularly the subtle tether binding him to Romola. Unbeknownst to him, the very joy of escaping entanglement was dulling the moral sensibilities required to avoid future ones. Still, the sale of the ring seemed trivial compared to the lingering dilemma regarding little Tessa. She lived under a charming delusion, likely filling her small mind with hopes destined for disappointment. Should he seek her out privately to correct her misunderstanding immediately, or allow time and circumstance to reveal the truth? Innocent dreams were delightful, yet they often shattered under the harsh light of daily life. That sweet, pouting, round-faced creature was impossible to forget. Tito considered taking her a gift to gauge whether her stepfather had grown crueler since her mother's death. Alternatively, he might expose the truth to her first, then inform Romola, hoping to secure a better destiny for the poor child.

No, he decided firmly. The unfortunate episode involving the *cerretano* and the forced marriage, compounded by his negligence in letting Tessa depart under false pretenses, must remain unknown to Romola. Since no explanation could fully erase Tessa's belief, there would always be a risk of accidental revelation. Moreover, even a child like Tessa might harbor a touch of bitterness upon discovering her love had been misplaced—indeed, she was undoubtedly in love with him. This realization suggested it was safer never to meet her again. Yet, he acknowledged that such a minor adventure, akin to a rural girl's fleeting infatuation, would eventually fade when a local farmhand offered genuine affection, prompting her to drop her secret and discover her freedom. "Well then," he concluded mentally, "farewell, Tessa! My kindest wishes!" He resolved that the affair of the *cerretano* would yield no further complications for him. People often believed their personal resolutions were unbreakable, and Tito was no exception.

As for the fifty-five florins received for the ring, Tito had already devised a plan. He intended to execute a clever scheme that would explain the missing ring to Romola while simultaneously shielding her mind from recurring thoughts of monastic vows—a notion he found deeply repugnant. Carrying this purposeful thought, he made his way to the Via Gualfonda to locate Piero di Cosimo, the preeminent artist of Florence, renowned for his fantastical mythological designs.

Upon entering the courtyard where Piero resided, Tito noted the heavy iron knocker wrapped thickly in wool and secured with cords. Recalling the painter's habit of stuffing his ears against unwanted noise, Tito was unsurprised by this defensive measure against visitors' clamor. He began by knocking gently, then escalated to shaking the door vigorously. To no avail. Just as he prepared to retreat, blaming himself for wasting time when he could wait until meeting Piero at Nello's, a small girl emerged carrying a basket of eggs. She approached the door, stood on tiptoes, and slid up a small iron plate set in grooves. Peering through the opening, she called out in a high-pitched voice, "Messer Piero!"

Moments later, the sound of bolts retracting echoed through the air. The door swung open, and Piero appeared clad in a red nightcap and a loose brown serge tunic, his sleeves rolled up to his shoulders. He cast a startled glance at Tito but, without acknowledging him further, reached out to take the basket from the child. After re-entering the house and emerging shortly thereafter with the empty basket, he asked, "How much to pay?"

"Two *grossoni*, Messer Piero; they are freshly boiled, my mother insists," the girl replied.

Piero extracted coins from a leather pouch at his belt, and the little maid trotted away, casting admiring looks upward at the imposing young gentleman. However, Piero's gaze lacked warmth as he addressed Tito. "What brings you to my door, Messer Greco? I saw you this morning at Nello's. Had you asked then, I would have informed you that I do not entertain strangers without prior knowledge of their business and agreement."

"Pardon, Messer Piero," Tito responded with his characteristic equanimity. "I acted without due reflection. My mind was occupied solely by your remarkable talent for conceiving whimsical inventions, and a sudden urge to commission something similar drove me here."

Piero's eccentric mannerisms were so notorious among the populace that this reception could hardly be construed as an insult. Even if Tito had sensed hostility, his natural inclination toward winning favor outweighed any spark of resentment. Piero made a habitual grimace, grinning broadly, stretching the corners of his mouth, and furrowing his brow to obscure his true feelings beneath a mask of defiance. "And what might this need be?" he asked after a brief pause. Internally, he felt tempted by the prospect of applying his inventive genius.

"I require a delicate miniature motif drawn from poetic fables, which you surely know how to synthesize," Tito explained. "It must be painted on a wooden case—I will demonstrate the scale—as a triptych. The interior may feature simple gilding; my request concerns the exterior. It is a favorite theme among Florentines: the Triumph of Bacchus and Ariadne, yet I desire a novel interpretation. A passage from Ovid will provide the necessary inspiration. Young Bacchus must be seated in a vessel, his head wreathed in grape clusters, wielding a spear entwined with vines. Dark-berried ivy should coil around the masts and sails; the oars must be thyrsi, and flowers should adorn the stern. Leopards and tigers must crouch before him, while dolphins play nearby. Furthermore, I wish to include the fair-haired Ariadne beside him, rendered immortal with a golden crown—an addition not found in Ovid, but you will grasp the vision. Above them, youthful Loves, skilled in your depiction, should shoot roses from the tips of their arrows..."

"Enough!" Piero interjected sharply. "I possess Ovid in the vernacular. Locate the passage myself. I dislike being suffocated by another's ideas. Come inside."

Piero guided Tito through the first chamber, where a basket of eggs rested on the open hearth amidst a pile of discarded shells and ash. Strangely juxtaposed with this sordid clutter was a low ebony bedstead draped carelessly with a rich Oriental carpet, appearing as though it had once covered the steps leading to a Madonna's throne. Nearby stood a carved *cassone*, or large chest, adorned with painted motifs. Beyond these essentials, the room contained merely casts, wooden steps, easels, and rough boxes, all festooned with cobwebs.

The adjoining space was even larger yet more densely packed. It appeared Piero was preparing for the Feast, for the double doors beneath the window—which allowed light to flood the studio from above—stood ajar, revealing a garden or rather a tangled thicket. Fig trees and vines sprawled wildly among nettles and hemlock, while a tall cypress rose defiantly from a stifling mass of yellowish mulberry leaves. This damp luxuriance seemed to seep inward; in one corner, amidst a chaotic heap of carved marble fragments and rusted armor, tufts of long grass and dark feathery fennel had taken root. A large stone vase, tilted precariously, appeared to pour forth ivy streaming across the floor.

Along the walls hung numerous sketches in pen and oil: fantastical sea monsters, dances of satyrs and maenads, Saint Margaret's resurrection from the dragon, Madonnas bathed in celestial light, botanical studies, and grotesque heads. On irregular, rough shelves, a few books lay scattered among drooping ears of corn, ox horns, pieces of dried honeycomb, stones encrusted with rare lichens, skulls, bones, peacock feathers, and large bird wings. Rising from the dirty floor litter were life-sized figures: one dressed as a Vallombrosan monk, bizarrely topped with a helmet bearing a barred visor; another smothered in brocade and skins hastily thrown over it. Amidst this eclectic still life, several speckled and white pigeons strutted or perched, too tame to flee at the approach of humans. Three plump toads crawled intimately near the threshold, while a white rabbit, seemingly the model for the creature frightening Cupid in the central painting of Mars and Venus, twitched its nose contentedly atop a box filled with bran.

"And now, Messer Greco," Piero instructed, gesturing for Tito to

Messer Bernardo del Nero remained as steadfast in his counsel as Romola had anticipated. His advice—that the marriage be postponed until Easter—held firm, and Bardo, wholly guided by his wise and pragmatic friend, saw no reason to dissent. Yet, even Bernardo, though immune to the personal charm Tito exerted over others, could not entirely ignore the seductive logic of success that invariably sways worldly men. Tito was ascending rapidly through the highest circles. He had secured the favor of the young Cardinal Giovanni de' Medici, who had even hinted at inviting him into his scholarly entourage for an upcoming journey to Rome. This brilliant Greek, possessed of a tongue that spoke freely yet never quarreled, became a sought-after presence at lavish suppers along the Via Larga and at Florentine games where he did not claim to compete, yet admired the unparalleled skill of Piero de' Medici with effortless grace.

By an unbroken chain of cause and effect, Tito's reputation as a delightful companion in "magnificent" society cast a lustrous glow upon his learning and talents; indeed, he was sufficiently accomplished that praise of him required no exaggeration. Messer Bernardo harbored old prejudices and attachments, which now began to outweigh the newer, weaker bias against the supple young Greek stranger. To the aged Florentine, it was impossible to disregard the endorsement of standing well with the city's most distinguished families. Since Tito had become fully integrated into that elite circle whose opinions defined social worth, it seemed irrational to deny that there were no longer any obstacles to accepting such a son-in-law for Bardo and such a husband for Romola.

Undeniably, Tito's arrival marked the dawn of a new era for both father and daughter, surpassing even the initial promises. The blind old scholar, whose proud integrity refused to participate in the deceitful flattery and equally excessive condemnation that wove the fabric of learned discourse, had fallen into neglect among his fellow citizens. When mentioned, the common refrain was that while his blindness and the loss of his son were tragic, he was tedious in defending the value of his own labors. His discontent appeared inconsistent in a man who had previously ignored religious rites and rejected offers of patronage unless they included ordination—a prerequisite for securing support. However, since Tito's arrival, the monotonous shadow surrounding Bardo's name had lifted. The old man ceased his complaints, and the fair daughter was no longer confined in dowerless pride, waiting for a *parentado*.

The winning manners and rising favor of the handsome Greek, destined to fulfill the dual roles of son and husband, transformed the situation into something thoroughly friendly. Visitors frequently enlivened the quiet library, drawn by an indefinable urge to renew contact whenever an old acquaintance became the topic of fresh conversation. Young men, invited once by Tito, readily returned when they met him on his way to the Via de' Bardi, resting their hands on his shoulder to fall into easy chat. It was a pleasure to behold Romola's beauty; seated with a certain grandeur, speaking with gravity, smiling with modesty, and radiating an aura of queenliness—as described by the old Firenzuola—she seemed to unfold like a strong white lily under this genial breath of admiration. To her, this new bright life intertwined with Tito's love was indistinguishable from joy itself.

Tito had also strengthened Bardo's hope that, before his death, he might secure the long-desired protection for his library. He hoped it would not be absorbed into another collection or transferred to a monastery under a new name, but would remain forever the Bardi Library, open to the Florentines. While the old habit of trusting the Medici persisted as long as their influence dominated the state, Tito, having gained the ear of Cardinal Giovanni, could perhaps achieve what Bernardo could not: demonstrating to a learned audience the unique value of Bardi's collection. Tito spoke sanguinely of this outcome, eager to comfort the old man, aware that Romola rewarded these gentle words with an adoration that no direct compliment to herself could elicit.

This matter of the library became the subject of numerous discussions between Bernardo and Tito after Christmas passed and the wedding approached, always conducted away from Bardo's hearing. Bardo clung to a vague belief, which they dared not disturb, that his property outside the library was sufficient to cover all demands. He refused, except during moments of angry despair, to acknowledge that the will disinheriting Dino left Romola heir only to debts, or that he needed patronage beyond ensuring a separate location for his books in exchange for a deed gifting them to the Republic.

"My opinion," Bernardo told Romola beneath the loggia, "is that since you are to marry and Messer Tito possesses a competent income, we should begin winding up affairs. We must ascertain exactly the sum required to shield the library from creditors, rather than allowing debts to accumulate further. Your father requires nothing but his daily shred of mutton and macaroni; I believe Messer Tito may undertake to provide this for the remainder of your father's life, effectively replacing the *morgen-cap*."

"Tito has always known that my life is bound up with my father's," Romola replied. "He is kinder to my father than I am; he delights in making him happy."

"Ah, he is not made of the same clay as other men, is he?" Bernardo smiled. "Your father tried to shut out women's folly by cramming you with Greek and Latin, yet you proved just as ready to believe in the first pair of bright eyes and soft words as if you knew nothing but Paternosters, like other Christian daughters."

"Now, godfather," Romola said, shaking her head playfully, "as if only bright eyes and soft words made me love Tito! You know better. You know I love my father and you because you are both good, and I love Tito because he is so good. I see it, I feel it, in everything he says and does. And if he is handsome, too, why should I not love him the better for that? Beauty seems to me part of the finished language by which goodness speaks. You must have been a very handsome youth yourself, godfather"—she looked up with a happy, loving smile at the stately old man—"about as tall as Tito, with very fine eyes. Only you looked a little sterner and prouder, and..."

"And Romola likes to keep all the pride to herself?" Bernardo teased, not impervious to this charming coaxing.

"However, it is fortunate that in one regard Tito's demands are more modest than those of any Florentine husband of suitable rank we might have found for you; he asks for no dowry."

Thus, the arrangement was settled between Messer Bernardo, Romola, and Tito. Bardo assented with a wave of his hand when Bernardo informed him that it was time to sell property and clear debts, accustomed as he was to view finances as a dense thicket his imagination never penetrated. Meanwhile, Tito set about earning Bernardo's respect by inquiring with his quick wit into Florentine monetary matters—the secrets of the *Monti*, the valuation of real estate, and the profits of banking.

"You will soon forget that Tito is not a Florentine, godfather," Romola observed. "Watch how he learns everything about Florence."

"It seems to me he is one of the *demoni*, children of no particular country," Bernardo replied, smiling. "His mind is too nimble to be weighed down by all the burdens we men carry in our hearts."

Romola smiled back, filled with happy confidence.

The final week of the Carnival had arrived, transforming the streets of Florence into a cacophony of noise and color. Masqued processions wound through the city, their chants echoing the new traditions introduced by Lorenzo the Magnificent. In the Piazza, under a pale, frosty sky, the *rigoletto*—a lively round dance—was performed with vigor. Yet, beneath this festive veneer lurked a darker undercurrent. Practical jokes ranged from harmless confetti to malicious projectiles; indeed, stones were the weapon of choice for the city's unruly youth. These boys and striplings, a formidable force in Florentine crowds, had grown as loud and chaotic as cicadas during the height of Carnival. Their immemorial custom was to block passersby with poles, demanding tribute in the form of supper or bonfire contributions, before concluding their revelry with the standing entertainment of stone-throwing. This sport was rarely monotonous; the resulting injuries varied wildly, and death was not uncommon. Thus, the pleasures of the Carnival were checkered, a grotesque tapestry of grossness and barbarity that a truthful painter would struggle to depict without turning the canvas to the wall. Only for the sake of history, to justify later reforms born of ignorance, could such scenes be justified.

Yet, amidst the chaos, there remained a more innocent, picturesque merriment, fueled by the quick animal spirits and sensitive hearts of the Florentines. Unlike the heavy stupor of the north or the stealthy ferocity of the south, where brawls often ended in daggers, the Florentine spirit retained a certain wild vitality.

High morning broke, but the carnival spirits lingered, lounging and recounting the night's jests. Among them walked Tito Melema at a brisk pace toward the Via de' Bardi. Beside him strode young Bernardo Dovizi—the same man who would later appear in Raphael's portrait as the keen-eyed Cardinal da Bibbiena. Though they moved through the throngs of the Por' Santa Maria, their conversation bore no relation to the sights and sounds surrounding them. Instead, they spoke animatedly, gesturing and smiling, yet casting frequent, wary glances around them. At the turn toward the Lung' Arno, leading to the Ponte Rubaconte, Tito's attention sharpened. In a rapid survey of the crowd, he spotted someone he desperately wished to avoid recognizing at that precise moment.

His mind was consumed with anticipation. Today marked a unique occasion in his life: his betrothal, scheduled for the evening. The arrangement had come suddenly. Preparations for the marriage had been underway for some time, primarily Tito pouring his florins into furnishing rooms in Bardo's dwelling, which had previously been sparsely equipped. Originally, both the betrothal and the wedding were to be deferred until after Easter, once Tito's year of probation, demanded by Bernardo del Nero, was complete. However, an urgent proposal arrived inviting Tito to accompany Cardinal Giovanni to Rome. There, his superior knowledge of Greek would assist Bernardo Dovizi in organizing a library—an opportunity clearly aligned with Tito's ascent. Unable to decline such a clear path to advancement, Tito had urgently petitioned that the betrothal occur before his departure. With the engagement formalized, the delay before the actual marriage upon his return would be minimal, and the pain of separation mitigated by the binding nature of their pledge. Legally, the exchange of rings and signing of mutual contracts constituted more than half of a marriage, leaving only the nuptial benediction to finalize it.

Romola's feelings had mirrored Tito's desire, and the elders' consent had been secured. Now, amidst frantic preparations for his imminent departure, Tito hastened to snatch a morning visit from Romola. He needed to exchange final words before their union in the evening. Recognition was unlikely to bring pleasure today, especially from Tessa.

It was unmistakably Tessa whom Tito had glimpsed moving with a timid, forlorn expression toward the bend in the Lung' Arno. As he continued his discussion with Bernardo Dovizi, an uncomfortable undercurrent of awareness settled over him. Tessa had seen him; she would certainly follow. Escape along the direct route by the Arno and over the Ponte Rubaconte was impossible. Yet, she dared not approach while he was accompanied, and Tito would maintain Dovizi's presence until they reached Bardo's door.

Quickening his pace, Tito spun new topics of conversation, but the sensation of being pursued intensified. Irritating, perhaps even more so because a flicker of tenderness and pity for the poor girl made his resolve to evade her silently feel like an unpalatable compromise. Undeterred, he guided his companion to the door, skillfully delivering his "goodbye" without turning his face in a direction where importunate blue eyes might catch him. Ascending the stone steps, he attempted to banish unpleasant thoughts, convincing himself that Tessa might not have seen him, or, if she had, might not have followed.

However, human perception operates independently of will. Before Tito could fully dismiss the notion, he knew—with absolute certainty—that a small figure in a white hood stood near the doorway. He sensed the truth before he felt the firm grasp laid upon his arm. It was not a timid touch, but a desperate clutch. Tessa, startled by his swift departure, had surged forward with a final, bold effort.

When Tito halted and turned, fear etched her features. She dreaded the consequence of her audacity.

"Tessa!" Tito exclaimed, his voice sharper than she had ever heard it. "Why are you here? You must not follow me—you must not loiter at doorways waiting for me."

Her blue eyes widened with unshed tears, and she remained silent. Tito feared ridicule more than anything else; the image of a high-street nobleman in the Via de' Bardi accompanied by a peasant girl staring pathetically at him was unbearable. Bernardo del Nero, or some other dangerous figure, could appear at any moment. Even absent the day of his betrothal, the scene would be awkward. Yet, driving Tessa away with harsh words was impossible.

That cursed folly regarding the *cerretano* resurfaced, buried for months only to bloom now as an unseasonable weed of vexation. He could not speak cruelly, so he spoke hurriedly instead.

"Tessa, I cannot—must not talk to you here. I will go to the bridge and wait for you. Follow me slowly."

He turned and walked rapidly toward the Ponte Rubaconte, leaning against the wall of a quaint house overlooking the approach Tessa would take. To see her advance, her round face pale and sorrowful since their parting at the Nunziata, would have softened even a harder heart. Fortunately, the bridge was seldom crowded, and at that moment, it was nearly empty.

As soon as she approached, Tito spoke. "Now, Tessa, I have very little time. Do not cry. Why did you follow me this morning? Never do so again."

"I thought," Tessa whispered, fighting back a sob that threatened to break loose at the sharpness of his tone, "I thought you wouldn't be so long before you came to take care of me again. And the *patrigno* beats me, and I can't bear it any longer. Always when I come for a holiday I walk about to find you, but I can't. Oh, please don't send me away from you again! It has been so long, and I cry so now because you never come to me. I can't help it; the days are so long, and I don't mind the goats or the kids... I can't—"

Sobs tore through her quickly, great tears streaming down her face. Tito realized he could not simply reject her. Sending her away was necessary, yes, but telling her truths that would plunge her into hopeless grief was impossible. He saw deeper troubles looming, but the immediate pressure of the moment outweighed distant consequences.

"Tessa, my little one," he said, adopting his old, caressing manner, "you must not cry. Endure the cross *patrigno* a little longer. I will return to you. But I am going now to Rome—a very long way off. I shall return in a few weeks, and then I promise to come and see you. Promise me to be good and wait for me."

It was the familiar voice; the sound alone was half sufficient to soothe Tessa. She looked up with trusting eyes, still shimmering with tears, sobbing despite her utmost efforts to comply.

"Promise me, my Tessa," he urged gently.

"Yes," she whispered.

"But you won't be long?"

"No, not long. But I must *go now*. Remember what I told you, Tessa. No one must know you ever see me, or you will lose me forever. Go straight home when I leave you, and never follow me again. Wait for me to come. Good-bye, my little Tessa: I *will* come."

There was no alternative. He had to turn and depart without looking back to witness her reaction, for he had no time to spare. Once he rounded the corner into the Via de' Benci, where he could no longer see the bridge, he knew Tessa would obey. She was too trusting and compliant not to do exactly as instructed.

For that day, the difficulty was resolved. Yet, this resurgence of Tessa's presence, occurring at a moment when he could not deceive her, left an unpleasant mark on his memory. Tito's mind, however, was currently saturated with the hopeful glow of his first love, intertwined with ambitions that promised a bright future.

It was November 17th, 1494. More than eighteen months had passed since Tito and Romola were last united in the joyous light of Easter, a day marked by a shower of comfits thrown in the ancient Greek style, signaling that heaven itself would sprinkle sweetness upon their shared life. Since that springtime, however, the landscape of Florence had shifted dramatically. Just as every blossom and fruit depends on the lifeblood flowing through the roots of the tree, the destinies of Tito and Romola were now tethered to the sweeping political tides reshaping Italy.

Only a week prior, the spirit of antiquity had surged back into the hearts of the Florentines. The great bell of the palace tower had shattered the silence with an alarm call, summoning the populace to arm themselves with rusted weapons, farming tools, and makeshift clubs to expel the Medici. The Gate of San Gallo had slammed shut on Piero de' Medici, fleeing toward Bologna with his mercenaries in panic, while his younger brother, the Cardinal, escaped disguised as a Franciscan friar, with a bounty placed on both their heads. In the aftermath, houses were sacked according to old custom; the shameful portraits of former conspirators were scrubbed from public walls; and the exiled enemies of the Medici were welcomed home. The fledgling tyrants had been evicted from their golden nest in the Via Larga, and the Republic had regained its voice.

Yet, a mere week later, the same grand palace in the Via Larga was being transformed to receive a new occupant. If the colorful drapes covering the streets, the banners streaming from windows, and the carpets stretching across every step were proof of celebration, then Florence was indeed rejoicing in anticipation of its distinguished guest. A vibrant river of color flowed from the palace, winding past the Cathedral, through the great Piazza della Signoria, and across the Ponte Vecchio to the Porta San Frediano, the gateway facing Pisa. Near this gate, a platform and canopy had been erected for the ruling Signoria.

Messer Luca Corsini, a doctor of law, felt his heart flutter with the weight of a Latin oration awaiting him. Every senior elder of Florence was preparing for the procession, smoothing their chins and adjusting their fine silk *luccos*. Young nobles inspected their new tunics, styled after the French fashion, designed to impress the stranger with their unique elegance. Meanwhile, a vast assembly of the clergy—from the Archbishop in his radiant vestments to trains of black, white, and grey-robed monks—conferred early in the morning. They debated how best to arrange their relics, sacred banners, and consecrated jewels to honor the arrival of the illustrious visitor, scheduled for three o'clock in the afternoon.

This was an unprecedented guest. He had crossed the Alpine passes leading an army Italy had never witnessed: thousands of formidable Swiss mercenaries, hardened to fight for love, hate, or coin; a host of proud cavaliers; and an infantry where one in a hundred carried an arquebus. Worse still, he brought bronze cannons that fired iron balls instead of stones, pulled by horses rather than oxen, capable of breaching walls before a city could repair the damage.

Some hailed the newcomer as Charlemagne, the legendary restorer of Florence and conqueror of degenerate kings; others compared him to Cyrus, the liberator of the chosen people restoring the Temple. His ambitions were equally grandiose: to march through Italy amid jubilant crowds, resolve conflicts in Rome, seize the Kingdom of Naples by hereditary right and force, and launch a crusade against the Turks, aiming to slaughter or convert them to Christianity. This plan seemed fitting for the "Most Christian King," the son of the cunning Louis XI, whose France had risen to become the preeminent Christian monarchy. This figure, an antitype of Cyrus and Charlemagne, was none other than Charles VIII of France.

On the surface, the event seemed destined to stir the collective memory of humanity's greatest epochs. Indeed, a widespread conviction held that the arrival of the French king and his army was a miracle of Providence—a moment when marble statues might sweat, phantom warriors might fight in the sky, and beasts might birth monsters. This belief rested less on the sheer scale of the invasion and more on the moral atmosphere of the times, giving rise to profound presentiments. These emotions found their most striking expression in the voice of a single man: Fra Girolamo Savonarola, Prior of the Dominican convent of San Marco.

On a September morning, as news of the French advance rang in everyone's ears, Savonarola preached in the Florence Cathedral from the text, "Behold, I, even I, do bring a flood of waters upon the earth." He believed this was divine guidance, interpreting the "flood" as a symbol of both avenging wrath and purifying mercy, specifically pointing to the French army. His audience, comprising some of the era's most cultivated minds, listened with shuddering awe. Savonarola possessed a rare power to imprint his convictions upon others, swaying varied souls alike. Four years prior, he had proclaimed from Florence's main pulpit that a scourge was descending upon Italy to purge the Church. He appeared to hold a mission akin to the Hebrew prophets, casting the Florentines as a second chosen people.

While prophecies were not unknown in that age—often resembling a dim candlelight flickering in small corners of fate—Savonarola stood apart as a monumental exception. Where others offered vague whispers of the future, his vision burned like a mighty beacon guiding and warning humanity. To the soberest observers, the supernatural quality of his foresight gained credibility from the peculiar stagnation of the age. By late 1492, following the death of Lorenzo de' Medici and Tito Melema's arrival, Italy enjoyed a deceptive peace. There was no fear of famine, as harvests were bountiful; new palaces rose in every city, and villas dotted the hills. Those with excess lived without fear of the poor, whose armor had rusted and who had grown tame, paying for mercenary armies as easily as they bought goods from Smyrna. Even the threat of the Turk had faded, with the Pope preferring bribes for potential poisoning over plans for war or conversion.

The world seemed a comfortable establishment for the fortunate few, a realm where lust, obscenity, treachery, and murder were not only tolerated but managed effectively. Adorned with the patronage of learning and art, flattery was available in exquisite Latin, and artists painted both the holy and the profane with equal skill. The Church, though reportedly disgraced in its leadership and lacking spiritual vitality, prospered more than ever. The heavens smiled above, and the earth remained still. Yet, amidst this festive facade, a man named Savonarola had spent over two years preaching of an impending scourge, declaring that the world was unfit for hypocrites, libertines, and oppressors.

From beneath these smiling skies, he had seen a sword hanging—the sword of God's justice, soon to descend with purifying punishment. Born in Ferrara seventeen years earlier, the stark contradiction between men's actions and their professed beliefs had nearly destroyed his appetite for worldly life, driving him to the cloister at twenty-three. He believed God entrusted the Church with the sacred lamp of truth, only to see it corrupted into a tomb hiding that light. As scandals multiplied and hypocrisy turned to impudence, he asked: Had the world lost its righteous ruler? Was the Church abandoned? His answer was firm: No. The Sacred Book revealed that when the wickedness of the chosen people cried out, God's judgment inevitably followed. Reason itself demanded vengeance to break the cycle of evil. Unless the Church was reclaimed, how could the promise of converting the heathens and uniting the world under one law be fulfilled?

Savonarola's faith was fueled by visions, but its true strength lay in his burning indignation at injustice and his fervent trust in an Unseen Justice that would restore purity. To his ambitious soul, which craved great ends achieved through willpower, faith in a righteous ruler merged seamlessly with faith in immediate divine intervention.

Beneath the glittering mask of ecclesiastical and secular dignity, tensions simmered across Italy. Ludovico Sforza of Milan, a patron of Leonardo da Vinci, coveted the ducal crown for himself rather than letting it rest on a weak nephew vulnerable to poisoning. He feared the Spanish-born King Ferdinand and his son Alfonso of Naples, who despised the treachery required to depose relatives for a Lombard usurper. The Neapolitan royals, in turn, feared their suzerain, Pope Alexander Borgia. All watched Florence nervously, fearing its strategic position might tip the balance. Everyone, including the cautious and powerful Venice, eyed the shifting alliances.

Lorenzo de' Medici had previously maintained a delicate equilibrium, balancing alliances between Naples, the Pope, and Milan. However, his son Piero's rash vanity shattered this stability. Suspecting a conspiracy, Ludovico Sforza decided to invite the French king to intervene, leveraging Charles VIII's claim to Naples as an heir of the House of Anjou. Ambassadors, termed "orators" in those rhetorical ages, traveled constantly. A recalcitrant cardinal, refusing to recognize a pope elected by bribery, joined the chorus, urging the French king with fiery rhetoric. The young king listened willingly.

By 1493, rumors swelled that Charles VIII would cross the Alps with a massive army. Italians, accustomed since the fall of the Roman Empire to seeking arbiters from afar, began viewing his approach as a chance for justice. Savonarola heard in these rumors the confirmation of

The sky above was a dull, slate-grey, yet this offered little solace to the Piazza del Duomo, which was already draped in the festive hues of a holiday. Blue canopies stretched overhead, punctuated by constellations of yellow lilies and golden coats of arms. Banners unfurled like sheaves of wheat at the corners of the Baptistery, though the grand marble steps of the cathedral remained bare; the stone was currently worn smooth by the countless, ordinary feet of the populace. It was the hour for Advent sermons, a solemn duty that could not be neglected even amidst the carnival spirit that had flushed the streets with color. Yet, not everyone hurried toward the altar. Citizens of every rank moved with the brisk urgency of those burdened with errands, while clusters of talkers scattered across the square debated loudly. Some appeared eager to miss the sermon entirely, their expressions betraying no leisurely enjoyment of the opening festivities. Instead, many engaged in intense, whispered discussions, constantly scanning the horizon for newcomers with wary glances.

At the edge of the square, where the artificial rainbow light of the celebration faded into the somber grey of the morning falling upon the stone houses of Via de' Cerretani, stood a stark contrast to the jubilation within. A dense cluster of workers gathered there, their clothes stained with labor, most bearing tools that doubled as weapons. Their bare, muscular arms and soiled garments highlighted the sharp divide between them and the bright spectacle of the Piazza. They listened intently to Ser Cioni, a notary whose biting wit had once drawn scorn in the market two and a half years prior. Now, however, speaking with the complacent humor of a man whose faction held sway, he addressed the crowd with confidence.

"Never speak to me," Ser Cioni declared in his incisive voice, "of bloodthirsty Swiss or fierce French infantry. They belong in mountain passes, not our streets. History shows us that peasants have shattered the finest armies of our condottieri simply by trapping them between steep cliffs. Tell me, Florentines, what reason have you to fear an army marching through your own thoroughfares?"

"That is true, Ser Cioni," replied a man whose hands were permanently stained crimson by dye, resembling fresh blood, with a small hatchet tucked into his belt. "And those French cavaliers who paraded in their tight doublets recently tasted our hospitality. While I carried my cloth near Ognissanti, I saw my masters strutting by, looking as if they considered the homes of the Vespuccis and Aglias too humble for them. They eyed us Florentines like proud roosters, pitying our inability to strut properly. 'Yes, my fine *Galli*,' I told them, 'stick out your stomachs; I have a meat-axe in my belt that will slide right inside you.' Then, suddenly, the great bell rang—the *vacca muglia*—and chaos erupted. No matter what happened, I dropped my cloth, grabbed my axe, and chased after the cavaliers toward Vigna Nuova. When Guccio caught up, I asked, 'What is it?' He said, 'I think it's the Medici returning.' *Bembè!* I expected no less! We raised a barricade, trapping the Frenchmen. Our *Ciompi* swarmed forward, and one wields a great scythe that clipped the plumes from a nobleman's helmet. True enough! The women rained stones to scare them. Alas, Piero de' Medici never returned. What a pity; we would have stripped him of both legs and wings before letting him leave."

"Well spoken, Oddo," chimed in a young butcher, knife at his hip. "Piero won't return anytime soon. He looked terrified as a hunted chicken when we mobbed him in the piazza. He is a coward; otherwise, he would have made a proper stand with his horsemen. But we shall swallow no more Medici, regardless of what the French King demands."

"But I dislike those French cannons," grumbled Goro, a man notably rotund despite two years of accumulated grievances. "May Saint John protect us! If Messer Domeneddio truly cares for us as Brother Cioni claims, why send the French to Naples instead?"

"Aye, Goro," the dyer agreed. "That is a question worth pondering. You are not quite the fool I thought. Why couldn't they have gone via Bologna? Or even Arezzo? We wouldn't have minded them heading to Arezzo."

"Fools!" Ser Cioni began, attempting to rally them. "It will be for the glory of Florence—"

His speech was cut short by a sudden cry: "Look there!" Several voices shouted in unison as attention shifted to a party approaching from Via de' Cerretani.

"It is Lorenzo Tornabuoni and one of the French nobles residing with him," Ser Cioni muttered, dripping with disdain. "He feigns satisfaction—that deep, calculating Tornabuoni—but he is a Medicean at heart. Beware."

The approaching group was indeed striking. Beside the distinguished Lorenzo Tornabuoni walked a French nobleman dressed in splendor, his white linen elaborate and embroidered with gold. Two other Florentines of high birth, attired for the procession, accompanied them. To the left of the Frenchman stood a figure that rivaled any brocade in brilliance: a man clad entirely in black, though destined soon to wear a red mantle as Latin Secretary to the Ten. This was Tito Melema.

Tito had proven invaluable in dealings with the French guests, leveraging his knowledge of Southern Italy and his fluency in French, honed during his youth and practiced frequently at the French camp in Signa. Fortune smiled upon him; he moved with his characteristic graceful ease, smiling and explaining, seemingly unaware of the subtle shift occurring within him. It was the mark of maturity leaving moral youthfulness behind—the conscious adoption of a role in life. His features remained soft, his eyes clear, yet something intangible had vanished, much like the fading light of dawn.

The Frenchman sought final instructions on ceremony before returning to Signa, pausing now to survey the Piazza del Duomo where the royal procession would halt for prayer. As the party entered the square, they drew every eye, though the gaze was mixed with suspicion rather than pure admiration. Whispers flew regarding the Frenchman's hoof-shaped shoes—a delicate insult disguised as praise for royal excess—and snarls directed at "Mediceans" were carefully suppressed. Lorenzo Tornabuoni, skilled in masking annoyance, maintained his composure, while Tito, naturally inclined to disarm hostility with good humor, felt the detached perspective of an outsider observing local passions.

Stopping to gain a clear view of the cathedral, the group noticed the festoons and heraldic devices hanging over the central door. Tornabuoni signaled for Piero di Cosimo, who was typically loitering outside Nello's shop. An animated discussion ensued, becoming delightfully absurd as the Frenchman marveled at Piero's pungent observations, which Tito translated word-for-word. Even hostile onlookers grew curious, their faces twisting into expressions of half-amusement, half-humiliation as they realized they were excluded from the joke spreading like infection. For Tito, it was a moment of pure delight; as the sole interpreter capable of rendering the scene so amusing, he reveled in the approving glances cast his way, basking in the warmth of public favor.

As the rainbow light bathed the laughing group, the serious churchgoers had already slipped inside. Meanwhile, in the shadowed periphery of the unadorned streets, a darker reality unfolded. Newcomers arrived, devoid of linen and silk, their spirits far from merry. Here too, the French attire and strange footwear stood out, but they were surrounded by an increasingly large crowd of non-admiring Florentines. Leading this throng were three men in ragged clothing, their hands bound tightly by cords. Ropes encircled their bodies, allowing their captors to throttle them instantly should rebellion occur. Holding these ropes were French soldiers, who used broken Italian phrases and tugs on the cords to provoke pleas for mercy.

Two of the prisoners complied, holding out their bound hands to every passerby in piteous tones: "For the love of God and the Holy Madonna, give us something for our ransom! We are Tuscans; we were captured in Lunigiana."

The third man, however, remained stubbornly silent despite the stinging blows of the knotted cord. He differed vastly from his companions. Young and hardy, the others resembled sturdy vagrants in their scant attire. This man, conversely, was well past old age, likely fifty-five or older. His beard, grown long in neglect, and the thick, straight hair framing his bald head were nearly white. Though emaciated, his thickset frame remained firm and upright, radiating an energy that defied his years. This vitality shone in his dark eyes and strong brows, which burned with a strangely isolated intensity against his yellow, bloodless, deeply wrinkled face and lank grey hair. Yet, there was a fitfulness in his gaze; after flashing with fierce scrutiny at windows and faces, his eyes would drift away with a lost, wandering look. His lips stayed motionless, his hands held resolutely down. He refused to beg.

The sight provoked growing exasperation among the Florentines. Initially, many gave money—some reflexively responding to appeals in God's name, others driven by an unquestioning awe of the French soldiery, fueled by tales of their cruelty and relied upon as a shield against further insolence. But as the procession moved deeper into

"You are easily frightened, though," Piero remarked, letting out another scornful laugh. "My portrait does not quite capture the essence of the original. Still, the old fellow certainly possessed a tiger's gaze; I must return to the Duomo and study him once more."

"It is hardly pleasant to be seized by a madman, if indeed he is one," Lorenzo Tornabuoni replied, offering a polite defense for Tito's reaction. "Though perhaps he is merely a ruffian. We shall soon learn the truth. I believe we ought to determine if we possess sufficient authority to halt this disturbance between our citizens and your countrymen," he added, turning his attention to the Frenchman.

They moved forward toward the throng, swords drawn, while the quiet onlookers formed an escort around them. Tito followed suit; it was imperative that he ascertain what others already knew regarding Baldassarre. The initial paralysis of terror was rapidly giving way to the quick-witted strategies that mortal danger forces upon the timid. The mob of men and boys, more inclined to jeer at the soldier and torment him than to deal him serious wounds, parted instantly at the sight of gentlemen bearing drawn blades. The French soldier was immediately questioned.

He and his companions had merely escorted their prisoners into the city to solicit funds for their ransom. Two of the captives were Tuscan soldiers captured in Lunigiana; the third, an elderly man, had been traveling with a group of Genoese, coming to blows with French foragers near Fivizzano. While the old man appeared mad, he proved harmless. The soldier knew nothing further, being unable to comprehend a single word the elder spoke. Tito listened intently, yet remained deaf to everything else until specifically addressed.

It was Tornabuoni who broke the silence. "Will you accompany us back, Melema? Or, since Messere is departing for Signa, would it be wise to follow the current fashion and attend the Friar? He will be speaking with the force of a torrent at flood stage this very morning. It is what we must all do, you know, if we wish to preserve our Medicean skins. *I* myself would go, had I the leisure."

Tito's face had regained its color, and he managed a display of gaiety. "Naturally, I number myself among the admirers of the inspired orator," he smiled. "Unfortunately, however, I shall remain occupied with the Segretario until the time of the procession."

"I intend to enter the Duomo to examine that savage old man again," Piero declared.

"Then, Piero mio, have the charity to show him to one of the hospitals for travelers," Tornabuoni instructed gently. "The monks may discover whether he requires confinement within a cage."

The group dispersed, and Tito set off for the Palazzo Vecchio, where he expected to meet Bartolommeo Scala. Though the distance was short, for Tito it felt stretched out like the agonizing minutes of a waking dream. The brief stretches of street and piazza were filled with memories, forebodings, and torturing fears that might have spanned months. He felt as though a serpent had begun to coil tightly around his limbs.

Baldassarre alive and present in Florence represented a living vengeance—a coiling snake that would not rest until it had crushed its prey. Such a man did not allow injuries to pass unavenged; his love and hatred burned with a passionate fervor that subjugated every other aspect of his being, driving him to sacrifice himself to his passions as if worshipping a deity capable of self-destruction. Baldassarre had loosened his grip and vanished. Tito understood this perfectly: it meant the vengeance was being calculated to ensure its certainty.

If he had not uttered those decisive words—"He is a madman"—if he could have summoned the mental fortitude necessary to openly acknowledge his recognition of Baldassarre, would the risk not have been diminished? He could have claimed he possessed positive evidence of Baldassarre's death. The only individuals capable of contradicting him were Fra Luca, who was dead, and the crew of the companion galley that had delivered news of the pirate encounter. The odds were infinitely against Baldassarre having reunited with anyone from that crew. Tito thought bitterly that a timely, well-crafted falsehood might have spared him from fatal consequences. Yet, uttering such a lie would have demanded perfect self-command during a moment of convulsive shock. He realized now that he had spoken without preconception; the words had leaped forth like a sudden birth, conceived and nurtured in the dark.

Tito was confronting an inexorable law of the human soul: we prepare ourselves for sudden deeds through the repeated choices of good or evil that gradually shape our character. His only remaining hope lay in Baldassarre's failure to find his revenge. And then, Tito grasped a thought more actively cruel than any he had previously entertained: Could his own unpremeditated words contain a grain of truth? Enough truth, at least, to support his denial should Baldassarre attempt to identify him?

The old man looked strange and wild. With his eager heart and sharp mind, suffering was likely enough to have driven him mad. If this were true, the vengeance seeking to inflict disgrace might be thwarted. However, there existed another form of vengeance that no clever lying could deflect. Baldassarre belonged to a lineage for whom the thrust of a dagger feels as natural an impulse as the spring of a tiger's claw.

Tito shrank with shuddering dread from disgrace, yet he also harbored a physical terror inseparable from a soft, pleasure-loving nature—one that prevents a man from facing wounds and death as a welcome release from shame. His thoughts instantly raced toward some hidden defensive armor that might protect him from a vengeance no subtlety could parry. He marveled at the power of the passionate fear that consumed him. It was as if he had been struck by a blighting disease, suddenly transforming the joyful senses of youth into pure pain.

One resource remained open to Tito. He could have turned back, sought out Baldassarre again, and confessed everything—to him, to Romola, to the entire world. Yet, he never considered this path. True repentance, which severs all ties to evil, demands more than mere selfish fear. He possessed no conviction that truth offered strength or safety; the only strength he trusted lay in his ingenuity and his dissimulation.

Now that the initial shock, which had revealed the treacherous signs of fear, had passed, he hoped to navigate all emergencies through cool deceit and defensive preparation. It was a defining feature of Tito's experience at this critical juncture that no direct plan to rid himself of Baldassarre ever crossed his mind. Every other possibility ran through his thoughts, even fleeing Florence itself, but he never devised a scheme to remove his enemy. His dread generated no active malice, and he would still prefer not to cause pain to any mortal. He had simply chosen to make life easy for himself—to carry his human burden in a way that pinched him nowhere. That choice had, at various points, landed him in unexpected predicaments.

The question now was no longer whether he should share the common pressure of destiny with his suffering fellow-men, but whether all the resources of lying would suffice to save him from being crushed by the inevitable consequences of that habitual choice.

When Baldassarre forced his way behind the heavy curtain, his wrists bound tight and a rough rope encircling his neck and torso, he halted abruptly at the threshold. The sight before him stole the breath from his lungs. He had anticipated the usual desolation of a church viewed merely as a building: a cavernous nave stripped of life, save for cold stone emblems, side altars where candles burned with feeble, flickering light, and statues that stared back with pale, rigid indifference. Perhaps a handful of worshippers in the distant choir would offer a monotone chant to the empty air. Instead, he found a sea of humanity.

It was a vast multitude, a tapestry woven from every thread of Florentine society—from magistrates and ladies of noble birth to rough-handed artisans and weary peasants. All were turned upward, their faces bathed in a breathless silence, fixed upon the pulpit situated at the angle between the nave and the choir. There stood a Dominican friar, a man of strong features and dark hair, gripping a crucifix as he preached.

For the first few minutes, Baldassarre took no note of the sermon's content. Though his entry had been silent, several pairs of eyes near the door had snapped toward him with immediate suspicion. The ropes around his limbs were undeniable proof of his status as an escaped prisoner. Yet, in a sanctuary, escapees were granted refuge. Furthermore, his advanced age and the wild misery etched onto his face were calculated to evoke pity rather than fear. As he stood motionless, his gaze drifting aimlessly from the throng to the patterned pavement beneath his feet, the observers lost interest in him, drawn back into the magnetic pull of the speaker.

Among those who had initially scrutinized him were Romola's eyes. Having entered late through a side door, she commanded a perfect view of the main entrance. She had watched Baldassarre with prolonged attention; the grey hairs threading his beard and the unmistakable stamp of profound suffering on his weathered face struck a deep chord within her. The cord tightening his throat confirmed her fears, stirring in her heart the specific sensitivities toward the sorrows of age that her entire life had cultivated. She imagined that their eyes had met in that fleeting moment of recognition, but Baldassarre remained oblivious. He possessed only a startled, fragmented awareness of the scene—a flash of consciousness that did not interrupt the fierce, tumultuous storm of emotion raging inside him since his confrontation with Tito. Memories of the past surged forward like delirious visions, twisted by thirst and anguish. No coherent thought regarding the future could form amidst this internal fire; the closest approximation was a bitter realization of his fading strength and a vague, overarching resolve born of total distrust.

Suddenly, he felt his very bones resonate with a booming cadence, sounding like the thunderous echo of his own inner turmoil. A voice, penetrating his marrow with an accent of triumphant certainty, declared: *"The day of vengeance is at hand!"*

Baldassarre shuddered and looked up. Too distant to discern details, he saw only the silhouette of the preacher, arm outstretched, lifting the crucifix high. Yet, he panted for those threatening words as if they were a promise of salvation. After a brief pause, the preacher slowly lowered his arm, set the crucifix down on the pulpit's edge, and folded his arms across his chest. He scanned the crowd, seemingly determined to lock eyes with every single face.

"All ye in Florence are my witnesses," the voice boomed, "for I spoke not in a corner. You are my witnesses that four years ago, ere signs of war or tribulation appeared, I proclaimed the coming scourge. I raised my voice like a trumpet to the prelates, princes, and people of Italy, declaring: 'The cup of your iniquity is full.' Behold, the thunder of the Lord gathers; it shall fall and shatter the cup. Your iniquity, which you taste as pleasant wine, shall be poured out upon you, becoming molten lead."

He pointed accusingly at the clergy. "And you, O priests, who mock, saying, 'Ha, ha! There is no Presence in the sanctuary; the Shechinah is naught; the Mercy-seat is bare; we may sin behind the veil unpunished!' To you, I declare, the presence of God shall reveal Himself as a consuming fire. Your sacred vestments shall turn to winding sheets of flame; for sweet music there shall be shrieks and hissing; for soft couches, thorns; and for the breath of wantons, pestilence. Trust not in your gold and silver, nor in your high fortresses. Though walls be of iron and citadels of adamant, the Most High shall instill terror into your hearts and weakness into your councils until you are confounded and flee like women. He shall break in pieces mighty men without number, replacing them with others. For God can no longer endure the pollution of His sanctuary; He will thoroughly purge His Church."

"And since Scripture declares that God reveals His purposes only to His servants the prophets, He has chosen me, His unworthy servant, making His purpose present to my soul through the living Word and the deeds of Providence. By the ministry of angels, He has shown it to me in visions. His word possesses me so completely that I am but a forest branch when the wind of Heaven blows through it; I cannot remain silent, even if I should be mocked by scorners. For four years, in obedience to the Divine Will, I have preached three truths delivered to me by the Lord: *In these times God will regenerate His Church*; *Before this regeneration comes the scourge over all Italy*; and *These things will come quickly*."

"But hypocrites, cloaking their hatred of the truth with a display of affection, have told me, 'Come now, Frate, abandon your prophecies; it suffices to teach virtue.' To them I answer: 'Yes, you whisper in your hearts that God dwells afar off, that His word is a parchment penned by the dead, and that He does not act as in days of old—rebuking nations, punishing oppressors, or striking down unholy priests as He struck the sons of Eli. But I cry again in your ears: God is near, not afar! His judgments do not change. He is the God of Armies; the strong men who march to battle are His ministers, just as storm, fire, and pestilence are. He drives them by the breath of His angels, and they descend upon the chosen land that has forsaken the Covenant. And thou, O Italy, art that chosen land. Has not God placed His sanctuary within thee, and hast thou polluted it? Behold, the ministers of His wrath are upon thee—they stand at thy very doors!'"

Savonarola's voice had climbed to a peak of impassioned force before falling suddenly into silence. His hands dropped, clasping calmly before him. This silence proved more potent than his shouting; it acted as a powerful spell upon the congregation. Across the vast cathedral floor, men and women sat frozen, faces upturned like breathing statues, until the voice returned in clear, low tones.

"Yet there is a pause—even as in the days when Jerusalem was destroyed, there was a pause that the children of God might flee. There is stillness before the storm: lo, there is blackness above, yet not a leaf quivers; the winds are stayed that the voice of God's warning may be heard. Hear it now, O Florence, chosen city in the chosen land! Repent and forsake evil; do justice; love mercy; cast out all uncleanness from among you, that the spirit of truth and holiness may fill your souls and breathe through your streets and homes. Then the pestilence shall not enter, and the sword shall pass over you leaving you unhurt."

"For the sword hangs from the sky; it quivers; it is about to fall! *The sword of God upon the earth, swift and sudden!* Did I not tell you years ago that I beheld the vision and heard the voice? And behold, it is fulfilled! Is there not a king with his army at your gates? Does not the earth shake with the tread of horses and the wheels of swift cannons? Is there not a fierce multitude capable of laying bare the land like a sharp razor? I tell you the King of France with his host is the minister of God: God shall guide him as a hand guides a sharp sickle, and the joints of the wicked shall melt before him, mowed down as stubble. He who flees shall not escape, and he who escapes shall not be delivered. And the tyrants who built thrones from the vices of the multitude, and the unbelieving priests who traffic in souls and fill the sanctuary with fornication, shall be hurled from their soft couches into burning hell; and the pagans and those who sinned under the old covenant shall stand aloof and say: 'Lo, these men have brought the stench of a new wickedness into the everlasting fire.'"

"But thou, O Florence, accept the offered mercy. See! The Cross is held out to you; come and be healed. Which nation in Italy has received a token like yours? The tyrant is driven out; the men holding bribes in their left hand and rods in their right have departed, and no blood has been spilled. Now, cast away every other abomination, and you shall be strong in the strength of the Living God. Wash yourselves from the black pitch of your vices, which have made you akin to heathens; cast out the envy and hatred that have turned your city into a nest of wolves. Then no harm shall

While Baldassarre was seized by the voice of Savonarola, he remained unaware that another man had slipped through the rear doorway and stood nearby, watching him intently. It was Piero di Cosimo. The painter paid no attention to the sermon; he had come solely to observe the escaped prisoner. As the preacher paused, allowing the congregation to surrender to a wordless emotional surge, the newcomer stepped forward and touched Baldassarre's arm.

The painter looked around, tears still tracing slow paths down his cheeks, though a vigorous sigh suggested he felt the outburst was over. Speaking in a low tone, Piero asked, "Shall I cut your cords for you? I've heard how you were captured."

Baldassarre did not answer immediately. He cast a suspicious glance at the intruder. Finally, he replied, "If you will."

"Better come outside," Piero suggested.

Baldassarre looked at him with renewed suspicion. Seeing the doubt in the prisoner's eyes, Piero smiled, drew a small knife, and severed the bonds. He began to suspect that the notion of the prisoner's madness might not be entirely unfounded; there was something peculiar about the set of his face.

"Well," Piero mused internally, "if he causes trouble, he'll be tied up again quickly enough. The poor devil deserves a chance, at least."

"You seem afraid of me," Piero whispered, pressing further. "You don't wish to tell me anything about yourself?"

Baldassarre crossed his arms, savoring the long-forgotten sensation of muscle tension. He met Piero's gaze with less suspicion now, his tone carrying a quiet resolve. "No, I have nothing to tell."

"As you please," Piero conceded with a shrug. "But perhaps you seek shelter? We Florentines are notoriously hospitable to visitors with torn doublets and empty stomachs. There is a hospital for poor travelers outside every gate. If you wish, I can guide you there. Besides, there is no danger from your French soldier; he has been dispatched."

Baldassarre nodded silently, accepting the offer, and the two men left the church together.

"You wouldn't mind sitting for a portrait, would you?" Piero asked as they walked along the Via dell' Oriuolo toward the Gate of Santa Croce. "I am a painter, and I would pay you well for the likeness."

Suspicion flickered back into Baldassarre's glance. "No."

"Ah!" Piero said curtly. "Very well. Go straight ahead; you will find the Porta Santa Croce. Outside lies a hospital for travelers. So, you refuse any further service from me?"

"I thank you for what you have already done. I require nothing more."

"It is well," Piero muttered, turning away. *A mysterious old tiger,* the artist thought. *Truly worthy of a canvas. Ugly, carved with deep lines, looking as if the plow and the harrow had tilled over his heart. A fine contrast to my bland, smiling Messer Greco—my Bacco trionfante, who has married the fair Antigone in defiance of all history and propriety. Ah! His scholar's blood curdles uncomfortably at the sight of that old fellow's claw!*

When Piero re-entered the Piazza del Duomo, the vast multitude that had listened to Fra Girolamo was streaming out through every door. Their hurried departure testified to the profound importance they placed on the sermon, which had detained them from their daily labors. Leaning against an angle of the Baptistery, the artist watched the departing throng, delighting in the variety of their garments and the sharp, characteristic expressions on their faces—faces reminiscent of those Masaccio had painted fifty years prior, and ones that Domenico Ghirlandaio had not yet fully mastered.

That morning marked a peculiar occasion. The friar's audience, usually diverse, represented the full spectrum of Florence's classes and political factions more completely than ever. There were men of high birth, accustomed to public office, who had recently become prominent not only as enemies of the Medici and champions of popular government but as devoted *Piagnoni*, embracing the Frate's doctrines with fervor. Among them were figures of authoritative presence like Francesco Valori, possessing a hot, arrogant temperament greatly gratified by the immediate divine authority seemingly granted to bring about freedom in their own image. Others, like Soderini, displayed less of the ardent *Piagnone* and more of the seasoned politician.

There were also men of family, such as Piero Capponi, brave lovers of sober republican liberty who preferred action to argument, holding no specific objection to any idea that expelled the Medici and made room for public spirit. At their sides stood doctors of law, whose studies of Accursius and his peers had not extinguished their ardor; they too had become enthusiastic *Piagnoni*. One such was Messer Luca Corsini, who, on a memorable occasion yet to come, would raise his learned arms in street-level stone-throwing for the cause of religion, freedom, and the Frate.

Among the dignitaries carrying their black *lucco* or fur-lined mantles with habitual authority, there was a sprinkling of men with contemplative, sensitive faces: scholars bearing illustrious names like Strozzi and Acciajoli, already inclined to take the cowl and join the community of San Marco. There were artists, stirred to new heights by Savonarola's teaching, such as the young painter who had recently surpassed himself in his fresco of the divine child within the Frate's bare cell—an unconscious precursor who would one day himself wear the tonsure and be known as Fra Bartolommeo.

There was the mystic poet Girolamo Benevieni, perhaps hastening to deliver news of the beloved Frate's imminent arrival to his friend Pico della Mirandola, a man destined never to see another dawn. Well-born women appeared, attired with such scrupulous plainness that their refined grace served as the sole distinction separating them from their less aristocratic counterparts. Predominant among the crowd were the genuine *popolani* or middle class, representing both the Major and Minor Arts, acutely aware of purses threatened by war taxes.

Perhaps most striking and varied were the streams of poorer tradesmen and artisans. Their faith and hope in the Frate's divine message ranged from a rough, undiscriminating trust in him as the friend of the poor and enemy of the oppressive rich, to an eager consumption of biblical subtleties that captivated the sedentary artisan. These men found illumination in the long, dim spaces beyond their workbenches, where stitching seemed bathed in a pale flame resembling the light of Divine Science.

Yet, scattered among these devoted disciples were many who were not his followers at all. Some were Mediceans who, driven by fear and policy, had begun to display a feigned deference to the prevailing popular spirit. Others were sincere advocates of free government who viewed Savonarola merely as an ambitious monk—part sagacious, part fanatical—who had become a powerful instrument in the hands of the people and thus an unavoidable social fact. Even bitter enemies were present: members of the old aristocratic anti-Medicean party determined to seize control once more for certain chief families, or licentious young men who detested him as Florence's ultimate killjoy.

For the sermons in the Duomo had evolved into political events, attracting ears tuned to curiosity and malice as readily as to faith. Men of ideas, like the young Niccolò Machiavelli, attended to observe and report to friends in country villas. Men of appetite, such as Dolfo Spini, sought to hunt down the Frate as a public nuisance depleting game supplies, lying in wait for grounds of accusation.

Perhaps no preacher ever wielded such massive influence as Savonarola, nor faced such heterogeneous material to shape. A secret to this immense power lay in the highly mixed character of his preaching. Baldassarre, wrought into an ecstasy of self-martyring revenge, was merely an extreme case among the partial and narrow sympathies of that audience. In Savonarola's words, there were strains appealing to the finest susceptibilities of human nature, alongside elements that gratified low egoism, tickled gossiping curiosity, and fascinated timorous superstition.

His need for personal predominance, his labyrinthine allegorical interpretations of Scripture, his enigmatic visions, and his false certainty regarding Divine intentions never ceased, within his own large soul, to be ennobled by fervid piety, a passionate sense of the infinite, active sympathy, and a clear-sighted demand for the subjection of selfish interests to the general good—a trait shared by the greatest of mankind. But for the mass of his audience, the entire weight of his preaching rested on his strong assertions of supernatural claims, his denunciatory visions, and the false certainty that gave his sermons the urgency of a political bulletin. Once he had mastered that audience, it became necessary to his nature—and to their supposed welfare—to maintain that mastery.

The result was inevitable. No man struggles to retain power over a mixed multitude without suffering corruption; his standard must inevitably descend to meet their lower needs rather than rising to his own best insights. The mysteries of human character were rarely presented in a manner better suited to checking the judgments of facile knowingness than in the figure of Girolamo Savonarola. Yet, we can afford him a reverence that requires no closing of the eyes to fact if we view his life as a drama accompanied by great inward modifications during its outward changes. Up to this point, when his direct intervention in political affairs had only just begun, it is probable that his imperious need for ascendancy

By six o'clock that evening, most of Florence breathed a sigh of relief that the arrival of the new "Charlemagne" had finally concluded. When the rolling of drums, the blaring of trumpets, and the heavy tramp of hooves along the Pisan road merged with the ringing of excited church bells, it was indeed a magnificent spectacle for those perched on turreted rooftops. From their vantage points, overlooking the winding procession against the backdrop of green hills and valleys, they watched a terrifying yet splendid parade unfold. Though the lack of sunlight dimmed the brilliance of banners, spears, and plumed helmets, the absence of choking dust allowed the details to stand out with stark clarity.

Tall and sturdy Scottish archers marched alongside ponderous Swiss halberdiers, while nimble Gascon troopers seemed ready to scale walls at a moment's notice. The cavalrymen appeared as true knights-errant, mounted on chargers and wielding indomitable lances. It was a comforting sight to behold such an array of strength and splendor, filled with nobles, knights, and pages of noble birth, adorned with jeweled sword hilts, embroidered satin scarves, gold chains, and the transcendent canopy borne aloft by select youths above the head of the Most Christian King. As an old diarist noted, simply halting his spelling just behind the sheer wonder of the event: *"Fù gran magnificenza"* (It was great magnificence).

For the Signoria, however, who waited on their platform near the gates before marching out to greet the mighty guest, the grandeur was obscured by uneasy feelings. Had Messer Luca Corsini been able to deliver his prepared Latin welcome without interruption, the scene might have been less chaotic. Instead, a sudden rainstorm caused impatience among both men and horses, breaking off his carefully studied speech and forcing the scholarly representatives of the city to offer a clumsy greeting in impromptu French. This sudden confusion proved to be a stroke of luck for Tito Melema. Stationed behind the Signoria as a secretary, he found himself thrust into the mix when the crowd was pressed by the approaching horses.

"Somebody step forward and say a few words in French," urged Soderini. No one of high rank dared risk another public failure. "You, Francesco Gaddi—you can speak." But Gaddi, doubting his own quick wit, hesitated. Pushing Tito forward, he declared, "You, Melema."

Tito stepped forward instantly. With the natural grace of a man accustomed to deference, he delivered the necessary few words in French on behalf of the Signoria, then bowed gracefully and yielded the path to the King. The presence of mind that had deserted him during the terrifying crisis of the morning returned with full force. It was a reliable servant that never abandoned him unless danger was visibly present. Yet, when later complimented on his timely intervention, Tito merely laughed it off as trivial, allowing Gaddi to claim the credit for the improvised welcome. After all, vanity is often the truest test of character.

Other aspects of the day, however, did not go as planned. The Florentine procession of clergy and laypeople had become hopelessly congested, forced to detour through back streets to reach the Cathedral. Furthermore, the young monarch, seated beneath his canopy with a lance resting on his thigh, bore little resemblance to the legendary Charlemagne; he looked more like a hastily modeled grotesque. One might have wished that the "Champion of the honor of women" possessed legs of normal length rather than a miserable stump, a mouth less like a reptile's slit, and features less exaggerated. Still, his thin leg rested upon cloth of gold and pearls, and his face was merely a small interruption amidst the sea of black velvet, gold, rubies, and the blazing embroidery of the canopy. *"Fù gran magnificenza."*

With cries of *Francia, Francia!* echoing through the streets, the people tore the canopy to shreds as an ancient custom dictated, claiming it as their spoil. Royal lips touched the altar, and despite the various mishaps, the King and his retinue were lodged in the Palace of the Via Larga. The nobility scattered to their great homes, and the formidable army camped in the fields of Prato. The official business of the day was done.

Yet, the streets retained a startling appearance unseen by Florentines under the November stars.
