Gone was the usual gloom, broken only by feeble lamps before saints' images or the red glow of open doorways. Now, lamps hung from every window, illuminating the thoroughfares so brightly that pedestrians walked as securely by night as by day. *"Fù gran magnificenza."*

At approximately eight o'clock, Tito Melema walked alone along these illuminated streets, heading home from the Piazza di Santa Croce via the Via dei Benci. Having spent the entire day under the weight of hidden anxieties, he had managed to slip away unnoticed from the post-dinner festivities. Now, free to confront his situation calmly, he hoped to adjust his mindset to the probable outcomes and shed his childish fears. He regretted only that he had not recognized Baldassarre earlier; had he done so, perhaps the outcome would have been happier for everyone, sparing him the guilt of knowingly inflicting suffering on his father. Yet, there was no turning back now. Conflict was impossible; the only path remaining was self-preservation.

Lost in these thoughts, Tito moved toward the angle where the Via dei Benci met the Borgo Santa Croce. Suddenly, his ears were pierced not by the music of revelry, but by the rhythmic clang of labor—the sound of the anvil.

"What keeps the giant working so late?" Tito wondered, quickening his pace. The noise sparked a welcome idea. "Much better for me; I can handle that small errand tonight instead of tomorrow morning."

Preoccupied though he was, he paused briefly in admiration as he approached the workshop. The wide doorway, situated at the corner of a massive block of houses, was topped by a loggia with fluted tile roofing and supported by stone columns bearing rough capitals. Framed in the warm red light spilled from within, the silhouette of Niccolò Caparra stood out in stark black relief. His huge arms rose and fell rhythmically, alternating between hiding and revealing the profile of his firm mouth and powerful brow. Beside him, two smaller figures—one at the anvil, the other pumping the bellows—accentuated his imposing massiveness.

Tito cast a shadow across the threshold, standing silent, knowing it was futile to speak until Niccolò took notice. This did not happen until the smith had sharpened the head of an axe to perfection and tossed it aside. Then, Niccolò nodded with an unceremonious but good-natured gesture, resting his hammer on his hip.

"What is it, Messer Tito? Business?"

"Assuredly, Niccolò; otherwise, I would not dare interrupt you while you work past hours, taking that as a sign your work is urgent," Tito replied smoothly.

"I've been at the same task all day—forging axes and spearheads. Every fool who passes my shop sticks his head in to ask, 'Niccolò, will you not come see the King of France and his soldiers?' And I answer, 'No: I do not wish to see their faces; I wish to see their backs.'"

"Are you making arms for the citizens, then, Niccolò, so they may have something better than rusty scythes and pitchforks in case of trouble?"

"We shall see. Arms are good, and Florence may soon need them. The Frate tells us we shall regain Pisa, and I agree with him. But I would like to know how that promise will be kept if we do not secure plenty of quality weapons. The Frate sees far ahead; I believe that. But he understands that birds cannot be caught by blinking at them, as some of our people pretend. He sees sense, not nonsense. However, you are a bit of a Medicean, Messer Tito Melema. Well, so was I once, before the wine began to turn sour. What brings you here?"

"Simply to inquire about the price of that fine coat of mail I noticed hanging here recently. I intend to purchase it for someone who requires protection beneath their doublet."

"Let him come and buy it himself," Niccolò replied bluntly. "I am particular about what I sell and to whom. I prefer to know my customers."

"I understand your scruples, Niccolò. But this is defensive armor; it cannot hurt anyone."

"True: but it may give the wearer a false sense of security if they decide to cause harm. No, no; this is not my own work. It is fine craftsmanship by Maso of Brescia. I would be reluctant to see it cover the heart of a scoundrel. I must know who will wear it."

"Well, then, to be perfectly honest, Niccolò mio, I want it myself," Tito admitted, sensing further persuasion was pointless. "In fact, I am likely to undertake a journey soon—and you know what traveling entails in these turbulent times. Do you suspect me of treason against the Republic?"

"No, I know nothing harmful of you," Niccolò said, returning to his blunt manner. "But do you have the funds to pay? You frequent my shop often enough to recognize my signs; you have seen the burning account books. I trust no one. The price is twenty florins, discounted because it is second-hand. You are unlikely to carry such a sum. Perhaps we can arrange payment for tomorrow

While Tito hurried across the bridge, the newly purchased armor hidden beneath his cloak, Romola paced the floor of the old library, her thoughts fixed on him and her heart longing for his return. Few faces had remained shut within their windows that day to watch the entry of the French King and his nobility; Romola's was one of the few. Since her father's sudden death three months prior, she had attended no festivities.

"Will Tito not come to write?" he had asked earlier that evening, long after the bell had tolled the customary hour. He had hesitated to ask, fearing a refusal, yet Romola had seen in his attentive gaze and restless movements that nothing else occupied his mind. "No, father," she had replied gently, offering a soft excuse. "He must attend a supper at the Cardinal's. Everyone desires his company so greatly."

"Aha!" Bardo had seemed momentarily pacified by this hope. "Perhaps he will bring some definite news regarding the library; the Cardinal promised last week..." He fell silent for a moment, then flushed suddenly. "I must proceed without him, Romola. Fetch the pen. He brings me no new text to annotate, yet I must speak on the New Platonists. I shall die, and nothing will remain undone. Haste yourself, my Romola."

"I am ready, father," she had said instantly, holding the pen aloft. Yet, silence followed. Romola ignored it for a while, accustomed to pauses in dictation, but when she finally glanced around inquiringly, her father's posture had not shifted. "Father, I am entirely ready!" Still, Bardo remained mute, and his silence was never broken again.

Looking back on that hour, Romola felt a sting of indignation toward herself. Even amidst the first surge of grief, an irrepressible thought had mingled: *Perhaps my life with Tito will be more perfect now.* The dream of a triple existence, united in undivided happiness, had not been fully realized. Ideally, a rainbow shower of sweets should contain invisible seeds of bitterness; the crowned Ariadne, beneath falling roses, had increasingly sensed unexpected thorns.

"It was not Tito's fault," Romola told herself repeatedly. He remained gentle to both her and her father. However, she now saw clearly that the nature of things dictated that no one but herself could endure, month after month, year after year, the monotony of her father's exacting demands. Even she, whose youthful passion and faith stemmed from her deep sympathy with him, had not always been patient; inward rebellion had often stirred within her. True, before their marriage—and for some time thereafter—Tito had appeared more tireless than she. But naturally, the burden felt lighter when novel. One assumes a load with confident readiness, and the growing weight remains tolerable until the desire for relief becomes irresistible.

She chastised her younger self for ignorance, vowing now to make no unfair demands on the man to whom she had offered her whole womanhood. The sadness that clung to her as she watched her father slowly sink from elation into fresh disappointment—while Tito offered bland excuses for withholding his share of their joint work—was not Tito's fault. It was their inevitable destiny. If he stayed away, it was because they could rarely be truly alone. His caresses remained tender; if she timidly pleaded for him to stay with her father instead of accepting non-peremptory engagements, he excused himself with charming gaiety, lingering with fond playfulness before departing. Thus, she felt only a small ache of heartbreak amidst her love, then turned to soothe her father's vexation.

Yet, inwardly, her imagination struggled to reconcile Tito's goodness with his inability to sacrifice the vibrant social pleasures that appealed more intensely to a bright spirit like his than to ordinary men. She, too, craved gaiety and admiration; she willingly surrendered them for her father's sake, and would have sacrificed much more for even a slight wish of Tito's. Their natures differed widely, perhaps merely reflecting the inherent gap between man and woman, wherein her affections proved more absorbing. If there were other differences, she persuaded herself the inferiority lay with her. Tito was kinder, better tempered, less proud and resentful. He held no angry retorts, met all complaints with perfect sweetness, and slipped quietly away from unpleasant duties.

Large natures, when not gripped by strong, unquestioning emotion, tend to suspect themselves, doubting the truth of their own impressions, aware of horizons beyond their reach. Romola was driven to doubt herself all the more by the necessity of interpreting her disappointment in her marriage in a way that satisfied both her love and her pride. Was there no milder word than "disappointment"? Perhaps all women suffered the disillusionment of ignorant hopes, if only they knew the experience.

Still, her lot held something peculiar: her relationship with her father demanded unusual sacrifices from her husband. Tito had once believed his love would render these sacrifices easy; his love had not been sufficient. She was not justified in resenting a self-deception. Resentment must not arise; all endurance seemed easier to Romola than admitting that Tito acted unworthily. Any new heartache she felt during her solitary hours with her father in his final months was not her husband's fault. Now, however, a hope arose—one that would manifest even in the first moments after her father's place became empty—that there was no longer any pressing claim to divide her from Tito. Their young lives would merge into one current, and their true marriage could finally begin.

Yet, a sense of guilt toward her father colored this hope, born from his death, intensifying her anxiety to fulfill his supreme wish. This piety toward his memory served as the sole atonement for thoughts that bordered on joy at his passing. The laborious, simple life, pure from vulgar corruption but embittered by frustrated hopes, imprisoned in total darkness—a long seedtime yielding no harvest—had ended. What remained, aside from the tablet in Santa Croce and the unfinished commentary on Tito's text, was the collection of manuscripts and antiquities, the fruit of fifty years of toil and frugality. Fulfilling her father's lifelong ambition for this library was a sacramental obligation for Romola.

The precious relics were safe from creditors. When the deficit was calculated, Bernardo del Nero, though not among Florence's wealthiest, had advanced nearly a thousand florins—a vast sum then—accepting a lien on the collection as security. "The State will repay me," he had told Romola, treating the service lightly despite the inconvenience it cost him. "If the Cardinal finds a building, as he seems to promise, our Signoria may complete the rest. I have no children; I can afford the risk."

But within the last ten days, all hopes tied to the Medici had evaporated. The famous Medicean collections in the Via Larga faced dispersal. French agents already argued that such exquisite antique gems, gathered by Lorenzo, rightfully belonged to the first nation in Europe. The Florentine State, having seized the Medicean library, was likely to seek a buyer. With a war to reclaim Pisa looming and the certainty of paying heavy subsidies to the French King, the State preferred coin to manuscripts.

To Romola, these grave political shifts held significance primarily through their impact on her father's legacy. Raised in learned seclusion, she had viewed heroic deeds and great principles as antithetical to the vulgar present, considering the Pnyx and Forum more worthy than the councils of living Florentines. Now, the expulsion of the Medici mattered to her little more than the extinguishing of her best hope for the library. Though she knew the times were perilous for friends of the Medici, like her godfather and Tito, and that superstitious shopkeepers and the rabble brimmed with suspicion, her newfound keen interest in public events—the outbreak of war, the French King's visit, the impending state changes—was ignited solely by love and duty to her father's memory.

All of Romola's ardor had concentrated in her affections. Her participation in her father's scholarly pursuits had been little more than a toil endured for his sake; Tito's airy brilliance held no attraction for her that did not merge into the deeper sympathies of young love and trust. She had touched no mind capable of stirring the larger possibilities of her nature; they lay folded and crushed like embryonic wings, contributing nothing to her consciousness except an occasional vague unease.

However, this new personal stake in public affairs compelled her to understand precisely how Fra Girolamo's preaching might sway events. Discussions of state transformation filled the air, and everything she learned from Tito, whose secretaryship immersed him in the heart of public business, only fueled her eagerness to hear for herself what was currently tearing Florence apart. That morning, for the first time, she had attended one of Fra Girolamo's Advent sermons in the Duomo.

After Tito departed, she had formed a sudden resolve. Visiting the spot where her father lay buried in Santa Croce, she had walked on to the Duomo. The memory of her last scene with Dino remained vivid, yet it had receded behind the experiences and anxieties of her married life. The new sensibilities and questions it had half-awakened were quieted again by that submission to her husband's mind characteristic of a wife who loves with passionate devotion and full reliance.

She remembered the effect of Fra Girolamo's voice and presence, expecting his sermon to move her despite his narrow-mindedness. Indeed, it deepened her previous impression that this fanatical preacher of tribulation was a man deserving of personal regard and reverence. His denunciations and exhortations merely arrested her attention. She felt no terror

Four days later, Romola set out for the house of Piero di Cosimo on the Via Gualfonda. The streets she traversed were thick with a peculiar tension. On one side stood Frenchmen, gazing at Florence with an air of condescending superiority; on the other, Florentines stared back with growing irritation. The dynamic was far from harmonious. Having initially played the role of gracious hosts, presenting wondrous religious mysteries with floating clouds and angelic machinery, the city now found its patience worn thin. After the royal guest had indulged in "Most Christian" glances at balls and suppers, the veneer of diplomacy cracked. To the new "Charlemagne," Florence appeared less a sovereign ally and more a conquered prize; he spoke of leaving a viceroy behind and hinted at restoring the Medici. This logic seemed absurd to those who knew that the sole offense of Florence against the French crown was the unpopular policy of Piero de' Medici, whom the people had already cast out. Determination to resist hardened among the citizens within the Old Palace and manifested boldly on street banners designed to mock the insolent visitors. Consequently, the thoroughfares became unsafe promenades for ladies of quality. Yet, Romola, veiled in black and accompanied by the elderly Maso, felt secure from prying eyes, driven instead by an urgent need to visit the painter.

Piero's work on a copy of her father's portrait—as Oedipus—remained unfinished. Known for his erratic habits, the artist would sometimes abandon a commission for months or hide it away in a corner, only to resurface it weeks later. Though Romola was his favorite and he generally eager to please, his whimsical nature meant no promise could be fully trusted. Having heard the previous week that the piece might be ready, she grew nervous. She feared that without a physical record, the image of her blind father might fade from her mind. Driven by a profound sense of duty and love—a devotion that seeks its own completeness through perfect loyalty—she felt compelled to ensure the preservation of his likeness.

By special favor, Romola was permitted to enter the studio unannounced. She lifted the iron slide and called out in a soft, flute-like tone, mimicking the gesture of a little girl once offered eggs by the same master. Piero answered quickly, but when he opened the door, his explanation was surprisingly mundane.

"Ah, Madonna Romola, is it you?" he said. "I thought my eggs had arrived; I wanted them."

"I have brought you something better than hard eggs, Piero," Romola replied, smiling as she adjusted her veil. "Maso has secured a basket of cakes and *confetti* for you."

She handed the basket to Maso and stepped inside. "I know you enjoy these treats when they arrive without effort. Confess, do you?"

"Yes, whenever they come to me as effortlessly as the light," Piero admitted, crossing his arms and looking down at the sweets as Romola unveiled them. His gaze then rose to her face and hair, filled with a painter's appreciation as her heavy hood slipped back. "But I suspect these sweetmeats are intended to silence you while you scold me. Very well, proceed to the next room; you will find I have made progress on the picture, though it remains incomplete. Remember, I never promised—" quoting the proverb, *"He who promises and does not keep, shall never have his soul in order."*

The door leading to the wild garden was shut, and the painter was indeed working. However, he was not attending to Romola's commission. That canvas stood propped against the wall, and as Piero bent to lift it into the proper light, he inadvertently revealed another work beneath it: an oil sketch of Tito. It was significantly smaller than the other pieces, tucked deep within the arrangement, and Piero, distracted by a minor detail in the painting he held, failed to notice what he had exposed until he moved to place the Oedipus portrait on an easel.

Romola gasped, her face flushing with shock. "That is Tito!"

Piero glanced around, offering a silent shrug of vexation at his own lapse in memory. While Romola continued to stare at the sketch in bewildered alarm, she turned to the painter with a mixture of confusion and dread.

"What a strange picture!" she exclaimed. "When did you paint this? What does it signify?"

"A mere whim," Piero dismissed, removing his skullcap and scratching his head while making a characteristic grimace to deflect deeper inquiry. "I needed a handsome young face, and your husband's was perfectly suited."

He moved toward the sketch, holding it with his back to Romola, feigning a casual inspection before attempting to set it aside as unworthy of display. But Romola, recalling the distinctive chain-mail armor she had seen on Tito, felt a chill run through her. The coincidence of the armor linking Tito to the concept of fear struck her deeply. Stepping close to the painter's elbow, she insisted, "Do not put it away; let me look again. That man with the rope around his neck—I saw him. I saw you approach him in the Duomo. What drove you to include him in a picture with Tito?"

Seeing no better path than partial honesty, Piero hesitated. "It was an accident. The man was fleeing—running up the steps—and grabbed onto your husband. Perhaps he stumbled. I happened to be present, saw the scene, and thought the savage-looking old fellow made a suitable subject. But it is worthless; merely a freakish daub."

With an air of finality, he moved the sketch away and placed it high on a shelf. "Come and view the Oedipus."

His excessive eagerness to conceal the sketch had achieved the opposite of his intent: it confirmed Romola's suspicion that there was something troubling regarding the encounter. Pride and delicacy, however, prevented her from pressing further; to question him might imply she harbored doubts about her husband's character. Instead, she spoke quietly, "He was a strange, pitiful-looking prisoner. Do you know anything else of him?"

"No more," Piero replied smoothly. "I merely showed him the way to the hospital. Now, observe the face of Oedipus; it is nearly complete. Tell me your thoughts."

For a long moment, Romola studied the portrait in silence. Finally, she turned to him with bright, tear-filled eyes. "Ah, you have achieved exactly what I desired. You have captured the listening look. My dear Piero, I am deeply grateful."

"Now, that is precisely what I cannot endure in women," Piero snapped, kicking aside clutter on the floor. "You pour forth emotions where none are warranted. Why should you thank me for a painting you paid for, especially when I make you wait? If I paint, surely it is for my own pleasure and credit to execute it well. Should you thank a man for not being a rogue or a fool? It suffices if he thanks God for granting him such virtue. Women imagine walls are held together with honey."

"You crusty Piero! I forgot how irritable you can be," Romola smiled, wiping away tears as she offered him a crisp sweetmeat from the basket.

Piero accepted the treat with the grace of a bear dreaming of pears—he genuinely enjoyed the sweetness despite his rough exterior, accustomed to hiding both his joys and sorrows behind a shaggy coat. "It is excellent, Madonna Antigone," he remarked, reaching for another. He had subsisted on hard eggs for two weeks.

Standing opposite him, Romola felt her mounting anxiety momentarily suspended by this naive enjoyment. Soon, however, she prepared to leave. "Good-bye, Piero," she said, placing the empty basket down. "I promise not to thank you if you finish the portrait promptly and well. In that case, I shall remind you that you were bound to do it for your own reputation."

"Good," Piero replied curtly, assisting her neatly with her mantle and veil.

Once the door clicked shut behind her, Piero breathed a sigh of relief. "I am glad she asked no more questions about that sketch," he thought. "I would be distressed if she guessed I considered her fine husband a fitting model for a coward. But I treated it lightly; she will not dwell on it."

Yet, Piero proved too sanguine, falling prey to the common error of open-hearted men attempting subtle deception. As Romola walked home, the image of the sketch weighed heavily on her mind. She could not reconcile the facts: the chain-armour and the encounter described by Piero, both occurring on the morning the armor was acquired. Had the painter witnessed the look of terror on Tito's face? What did it all signify?

"It means nothing," she whispered to herself, trying to steady her resolve. "It was merely a coincidence. Shall I ask Tito about it?"

Her reason finally prevailed: "No. I will not question him about anything he has not voluntarily shared. To do so would be an offense against the trust I owe him."

But her heart countered, trembling with fear: "I dare not ask him."

There existed a terrible fracture in their bond. Like a person clutching something precious, terrified that it might be broken, she froze, unable to move hastily toward the truth.

"The old fellow has vanished; he headed toward Arezzo the next morning. I suppose he didn't care for the scent of the French after spending time as their prisoner. I stopped by the hospital to ask if those monkish cooks had managed to determine whether he was in his right mind. They told me he showed no signs of madness—merely ignored questions entirely, appearing as though he were tending a vineyard twenty miles away. He was a mysterious old tiger. I would have loved to learn more about him."

These words were spoken by Piero di Cosimo within Nello's shop on the twenty-fourth of November, exactly one week following the arrival of the French forces. A gathering of six or seven souls had assembled at the unusually late hour of three in the afternoon. It was a day when all of Florence buzzed with anticipation regarding a decisive political turning point. Every public resting place was packed, and every shopkeeper without a spouse or deputy to manage the premises stood at his doorway, thumbs hooked in his belt. Meanwhile, the streets were dotted with artisans pausing or drifting lazily like floating debris, poised to surge forward with impetuosity should any spark catch their attention.

Nello had been strumming his lute while half-sitting on a board propped against the shop window, keeping a vigilant eye on the piazza. "Ah," he declared, setting down the instrument with emphasis, "for a gold florin, I would not have missed that spectacle of the French soldiers waddling in their broad shoes after their fleeing prisoners! That is the penalty for leaving my shop to shave magnificent chins. It is always thus: whenever I depart from this navel of the world, something significant happens in my piazza."

"Yes, you should have been there," Piero remarked in his cutting tone, "to witness your favorite Greek trembling as though Satanasso himself had seized him. I delight in seeing your perpetually smiling gentlemen caught in a sudden gale, forced to reveal their linings despite their best efforts. What color do you imagine a man's liver turns when he resembles a bleached deer the moment a chance stranger grabs him?"

"Piero, keep your vinegar reserved as a condiment for your own eggs!" Nello retorted. "What fault does my *bel erudito* bear for looking startled when he felt a pair of claws upon him and spotted an unchained madman at his side? Your scholar is not akin to those beastly Swiss and Germans, whose heads are suited only for battering-rams and whose appetites are so voracious they consider swallowing a cannonball before breakfast acceptable. We Florentines value qualities in a man beyond that vulgar commodity known as bravery, which is acquired by hiring dullards at a price per dozen. Tell you what: once men realized they possessed greater intellect than oxen, they set the oxen to pull plows for them. And when we Florentines discovered we held superior minds to other nations, we commissioned them to fight for us."

"Treason, Nello!" a voice cried from the inner room. "That contradicts the doctrine of the State. Florence is currently grinding its weapons, and the latest authenticated vision proclaimed by the Friar depicts Mars standing atop the Palazzo Vecchio, his arm resting on the shoulder of Saint John the Baptist, who offers him a piece of honeycomb."

"It is well, Francesco," Nello replied calmly. "Florence possesses a few thick-skulled individuals suitable for bombarding Pisa; meanwhile, the finer spirits remain at home to perform the thinking and the shaving. As for our Piero here, if he prizes valor so highly, let him take his largest brush as a weapon and his palette as a shield, challenging the widest-mouthed Swiss he encounters in the Prato to single combat."

"Go on, Nello," growled Piero. "Your tongue rolls on as usual, like a mill when the Arno is swollen—regardless of whether there is grain to grind."

"Excellent grain, I assure you. It would be as logical to expect a weather-beaten painter like you to enjoy having a javelin thrust into you as it would be to anticipate a man whose wit has been honed by the classics enjoying having his handsome face torn by a wild beast."

"There you go again, assuming people will insert their legs into a sack merely because you term it 'hosen,'" Piero countered. "Who suggested a wild beast, or an unarmed man charging into battle? Combat is a trade, and it is not mine. I would be a fool to pursue danger, yet I could confront it if it sought me out."

"How is it you fear the thunder so greatly, then, my Piero?" Nello pressed, determined to expose the accuser. "One might assume you understand why a trivial matter shakes one man while leaving others unmoved—you who hide among the rats the moment a storm approaches."

"That stems from a particular sensitivity to loud noises; it has nothing to do with my courage or my conscience."

"Well, perhaps Tito Melema shares a similar sensitivity to being unexpectedly seized by prisoners escaping from French soldiers. Humans are born with antipathies; I personally cannot abide the smell of mint. Tito was born with an aversion to elderly prisoners who stumble and clutch. Ecco!"

A general laugh erupted at Nello's defense, revealing clearly that Piero's dislike for Tito was not shared by the assembly. The painter, wearing an indecipherable grimace, pulled tow from his bag and stuffed his ears in indignant contempt, while Nello continued triumphantly.

"No, my Piero, I cannot afford to have my *bel erudito* disparaged; nor can Florence, with her scholars molting off her at the tender age of forty. Our Phoenix Pico has ascended straight to Paradise, as the Friar informs us; and the incomparable Poliziano departed not two months ago—to well, let us hope he has not joined the eminent scholars in the Malebolge."

"By the way," interjected Francesco Cei, "have you heard that Camilla Rucellai has surpassed the Friar in prophecy? Two years ago she foretold Pico would die during the season of lilies. He passed in November. 'Not at all the time of lilies,' scoffed the critics. 'Go to!' replies Camilla; 'I meant the lilies of France, and they seem close enough beneath your noses.' I say, 'Euge, Camilla!' Should the Friar prove that any of his visions have been fulfilled as accurately, I shall declare myself a Piagnone tomorrow."

"You are somewhat too flippant regarding the Friar, Francesco," observed Pietro Cennini, the scholar. "In these recent weeks, we owe him dearly for preaching peace, quietude, and the abandonment of factional strife. Those of small discernment would gladly see the populace slip the Friar's leash just now. And if the Most Christian King remains obstinate regarding the treaty today, refusing to sign terms fair and honorable to Florence, Fra Girolamo is the man we must trust to bring him to reason."

"You speak truth, Messer Pietro," Nello agreed. "The Friar is one of the firmest nails Florence has to hang upon—at least, that is the opinion of the most respectable chins I have the honor of shaving. Yet, young Messer Niccolò remarked earlier this morning—and doubtless Francesco implies the same—there exists a wonderful elasticity in interpreting visions, comparable to stretching Dido's bull-hide. It appears a dream may signify whatever follows it. As our Franco Sacchetti notes, a woman dreams overnight of a serpent biting her, smashes a drinking cup the next day, and cries out, 'Behold, I thought something would occur—it is plain now what the serpent meant.'"

"But the Friar's visions are not of that sort," stated Cronaca. "He declares not only what will happen—that the Church will be scourged and renewed, and the heathens converted—but insists it shall happen swiftly. He is no slippery pretender providing loopholes for himself; he is—"

"What is this? What is this?" Nello exclaimed, leaping from the board and pressing his head out the door. "People are streaming into the piazza, shouting. Something must have occurred in the Via Larga. Aha!" he burst forth with delighted astonishment, stepping outside laughing and waving his cap.

The remainder of the company hastened to the threshold. News from the Via Larga was precisely what they awaited. Yet, they were not slightly surprised by the manner of its arrival. Seated upon a bench, seemingly snatched from the street and hoisted above the shoulders of the mob, sat Tito Melema, smiling in amused resignation at the compulsion he faced. His cap had slipped from his head, dangling by the *becchetto* loosely wound around his neck. Upon spotting the group at Nello's door, he raised his fingers in a gesture of beckoning recognition. Within the next minute, he had leaped from the bench onto a cart laden with bales stationed in the broad space between the Baptistery and the steps of the Duomo, while the crowd swarmed around him with the noisy eagerness of poultry awaiting feed.

Silence fell when he began to speak in his clear, mellow voice.

"Citizens of Florence! I possess no warrant to deliver this news save your own will. Yet the tidings are good, and will harm none in hearing. The Most Christian King is signing a treaty honorable to Florence. But you owe this to one of your citizens, a man who spoke words worthy of the ancient Romans—you owe it to Piero Capponi!"

Immediately, a roar of voices erupted. "Capponi! Capponi! What did our Piero say?" "

It was the first time Baldassarre had ventured into the Piazza del Duomo since his flight. A burning desire seized him to hear the renowned monk preach once more, yet he recoiled from returning to the very spot where he had stood half-naked, his hair unkempt, a noose around his throat, branded by the crowd as a lunatic. The humiliation was too raw, too vivid, to be erased simply by the transformation of his appearance. When Tito had spoken those words—"Some madman, surely"—it was not merely their cruelty that stung like a viper's bite; it was the bitter, instantaneous realization that Baldassarre might indeed be powerless to disprove them. Alongside his consuming urge for vengeance rose a chilling uncertainty: could he actually achieve it? It felt as though Tito had been guided by a devilish prompter, whispering Baldassarre's darkest secrets directly into the traitor's ear.

Baldassarre was not mad. He bore the tragic mark of sanity: the clear, agonizing awareness of his own shattered faculties. He measured his own weakness. Yet, with every surge of vindictive rage, a primal caution stirred within him, akin to a wounded beast or an insect trembling on crumbling ground. This distrust, this refusal to take any step that might reveal his condition, had led him to reject Piero di Cosimo's friendly overtures. Even at the hospital, he had offered only a fabricated tale of capture by the French on his journey from Genoa. However, his age and the peculiar cadence of his speech marked him as unlike the usual beggars or travelers housed there. Moved by pity, the monks bestowed upon him a coarse woolen tunic against the chill, a pair of peasant shoes, and a handful of *danari*, the smallest Florentine coins.

He departed for the road to Arezzo at dawn but halted at the nearest town. Using two of his coins, he paid a barber to shave him and clip his hair short, restoring his former style. The barber possessed a small hand-mirror of polished steel. Years had passed since Baldassarre had gazed into such a reflection. Now, as his eyes met the glass, a sudden, jarring thought pierced his mind: *"Does Tito truly not recognize me?"* The thought arrested his impulsive momentum, sending a shock through him; his hand trembled like a leaf as he reached for the mirror. He needed to see himself before shaving. The barber, noting his shake, held the glass steady.

No, he was not altered beyond recognition. He knew the depth of those wrinkles from three years prior; they were merely deeper now. The skin remained rough and clumsy, forming shallow bumps on his brow like cryptic symbols, only yellower, resembling a lifeless rind. That shaggy white beard was no disguise to eyes that had studied him closely for sixteen years, or to eyes that should have expected to find him changed, much as one searches for a beloved among the debris washed ashore. Something had shifted in his gaze, yes—but a shift that should only have made recognition more startling. Is not a known voice all the more piercing when it emerges as a desperate cry? Doubt was folly; he knew Tito recognized him. Pushing the mirror away, Baldassarre felt the surging currents of hatred and vengeance resume their flow.

He retraced his steps toward Florence but refused to enter the city until dusk. Turning aside from the main road, he sat by a tranquil pool shaded by alders still holding yellow leaves. It was a calm November day, and the still water seemed a perfect mirror for his soul. He wanted to scrutinize himself slowly, something he had feared to do before the barber. Sitting on the pool's edge, he leaned forward, staring intently at his reflection. Was there a wandering, imbecilic quality in his face, mirroring the chaos within? Not now. Under his gaze of eager inquiry, his eyes burned with intense purpose. But in other moments? Yes, undoubtedly. During the long hours of vague longing for a forgotten past, when he sat in lonely darkness visited by mocking whispers and phantom figures that drifted away from his grasping hands, his expression must have been one of frustrated bewilderment. Worse still, when the thick cloud of confusion briefly parted, offering a glimpse of hope before crashing down again, leaving him helpless—his face must have shown the blank terror of sudden blindness.

Could he prove anything? Could he even begin to speak with the confidence that his train of thought would not fracture? Would anyone believe he had once possessed a mind brimming with rare knowledge, engaged in profound contemplation, fluent in varied tongues? All of it had slipped away—that laboriously gathered treasury. Was it utterly lost, like water poured into the vast ocean? Or was it still trapped within him, locked behind a barrier that might someday shatter? He clung desperately to this hope. Since the day he dragged himself weakly from his straw bed, feeling a new darkness descend even in sunlight, his mind had undergone transformations—some gradual, others sudden flashes. As his physical strength returned, so did his self-command and the energy of his will. He regained the memory of the parts of his life woven tightly with emotion, and felt increasingly the painful ache of lost knowledge. Yet, occasionally, when strongly agitated, he seemed momentarily to reclaim his former self, as an old man might briefly recall the vigor of his youth. He saw Greek texts clearly again; his mind moved freely among familiar concepts. It was but a flash, and the encroaching darkness made the loss seem more horrific, but perhaps it could return and stay long enough for him to execute his revenge—devising a torment so exquisite that a mere arm could never inflict it.

Rising from his crouch, Baldassarre folded his arms and concentrated all his mental force on the path ahead. He must wait for knowledge and opportunity, and during this waiting, he needed a means of survival without begging. His greatest fear was being perceived as a foolish, helpless old man. No one must know that half his memory was gone; the lost strength might return, and even a brief resurgence could suffice. He knew how to begin gathering intelligence on Tito. The name "Bratti Ferravecchi" had become etched into his mind through constant repetition. A man in Genoa, who had seen Tito's ring, had informed him that the gemstone was purchased in Florence from a young, well-dressed Greek with a handsome dark face, at the shop of a secondhand dealer named Bratti Ferravecchi, located in the street also called Ferravecchi.

This revelation sent Baldassarre into a violent turmoil. Until then, he had clung tenaciously to his faith in Tito, refusing to believe he had been deliberately abandoned. Initially, he reasoned, "My piece of parchment never reached him; that is why I toil still at Antioch. But he is searching; he knows where I was lost; he will find me eventually." Later, taken to Corinth, he convinced his captors, promising a ransom, that inquiries would be made regarding his fate in Antioch. At Corinth, he thought joyfully, "Here, finally, he must find me. Whichever way he turns, he will encounter me." But before another year passed, illness struck, leaving him body and mind shattered, worth less than nothing save for a ransom that never arrived. Sitting helpless in the morning sun, he concluded, "Tito has drowned, or they have imprisoned him too. I shall see him no more. He set out after me, but misfortune overtook him. I shall never see his face again."

In his new frailty and despair, supporting his head in his hands with vacant eyes and uncertain lips, he appeared so hopelessly imbecile that his owners gladly discarded him, handing him over to a compassionate Genoese merchant aboard his galley. Through months of travel across the Aegean and the coast of Asia Minor, Baldassarre regained his physical strength, yet upon landing in Genoa, he felt such profound desolation that he almost wished he had died of his illness in Corinth. One possibility prevented suicide: Tito might live, imprisoned or destitute. If so, there remained a faint, distant hope that he might find his child, his cherished son, once more; that he might clasp hands and meet face-to-face with the only being who remembered him as he was before his mind broke.

It was in this state of feeling that he encountered the stranger wearing Tito's onyx ring. Though Baldassarre could not have described the ring beforehand, its sight stirred dormant fibers, and he recognized it instantly. The fact that Tito, nearly a year after their separation, lived in apparent prosperity in Florence, selling the gem he should have reserved for direst need, was a reality Baldassarre shrank from confronting. He preferred to be stunned and bewildered by it rather than forced to think distinctly. He tried to feel only the joy of beholding Tito again. Perhaps Tito believed his father dead; somehow, the mystery would resolve. "At least I shall meet eyes that remember me. I am not alone in the world." Again, Baldassarre declared, "I am not alone; my revenge walks beside me."

As the instrument of this revenge, something external and subservient to his true existence, he bent once more to examine his reflection with hard curiosity. He did not care for the withered, forsaken old man whose soul was like a deserted home, cold and bare. Human passion, whether low or high, reaches a point where it ceases to be purely egoistic, becoming a fire within us to which everything else is merely fuel. He stared at the pale, black-browed image in the water until he identified it with the self from which his revenge

"My dear Romola," Tito said on the second morning following his impassioned speech in the Piazza del Duomo, "I shall be receiving distinguished guests today. The Count from Milan returns, accompanied by the Seneschal de Beaucaire, the favorite of the King of France. I know you have no inclination to endure the smiling formalities required by such rustling magnates, figures we are unlikely to encounter again. Since they intend to inspect our antiquities and library, perhaps you should set aside your work for the day and accompany your cousin Brigida."

Romola perceived the underlying wish in this suggestion and immediately agreed. Yet, moments later, returning to the room wrapped in her hood and mantle, she remarked, "Oh, what a long breath Florence will draw when the gates swing wide and the last French soldier walks out! Even you seem weary, Tito, despite your patience; confess it. Ah, your brow feels warm."

Tito was leaning over his desk, his pen moving steadily across the parchment. She had reached out to lay her hand upon his head, intending a final caress. This posture was familiar to him; usually, when he felt her touch, he would lift his head, lean back slightly, and gaze upward at her. But now, he found himself as incapable of raising his head as if her hand were a heavy, leaden cowl resting upon him. Instead, speaking in a light, casual tone while his pen continued its journey, he replied, "The French are as eager to depart from Florence as wasps leaving a ripe pear once they have finished feeding."

Sensing sharply the absence of his customary emotional resonance, Romola withdrew her hand and declared, "I am going, Tito."

"Farewell, my sweet one. I must remain here. Take Maso with you."

Still, Tito did not look up. Romola departed without further word. In the quiet rhythm of marriage, even the slightest shifts mark epochs. That morning, for the first time, she acknowledged within herself not merely that Tito had changed, but that he had altered specifically toward her. Could the fault lie with her? She might have suspected so had it not been for the tangible evidence of the armor and the painting—facts suggesting an external catastrophe entirely mysterious to her.

The moment Romola stepped outside, however, Tito lowered his pen and lifted his gaze, finding a delightful security in facing only parchment and broken marble. A profound disgust rose within him at his failure to look up and treat her as he always had. If he could choose, he would strive to be kinder than ever; yet he could not suddenly master an involuntary shrinking from her presence. This retreat stemmed paradoxically from the very traits that drove him to desire constant displays of affection.

He stood poised to take a step certain to provoke her deepest indignation—a path fraught with unpleasantness that he would have to traverse to earn her forgiveness. Tito possessed a nature utterly devoid of defiance or impudence; every instinct leaned toward preserving her tenderness. He was not plagued by sentimental scruples, which he had quickly dismissed as irrelevant to practical utility. Yet, his lack of scruples did not shield him from the dread of discomfort. Unscrupulousness removes many burdens, but not toothaches, wounded vanity, or the crushing sense of loneliness. Against these, the world offers no defense save a sound jaw and a just, loving soul. At this precise moment, Tito felt acutely that no strategy could spare him the pain of the inevitable collision with Romola; no smooth persuasion could soften the blow driving him forward like a timid beast urged toward a desperate leap by the terror of teeth and claws closing in.

A secret conviction had long held that his stubborn adherence to Bardo's wishes regarding the library had devolved into sentimental folly, robbing both himself and Romola of vital advantages. Perhaps this realization would never have crystallized into action without the events of the past week, which introduced a pressing new motive alongside a rare opportunity. Indeed, it was only after his dread intensified upon seeing Baldassarre regain his composure—and only after he began to fear he might be forced to flee Florence—that he resolved to exercise his legal right to sell the library before the chance presented by the French and Milanese bidders vanished. For if exile became necessary, he refused to depart as a destitute wanderer. Accustomed to an agreeable existence, he sought to retain the means for maintaining it. Among his desires was to carry Romola with him, and, if possible, to carry no infamy. Success had awakened in him an appetite for the pleasures afforded by advantageous social standing. To decamp under dishonor, even laden with diamonds, appeared to him not as a temptation, but as a hideous alternative, inviting the life of an adventurer.

He could not achieve independence even among those Florentines who treated him with mere respect, let alone with Romola. She was the wife of his first love; he still loved her. She belonged to the essential furniture of his life, from which he shrank at the thought of separation. He winced under her judgment, unsure how far her revulsion might extend. All the power a husband typically wields over a wife—the power that drives men to betrayals a lover would never dare—proved insufficient to quell Tito's unease. This was the leaden weight that had overwhelmed his will, preventing him from lifting his head to meet her eyes. Her pure light illuminated too clearly the prospect of an approaching struggle.

Yet, there was no help for it. If they were to leave Florence, they needed money. Indeed, Tito could not arrange his life to his satisfaction without a considerable sum. This obsession with securing financial stability had been the root of all his transgressions. He would have been capable of any sacrifice that did not involve personal discomfort.

The distinguished visitors arrived and departed; the bargains were struck. When Romola returned home that evening, nothing grave was spoken. Tito was merely gay and chatty, pouring out stories and descriptions of the French visit with a liveliness Romola had not seen before. She sensed an effort in his cheerfulness, interpreting it as a sign of contrition born of knowing he had wounded her earlier. Internally, she ached at this visible widening of the distance between them—that there existed an offense neither dared to name.

The following day, Tito stayed away until late at night. It proved to be a significant day for Romola, for Piero di Cosimo, spurred by guilt over potentially causing her pain in the previous week, had dispatched her father's portrait. She had propped it against the back of his old chair and gazed at it for some time when the door opened behind her, revealing Bernardo del Nero.

"It is you, godfather! How I wish you had come sooner! It is already growing dusk," Romola said, moving toward him.

"I stopped by simply to share good news, knowing Tito had not yet returned," Bernardo replied. "The French King departs tomorrow; it is finally high time. There was another skirmish this morning between our citizens and his soldiers, but now there is hope the city will restore order and trade resume."

"That is joyful," Romola responded. "But it seems sudden. Tito seemed to believe yesterday there was little prospect of the King leaving soon."

"He has been well barked at, that is the reason," Bernardo smiled. "His own generals opened their throats quite effectively, and eventually, our Signoria unleashed the city's mastiff, Fra Girolamo. The Cristianissimo was frightened by that thunderclap and issued orders to move. I fear there will be little unity among us once he is gone, but at least all parties are relieved not to have Florence stifled by soldiery anymore. The Friar barked successfully this time."

"Ah, what is this?" Bernardo asked, noticing Romola leading him before the portrait, her arm around his. "Let us see."

He began uncoiling his long scarf as she arranged a seat for him. "Do you not want your spectacles, godfather?" Romola asked anxiously, wishing he might see exactly what she saw.

"No, child, no," Bernardo replied, uncovering his grey head as he sat with firm erectness. "For viewing at this distance, my old eyes are perhaps superior to yours. An old man's eyes are like an old man's memory; they are strongest for things far away."

"It is better than having no portrait at all," Romola said apologetically after a brief silence. "It resembles him less than the image I hold in my mind, though that image may fade with the years."

She rested her arm on the old man's shoulder, drawn to him by their shared reverence for the dead. "I do not know," Bernardo mused. "Almost, I think I see Bardo as he was in his youth more clearly than this picture shows him in his old age. Your father possessed great fire in his eyes when young. It was something I could never fully grasp—that he, with such an impatient spirit, could dedicate himself to books and dwell among shadows his whole life. Nevertheless, he poured his heart into it."

Bernardo gave a slight shrug at the end, but Romola detected in his voice a sentiment resonating with her own. "And he was disappointed to the very end," she said involuntarily. Immediately fearing her words might sound like an accusation against Tito, she hastened to add, "If only we could witness his longest, dearest wish fulfilled precisely according to his mind!"

"Well, so we may," Bernardo said kindly, rising to put on his cap. "Times are cloudy now, but fish are caught by waiting. Who knows? When the wheel turns sufficiently, I may yet be Gonfaloniere before I die; and no creditor can touch

The following day, Romola joined the general excitement sweeping through Florence regarding the imminent departure of the French forces. Beyond the shared civic relief, she harbored a vague, almost superstitious hope that the specific anxieties plaguing her regarding Tito—burdens that had arrived alongside the foreign guests—might dissipate with them. Though the French had occupied the city for barely eleven days, Romola felt a depth of acute unhappiness in that span she had never previously known. Since their arrival, Tito had donned a kind of hateful armor; while she could not logically discern why their exit should cure his fear, the terrifying image of a prisoner seizing him, as depicted in Piero's sketch, haunted her thoughts whenever she considered the cause. Yet, simply by their absence, she hoped to shed something deeply intertwined with her own suffering.

Wrapped in her mantle, she stood beneath the upper loggia, watching for glimpses of the troops and the royal entourage crossing the bridges toward the Porta San Piero, the gate facing Siena and Rome. Even after the gates were sealed, she lingered, eager to feel the building vibrate with the great tolling of the bells signaling the evacuation. Dusk had fallen by the time she descended into the library. Lighting her lamp, she resolved to quell the agitation that had rendered her idle all day and return to her work of copying the catalogue. Tito had departed early, and she did not anticipate his return immediately. Once he arrived, she planned to retreat to the charming salon adorned with dancing nymphs and birds—a refuge she had claimed every evening since he deemed the library too chilly and gloomy.

To her astonishment, she had not worked long before Tito entered. Her immediate thought was of how desolate he must feel in the vast darkness of the room, illuminated only by a solitary oil lamp at the far end, with the fire nearly extinguished. She rushed toward him. "Tito, dearest, I did not know you would return so soon," she said, her voice tinged with nervousness as she raised her white arms to undo his *becchetto*.

"Am I not welcome?" he replied, offering one of his brightest smiles. He embraced her but playfully held his head back, preventing her from kissing him immediately.

"Tito!" she exclaimed, the word vibrating with affectionate reproach. He responded with a fond kiss, stroking her hair in his customary manner, seemingly unconcerned with removing his cloak. Romola quivered with delight. All the turmoil of the day had sharpened her sensitivity to the return of this familiar tenderness. *"It will come back,"* she whispered to herself. *"The old happiness will perhaps return. He is like himself again."*

In truth, Tito was exerting immense effort to appear as his former self; his heart hammered with anxiety. "If I had expected you so soon," Romola said, finally helping him remove his wrappings, "I would have prepared a small celebration for this joyous ringing of the bells. I did not intend to remain in the library upon your return."

"Never mind, sweet," he dismissed casually. "Forget the fire. Come, sit down."

A low stool stood beside his chair, Romola's usual perch during their conversations. Resting her arm on his knee as she once did on her father's, she gazed upward at him. He had never yet noticed the portrait looming nearby, nor had she brought it up, contemplating it with growing intensity.

"I have enjoyed the sound of the bells for the first time, Tito," she began. "I loved being shaken and deafened by them; I fancied myself a Bacchante possessed by divine rage. Do the people seem joyful tonight?"

"Joyful in a sour, pious fashion," Tito shrugged. "Truth be told, those left behind in Florence have little cause for joy. The most logical grounds for gladness would be to have fled the city entirely."

He struck the desired note effortlessly, without apparent premeditation. Speaking with no emphasis, yet maintaining a gravity that prompted anxious concern from Romola, he continued, "Why, Tito? Are there fresh troubles?"

"No need for new ones, my Romola. Three powerful factions exist in the city, all poised to tear each other apart. Should the Frate's party prove strong enough to silence the others—as seems likely—life will be as pleasant and amusing as a funeral. They are already simmering with plans for a Great Council; if established, the man who sings sacred Lauds the loudest will be the most eligible for office. Furthermore, the city will be drained by the massive subsidy paid to the French King and the war to reclaim Pisa. Without the rule of fanatics, the prospect remains dismal. On balance, Florence will be a delightful haunt for those who spend their evenings in crypts lashing themselves, but for everyone else, the exiles fare better. Personally, I have given serious consideration to leaving Florence, my Romola."

She recoiled. "Tito, how could we leave? Surely you do not think I could abandon it—at least, not yet, not for a long while."

Her voice grew cold and trembling; finding speech difficult, she sensed he must know the depths of her attachment. "That is merely a fabrication of your own imagination, my sweet one. Your secluded existence has led you to place undue weight on trivial matters. Remember, before our marriage, I expressed a wish to reside elsewhere than in Florence. Had you traveled more, seen more people, you would understand what I mean when I say the Florentines remind me of their cutting spring winds. I prefer those who approach life less eagerly. It would benefit your Romola to witness a new life, to dip her feet in the soft waters of forgetfulness."

He leaned forward, kissing her brow and resting his hand on her hair, yet she felt his caress no more than if he had touched a mask. Agitated by the chasm widening between their minds, she remained unaware of his physical contact.

"Tito, it is not because I imagine Florence to be the most pleasant place in the world that I refuse to depart. It is because—and because—we must fulfill my father's wish. My godfather is elderly, seventy-one years old; we cannot leave him alone."

"It is precisely those superstitions clinging to your mind like dimming clouds that constitute a major reason why I wish we were two hundred leagues distant. I am compelled to protect you against your own will. If those dear eyes, so tender in their gaze, perceive falsely, I must see for them and save my wife from wasting her life on impracticable dreams."

Romola sat silent and motionless. She could not ignore the trajectory of his words: he sought to convince her that the library could be deposited in a monastery or otherwise disposed of to sever their ties to Florence. Determined never to submit her mind to his judgment on this matter of filial duty, she prepared internally to endure whatever pain resistance required. However, this resolve remained latent in these initial moments, crushed by the heart-rending realization that she and Tito were now openly divided in their desires.

He welcomed her silence. Despite fearing the strength of her feelings, it was impossible for him, confined within the narrowness characteristic of clever but unimpassioned men, not to overestimate the persuasiveness of his own logic. His actions did not appear sinister to himself, nor did his imagination allow him to foresee exactly how they appeared to Romola. Continuing in a gentle, admonishing tone, he said, "You know, dearest—you whose clear judgment has always been evident—that the idea of isolating a collection of books and antiquities, attaching a single name to them forever, offers no valid, substantial good for its object. Moreover, it is liable to defeat in a thousand ways. Consider the fate of the Medici collections! For my part, I deem it even blameworthy to entertain such petty notions of appropriation. Why should anyone be reasonably glad that Florence possesses the benefits of learned research and taste more than any other city? I comprehend your sentiment regarding the wishes of the dead, but wisdom imposes limits on such sentiments, lest lives be wasted in futile devotion—much like praising deaf gods eternally. You dedicated your life to your father while he lived; why demand more of yourself now?"

"Because it was a trust," Romola replied, her voice low but distinct. "He trusted me, he trusted you, Tito. I did not expect you to feel differently, but I did expect you to recognize that."

"Yes, dearest, naturally I would feel so concerning your father's actual welfare or happiness. But there is no question of that now. If we believed in purgatory, I would be as anxious as you to ensure masses were said; and if I believed it could pain your father to see his library preserved and utilized in a manner contrary to his intent, I would share your strict views. However, a little philosophy should teach us to free ourselves from these air-woven fetters that mortals bind around themselves, spending their lives in misery under the mere imagination of weight. Your mind, which seizes ideas so readily, my Romola, is capable of distinguishing between substantial good and these brain-wrought fantasies. Ask yourself, dearest, what possible good these books and antiquities offer, stored together under your father's name in Florence, compared to if they were dispersed or carried elsewhere? Indeed, is not the very dispersion of such treasures among hands that know how to value them a means of extending their utility? This rivalry among Italian cities is petty and illiberal. The loss of Constantinople was a gain for the entire civilized world."

Romola remained under the painful pressure of the new revelation Tito was unveiling about himself, unable to find a strong outlet for her resistance. As his fluent

Baldassarre wandered through Florence seeking the cheapest shelter, his path inevitably drawn toward the only uneven patch of land within the city walls. From this vantage point, elevated above the rooftops, one could glimpse the protective hills and the vast valley beyond, visible only through the gap carved by the Arno River. While part of this terrain was the famous stone quarry of Bogoli, Baldassarre ascended the slope descending behind the Via de' Bardi—the hill known locally as San Giorgio. Bratti had confirmed that Tito resided on this street, and Baldassarre hoped to find a hiding place among the scattered dwellings here. Having grown accustomed to the open sky over the years, the claustrophobic, narrow alleys of Florence intensified his feelings of isolation and the foggy decay of his memory.

The hill was largely garden land, dotted with sparse habitation, until he reached a jagged expanse of rough ground scarred by ancient landslides that had destroyed homes centuries prior. Perched atop this broken earth stood a peculiar, truncated tower-like structure capped with fluted tiles. Beside it lay a small outhouse wedged against a crumbling stone wall. Beneath the sprawling branches of a dying mulberry tree, whose final leaves drifted into the open doorways, an aged, weather-beaten woman untied a goat with two kids. One section of the outbuilding housed livestock, but the other stood empty save for a few tools and a bed of straw. It was exactly the refuge he needed.

He approached the old woman, but she remained oblivious until he shouted directly into her ear. Initially, she merely shook her head, muttering "No lodging" in the flat, muffled cadence of the deaf. Undeterred, Baldassarre persisted, explaining that he was a destitute stranger from overseas who could not afford an inn, offering merely a few *quattrini* weekly for permission to sleep on the straw. The woman regarded him with suspicion, whispering to herself, until a new idea sparked. She retrieved a hatchet, pointed to a log half-buried under debris in a corner, and offered him a bargain: if he chopped the wood, he might spend one night in the outhouse.

Agreeing immediately, Baldassarre began to work while Monna Lisa watched with arms akimbo, a smile of shrewd satisfaction playing on her lips. *"It's been lying there since my husband died,"* she murmured to herself. *"What difference does it make? I might as well throw a log on the fire. He chops well, despite his foreign tongue and odd appearance. I couldn't hire anyone cheaper. Besides, if he only needs straw, I can make him run errands up and down the hill. Who cares? Hidden sins are half-forgiven ('Peccato celato e mezzo perdonato'). He's a stranger; he won't notice *me*. I'll tell her to keep quiet."*

Her thoughts drifted to a pair of bright blue eyes currently fixed upon a large circular hole in the shutter of the upper window. The shutter was closed not out of malice, but because the opposing window boasted glass—a rare luxury. Since the weather was cool, the four-inch aperture sufficed for spying. Unfortunately, the hole was positioned too high, forcing the small observer to balance precariously on a rickety stool. Yet, Tessa would endure any discomfort for the thrill of watching the world. Drawn by the strange voice earlier, she had crept to the opening and remained there until the wood was split and Baldassarre entered the darkness of the outhouse, settling onto the straw as twilight fell.

A powerful urge seized Tessa. She wanted to share her supper with the old man, to converse with someone who wasn't deaf like Monna Lisa. Strangers were fascinating; they traveled from afar and vanished, living in no fixed place. Though she knew it was disobedience, and thus a sin requiring confession at Easter alongside her occasional napping during prayer, the temptation was overwhelming. There was nothing else to confess anyway, and she possessed ample idle time. With a flushed, pouting face, she turned from the window. Dressed in the attire of a peasant girl prepared for a festival—a clean dark-green serge gown with a red sash, red glass beads around her neck, and her curly brown hair neatly pinned with silver—she wore a single new pride: a fine gold ring. Sitting on her stool, her heart fluttered with the excitement of a planned transgression. She had been strictly forbidden to make friends, warned that doing so would turn her happiness into lead, yet she had obeyed so thoroughly that she kept her face veiled in church. This old man, however, was neither a friend nor a threat; he was a sleeping stranger who likely knew nothing of her cruel stepfather, Nofri. If she fed him, he would be grateful and silent. Monna Lisa's objections would be dismissed simply because Monna Lisa was deaf.

Supper was ready: a mountain of cheesy macaroni, fragrant enough to charm any guest. Tripping downstairs with a lantern in one hand and a wooden bowl in the other, Tessa knocked on the outhouse door. Inside, Baldassarre, lost in melancholy, blinked in surprise as the light revealed a tiny maid bursting with joy.

"I've brought you supper!" she cried, leaning close to shout into his ear as if addressing the deaf. "Eat while I stay with you."

Distrust warred with surprise, but Baldassarre found no reason to reject the gesture. He sank back onto the straw as Tessa placed the bowl on his lap, set down the lantern, and crossed her hands, nodding significantly. *"Yes, you may truly eat it,"* her smile conveyed. In her haste, she had momentarily forgotten that he could hear, falling back on her habit of dramatic gestures and shouting.

The offer was welcome; he had been gnawing dry bread and craved warmth. Tessa watched him eat silently, noting that his initial fierce gaze had softened. Venturing closer, she whispered, "Do you like my supper?"

Baldassarre returned a look of gentle pity, akin to a kindly dog unable to smile. "Yes," he replied softly. "But I can hear well. I am not deaf."

"It is true; I forgot," Tessa admitted, clasping her hands delightedly. "But Monna Lisa is deaf. She is kind, and I am not afraid of her. We live well; I can have nuts whenever I wish. I used to work, but I hated it. I loved feeding the mules, especially poor Giannetta. Now we only have a goat and two kids. I used to talk to the goat because Monna Lisa was always busy. But now I have something else... can you guess?"

She tilted her head, challenging him with a mischievous grin.

"No," Baldassarre answered dreamily, setting aside his bowl. The girl reminded him of memories from his own youth.

"You like me to talk to you, don't you?" Tessa pressed. "But you must promise not to tell anyone. Should I fetch some cold sausage?"

He shook his head, appearing mild and harmless. Encouraged, Tessa continued, "Well, then, I have a little baby. Such a lovely *bambinetto*, with tiny fingers and nails! It was born at Christmas, Monna Lisa says. I was married one Christmas, a long time ago, and nobody knew. Oh, Santa Madonna! I didn't mean to tell you that!"

She stiffened, biting her lip as if expecting a shock, but Baldassarre seemed indifferent, perhaps due to the nature of strangers. "Yes," she persisted aloud, "you are a stranger. You live nowhere and know no one, do you?"

"No," Baldassarre murmured, thinking more than answering. "I only know one man."

Tessa's face instantly tightened with anxiety. "His name is not Nofri, is it?"

"No," Baldassarre noticed her fear and clarified.

"That your husband's name?" she asked, mistaking his caution for confirmation.

The error amused her greatly. Laughing and clapping her hands, she declared, "No, indeed! But I cannot tell you about my husband. You'd never imagine what he is—he isn't like Nofri at all!" She laughed again at the absurdity of comparing her husband to the gruff stepfather.

"But I don't see him often," she said seriously. "Sometimes I pray to the Holy Madonna to send him more frequently, and once she did. But I must return to my baby. I'll bring it tomorrow to show you. You'd like to see it. Sometimes it cries and makes faces, but only when hungry, Monna Lisa says. You wouldn't believe it, but Monna Lisa had babies once, and they are all dead old men. My husband says she will never die now because she is 'so well dried.' I am glad, for I love her. Would you like to stay here tomorrow?"

"I would like to have this place to rest in," Baldassarre said. "I will pay for it and cause no trouble."

"No, indeed. You are not a bad old man. But you look sorrowful. Is there something you will cry about when I leave you alone? I used to cry once."

"No, child. I think I shall cry no more."

"That is right. I'll bring you breakfast and show you the baby. Goodnight."

Tessa departed with her lantern and bowl, closing the door behind her. To Baldassarre

Messer Naldo returned sooner than anyone anticipated. On the evening of the twenty-eighth of November, merely eleven days after his last visit, he appeared, proving he had not ventured far beyond the mountains. The scene that unfolded that night in the Via de' Bardi offers a key to understanding the impulse that drew Tito's steps toward the hill of San Giorgio.

When Tito first secured this dwelling for Tessa upon his return from Rome, more than a year and a half prior, he convinced himself he acted solely under the gentle compulsion of his own kindness following the unfortunate incident that led the innocent Tessa to believe he was her husband. While it was true that his kindness flowed naturally toward such a trusting, pretty creature—one impossible to ignore without a desire to caress—it was equally true that Tito harbored feelings of benevolence toward her separate from any calculated gain. Though her charms and chatter were delightful in idle moments, he might have preferred freedom from her company; for he was not in love with Tessa. For the first time in his life, he was consumed by a passion for a different woman, one whose mere presence vibrated through his hours, whose sweeping tresses across his cheek held a power no mere petting could match. All his youthful idealism had been awakened by Romola. His spirit was too refined, his intellect too bright, to succumb to the habits of a coarse pleasure-seeker.

Yet, Tito had woven a net around himself and Tessa that he felt powerless to break. In the initial moments after the mock marriage, he had allowed her to remain under an illusion driven by a distinct calculation of his future needs. Since that pivotal moment, however, the web seemed to spin itself autonomously, growing beyond his control. The lines between kindness and self-indulgence blur easily in a soft nature like Tito's. The irritation he felt during Tessa's pursuit on the day of his betrothal, coupled with his sincere intention to reveal the truth when he promised to see her again, served as proof to him that his ultimate decision to leave her believing the lie and provide her a home stemmed purely from overwhelming kindness. In these early days of his devotion to Romola, he desperately needed a self-justifying narrative. He had learned to find comfort in the fact that she was deceived about certain matters. Every intense emotion constructs its own conscience, its own form of piety—much like a son's love for a mother, which can endure even amidst profound moral corruption. Thus, Tito could not yet find peace in committing a secret offense against his wedded love.

Consequently, he exercised extreme caution to preserve the secrecy of this transgression. Monna Lisa, who rarely left her quarters except for specific errands or annual confession, knew nothing of his true identity or whereabouts. She only knew that he paid her generously to ensure her comfort, cared little for the details, and had grown fond of Tessa, finding satisfaction in the duties for which she was compensated. An air of mystery undoubtedly surrounded the arrangement, given that Tessa was a peasant girl (*contadina*) while Messer Naldo was a gentleman. However, to Monna Lisa, he might have been a legitimate husband. Tito had thoroughly terrified Tessa into silence regarding their union, warning her that breaking that silence meant never seeing him again. Furthermore, Monna Lisa's deafness necessitated premeditated speech, saving Tessa from the careless revelations she had accidentally made to Baldassarre. For a long time, Tito's visits were so infrequent that it seemed plausible he undertook journeys between them. These trips were motivated primarily by a desire to ensure Tessa's welfare. Although he always found the visit more pleasant than the anticipation of it—renewing his appreciation for that pretty, ignorant lovingness and trust—he did not yet feel an urgent necessity for them. Nevertheless, he was resolved, if possible, to maintain the simplicity upon which her charm depended. He intended to keep Tessa a genuine *contadina*, refusing to place the humble wildflower among conditions that would strip it of its grace. He would have been horrified to see her dressed in attire befitting a higher station; the sharp wit of her conversation would vanish if her world expanded, if her pleasures lost their childish innocence. The squirrel-like joy of enjoying nuts at leisure defined the standard of luxury he provided. Through this strategy, Tito preserved Tessa's charm from defilement and, coincidentally, spared himself from escalating expenses that were already burdensome to a man whose funds were fully committed to his established lifestyle.

Briefly summarized, this was the trajectory of Tito's relationship with Tessa until very recently. It is true that before Bardo's death, the knowledge that Tessa waited up the hill, offering a chance for an agreeable hour, occasionally induced him to flee the weariness of the old man. When, lacking other engagements, he might otherwise have endured the fatigue patiently alongside Romola, the thought of Tessa provided an escape. However, the moment he truly felt a hunger for Tessa's ignorant lovingness and faith in *him* arrived only quite lately, marked by circumstances as indelible as the onset of a disease that permanently damages sight and hearing. It was the day he first encountered Baldassarre and purchased the armor. That night, returning across the bridge with the coat of mail in his hands, he felt an irresistible dread of immediately confronting Romola. Like Tessa, she knew little of the actual world and trusted him; yet, he possessed an uneasy awareness that behind her frank eyes lay a nature capable of judgment. Any misplaced trust she held did not stem from a brutish inability to perceive, but from a nobility that might serve as an alarming test. He craved a measure of ease, a respite from self-control after the agitation and exertion of the day. He sought a place where he could prepare his mind for tomorrow without concern for his current behavior. Within reach was a sweet, adoring creature whose presence was as safe and unrestrained as that of her own children—someone who would believe any tale and remain utterly unaffected by public opinion. So, that evening, while Romola waited and listened for him, he turned his steps up the hill.

It is therefore no surprise that his path followed the same course eleven days later, on this evening when he had recoiled under Romola's first outburst of scorn. He could not wish Tessa in his wife's place, nor could he refrain from hoping his wife would be thoroughly reconciled to him; for it was Romola, not Tessa, who belonged to the realm where the greater desires of an ambitious, capable man must inevitably reside. Still, he sought refuge from a standard that was disagreeably rigorous, one from which he could not liberate himself merely by dismissing it as folly. Tessa's small soul offered that inviting sanctuary.

It was barely past eight o'clock when he ascended the stone steps to Tessa's door. Usually, she heard his entry and rushed to greet him, but not tonight. Upon opening the door, he discovered the cause. A single dim light burned above the dying embers, illuminating Tessa in a kneeling posture beside the bed where the infant lay. Her head had slumped onto the pillow, and her brown rosary, which usually hung above the pillow over the image of the Madonna and golden palm branches, lay loosely gripped in her right hand. She had drifted into sleep over her beads. Tito moved lightly across the small room and sat close beside her. She had likely sensed the creak of the door as part of her dream, for she had not been watching him two moments prior. She opened her eyes without a start, remaining perfectly still as she gazed at him, as if the awareness of his smiling presence shut out any impulse to disturb that happy passivity. When he placed his hand under her chin and leaned to kiss her, she whispered:

"I dreamed it, and then I said it was a dream—and then I woke, and it was true."

"Little sinner!" Tito replied, pinching her chin. "You haven't finished half your prayers. I'll punish you by refusing to look at your baby; he is ugly."

Tessa disliked those words, despite Tito's smile. A look of pouting distress crossed her face as she bent anxiously over the child.

"Oh, that isn't true! He is prettier than anything. You don't think he's ugly. You'll look at him. He's even prettier than when you saw him before—only he's asleep, so you can't see his eyes or his tongue, and I can't show you his hair—but it grows, doesn't that seem wonderful? Look at him!
