It's true his face looks very similar when he sleeps; there isn't as much to see as when he's awake. If you kiss him very gently, he won't wake. Do you want to kiss him?"

Satisfied, Tito bestowed a butterfly kiss upon the small bundle, then placed his hand on her shoulder and turned her face toward him. "You prefer looking at the baby to looking at your husband, you false one!"

Still kneeling, she now rested her hands on his knee, gazing up at him like one of Fra Lippo Lippi's round-cheeked adoring angels.

"No," she said, shaking her head. "I love you best always, but I want you to look at the bambino and love him. Before, I only wanted you to love me."

"And did you expect me to return so soon?" Tito asked, inclining toward making her chatter. He still felt the aftershocks of the agitation he had endured, feeling like a man violently jarred; this was the easiest relief from silence and solitude.

"Oh, no," Tessa replied. "I've counted the days—I started

For several days, Tito saw little of Romola. The silence between them was heavy, broken only by the quiet rustle of packing cloths and the distant murmur of strangers moving through the house. The next morning, he approached her with gentle caution. "It would be better," he said softly, "if you moved your personal effects from the library. Agents are arriving to pack the antiquities."

He leaned forward to press a kiss to her brow, suggesting she retreat to her own chamber, where the small painted tabernacle stood—a sanctuary away from the clamor of unfamiliar footsteps. Romola assented quietly, offering no sign of emotion. The night before had been a sleepless vigil for her; though her frame remained strong, sensation had dulled into a continuous, throbbing ache, as if she had been stunned and bruised by invisible blows. Tito sensed her illness but dared not speak of it. Seeing her hand and brow turned to ice, he merely fetched a fur-lined mantle and draped it lightly over her shoulders.

In every brief interval he returned, the scene repeated itself: he attempted to soothe her with unobtrusive acts of tenderness, words of affection, but she seemed to have lost the capacity to speak or even meet his gaze.

"Patience!" he murmured to himself. "She will recover, and forgive at last. The bond between us must endure."

There is a peculiar dynamic when the struck one remains silent and composed; the striker often slides effortlessly into the role of the aggrieved, feeling no sting of his own violence while acutely aware of his own benevolence. Yet, Tito was not naturally prone to such self-pity. His nature bent toward reconciliation; he would have endured much just to feel Romola's hand rest once more upon his head, as it had that morning when he first recoiled from looking at her. Still, waiting held less difficulty for him because his life outside the walls had grown increasingly captivating.

A course of action, born of deep-seated character, often traces back to a single spark. Since the moment in the Piazza del Duomo, when Tito, perched atop bales, had tasted the sharp thrill of pleasing crowds with clever phrases, his imagination had drifted toward political activity. The turbulent life of Florence seemed ripe for engagement. However, a fresh terror of Baldassarre had previously blocked this path, driving him to sell the library as a precaution against fleeing the city. Now, that dread had receded. He knew he must still wear his armor, preparing his wit and composure for future demands, but he no longer felt compelled to abandon Florence for foreign fortunes.

His father had rejected the offer of atonement, forcing Tito into open defiance. An elderly man, displaced and memory-fogged, was too weak to challenge him. Tito's latent desires were crystallizing into explicit plans. As the fiery heat of youthful passion cooled, life began to resemble a game for him—one blending skill and chance. The game unfolding in Florence promised to be swift and thrilling: a struggle of revolution and faction, filled with covert maneuvers where brilliant ingenuity could discard inconvenient beliefs, save perhaps the notion that ginger burns the tongue, and claim the high road of wisdom.

The departure of the French guests left Florence vibrating with anxiety, like a colony of ants sensing a looming threat. *"How shall we raise funds for the King of France?"* the whispers ran. *"How do we handle the stubborn Pisan rebels? Most urgently, how do we reform our government to ensure fair election of magistrates and voting on laws?"* Until these questions found answers, commerce threatened to stall, and the vast body of workers, denied citizenship and the vote, risked turning impatient.

Something had to be done. First, the great bell rang, summoning the citizens to a parliament in the Piazza de' Signori. When the crowd pressed tight, hemmed in by armed guards at every exit, the Signoria—the Gonfaloniere and the eight current Priors—stepped onto the platform before the Old Palace, beside the stone lion. They proposed granting dictatorial authority for one year to twenty leading men of the city, empowering them to select all magistrates and restructure the government. The people roared their approval, feeling themselves the electors of these Twenty. Such a "parliament" was an ancient Florentine custom, designed to make the will of the few appear as the choice of the many.

While the shouting in the square died down quickly, the debates within the palace raged on. Was Florence to adopt a Great Council, modeled after Venice, where all officials and laws were voted upon by a broad base of qualified citizens, regardless of rank or party? Or would governance follow a narrower, less populist scheme, preserving the hereditary dominance of noble families against the interference of shopkeepers?

Doctors of law argued day and night, extending far into the darkness. Messer Pagolantonio Soderini presented compelling arguments for the popular model; Messer Guidantonio Vespucci countered with equally sound reasoning for an aristocratic form. It was a debate over whether to boil or roast meat, a choice already prejudged by the tastes of the disputants. Their eloquence might have continued indefinitely without altering the outcome, serving only to delay the inevitable. Inside the palace, the majority holding power sided with Vespucci, favoring moderation. Outside, the masses, aware of their powerlessness and grievances, feared nothing so much as stagnation.

Yet, a force was gathering outside the palace walls, shaping the vague desires of the multitude into a determined will. That force was the preaching of Savonarola. Driven by both a spiritual compulsion to guide the people and the pragmatic needs of politicians who required his support to pass measures, Fra Girolamo rapidly shifted his sermons from the general to the specific. He moved from urging citizens to sacrifice private passions for the public good, to prescribing exactly what form of government would achieve that end. From *"Choose whatever is best for all"* evolved the command: *"Choose the Great Council,"* followed by the assertion, *"The Great Council is the will of God."*

To Savonarola, these were identical truths. The Great Council was the only viable mechanism to express a public will large enough to counteract the corrupting influence of partisan interests; it was the sole path toward honest, impartial public service. As Florence's government purified itself, becoming immune to schemes based on the moral degradation of others, the Florentine people would approach the state of a pure community, worthy of leading the renewal of the Church and the world. Fra Girolamo's mind never rested until it reached this sublime horizon. He harbored no private malice, sought no petty gratification. Even in his final, terrible days, when shame, torture, and the fear thereof stripped away every hidden weakness of his soul, he could tell his relentless judges: *"Do not wonder if it seems to you that I have told but few things; for my purposes were few and great."*

More than three weeks elapsed before the final contents of the library were packed and removed. During this time, Romola refused to shut her eyes or ears to the proceedings. The exhaustion born of violent emotion often breeds a dreamlike disbelief in the reality of its cause; thus, in the evenings, once the workmen had departed, Romola would take her hand-lamp and wander slowly through the chaos of straw and wooden crates. She paused at every empty pedestal, every familiar object laid low, driven by a bitter urge to confirm that there was a sufficient reason for her love's departure and the world's newfound barrenness. Yet, as the nights wore on, her visits became less about seeking confirmation and more about sustaining herself. This vivification of pain and despair regarding her father's memory remained the sole vitality left in her affections.

On the twenty-third of December, she knew the last packages were destined for removal. She rushed to the loggia atop the house, unwilling to miss the final pang of watching the slow wheels traverse the bridge.

It was a cloudy day, approaching dusk. The Arno flowed dark and shivering; the hills stood mournful. Florence, encircled by its stone towers, possessed a silent, tomb-like appearance, cast by unbroken shadows upon the city viewed from above. Santa Croce, where her father lay interred, was swallowed by this darkness. Slowly crawling across the bridge and vanishing up the narrow street was the white load, resembling a cruel, deliberate fate carrying away her father's lifelong hope to bury it in an unmarked grave. Romola felt less that she was witnessing this event herself than that her father, helpless beneath the imprisoning stones where her hand could not reach to comfort him, was conscious of it.

She remained motionless even after the load had disappeared, indifferent to the cold, soothed by the gloom that seemed to cloak her like a mourning garment, shutting out the discord of joy. Suddenly, the great bell in the palace tower rang out a mighty peal—not the hammer-sound of alarm, but an agitated cry of triumph. One by one, every other bell in every other tower caught the vibration, joining the chorus. As the swell grew until the air seemed composed of sound alone, little flames, vibrating as if ignited by the noise, burst forth between the palace turrets and the girdling towers.

That sudden clangor and leaping light struck Romola like sharp wounds. These were the triumphs of demons celebrating the success of her husband's treachery and the desolation of her life. Less than three weeks prior, she had been intoxicated by the sound of those very bells; in the gladness of Florence, she had heard a prophecy of her own joy. Now, however, the general jubilation seemed cruel to her. She stood aloof from that common life—from that Florence flinging out its loud exultation to deafen the ears of sorrow and loneliness. She realized she could never join hands with gladness again, save with those whom the hard nature of joy was designed to forget. In her bitterness, she felt all rejoicing to be mockery. Men shouted paeans with souls heavy with grief, then glanced at their neighbors' faces to verify if such a thing as true joy existed. Romola had lost her belief in the happiness she once thirsted for; it appeared to her now as a hateful, smiling, soft-handed entity with a narrow, selfish heart.

Covering her ears, she ran down from the loggia and hurried across the antechamber, only to be startled by the unexpected arrival of her husband, who came seeking her.

His step was elastic, bearing a radiance of satisfaction somewhat unusual. "What! The noise was a little too much for you?" he asked, noting how she clutched her hands tighter against her ears at the sight of him. He took her gently by the wrist, drawing her arm within his, leading her into the salon adorned with dancing nymphs and fauns. "Florence has gone quite mad with acquiring its Great Council, which promises to end all evils under the sun, particularly the vice of merriment. You may well look stunned, my Romola, and you are cold. Do not stay so late under that windy loggia without proper wrappings."

He continued, "I was coming to tell you that I am suddenly summoned to Rome regarding some learned business for Bernardo Rucellai. I depart immediately to join my party at San Gaggio tonight, so we may start early tomorrow. I need give you no trouble; my packages are already prepared. It will not be long before I return."

Tito knew he could expect nothing from her but quiet endurance. He dared not even venture to kiss her brow that evening, merely pressing her hand to his lips before leaving. Tito sensed that Romola was a more unforgiving woman than he had imagined; her love was not that sweet, clinging instinct, stronger than all judgment, which he now recognized as the great charm of a wife. Still, this petrified coldness was preferable to a passionate, futile opposition. Her pride and her ability to recognize the futility of resistance held a certain convenience for him.

Once the door closed behind Tito, Romola shed the look of cold immobility that descended upon her like an inevitable frost whenever he approached. Inwardly, she was far from a state of quiet endurance. The days following the scene that had severed her from Tito had been filled with active planning and preparation for the fulfillment of a purpose.

Her first action was to summon old Maso. "Maso," she said in a decided tone, "we take our journey tomorrow morning. We shall now be able to overtake the first convoy of cloth while they await us at San Piero. Arrange the two mules tonight; be ready to set off with them at dawn and meet me at Trespiano."

She intended to travel with Maso as far as Bologna, then send him back with letters to her godfather and Tito, informing them she had departed and never intended to return. She had orchestrated her departure so perfectly that its secrecy would be absolute, hiding her broken love and shattered life from vulgar scrutiny. Bernardo del Nero had been absent at his villa, eager to evade political suspicion by tending to his lands. She had settled his debt without a personal interview. He did not even know the library had been sold, left to conjecture that some sudden stroke of fortune had enabled Tito to raise the necessary funds. Maso had been informed only that her journey was a secret; doing precisely as she commanded was the thing he cherished most in his withered, wintry age.

Romola did not intend to sleep that night. Having fastened the door, she took her taper to the carved and painted chest containing her wedding clothes. The white silk and gold lay there, along with the long white veil and the pearl circlet. A great sob rose within her as she beheld them; they seemed the shroud of her dead happiness. In a tiny gold loop of the circlet, a sugar-plum had lodged—a pink hailstone from a shower of sweets. Tito had discovered it first and declared it should always remain there. At certain moments—and this was one of them—Romola was swept, by a sudden wave of memory, back into the time of perfect trust. She felt again the presence of the husband whose love made the world as fresh and wonderful to her as to a child sitting in stillness among sunny flowers. She heard his gentle tones, saw his soft, truthful eyes, and breathed again that vast freedom of the soul granted by the faith that the being nearest to us is greater than ourselves. In those brief instants, tears invariably rose: the woman's lovingness felt something akin to what a bereaved mother feels when tiny fingers seem to lie warm on her bosom, yet feel like marble to her lips as she leans over the silent bed.

However, something else lay in the chest alongside the wedding garments: a dark, coarse bundle wrapped tightly. Turning her eyes from the white and gold to the dark mass, her tears began to subside as her hands touched the serge. That coarse roughness anchored her firmly in the present, from which love and delight had vanished. Unfastening the thick white cord, she spread the bundle on the table. It was the grey serge dress of a sister belonging to the Third Order of Saint Francis, a member of the *Pinzochere*—a personage Florentines knew well, living in the world but devoted to deeds of piety. Romola intended to wear this dress as a disguise and resolved to don it immediately, ensuring that if she required sleep before morning, she would awaken in perfect readiness to leave.

She removed her black garment. As she thrust her soft white arms into the harsh sleeves of the serge mantle and felt the hard girdle of rope pinch her fingers as she tied it, she welcomed those rude sensations. They aligned with her new scorn for that thing called pleasure, which rendered men base—that dexterous contrivance for selfish ease, that shrinking from endurance and strain when others bowed beneath burdens too heavy for them, a trait that now united her with her husband. Gathering her long hair, she pulled it tight from her face, binding it in a great hard knot at the back of her head. Taking a square piece of black silk, she tied it close across her forehead and under her chin, then drew the cowl over it. Lifting the candle to the mirror, she verified that her disguise would be complete to anyone who had not lived intimately near her. To herself, she looked strangely like her brother Dino; the full oval of the cheek needed only to be wasted, and the eyes, already sad, only to sink further. Was she becoming more like him in anything else? Only in this: she now

The first sound of the morning was a sharp rap at the door. Romola woke to find the room bathed in the stark, cold light of dawn. Maso stood there, having come for the traveling wallet. When she opened the door, the old man started visibly; the graceful silhouette he had grown accustomed to—the woman crowned with the brightness of her hair—was replaced by the heavy folds of a grey mantle and a pale face obscured by a dark cowl.

"It is well, Maso," Romola said, striving for a voice of absolute calm to soothe his alarm. "The wallet is ready. Go on quietly; I shall not be far behind. Once you pass the gates, you may walk slower, for I hope to catch up with you before you reach Trespiano."

She closed the door behind him, leaving the silence of the house intact. Then, her hand went to the key resting in the casket beside her bed. It was the original key to the little painted tabernacle. Tito had failed to drown it in the Arno; instead, it had ended up tucked into the corner of the embroidered *scarsella* worn beneath his purple tunic. Years later, Romola had discovered it there, setting it aside unused yet finding a quiet satisfaction in knowing it remained within reach.

The cabinet holding the tabernacle had been shifted near a window, where the pale morning light illuminated the painted figures just enough for Romola to recognize them. There was the triumphant Bacchus, laden with grapes and clutching a vine-covered spear, embracing the crowned Ariadne. Nearby, the Loves scattered roses around a wreathed vessel and cunning-eyed dolphins swimming in rippled seas, all framed by a floral border resembling a paradise garden.

On this chilling morning, however, the familiar images struck her with renewed bitterness and revulsion. They appeared as a more pathetic mockery than ever, a cruel irony to wake into such profound loneliness. They had served not as a sanctuary for sorrow, but as a deceptive screen. *Poor Ariadne!* she thought, her gaze lingering on the painting's expression of love. As if that radiant face, with its hyacinthine curls twisting like tendrils among the vines, truly held the deep secret of her life!

"Ariadne is wonderfully transformed," Romola murmured to herself. "She would look strangely out of place among these vines and roses now."

She picked up the mirror and gazed at her reflection once more. The sight was so jarring in the harsh daylight that she set it down again, feeling a recoil nearly as intense as when she had turned away from the joyful painting. Seeing her own face, veiled by the cowl, revived a primal dread: the fear that she might eventually be swept into the fellowship of some wretched superstition, joining the ranks of the howling fanatics and weeping nuns whom she had despised since childhood.

With hurried movements, she inserted the key into the tabernacle, unlocked it, and withdrew the crucifix without pausing to examine it. With trembling fingers, she threaded a cord through its small ring, hung it around her neck, and concealed it deep within the bosom of her mantle.

"For Dino's sake," she whispered to herself.

Yet, the task was not complete. There were still the letters for Maso to carry back to Bologna. They were brief. The first was addressed to Tito:

*"Tito, my love for you is dead; and therefore, so far as I was yours, I too am dead. Do not attempt to enforce any laws to fetch me back; it would bring you no happiness. The Romola you married can never return. I need say nothing further after the words I spoke to you during our last long conversation. If you believed them merely words of transient anger, you will now understand they marked an irreversible change.*

*I believe you will fulfill my wish regarding my bridal chest, sending it to my godfather, who gifted it to me. It holds my wedding clothes and the portraits and other relics of my parents."*

She folded the ring inside this letter and wrote Tito's name on the exterior. The second letter was for Bernardo del Nero:

*"Dearest Godfather,—Had I been able to serve your life by remaining, I would not have fled to such a distance. But now I am gone. Do not ask the reason; and if you love my father, strive to prevent anyone from seeking me. I could not endure life in Florence. I cannot bear to explain why. Help to cover my fate in silence. I have requested that my bridal chest be sent to you; when you open it, you will understand the cause. Please entrust all items belonging to my mother to my cousin Brigida, and ask her to forgive me for failing to bid her farewell.*

Placing the letters alongside the crucifix in the depths of her mantle, Romola felt a finality settle over her. Everything was done. She was prepared to depart.

No one stirred in the house. Moving almost as silently as a grey phantom, she descended the stairs and stepped into the silent street. Her heart hammered violently, yet she derived a strange pleasure from the sensation of her firm tread upon the broad flagstones—the swift movement felt like a chained resolution finally released. The urgent drive to execute her deed and the terror of encountering obstacles pushed away all sorrow. Reaching the Ponte Rubaconte, she sensed less the proximity of Santa Croce and more the widening yellow streak of morning light cutting through the grey. Unless she quickened her pace, she feared meeting faces.

Her direct route led straight to the Borgo Pinti, then along the walls toward the Porta San Gallo, passing through the Piazza di Santa Croce. Yet, she walked with steady, rapid strides through the square, refusing to look toward the church. The thought that curious or recognizing eyes might fall upon her, prompting indifferent minds to speculate on her private griefs, caused her to shrink physically, as though recoiling from imagined torture. She felt degraded even by the act of her husband, an act from which she suffered helplessly.

However, no signs indicated that eyes watched from the windows. This tall grey sister, with her firm step and the proud, hidden posture of her cowl, passed unnoticed. Her path avoided the bustle of early traffic, and by the time she reached the Porta San Gallo, she slipped through easily amidst a dispute over tolls for panniers of eggs and market goods entering the gate.

Out! Beyond the houses of the Borgo, she was past the last fringe of Florence. Above her, the sky stretched wide and boundless. She had entered a new existence—one of loneliness and endurance, yes, but also of freedom. She possessed the strength to sever the bonds she had once accepted in blind faith. Whatever happened next, she would no longer feel the warmth of soft, hated lips on her cheek, nor the suffocating breath of an odious mind stifling her spirit.

The bare winter morning and the chill air were welcome in their severity. The leafless trees and sombre hills were no longer haunted by the gods of beauty and joy, whose worship she had forever renounced.

Suddenly, the light burst forth with sudden intensity, casting long shadows across the road. It seemed the sun intended to chase away the greyness. Perhaps light is never felt more profoundly as a divine presence stirring the inarticulate sensibilities of our deepest lives than in these moments when it instantly awakens the shadows. A certain awe, inevitable in such a momentous act, became a conscious element in Romola's feelings as she found herself in the sudden presence of that impalpable golden glory and the long shadow of herself—a shadow that could not be escaped.

Hitherto, she had encountered only occasional countrymen with mules, and the winding nature of the road had kept her from noticing that Maso was not far ahead. But as she passed Pietra and climbed onto rising ground, she lifted the hanging roof of her cowl to look eagerly forward. Immediately, she lowered it again.

She had not seen Maso, but two monks approaching within a few yards. The edge of her cowl acted as a penthouse over her brow, blocking anything above eye level; for the last few moments, she had stared only at the brightness of the path and her own shadow, tall and shrouded like a dread spectre. Now, wishing she had not looked up, she realized her disguise made encounters with monks particularly unpleasant. They might expect pious passwords unknown to her. Walking with a careful appearance of unconsciousness until the black skirts of their mantles had passed her, she felt her heart beat uncomfortably. There was an unease in wearing this religious disguise, a shame at this studied concealment, heightened by the effort to appear unaware under actual scrutiny.

But the black skirts would soon disappear as they went downhill. Spotting a large flat stone against a cypress tree rising from a projecting green bank, she yielded to the impulse born of the slight shock she had received. She sat down to rest. Turning her back on Florence, she did not intend to look at the city until the monks were entirely out of sight. Raising the edge of her cowl once more, she discerned Maso and the mules at a distance where overtaking them was still possible, assuming the old man would linger in expectation. For now, she might simply pause. She was free and alone.

Tito's sudden journey to Rome, which cleared the path for Romola's departure, had been decided at a dinner merely the previous evening. Tito had left for that meal buoyed by pleasant anticipations: delicate meats, choice wines, and a distinguished assembly. The venue was the *Selva* or *Orto de' Rucellai*, the Rucellai Gardens, hosted by Bernardo Rucellai, a quintessential Florentine magnate.

The family name itself holds a symbolically apt meaning. Properly interpreted, it evokes a small lichen, commonly known as *orcella* or *roccella*, which clings to the rocks of Greek islands and the Canaries. Soaking up abundant sunlight, this plant releases, under specific conditions, a reddish-purple dye highly prized by mankind. By importing the secret of this dye, *oricello*, from the East to Florence, a merchant living nearly a century prior to Bernardo amassed immense wealth and secured the surname Oricellari—or *Roccellari*. On the Tuscan tongue, this swiftly evolved into Rucellai. Our Bernardo stood out vividly against this purple backdrop. He had elevated the family status by marrying the sister of Lorenzo de' Medici, hosting a wedding so magnificent it remained unmatched in Florentine history. For these merits, plus others, he was dispatched on embassies to France and Venice, elected Gonfaloniere, constructed a splendid palace, and completed the black-and-white marble façade of Santa Maria Novella. He cultivated a garden of rare trees, transforming it into classic ground by hosting the Platonic Academy after Lorenzo's death. A learned author of a new topographical treatise on ancient Rome, a collector of antiquities, and a master of pure Latin, the simplest biography of Bernardo reads like a laudatory epitaph. One might imagine the Greek and Roman Muses tearing their hair in despair at Nature's audacity to pack such a multitude of virtues into a single set of organs.

Bernardo's invitation to Tito arrived via Lorenzo Tornabuoni, delivered with such gravity that it implied a political purpose, regardless of the era's distracting public crises. Indeed, Tito was certain that party aims were to be advanced alongside the flavors of stewed fish and aged Greek wine. Bernardo was not merely influential; he was one of the Elect Twenty currently steering Florence. This assurance lifted Tito's spirits as he approached the Via della Scala, where the classical garden lay. Without it, he might have feared the company would be as dull as distinguished, recalling previous evenings in the Rucellai grounds where he had been forced to endure entire philosophical systems—from the origins of the universe to their final maturation in a speaker's tractate.

It was a dark evening. Only when Tito crossed the intermittent glow of a lamp hung before an image of the Virgin did his silhouette become distinct enough for recognition. To any observer watching his passage from light to light, the tall, graceful figure with his mantle folded around him appeared constantly trailed by a starkly different form: a thickset, elderly man in a serge tunic and felt hat. Their union might have seemed accidental, given the crowd on the streets. Yet, when Tito halted at the gate of the Rucellai gardens, the figure behind him stopped as well.

The *sportello*, the smaller gate door, was held ajar by a servant distracted by another task, failing to close it after the last arrival. Tito stepped inside quickly, handing his name to the servant and moving past the evergreen bushes, which shimmered like metal in the torchlight. The follower entered immediately.

"Your name?" asked the servant.

"Baldassarre Calvo," came the instant reply.

"You are not a guest; all guests have passed."

"I belong to Tito Melema, who has just entered. I am to wait in the gardens."

The servant hesitated. "I was ordered to admit only guests. Are you a servant of Messer Tito?"

"No, friend, I am not a servant; I am a scholar."

There are those to whom one needs only say, "I am a buffalo," in a tone of quiet confidence, and they yield. The porter stepped aside instantly. Baldassarre entered, hearing the door click shut and chain fastened behind him as he vanished among the glowing shrubs.

Those steady, firm answers signaled a profound transformation in Baldassarre since their last confrontation, when the dagger had snapped in two. The change had manifested dramatically. Just as Tito's shadow passed the hovel on his way home, Baldassarre sat in the state of post-passion tremors familiar to anyone prone to violent outbursts—a condition where physical paralysis coexists with extraordinary mental clarity, as if the dissipation of excited passion clears away a mist of fire, leaving pure lucidity behind. Unable to rise immediately, his limbs numb and hands shaking, he sat enveloped not by the usual dimness, but by crystal-clear images of the past. He relived a continuous sequence of a life that seemed a long apprenticeship for bitterness.

For several minutes, so absorbed was he in these visions that he failed to realize he was witnessing them as memories. Then, as that sudden clarity traveled across the distance and settled on the recent scene, he anchored himself in the present. He recalled Monna Lisa and Tessa. Ah! *He* was the mysterious husband; the one with another wife in the Via de' Bardi. It was time to retrieve the broken dagger and vanish, leaving no trace. Hiding his weakness was the only semblance of power left to him. Leaning forward, he grasped the dagger's fragments, then turned to the open book beside him.

It was a fine, large manuscript—an unusual volume of Pausanias. Moonlight bathed the page, illuminating the large capital letters at the top: **ΜΕΣΣΕΝΙΚΑ. ΚΒ′**. In former days, he knew Pausanias intimately; yet an hour or two prior, staring at that page had yielded no more meaning than black weather-stains on a wall. Now, however, the letters were magical signs conjuring a world. The beam of moonlight striking the text resurrected Messenia and its struggle against Spartan oppression.

Snatching up the book, he realized the light was too faint for further reading. It mattered not; he knew the chapter. He read inwardly. He visualized the stoning of the traitor Aristocrates—cast out by a whole people to lie unburied, replaced by a pillar inscribed with verses declaring how Time had restored justice to the unjust. Words arose within him, stirring infinite vibrations of memory. He forgot his age; he could almost shout. The light had returned, mother of knowledge and joy! In that exultation, his limbs regained strength. He sprang up with the broken dagger and the book, stepping out beneath the broad moonlight.

The air was biting and frosty, yet Baldassarre felt no chill, only the glow of conscious power. He paced the open spaces of the high ground, gazing down upon the domed and towered city sleeping darkly beneath its mountain guardians, the pale gleam of the river, and the valley fading toward snowy peaks. He felt himself master of it all. That sense of mental empire available to everyone in moments of exceptional clarity was amplified for Baldassarre by the long days and nights when memory had been little more than the awareness of loss. The city, once a weary labyrinth, was now malleable material for his designs. His mind scanned its affairs with flashing conjecture; he was once again a man who knew cities, his vision instructed by vast experience, savoring the keen delight of holding everything within the grasp of language.

Names! Images! His mind raced through this wealth without pause, like an inheritor entering a great estate. Yet, amidst this rushing eagerness, one dark deity presided in the innermost cell of his consciousness—one seemingly forgotten only while his sacrifice was being prepared. Once the initial triumph of recovered certainty had played out, his thoughts centered on Tito. That fair, slippery viper could not escape him now. Thanks to struggling justice, the heart devoid of tenderness possessed sensitive, selfish fibers vulnerable to the sharp point of anguish. The soul that bowed to no right now bowed to the great lord of mortals: Pain. He could penetrate every secret of Tito's life. Some he already knew, and the failure of the broken dagger, seeming like frustration, marked the true beginning of his achievement.

Doubtless, that sudden rage had shattered the obstruction stifling his soul. Twice before, partial returns of memory occurred due to sudden excitement: once defending himself from an enraged dog, once scrambling up a rock to escape crashing waves. Yes, but if this time, as then, the light faded and the dreary blank returned! This time the light was stronger, steadier. Yet, what guarantee existed that before dawn the dark fog would not engulf him again? Even fear felt like the onset of weakness. Alarmed that this excited vigil on the hill might drain his remaining strength, he searched anxiously for shelter, finally nestling against a heap of warm garden straw until sleep claimed him.

When he awoke, it was daylight. The first moments brought strange bewilderment: a man with a double identity. Had he woken to the life of dim-sighted sensibilities, akin to the sad inheritance of fallen greatness, or to the life of recovered power? Surely the latter, for the night's events flooded back: the recognition of the page in Pausanias, the surging return of facts and names, the sudden, wide prospect that had granted him such a moment as the Maenad experiences in glorious amazement upon waking atop a mountain

As they stepped into the elegant pavilion, Tito's keen eye immediately confirmed his suspicion: the gathering was political. Yet, it was a politics softened by camaraderie, a strengthening of faction through good fellowship. In those days, fine food and vintage wine were believed to sharpen political instincts; it was thought that post-prandial conversation allowed individuals to clarify their views with a lucidity impossible for those who had not first satisfied their hunger. Though the Florentines prided themselves on sobriety and thrift, wherever wealth accumulated, devotees of *Madonna della Gozzoviglia* (Good Cheer) and *San Buonvino* (Good Wine) inevitably arose. The Rucellai family stood among the few Florentine houses capable of sustaining such lavish tables. Consequently, no high-minded philosophical treatises were expected tonight. Nor would anyone object to the bronze bust of Plato surveying the scene, or to the frescoes depicting the Cardinal Virtues adorning the walls.

That bust of Plato had long since ceased to gaze upon transcendent ideals. Removed from Lorenzo's villa after his death, it now presided over the Platonic Academy's sessions in these gardens. Every year on the thirteenth of November, the anniversary of Plato's passing, it looked down from beneath laurel leaves upon a select assembly of scholars. They ate and drank moderately, yet discussed the Master's doctrines with perhaps less restraint. Among them were figures of note: Pico della Mirandola, once a Quixotic youth with cascading curls, amazed by his own intellect and later humbled by a consuming quest for inner perfection; Marsilio Ficino, the diligent, youthful philosopher nourished exclusively on Platonism until his mind grew soft and pulpy from the diet; Angelo Poliziano, the era's supreme literary genius, a poet whose words still seem to pulse with life; and Leon Battista Alberti, the revered elder statesman, a robust mind encompassing art, science, invention, and poetry. Behind them lay countless unnamed laborers whose contributions formed the unseen foundation of their heritage, much like the ploughing and sowing of generations past.

Bernardo Rucellai himself held a distinguished place in this academy even before becoming its patron. In his mid-forties, he possessed a haughty, dignified bearing, conscious of his impeccable Latin—a tongue, Erasmus noted, that could not be coaxed into speech by the sharpest German interrogators. He greeted Tito with particular favor, seating him between Lorenzo Tornabuoni and Giannozzo Pucci, both accomplished young members of the Medicean faction.

Conversation flowed lightly while a brass basin of scented water circulated for hand-washing. Rings glinted on white fingers beneath the candlelight, mingling with the fresh scent of imported French damask roses. The topic turned to the inevitable: "What would old Dante think," someone mused, "if he could return to life, strapped in his leather belt, to see silver forks upon the table?" Consensus held that modern customs would astonish ancestors, and vice versa.

As silver forks began to toy with the appetizing delicacies preceding the main course—morsels of liver cooked to a melting tenderness—the guests admired the enamelwork on the brass service centers. They praised the silver dish for *confetti*, a masterpiece by Antonio Pollaiuolo, whom papal patrons had lured from Florence to the opulence of Rome.

"Ah, I recall," said Niccolò Ridolfi, a middle-aged man whose negligent demeanor masked a lifetime of commanding authority. "I remember our Antonio complaining bitterly about his chiseling and enameling of these metal trifles. He threw himself into painting with a fury, declaring, 'The artist who casts his work into gold and silver pours his brain into the melting pot.'"

"That is a grim prophecy," Giannozzo Pucci remarked. "If this war with Pisa continues and the revolt spreads, our silver dishes may follow suit. I suspect Antonio's silver saints around the altar of San Giovanni will one day vanish from the faithful's sight to be worshipped more devoutly as coins."

"The Friar prepares us for this already," Lorenzo Tornabuoni added. "He tells the people that God rejects silver crucifixes alongside starving bellies, insisting the Church is best adorned with the gems of holiness and the golden hue of brotherly love."

"A sound doctrine of war finance," Bernardo Rucellai replied dryly. "But politics wait for the wine, Lorenzo. These matters are too heavy for roast meat and boiled dishes."

"Indeed," Niccolò Ridolfi agreed. "Luigi Pulci would insist this delicate kid requires an impartial palate. I recall him at Careggi, in his rambunctious mood, claiming that nothing corrupts the tongue like opinion. 'Opinion,' he said, 'spoils the saliva—that is why men crave pepper. Only skepticism offers a flavorless philosophy.' 'No,' retorted poor Lorenzo de' Medici, 'you are mistaken. Here sits Matteo Franco, an untainted skeptic demanding hotter sauce than any of us.' 'Because he holds a strong opinion of himself,' Luigi flashed back. 'He believes in the immortality of his own verses. He is a logician like that preaching friar who mapped the pavement of hell. Poor Luigi! His mind was like sharpest steel, touching nothing without cutting.'"

"And yet a gentle soul," Giannozzo Pucci countered. "His talk seemed merely bubbles bursting in the wind. What dithyrambs he sang of eating and drinking, yet he was as temperate as a butterfly."

The light chatter and substantial fare continued for some time. Following the roasts came the essential capon and game, crowned by the spectacular presentation of a peacock prepared à la Apicius. Cooked with feathers intact and arranged to resemble a living bird resting on the platter, it demanded the skill of a master carver to flip the classical, albeit insipid, bird onto its back, exposing the plucked breast for slicing. Unless one possessed an independent spirit willing to reject this expensive display for the simple digestibility of capon, everyone partook of the peacock. Few dared refuse. Tito quoted Horace and scattered his slice in tiny fragments; Bernardo Rucellai offered a learned comment on the ancient price of peacock eggs but declined to eat his portion; Niccolò Ridolfi balanced a bite on his fork while recounting a favorite tale of Luigi Pulci regarding a Sienese man who boiled a wild goose in its feathers to deceive guests into thinking it a pheasant.

Little actual peacock was consumed, yet the satisfaction lay in witnessing such extravagance, accessible only to the wealthiest. It would have been reckless to mock the flesh of peacocks or similar traditions when Fra Girolamo was preaching the disruptive doctrine that luxury was not the duty of the rich toward the poor.

Outside, amidst the chill shadows surrounding this center of warmth and savory smells, the lonely outcast walked in tightening circles. He paused among the trees, peering into the windows where the interior glowed brightly. He heard the laughter, saw Tito gesturing with careless elegance, and heard his voice rising and falling in the merry chaos of overlapping speech. Baldassarre's mind was agitated. He prepared himself for the moment he might breach this brilliant circle, deriving a savage pleasure from watching Tito's effortless gaiety, which seemed to be blinding the unsuspecting victim to coming torment.

Yet, the men seated among the flickering candles and gleaming goblets knew nothing of the pale, fierce face watching from the dark. Light can serve as a curtain as effectively as shadow.

As the conversation deepened, shedding its triviality, the sense of civic duty pressed heavily upon even the most indifferent minds. The teachings of the overpowering Fra Girolami dominated their thoughts. Before the stewed fish was cleared and the sweets presented, the Friar's name surfaced, and despite Rucellai's earlier warning against politics, the discussion resumed. Initially, with servants still present, it remained mere gossip: the results of the first day's voting for the Great Council; the fiery, domineering nature of Francesco Valori, who seemed to demand absolute rule by virtue of his austerity; and the obvious alignment between Soderini's speeches supporting the Great Council and the Friar's sermons, suggesting they were molded from the same dough.

"In my view," Niccolò Ridolfi stated, "the Friar possesses a sharper mind for public affairs than Soderini or any of the *Piagnoni*. You can count on Soderini being his mouthpiece rather than the other way around."

"No, Niccolò; I disagree," Bernardo Rucellai corrected. "The Friar has an acute mind and readily identifies what serves his ends, but it is unlikely that Pagolantonio Soderini, with his extensive experience and study of the Venetian Council, owes much to a monk for his ideas. No, Soderini loads the cannon; I admit the Friar provides the powder and strikes the match. He masters the people, and the people are beginning to master us. Behold!"

"Well," Lorenzo Tornabuoni said shortly after the servants departed and only wine circulated, "whether Soderini is indebted or not, *we* owe the Friar the general amnesty accompanying the Council scheme. We might have endured the fear of God and moral reforms passed by a majority of black beans, but the proposal that allows our Medicean heads to remain safely on our shoulders, sparing us the loss of property to fines, earns my warm approval. I believe nothing short of the Friar's dominance could have secured that. He stands as firm as a rock on promoting

When Romola sat down upon the rough stone beneath the cypress, the very air seemed to exhale a promise of liberty. The familiar confines of Florence's walls and its crowded thoroughfares lay behind her, receding into a widening distance. Her husband, Tito, was already riding toward Siena, moving away from her on one side of the horizon while she prepared to move further eastward on the other. The morning hung heavy and still, broken only by the vast depression of the roadside—a natural gulf separating her from the somber, sleeping calm of the mountains.

For the first time in her life, Romola felt truly alone in the presence of the earth and the sky. No human eye watched her; no social law dictated her movements. She breathed deeply, intoxicated by this sudden solitude.

Then, a voice cut through the silence, close to her ear.

"You are Romola de' Bardi, the wife of Tito Melema."

She knew that voice instantly. It had vibrated through her life more than once before. Because she recognized it, she did not turn around nor lift her eyes. Instead, she sat frozen, shaken by a profound awe that warred violently with an inner rebellion. It was one of the black-robed monks, daring to intrude upon her privacy, to interrupt her flight. That was all, she told herself. And yet, she felt as though Destiny—that capricious deity men often imagined—had suddenly descended, seizing her with tangible, flesh-and-blood fingers.

"You are fleeing from Florence in disguise," the voice continued, imperious and unyielding. "I hold a command from God to stop you. You are not permitted to flee."

Anger flared in Romola's chest at the intrusion, mounting with every syllable. She refused to turn, resenting the scrutiny of the examiner's gaze. Remaining perfectly motionless, she challenged him.

"What right have you to speak to me, or to hinder me?"

"The right of a messenger," the voice replied calmly. "You have donned a religious habit, yet you possess no religious purpose. You sought this garb merely as a disguise. But you were not allowed to pass undetected. It was revealed to me who you are: a woman attempting to escape the lot God has assigned you. You wish to hide your true name and your true station, choosing instead a new identity and a new existence ruled solely by your own will. I have been commanded to call you back. My daughter, you must return to your place."

With every word, Romola's spirit rose in sharper revolt. Determined not to display even a flicker of submission—fearing that any weakness would lead to total collapse—she spoke with increasing irritation.

"I will not return. I acknowledge no authority of priests or monks to dictate my actions. You have no power over me."

"I know," the voice conceded gently. "I know you have been raised in contempt for obedience. But listen closely: it is not the poor monk who claims dominion over you; it is Truth itself that commands you. And you cannot escape it. Either you submit, and it shall become your guide; or you resist, and it shall hang upon you like an iron chain, dragging you through eternity. But you *will* obey, my daughter. Your old servant returns with the mules; my companion has gone ahead to meet him. You will go back to Florence."

Suddenly, Romola sprang to her feet, her eyes blazing with defiance. She faced the speaker directly. It was Fra Girolamo. She knew him well enough now. They were nearly equal in height, their faces almost level. She had risen with a torrent of angry words ready to spill, but they died in her throat.

Fra Girolamo's gaze remained steady and calm. The impact of his look was entirely new to her, causing her anger to sink, ashamed and irrelevant. There was nothing transcendent or ethereal about Savonarola's face; it was not beautiful in the conventional sense. It was strong-featured, refined only by the rigors of mental discipline and bodily austerity. Yet, the effect of his glance upon Romola was immediate and overwhelming. It conveyed a deep, abiding interest in her, a care that existed apart from any personal affection.

It was the first time she had encountered a gaze where simple human fellowship manifested as a binding, strongly felt connection. Such a look constitutes half the vocation of a priest or spiritual guide, and Romola found herself unable to question his authority any longer. She stood silent, staring at him.

"You assert your freedom proudly, my daughter," he said softly. "But who is so base as the debtor who believes himself free?"

A stinging barb struck Romola, and her countenance altered as if a pale lightning had flashed across it.

"And you are fleeing from your debts: the debt of a Florentine woman; the debt of a wife. You are turning your back on the lot appointed for you, seeking to choose another. But can man or woman choose their duties? No more than they can choose their birthplace or their parents. My daughter, you are running from the presence of God into the wilderness."

As the heat of anger melted from her mind, it was replaced by a strange presentiment of the strength inherent in submission—if this man, whom she began to regard with a vague reverence, indeed possessed a valid law to offer her. Yet, it was impossible; he could not possibly understand what drove her. Nevertheless, she could not simply refuse guidance. Constrained by a newfound need for reverence amidst her resistance, the title she had never previously used slipped from her lips without premeditation.

"My father," she whispered, "you cannot know the reasons that compel me to go. None can know them but myself. None can judge for me. I have been driven by great sorrow. I am resolved to go."

"I know enough, my daughter," Savonarola replied, his tone illuminating with certainty. "My mind has been so far enlightened regarding you that I know enough. You are unhappy in your marriage; but I am not your confessor, and I seek to know nothing reserved for the seal of confession. I hold a divine warrant to stop you, which depends on no such private knowledge. You were warned by a message from Heaven, delivered in my presence—warned before your marriage, when you might still have lawfully chosen to remain free from the marital bond. But you chose the bond. And in willfully breaking it—I speak to you as a pagan, if the holy mystery of matrimony holds no sanctity for you—you are violating a pledge.

"Of what wrongs will you complain, my daughter, when you yourself commit one of the gravest sins a woman and a citizen can perpetrate: withdrawing in secret and disguise from a pledge sworn before God and your fellow-men? Of what grievances will you speak when you break the most fundamental law underpinning the trust between man and man—faithfulness to the spoken word? Is this the wisdom you have gained by scorning the mysteries of the Church? Not to perceive the bare duty of integrity, where the Church would teach you to see not merely integrity, but religion?"

Blood rushed to Romola's face, and she shrank back as if struck.

"I would not have put on a disguise," she began, but her voice faltered. She could not continue; the suggestion in the friar's words—that her conduct mirrored Tito's—shook her to her core.

"And to break that pledge, you flee from Florence," he pressed on. "Florence, where reside the only men and women in the world to whom you owe the debt of a fellow-citizen."

"I should never have left Florence," Romola murmured, her voice trembling, "as long as there was any hope of fulfilling a duty to my father there."

"Do you own no tie save that of a child to her father in the flesh?" Savonarola asked sharply. "Your life has been spent in blindness, my daughter. You have lived with those who sit aloof on a hill, looking down upon the lives of their neighbors with condescension. I know their vain discourses. They speak of the past, filling themselves with fancied wisdom while scorning God's work in the present. Doubtless you were taught that there were pagan women who understood how to live for the Republic, yet you never felt that you, a Florentine woman, should live *for* Florence. If your own people wear a yoke, will you slip from under it rather than struggle alongside them to lighten it? There is hunger and misery in our streets, yet you say, 'I care not; I have my own sorrows; I will depart, perhaps finding relief elsewhere.'

"While the servants of God strive for a law of justice, peace, and charity so that the hundred thousand citizens among whom you were born may be governed rightly, you regard this as insignificant—as if you were a bird spreading its wings to fly wherever it pleases in search of food. And yet you have scorned the teaching of the Church, my daughter. As if you, a wilful wanderer following your own blind choices, were inferior to the humblest Florentine woman who stretches out her hands with her own people, craving a blessing for them; who feels a close sisterhood with the neighbor kneeling beside her, regardless of bloodline; who contemplates the mighty purpose God has for Florence and waits, enduring, because the promised work is great and she feels herself small."

"I was not leaving for ease or self-indulgence!" Romola cried, lifting her head again, compelled to defend herself. "I was going to hardship. I expect no joy; it has departed from my life."

"Rise, my daughter," Fra Girolamo finally commanded. "Your servant waits nearby with the mules. It is time for me to proceed toward Florence."

Romola rose from her kneeling position. That silence had become a sacred ritual for her, cementing the state of yearning passivity she had recently embraced. In a single act—renouncing her plan to leave her husband—her will felt so thoroughly shattered that she required direction even for trivial matters. She lifted the edge of her cowl and spotted Maso and the second Dominican standing with their backs to her on the hill's edge, some ten yards away. Yet, she did not look at them immediately. Her gaze remained fixed on Savonarola, silent and expectant, as though the command to recall Maso must originate from him, not from her own voice.

"I will go and fetch them," he replied to her unspoken plea. "And I commend you, my daughter, to the Brother accompanying me. You seek to place yourself under guidance and to acquire that wisdom which has previously seemed foolishness to you. A primary gateway to this wisdom is the sacrament of confession. You require a confessor, my daughter, and I wish to entrust you to the care of Fra Salvestro, one of the brethren of San Marco, in whom I place my highest confidence."

"I would prefer no guidance other than yours, Father," Romola answered, her voice trembling with anxiety.

"My daughter, I do not act as your confessor. My vocation withdraws me from duties that would necessitate frequent contact with the laity and interfere with my specific responsibilities."

"Then shall I never be able to speak to you privately? If I falter, if—" Romola broke off, overcome by a sudden surge of agitation. A cold alarm seized her: she feared that the newfound strength derived from her renunciation might evaporate the moment Savonarola's direct personal influence faded.

"My daughter, if your soul requires the private word from my lips, you must inform me through Fra Salvestro, and I shall meet you in the sacristy or the choir of San Marco. Do not think I will cease to watch over you. I shall instruct my brother regarding you, ensuring he guides you into that path of labor for the suffering and the hungry to which you are called as a daughter of Florence in these times of dire need. I desire to see you among the weaker and less learned sisters as an apple tree stands out among the forest trees, so that your beauty and all your natural gifts serve merely as a lamp through which the Divine light shines more purely."

He paused, then added, "Now I shall go and summon your servant."

Once Maso had been sent ahead, Fra Salvestro stepped forward, and Savonarola led Romola toward him. She had already felt an internal shrinking away from a new guide who was a complete stranger to her; yet, to resist Savonarola's counsel would have been to adopt an attitude of independence at a moment when every ounce of her strength depended on the renunciation of that very independence. Consequently, her entire mental inclination was now directed toward performing what was painful rather than what was easy.

She bowed reverently to Fra Salvestro before meeting his eyes directly. However, when she raised her head and took him fully in view, her reluctance transformed into a palpable doubt. Some men possess a presence that naturally infuses trust and reverence; others demand that we bring our trust and reverence to them, pre-formed and ready. This distinction struck Romola sharply as Savonarola disappeared from her sight and Fra Salvestro Maruffi stood revealed in his place.

There was nothing manifestly repulsive in Fra Salvestro's face or manner—no hint of hypocrisy, no trace of coarseness. His features were actually handsomer than Fra Girolamo's, and his stature slightly taller. He was the long-standing confessor to many of Florence's leading figures, possessing vast experience as a spiritual director. Yet, his face bore the vacillating expression of a mind incapable of concentrating strongly upon one great emotion or belief—a trait fatal to influencing an ardent nature like Romola's. Such an expression does not necessarily signal insincerity; rather, it marks a shallow soul. One finds such individuals sincerely striving to fulfill a lofty vocation, earnestly composing their faces to utter sublime formulas, only to find their muscles twitch or relax despite their beliefs, much as prose intrudes where poetry should reign in those lacking divine frenzy.

Fra Salvestro possessed a peculiar susceptibility to visions, seemingly rooted in a constitution prone to somnambulism. Savonarola believed in the supernatural nature of these visions, whereas Fra Salvestro had initially resisted such interpretations, even rebuking Savonarola for his prophetic preaching. This served as further proof, if evidence were needed, that the relative greatness of men cannot be measured by their tendency to reject the superstitions of their age. Between the two, there could be no question of who was the great man and who was the lesser one. The disparity was measured precisely by the shift in Romola's feelings as Fra Salvestro began to address her with words of exhortation and encouragement.

After her initial angry resistance to Savonarola had subsided, she had forgotten the old dread of being dragged into the circle of fanaticism and sour, monkish piety. Now, however, the chilling breath of that fear stole over her once more. It could not decisively counteract the momentum her mind had just gained; it was akin to grey clouds closing over a sunrise, rendering her return path monotonous and somber. Perhaps of all somber journeys, the one undertaken after traversing a path with strong resolution is the one that most severely tests the fervor of renunciation.

As they re-entered the city gates, light snowflakes began to fall around them. The grey sister hurried homeward from the Piazza di San Marco, crossed the bridge again, and turned into the large doorway of the Via de' Bardi. Her footsteps left dark prints on the thin carpet of snow, and her cowl hung heavy and damp about her face. Inside, she ascended to her room, cast aside her serge habit, destroyed the farewell letters, restored all her cherished trifles to their places, loosened her hair, and donned her usual black dress.

Instead of embarking on a long, stirring journey, she was to sit in her accustomed spot. Snow tapped against the windows, and she was alone. She felt the dreariness, yet her courage remained high, reminiscent of a seeker who has discovered new signs of gold. She intended to navigate life by a fresh clue. She had poured all the energy of her will into renunciation. The empty tabernacle remained locked, and she placed Dino's crucifix outside it.

Nothing disrupted the outward monotony of her solitary home until night arrived like a white ghost at the windows. Yet, for Romola, this Christmas Eve of 1494 proved to be the most memorable of her life.

It was the thirtieth of October, 1496. The morning sky was clear, and a gentle autumnal breeze swept across the city. Yet, the Florentines paid little heed to the land breeze; their minds were consumed by the violent gales raging at sea. These storms seemed to conspire with every other force to refute the Friar's assertion that Heaven watched over Florence with special care. Terrible squalls had driven away from the coast of Leghorn several vessels from Marseilles laden with soldiers and grain. Florence stood in desperate need, first of food, and secondly of fighting men. Pale Famine stalked her streets, and her borders were threatened on all sides.

The French King, hailed as a new Charlemagne, had entered Italy in triumph, conquering Naples with ease, only to depart fifteen months prior. Rumors suggested he was now grieving the loss of a newborn son, causing him to lose even the languid intention of returning to settle grievances and reform the Church. In response, a "Holy League" had been formed under the leadership of Pope Borgia, ostensibly to "drive out the barbarians"—the French garrisoning Naples. While this sounded patriotic, it resembled an agreement among wolves to expel rival wolves merely to determine who could seize the largest share of the prey. Consequently, Florence was viewed less as a fellow wolf and more as a desirable carcass. Of all the major Italian states, Florence alone had refused to join the League, maintaining her allegiance to France. She had declined at her peril.

At this critical juncture, Pisa, fighting savagely for its liberty, was bolstered not only by forces from Venice and Milan but also by the German Emperor Maximilian, invited by the League. Maximilian arrived with troops to aid the Pisans in seizing Leghorn, while Venetian and Genoese ships blockaded the coast. If Leghorn fell, disaster would surely follow for Florence. Cut off from the sea and hemmed in by the Pope's hostility and the jealousy of smaller states, how could supplies reach her? The Florentine government had responded to this crisis with remarkable resolve, meeting losses and defeats with vigor, raising funds and recruiting soldiers, while not abandoning the traditional Italian method of conciliatory diplomacy. Despite the growing scarcity of food, they had decided, breaking with old precedent, not to exclude the starving peasants and mendicants fleeing other cities like birds from a blizzard.

These benevolent acts, characteristic of a regime dominated by Savonarola's disciples, drew sharp criticism. Disaffected citizens argued that the government pursued the wrong path for public welfare. They insisted Florence should join the League to align with powerful neighbors rather than inviting hostility through a futile attachment to a foreign ally. Furthermore, they believed the state should prioritize its own citizens instead of opening its gates to famine and pestilence in the form of destitute foreigners. Distress grew sharper daily, and murmurs grew louder. Compounding these difficulties, Fra Girolamo had been forbidden to preach for over a month by a mandate from Rome. However, upon receiving the terrifying news that the Marseilles ships had been repelled and no grain was arriving, the need for a voice capable of instilling faith and patience became irresistible. Defying the Papal command, the Signoria summoned Savonarola. Two days prior, he had returned to the pulpit of the Duomo, urging the people to wait steadfastly, promising that divine help would inevitably arrive. He boldly offered to strip off his friar's robe if God failed to save Florence despite their piety. Yet, on this bleak morning of the thirtieth, no signs of rescue were visible.

Perhaps, the citizens wondered, bringing the sacred Tabernacle of the Madonna dell'Impruneta into Florence and carrying it in a devout procession to the Duomo would invoke the Mother's mercy? For a century and a half, records showed that whenever Florence suffered from drought, flood, famine, plague, or war, fetching this potent image within their walls had secured deliverance. Wealth had been lavished upon her shrine, and lands granted until disputes arose over the honor of serving her. The Florentines were deeply convinced of her grace; the sight of her tabernacle within their walls was akin to clouds parting, leading to the proverb that they possessed a Madonna who would grant whatever they wished. Never had they needed her intercession more urgently.

The previous evening, the tabernacle containing the miraculous hidden image had been escorted from L'Impruneta, six miles beyond the Gate of San Piero facing Rome, and placed in the church of San Gaggio outside the walls. From there, it was to be retrieved in a solemn procession by all the fraternities, guilds, and authorities of Florence. But the Pitying Mother had not yet entered the city. Morning broke over unchanged misery and despair. Pestilence trailed the footsteps of famine. Hospitals were overflowing, and private courtyards had been converted into infirmaries, yet unsheltered want persisted. Early that morning, as was custom, members of the various burial fraternities carried away corpses that had perished by the roadside. Among the crowds moved sweet, well-born women in simple garments, diligently tending the sick and feeding the hungry.

One such figure was unmistakably Romola de' Bardi. Dressed in the simplest black serge, with a plain black veil hiding her hair except for the bands of gold rippling across her brow, she advanced from the Ponte Vecchio toward the Porta Santa Maria. Her path was blocked by a bier paused by members of the Company of San Jacopo del Popolo, searching for the unburied dead. The brothers at the front bent to inspect something, while a cluster of idle workers, their faces gaunt and sharpened by hunger, gathered around, speaking in a chaotic murmur.

"He's dead, I tell you! Messer Domeneddio loved him enough to take him!"
"And wouldn't it be wonderful if we could stretch out our legs and walk ahead with our heads two or three *bracci* further? Standing upright with hunger is no support."
"Well, he's an old fellow. Death got a bad bargain; life won out."
"No Florentine, ten to one! A beggar turned out of Siena. May San Giovanni protect us! They don't need soldiers to fight us; they send us an army of starving men."
"No, no! This man is a prisoner released from the Stinche. I know by the grey patch where the prison badge was."
"Hush! Help them! Don't you see the brothers are lifting him onto the bier?"
"It's possible he's alive if he could just look it. Maybe the soul is still inside if he had a drop of *vernaccia* to warm him."
"In truth, I think he isn't dead," said one of the brothers after lifting him. "He has probably just collapsed from lack of food."
"Let me try giving him some wine," Romola said, stepping forward.

She loosened the small flask at her belt, leaned toward the prostrate body, and with a skilled hand inserted a small ivory implement between his teeth, pouring a few drops of wine. The stimulus worked; the liquid was swallowed. She poured more until the old man's head shifted slightly toward her, and his eyes opened fully upon her with a vague look of returning consciousness. Only then did a sense of complete recognition strike Romola. Those wild, dark eyes opening in the sallow, deep-lined face, framed by a long white beard, were like an unmistakable signature to a remembered handwriting. The light of two summers had not faded that image in her memory: the picture of the escaped prisoner she had seen in the Duomo the day Tito first donned armor, the very man whose grasp had made Tito turn pale in the strange sketch found in Piero's studio.

A trembling seized her, a palpitation of fear. Now, perhaps, she was about to learn a secret more bitter than anything past. An impulse drove her to flee as from a horror, yet a stronger, imperious need compelled her to stay beside this old man whom her intuition told her her husband had harmed. In that moment of conflict, she remained leaning over him, her right hand ready to offer more wine, her left supporting his neck. Her hands trembled, yet their habitual kindness guided them without conscious direction.

Baldassarre looked at *her* for the first time. The isolation of Romola's recent troubles had prevented him from seeing the wife who lived in the Via de' Bardi, and the descriptions he had heard of the fair, golden-haired woman had vanished like yesterday's waves.

"Shall we not carry him to the steps of San Stefano?" Romola asked. "Then we shall not block the street, and you may proceed with your bier."

They moved only thirty yards before reaching the church steps. By then, Baldassarre was strong enough to sit up, propping himself against the doorway. The charitable brothers continued on, but the crowd of idlers, having nothing to do and much to say, had swelled. The sentiment toward the old man had shifted; knowing he was alive made the atmosphere less friendly, though Romola's presence tempered the comments into a quieter tone.

"They fed him a bite every day in the Stinche—that's why he can't manage without it. You and I, Cecco, know better what it feels like to sleep on an empty stomach."
"*Gnaffè!* That's why the Magnificent Eight released some prisoners so they could shelter honest folk. But if every thief is to be revived with good wine and wheat bread, we Ciompi had better fill ourselves in the Arno while the water

Returning from the hospital, an hour later than usual, Romola chose a path that circled widely toward the river, reaching the banks far from the Ponte Vecchio. Her intention was to hurry to San Stefano and seek Baldassarre. Though she dreaded hearing more of his condition, she felt a strange reluctance to leave him behind, as if doing so meant abandoning a vital claim upon her soul.

As she neared the junction where the Por' Santa Maria curved to her right and the Ponte Vecchio lay to her left, she found herself engulfed in a sudden throng of kneeling figures. Without hesitation, she joined them. The Great Cross of the Duomo was passing, leading a solemn procession. Being late, she waited until the massive timber had cleared before rising. The transition from kneeling to standing, with nothing to occupy her mind but the scene before her, made her acutely aware of her exhaustion.

A shopkeeper beside her spoke kindly, "Madonna Romola, you must be weary. Gian Fantoni would be delighted to offer you a seat in his house; his door is right there. Let me open it for you. He loves God and our Brother as we do. His home is yours."

Romola was used to such fraternal greetings from citizens whose faces she knew well from the crowds filling the cathedral. To her, the concept of "home" had shifted away from the lonely solitude of the Via de' Bardi. Instead, it now encompassed the entire towered circuit of Florence, a city where every street turn seemed to greet her with looks of appeal or friendship. Gladly accepting the invitation, she stepped through the open door, guided by the hose-seller to an upstairs window. There, a sturdy woman, dressed plainly among her three children in the garb of the Piagnoni, received her with deep reverence, seating her above bright hanging draperies.

From this vantage point, Romola watched the procession pour slowly between the lines of houses on the Ponte Vecchio, alongside the view of the river and the Lung' Arno stretching toward the bridge of Santa Trinita. Silence and sorrow characterized the march. No wailing chants broke the air; there was only the rhythmic tramp of footsteps and the soft sweep of woolen robes.

First came a long train of Florentine youth, their faces fresh and beardless, bearing high in the center the white image of the youthful Jesus, crowned with golden glory, standing beside the tall cross where thorns and nails awaited. Following them were the mysterious Companies of Discipline, bound by secret vows of self-chastisement and devout praise. Dressed in garments that covered their heads and faces entirely, leaving only their eyes exposed, they had shed their individual identities as sons, husbands, or fathers. They walked merely as symbols of a shared vow, each company distinguished by color and badge, yet united in a shrouded uniformity that expressed only fellowship.

In contrast, the multitude of monks appeared as distinct individuals despite their common tonsures and frocks. First flowed a white stream of reformed Benedictines, followed by the much longer column of the Frati Minori (Franciscans), clad in grey with knotted cords around their waists and some wearing wooden *zoccoli* sandals beneath bare feet. Perhaps the most numerous order in Florence, they were known for their zeal, their love of mankind, and their animosity toward the Dominicans. After the grey came the black of the Augustinians of San Spirito, men of cultivated countenances who had inherited Boccaccio's library and formed Florence's most learned circle during an age when knowledge was rare. Next appeared the white-over-dark habit of the Carmelites, followed by the unmixed black of the Servites, a famous Florentine order founded by seven merchants who renounced their wealth to adore the Divine Mother.

The hearts of the onlookers quickened, beating with a mixture of hatred and love, as a stream of black mantles over white scapularies crossed the bridge—the Dominicans. Those from Fiesole led the way. One black mantle parted by another, one tonsured head following another, suspense hung heavy in the air. Their mantles were coarse, many threadbare or ragged, for the Prior of San Marco had reduced the fraternities under his rule to the strictest poverty and discipline. Yet, amidst the worn line, one mantle stood out slightly less tattered, topped by a tonsured head that might seem unremarkable to a stranger unless they recognized it from bronze medals depicting the sword of God, or remembered it surrounded by an armed guard en route to the Duomo, or transformed by the inner fire of an orator addressing a rapt multitude.

As Savonarola's approach became clear, no one dared break the silence with a sound louder than the solemn tramp of feet. Nevertheless, his ear, like others, caught a mingled hiss that longed to become curses, interwoven with murmurs longing to be blessings. Perhaps it was the dominance of the hissing that caused two or three of his disciples in the foreground to fall to their knees as if something divine were passing. This movement of silent homage spread like a subtle shock along the street sides, leaving some unmoved while compelling others to bend their knees and lower their heads. Yet, hatred too gathered intensity; as Savonarola ascended the Por' Santa Maria, Romola saw someone at an upper window spit upon him.

Monks returned—the Frati Umiliati, or Humbled Brethren, from Ognissanti, heirs to a glorious tradition as early workers in the wool trade. Then came more monks, Vallombrosan and various Benedictines, whose careful forms and colors reminded the observant eye that reformers had long since arisen to mark a spiritual change through altered habits. Finally, the shaven crowns ended, giving way to the train of untonsured secular priests.

Following them marched the twenty-one incorporated Arts of Florence in long array, their banners floating proudly above them, declaring their distinct functions from bakers to judges and notaries. Then came the secondary officers of state, ascending from lesser to greater rank, until the line of seculars was broken by the Canons of the Duomo. They carried a sacred relic: the very head of San Zenobio, the immortal bishop of Florence, enclosed in silver, whose virtues were believed to have saved the city a millennium prior. This was the nucleus of the procession.

Behind the relic walked the Archbishop in a gorgeous cope, with a canopy held high above him. Following him came the mysterious, hidden Image. First concealed by rich brocade curtains enclosing an outer painted tabernacle, and within that, by an ancient inner shrine never opened in living memory—or perhaps in the memory of the fathers themselves. Inside rested the image of the Pitying Mother, discovered ages ago in the soil of L'Impruneta, crying out as the spade struck it.

Hitherto, this unseen Image had rarely entered the Duomo without rich gifts borne before it. Lists of precious offerings from emulous men and communities—veils, curtains, mantles—were usually recited. It was said the richest gift came from a poor abbess and her nuns, who, lacking funds, wove a mantle of gold brocade with their prayers, embroidered it with devotion, and saw their work presented to the Blessed Virgin in the great Piazza by two beautiful youths who spread white wings and vanished into the blue sky.

But today, no gifts preceded the tabernacle. No donations were offered except to the poor. This was the counsel of Fra Girolamo, whose preaching never urged gifts to invisible powers but focused solely on aid for visible needs. Altars had been raised at various church entrances where oblations for the destitute were placed. Not even a torch was carried. Surely the hidden Mother cared less for torches and brocade than for the wails of the hungry. Florence was in extremity; she had done her utmost and could only wait for a divine intervention beyond her own power.
