The friar in the torn mantle had promised help would surely come, and many of the faint-hearted clung to his word rather than the virtues of the unseen Image. Conversely, not a few fierce-hearted souls rejoiced secretly, hoping the friar's promise might prove false.

Slowly the tabernacle moved forward, and knees bent once more. Profound stillness reigned, for the train of priests and chaplains from L'Impruneta stirred no passion in the crowd. The procession was nearing its close with the Priors and the Gonfaloniere. The long train of symbolic companies, possessing a silent music that stirred the mind like a chorus, was fading from sight. Now, a faint yearning hope struggled against the accustomed despondency.

Romola, whose heart had swelled with half-fear and half-the enthusiastic fellowship born of the last two years, let out a deep sigh as if releasing a long-held mental tension. She remained on her knees, overcome by languor. Suddenly, something brightly colored flashed between the houses onto the distant bridge.

In an instant, Romola sprang up, stretching her arms and leaning from the window. Black drapery fell from her head, and the golden gleam of her hair and the flush on her cheeks seemed illuminated by a single burst of light. A shout erupted simultaneously. The last troops of the procession paused, all faces turning toward the distant bridge. But the bridge was already passed. A horseman pressed forward at full gallop along the Arno; the flanks of his bay horse, streaked with foam, appeared white from the speed of his passage. His cap flew loose from his red *becchetto*, and he waved an olive branch in his hand.

It was a messenger

As soon as the throng had swept past, Romola hurried down to the street and raced toward the steps of San Stefano. Cecco had drifted with the crowd into the Piazza, leaving her to find Baldassarre standing solitary against the church door. He held his horn-cup in his hand, waiting.

There was a startling transformation in him. The vacant, dreamy stare of a half-awaking consciousness had vanished, replaced by a ferocity that struck Romola with the force of a lightning bolt aimed directly at her. It was the gaze of a caged beast sensing its prey moving safely beyond the bars. Romola flinched as the look locked onto her, yet her immediate thought was that he had spotted Tito. As the hatred grated against her spirit, a fragile hope ignited within her: perhaps this man *was* the criminal, and her husband innocent. If she could bring Tito face-to-face with him now, she could finally find peace.

"If you will come with me," she said softly, "I can offer you shelter and food until you are strong again. Will you come?"

"Yes," Baldassarre replied, his voice low. "I shall be glad to regain my strength." He repeated the words as if murmuring to himself rather than addressing her.

"Come!" she invited, guiding him beside her down the quieter path toward the Arno and the Ponte Rubaconte.

"I suspect you are not a Florentine," she remarked as they approached the bridge.

He glanced around, silent. His suspicion was sharpened by physical weakness, the fog of confusion deepening around him. Yet, Romola studied him too, and there was a gentleness in her eyes that eventually drew a response.

"No," he answered cautiously. "I am no Florentine. I am a lonely man."

She noted his reluctance to speak further, fearing he wished to leave, so she did not press him. As they walked, her mind worked tirelessly, extinguishing the faint hope that her husband's situation was free of pain. If this old man had been in the wrong, why the dread and secrecy? They proceeded in silence until they reached the entrance of the Via de' Bardi. Suddenly, Baldassarre stopped, turning to look at her with a jolt, as if a shock had pierced him.

Moments later, Romola paused at the half-open door of the courtyard and faced him.

"Ah!" he exclaimed before she could speak. "You are his wife."

"His wife?" Romola asked, bewildered.

It would have been impossible for Baldassarre to summon a name. The sheer weight of Tito's image seemed to expel any verbal response. He offered no reply, staring at her with a strange, fixed intensity.

Romola pushed the door open wide, revealing a courtyard strewn with straw where four or five sick people lay. Tiny, pale children crawled or sat amidst them, gnawing on straws and gurgling.

"If you will come in," Romola said, her voice trembling, "I will find you a comfortable spot and bring you more food."

"No," Baldassarre refused. "I will not come in."

Yet he remained frozen, paralyzed by the crushing burden of impressions that left his mind too confused to choose a course.

"Is there nothing I can do for you?" Romola pleaded. "Let me give you money to buy food. It will be more plentiful soon."

She reached into her *scarsella*, holding out her palm with several *grossi*. She deliberately offered more than she would to anyone else in such dire straits.

He stared at the coins for a moment, then nodded. "Yes, I will take them."

She poured the coins into his palm, and he clutched them tightly.

"Tell me," Romola begged, "what shall you—"

But Baldassarre had turned away, resuming his walk toward the bridge. Crossing it, he headed straight up the Via del Fosso and turned abruptly into the shop of Niccolò Caparra, as though it were his destination.

Niccolò was currently leading a procession with the city's armorers, leaving only an apprentice behind. Weapons hung everywhere, gleaming in the dim light. Baldassarre's eyes scanned the array, hungry for something far more potent than bread. Though Niccolò would likely refuse to sell arms to a man bearing the marks of imprisonment, the apprentice, less observant and scrupulous, sold a sharp hunting-knife for three *grossi* without hesitation.

It was a compact blade, easily concealed within Baldassarre's tunic. He walked on, feeling a surge of renewed strength. That sharp edge could lend deadly precision to the thrust of an aging arm; at the very least, it was a companion, a power allied with him, even if it failed. It might shatter against armor, but was the armor guaranteed to remain? During those long months of confinement, vengeance had lain dormant, allowing baseness to grow complacent and secure.

The knife had been purchased with the traitor's own money. That was justice. Before accepting the coin, he had known exactly what he would do with it: acquire a weapon. And if possible, food as well—food to nourish the arm that would wield the steel, food to sustain the body that served as the temple of vengeance. Once he had eaten enough, he would be able to think and act. First, he must consider how to hide, lest Tito have him dragged away once more.

With this plan forming in his mind, Baldassarre navigated the narrowest alleys, buying meat and bread, then sat beneath the first loggia to eat.

The bells began to toll, swelling into louder and louder peals of joy. They vibrated through the air, binding him to a world that stood firmly against him. To Baldassarre, the sound was merely part of that hostile universe.

Romola watched him until he vanished around the corner into the Piazza de' Mozzi. Half relieved by his departure, half reproached for failing to seek the truth with greater determination, she wondered if she had missed a chance to alleviate some guiltless suffering. Yet, what could she have done if the truth carried the heavy burden of a painful secret regarding her husband? Was a wife not permitted to desire ignorance of her spouse's wrongdoing, especially when she alone must refrain from protesting or warning others against him? The thought stirred complex fibers of feeling, but in her weariness, she let it pass.

It was a time for rejoicing, for help had arrived for Florence. Turning back into the courtyard, she went to share the good news with her patients lying on their straw beds. Closing the door to muffle the overwhelming clamor of the bells, she threw back the black drapery covering her head so the women could see her clearly. Standing among them, she announced that corn was coming and that the bells rang in gladness at the news.

They all sat up to listen. Children trotted and crawled toward her, pulling at her black skirts, eager to be near her face. Exhausted as she was, Romola yielded to them, sitting down on the straw. The tiny, pale faces peered into her basket, tugging at her hair, while the weak voices around her chanted, "The Holy Virgin be praised!"

"It was the procession!" someone cried. "The Mother of God has had pity on us!"

Finally, rising from the heap of straw, too weary to attempt a smile, she said as she climbed the stone steps, "I will come by-and-by to bring you your dinner."

"Bless you, madonna! Bless you!" came the faint chorus, echoing the same tone they had used moments earlier to praise the unseen Madonna.

Romola cherished that music profoundly. Unlike some women who found comfort in the mere routine of caring for the sick and clothing the ragged, her early training had kept her aloof from such domestic labors. Without the inspiration of her deepest feelings, the work would have been irksome to her. Instead, it had become the sole unshaken refuge of her mind, the narrow path upon which clarity shone brightest.

Though the widening gulf between herself and Tito, bridged only by her futile attempts at submission, bred doubts about the validity of the bond she had sworn to uphold—even as she withdrew from her confessor, Fra Salvestro, or encountered the rigid disciples of Savonarola, feeling a sickening revulsion at their narrowness and a fierce resurgence of her old contempt for their superstition—she found stability in her acts of compassion.

Whatever else caused her uncertainty, the aid she gave to her fellow citizens confirmed that Fra Girolamo had been right to call her back. As his unforgettable words suggested, her place had never been empty; it had been filled with her love and labor. Florence needed her. The heavier her personal sorrow pressed, the greater her gladness in recalling the hours over the past two years when she had lightened the burdens of others.

All the ardor of her nature, which could no longer express itself solely in maternal tenderness for father and husband, had transmuted into an enthusiastic sympathy for the common life. She had ceased to believe her own lot could be happy, indeed, she had ceased thinking of happiness altogether. The single end of her existence appeared to be the diminution of sorrow. This enthusiasm was constantly revitalized by Savonarola's influence. Despite recoiling in disgust from the wearisome visions and allegories when repeated by others, her profound affinity for his passionate sympathy and the splendor of his aims retained its full power.

His burning indignation against the abuses and oppressions that plagued both Church and State kindled

Following the auspicious arrival of the messenger bearing the olive branch—a favor of fortune Tito had not dared to hope for—he was immediately tasked with duties of far more deliberate consequence. Pausing before the Palazzo Vecchio, he waited for the return of the *Dieci*, the body governing external and military affairs, to formally submit the findings of his clandestine mission to Pisa. This report served as the prologue to an open embassy led by Bernardo Rucellai, aimed at securing a peaceful accord between Florence, Emperor Maximilian, and the Italian League. Tito's aptitude for diplomacy was now fully proven; as he delivered his intelligence with clarity and precision, Bernardo del Nero, then a member of the *Dieci*, could not suppress his admiration. Though he would have preferred to hide it, Bernardo's initial disdain for Tito had resurfaced and intensified following the controversial sale of the library.

Romola had remained silent regarding the transaction, offering her godfather no explanation. Bernardo interpreted this silence as a command to avoid the subject, yet he was convinced that violating her father's wishes had inflicted a deep, festering wound upon her spirit. Observant of her demeanor, he noted further signs that her marriage brought her little happiness. "Ah," he murmured to himself, "this doubtless explains her turn toward Fra Girolamo and the *Piagnoni*—a shift I never anticipated. Women, unhappy and childless, inevitably drift either into folly or into a religion so austere they feel burdened with all of Heaven's labor. As for my poor child Romola, I maintain what I always said: stuffing her with Latin and Greek has rendered her less a woman and more a creature who spends her days merely pricking her fingers with needles. And as for her husband, a man employed everywhere because he is a tool with a smooth handle, I pray that Tornabuoni and his ilk do not find their fingers severed. Well, well, *solco torto, sacco dritto*—many a full sack comes from a crooked furrow; and he who insists on commanding only honest men will find his wages scant."

Holding firmly to the conviction that political agents could not be morally scrutinized, Bernardo chose to remain aloof from Tito's disrepute. Aside from matters reserved for Romola's sake, Bernardo possessed no concrete grievance against the useful Greek save for his nationality and his personal dislike. Tito's duplicity—feigning loyalty to the popular government while harboring Medicean sympathies—was a trait shared by half the Medicean faction; Tito merely executed it with superior skill. Consequently, Bernardo treated Tito with polite indifference, a coldness Tito mirrored with scrupulous, distant respect. In Florence, it was still widely believed that any service rendered to Romola's husband constituted a tribute to her godfather.

Having discharged his official duties at the Old Palace, Tito felt the weight of the day's public obligations lift. Exhausted and parched from hours of riding, he declined to return home immediately. There were items in his saddlebag (*scarsella*) and burdens on his mind that demanded release, but these opportunities required subtle timing; they could not appear sought-after. Instead, he strolled aimlessly toward the Piazza del Duomo. Although the religious procession had concluded, the bells continued to toll, and the populace moved restlessly through the streets, craving a tangible outlet for their jubilation. While the new discipline, championed by the Friar, insisted that Christ alone be King and that all pleasures conform to Christian modesty, a secret yearning persisted among the youths shouting *"Viva Gesù!"* for a burst of vigorous stone-throwing as thanks.

Tito, passing through the crowd, could not evade recognition as the bearer of peace. To shed the unwanted ovation and the barrage of eager inquiries, he deftly claimed that Meo di Sasso, the authentic messenger from Livorno, was arriving and could be found near the Porta San Frediano. Knowing more than he admitted, he used this fabrication to clear his path. Once free of importunity, he entered the Piazza del Duomo, scanning the square with an air of careless leisure, though his gaze was sharply focused on detecting a specific presence.

The termination of the procession at the cathedral meant an unusual density of loiterers and conversationalists around Nello's shop, precisely where Tito hoped to find his opportunity. As expected, a group clustered near the northern railings of the Baptistery. Spotting the tallest figure in the group, Tito feigned total indifference, merely turning his head to offer a perfunctory greeting as he tossed the end of his hood over his shoulder. Yet, the tall, broad-shouldered man thus acknowledged appeared formidable. He wore a richly embroidered tunic and abundant linen in the latest French style, with a sword and poniard of exquisite craftsmanship hanging from his belt. His hat, adorned with a crimson plume, seemed a defiant rejection of the somber Florentine dress code, recently exaggerated by the influence of the *Piagnoni*. Indefinable markers of youth accentuated the coarseness of his wide face and substantial waist, while his eyes held a crude, desecrating stare that would offend any refined sensibility.

He and his companions, similarly attired young men carrying weapons, exchanged jokes with an ostentatious laughter designed to prove their immunity to shame, despite obvious suspicions to the contrary. Indeed, there were ample grounds for such suspicion. This broad-shouldered figure with the red feather was Dolfo Spini, leader of the *Compagnacci*, or Evil Companions. These were the dissolute sons of the old aristocracy, enemies of the Medici, foes of the popular government, and bitter adversaries of Savonarola. Dolfo, heir to the great house overlooking the Santa Trinita bridge, had organized these youths into an armed band dedicated to defending extravagant feasts and carnal sins against the reforming pietists who threatened to render the world so chaste and temperate that existence itself became unbearable. Just that morning, he had proclaimed Florence doomed to famine and ruin due to its blind adherence to the Friar, arguing that salvation lay solely in joining the League and expelling the Frate to Rome, as the Pope had long commanded. Thus, it was suspected that Messer Dolfo Spini's heart burned with no pure joy at the unexpected reinforcements that seemingly fulfilled the Friar's predictions. The laughter erupting as Tito approached the group near Nello's door failed to dispel this unease.

Leaning against the doorpost at the center of the gathering was a close-shaven, keen-eyed figure named Niccolò Machiavelli. Though young, he had already pierced the small secrets of human egoism.

"Messer Dolfo's head," he remarked, "is more of a pumpkin than I imagined. I judge men's stupidity by the devices they trust to deceive others. Your dullest beast of all is he who grins and claims he does not mind, just after his shins have been kicked." Smiling as the circle opened to admit Tito, he continued, "If I were a trifle duller now, I should pretend to cherish this Melema, who holds a secretaryship perfectly suited to me—as if poorly paid Latin could love better Latin that is better paid! Melema, you are a pestilently clever fellow, very much in my way, and I regret hearing you've secured another stroke of luck today."

"Questionable luck, Niccolò," replied Tito, placing a friendly hand on his shoulder. "I possess nothing yet but being grabbed and breathed upon by wool-combers, leaving me as soiled and battered from riding as a letter-carrier from Bologna."

"Ah! You crave a touch of my art, Messer Oratore," said Nello, stepping forward at the sound of Tito's voice. "I perceive your chin bears yesterday's stubble. Come, consign yourself to the priest of all the Muses. Sandro, fetch the lather quickly!"

"In truth, Nello, that is precisely what I most desire at this moment," Tito said, sitting down. "That was why I directed my steps toward your shop rather than returning home immediately after concluding my business at the Palazzo."

"Yes, indeed, it is unfitting that you should present yourself to Madonna Romola with a rusty chin and a tangled mass of hair. Nothing undaint should approach the Florentine lily; though I see her constantly wandering like a sunbeam among the rags lining our corners—or perhaps she resembles a moonbeam now, for I met her yesterday and she looked as pale and worn as the fainting Madonna in Fra Giovanni's painting. You must attend to her, my learned friend: she observes too many fasts and vigils in your absence."

Tito offered a melancholy shrug. "It is too true, Nello. She has deprived herself of half her proper sustenance every day during this famine. But what can I do? A husband's influence is powerless against the Friar's."

"As are all other influences, including that of the Holy Father," interjected Domenico Cennini, who had entered with Tito. "Have you gleaned anything at Pisa regarding the prevailing winds in Rome, Melema?"

"Secrets of the council chamber, Messer Domenico!" Tito smiled, spreading his palms in a gesture of innocence. "An envoy must be as mute as a confessor."

"Certainly, certainly," agreed Cennini. "I ask for no violation of that rule. However, my belief is that if His Holiness were to drive Fra Girolamo to desperation, the Friar would move heaven and earth to convene a General Council of the Church—and succeed. I, for one,

That evening, the sky was heavy with the promise of rain, dark and foreboding. Romola returned from the Hospital of San Matteo, accompanied by Maso and a lantern, having visited the sick after vespers. Just as she emerged onto the street, she met her husband, Tito, stepping out of the Monastery of San Marco. Tito had ventured forth shortly after his arrival in the Via de' Bardi earlier that day and had seen little of Romola since. Seeing her, he immediately proposed to escort her home, dismissing Maso, whose short, hurried steps irritated him. Such official attentions were customary for Tito only when explicitly demanded; otherwise, he maintained a cool distance.

Between Tito and Romola, there existed no friction, no open quarrel. They were too hopelessly alienated in their inner lives to engage in the kind of conflict that seeks reconciliation. Instead, they navigated their shared existence with a careful adherence to a tacit truce. If Tito desired supper prepared in the old library, now transformed into a pleasant banqueting hall, Romola assented and ensured everything was arranged. Conversely, Tito left her entirely free in her daily habits, accepting her assistance with transcription and digesting texts, while in return providing the supplies she needed for her charitable works. Yet, despite this functional harmony, he constantly avoided exchanging glances with her. He pretended to believe she was elsewhere to avoid seeking her in her own rooms, and he playfully attributed to her a perpetual preference for solitude over his company.

In the early days following her decision to remain rather than flee, Romola had made timid attempts to restore a frank relationship between them. However, she realized that true reconciliation required open speech regarding their differences and a striving toward a moral understanding. Tito, conversely, could only be saved from alienation by a return to her former effusive tenderness—a sentiment that would require forgetting their differences. He cared for no explanation; he deemed a thorough accounting impossible. He wished for Romola to love him again, but for her, love was impossible. She could be submissive and gentle, capable of suppressing any sign of repulsion, but tenderness could not be feigned. Helpless before this reality, she recognized that her husband was indeed alienated from her. This realization reinforced her resolve to keep herself strictly outside any secrets he would never voluntarily share. Regarding his political actions, he sought to convince her that he viewed the Medici cause as hopeless and that he sincerely served the popular government, in which she now held a warm interest. Nevertheless, subtle impressions, like faint odors, unsettled her concerning his dealings with San Marco. She was torn between the dread of witnessing evidence that would confirm her suspicions and the urgent impulse to watch closely, fearing harm she might otherwise have prevented.

As they walked together that evening, Tito remarked, "The business of the day is not yet concluded for me. I shall conduct you to our door, my Romola, and then I must fulfill another commission. It may take an hour, perhaps longer, before I can return to rest, which I sorely need." He then spoke amusingly of events in Pisa until they approached a loggia near which a lamp burned before a picture of the Virgin. The street was usually quiet, and few people had passed them thus far, but now the sound of many approaching footsteps and confused voices grew louder.

"We shall not reach home dry unless we take shelter under this convenient loggia," Tito said hastily, urging Romola up the steps with a slightly startled movement. "Surely it is useless to wait for this light drizzle," Romola observed in surprise. "No," Tito replied, "I felt it growing heavier. Let us wait a moment."

With that keen awareness of the slightest indications characteristic of a mind habitually in a state of caution, Tito had noticed through the lamplight that the leader of the approaching group wore a red feather and a glittering sword-hilt. In fact, he was almost the last person in the world he wished to meet at that hour beside Romola. Earlier that day, he had already conducted one momentous interview with Dolfo Spini; the task he mentioned to Romola was a second meeting with that same man, a sequel to his visit at San Marco.

By a long-prearranged plan, Tito had acted as the bearer of letters to Savonarola—carefully forged documents. One bore, through a clever stratagem, the very signature and seal of the Cardinal of Naples, who among the Sacred College had exerted the greatest influence at Rome in favor of the Friar. These letters stated that the Cardinal, on his journey from Pisa and unwilling for strong reasons to enter Florence directly, yet eager to consult Savonarola at this critical juncture, intended to stop that very day at San Casciano, ten miles from the city. From there, disguised in the simple garb of a priest, he planned to ride out the next morning and meet Savonarola seemingly by chance, five miles along the road to Florence, two hours after sunrise.

The plot, initiated by these forged letters, was for Dolfo Spini, leading a band of his *Compagnacci*, to lie in ambush at a lonely spot five miles from the city gates. There, they were to seize Savonarola and the Dominican friar accompanying him according to monastic rule, delivering them to a small detachment of Milanese cavalry stationed near San Casciano, who would carry them into Roman territory. There was a significant chance that the penetrating Friar would suspect the trap and refuse to incur the risk of traveling beyond the city walls alone. Even when preaching, his friends insisted he be attended by an armed guard; here, however, he was asked to commit himself to a solitary road with no other companion than a fellow monk. To maximize the likelihood of his acceptance, minimal time was allotted for decision-making. The strategy relied on the assumption that the Friar's intense desire to unite internal and external forces toward a General Council, coupled with his expectation of immediate support from the Cardinal in his struggle with the Pope, would overcome his natural shrewdness and caution within the brief window provided.

Tito had previously received an audience with Savonarola, declining to entrust the letters to anyone else. With consummate art, he had led the Friar to infer their meaning, suggesting they referred to a rendezvous outside the gates. Consequently, he urged that Savonarola seek an armed guard from the Signoria, offering his own services to convey the request with utmost privacy. Savonarola had replied briefly that this was impossible; an armed guard was incompatible with the privacy required. Speaking with a flashing eye, he indicated his intent to accept the risk. Tito himself cared little for the outcome. He managed his affairs so skillfully that, regardless of the result, he expected to benefit. Whether Savonarola fell into the snare, yielding diamonds and papal patronage, or refused, Tito's adroit agency would strengthen his standing with both the Friar and Spini, making him more valuable as an agent of the Mediceans.

However, Spini was an inconvenient colleague. Though cunning enough to enjoy plots, he lacked the ability or self-command necessary for such a complex operation requiring secrecy. He often became intoxicated, for even sober Florence had its "beoni," or drunkards, both lay and clerical, who grew loud in taverns and private banquets. Despite agreements between him and Tito to maintain a cool demeanor in public, there was always the danger that an evening encounter might provoke Spini to burst forth with sudden affability. The delicate signal of casting the *becchetto* over the left shoulder was understood in the morning, but a mere hint might fail to deter a fraternal grasp of the shoulder in the evening.

Tito's chief hope was that Dolfo Spini had not spotted him. This hope would have been well-founded had Spini possessed no clearer view of Tito than Tito had of Spini. Yet, hidden in the shadows, Tito had seen himself illuminated for an instant by the direct rays of the lamp. In his own way, Tito was as distinct a figure as the captain of the *Compagnacci*. Romola's black-shrouded figure had escaped notice, and she now stood behind her husband's shoulder in the corner of the loggia.

Tito was not left to hope long. "Ha! My carrier-pigeon!" grated Spini's harsh voice, intended as an undertone, as his hand seized Tito's shoulder. "What did you run into hiding for? You didn't know it was comrades arriving. It's well I caught sight of you; it saves time. How fares the chase tomorrow morning? Will the bald-headed game rise? Are the falcons ready?"

Had Tito possessed a nature prone to rage, he would have felt it against this bull-faced accomplice, unfit to be either a leader or a tool. His lips turned white, but his agitation stemmed from the pressing difficulty of choosing a safe response. If he tried to hush Spini, it would only deepen Romola's suspicion; she knew him well enough to understand that if a strong alarm were raised in her, she could neither be silenced nor deceived. On the other hand, if he repelled Spini angrily, the wine-soaked *Compagnaccio* might become savage, more ready for retaliation than for reading motives.

He adopted a third course, proving that Romola still held a certain power over him—the power of dread. He pressed her hand, as if intending a signal to her, and said in a good-humored tone of camaraderie, "Yes, my Dolfo, you may prepare in all security. But take no trumpets with you."

"Don't be afraid," Spini said, somewhat piqued. "No need to play the learned scholar with me

Tito's intricate plans had been thwarted by trifling accidents that no calculating mind could foresee. Though he rarely strolled with Romola in the evenings, fate had conspired to place them together precisely on this fateful night, when her presence was utterly inconvenient to his designs. Life proved a labyrinthine game where even the most cunning strategies were undone by random chances, as unpredictable as falling dandelion seeds.

It was not that Tito cared deeply about the collapse of Spini's scheme; rather, he faced a delicate diplomatic dilemma. He needed to warn Savonarola without arousing suspicion among the populace, nor provoke the fierce, unchained wrath of Dolfo Spini. If he rushed to the monastery to alert the friar immediately following the delivery of the forged letters, the timing would inevitably breed unfavorable rumors linking the two events. Conversely, confronting Spini instantly required revealing the truth—that Savonarola had altered his intentions—which Spini would surely interpret as Tito having "changed his mind" and sabotaged the plot himself. By delaying the warning to Savonarola until morning, Tito risked losing the crucial window to inform Spini of the friar's hesitation, leaving the Compagnacci band to return in a fury of disappointed vengeance.

Yet, this was the peril Tito willingly accepted. He trusted his ability to soothe Spini, attributing the failure solely to the Friar's own prudence. Annoyed and irritable, Tito found himself unable to muster a smile—a rare feat for him—and resolved to avoid Romola entirely. Consequently, he did not return home that night.

Inside, Romola remained awake, undressed, staring into the darkness. She listened intently as the rain intensified, finding comfort in the stormy heavens, which seemed to act as a shield against human machinations, forcing men into paralysis. Yet, her peace was shattered by a dual torment: profound doubt regarding her husband's integrity and uncertainty about her own actions. What falsehoods might he have woven? What secret projects lay hidden from her knowledge? To trust Tito was to invite danger; to convince herself otherwise was futile. Was she not acting out of selfish pride by shrinking from the duty to warn others against him?

"If my husband were indeed a criminal, my place would be beside him in prison"—she acknowledged this grim possibility and accepted it. But could a wife stand idly by while her spouse inflicted harm that rendered him a malefactor, when she possessed the power to intervene? Prayer felt impossible; her agitated thoughts refused the quietude necessary for supplication. Her mind raced with morbid intensity, imagining scenarios where Tito would evade her threats. As dawn approached, the rain subsided, the wind scattered the clouds, and a clear morning revealed the world outside. Paradoxically, this clarity made her inner turmoil more unbearable.

Wrapping her mantle tightly, Romola sprinted to the loggia, scanning the vast landscape as if some external sign could dictate her next move. Could she perhaps approach her godfather and urge him to block Fra Girolamo's passage without revealing specific details? But such a measure might arrive too late. Distress gnawed at her as she realized she had gleaned no concrete information from Tito, and the clock had already struck seven. There was no alternative; she must proceed to San Marco.

She descended the stairs rapidly, ignoring the sick patients within, and stepped out onto the street. Moving swiftly toward the Ponte Vecchio via the Via de' Bardi, she cut directly through the heart of the city. The great Piazza offered a slim hope: she might intercept her husband there. Perhaps, by some miracle, he could reassure her of the Friar's safety, rendering her journey to San Marco unnecessary.

Upon reaching the courtyard of the Palazzo Vecchio, she peered eagerly into the pillared space, then scanned the piazza. The familiar figure, once etched in her heart by youthful affection and now scarred by suffering, was absent. Undeterred, she hastened toward the Piazza del Duomo. The square teemed with life: worshippers ascending and descending the marble steps, men pausing for conversation, and vendors laden with goods. Amidst this throng, Romola spotted her husband.

On his way from San Marco, Tito had diverted into Nello's shop and was now leaning casually against the doorpost. As Romola drew nearer, she observed him with shocking nonchalance. He held his cap in one hand, shook back his freshly combed hair, and conversed with an air of effortless ease. The stark contrast between this calm demeanor and the bitter anxieties he had instilled convulsed her with indignation; the fresh vision of his hardness deepened her terror. Standing nearby were Cronaca and two other regulars of San Marco.

A sudden thought seized her: *"I will force him to speak before these witnesses."*

Her light step carried her forward before he could retreat. As Cronaca announced, "Here comes Madonna Romola," Tito felt a sharp jolt as he realized he stood face-to-face with his wife. She appeared haggard from sleepless vigilance, yet her eyes burned with something far more intense than mere worry.

"Has the Frate crossed the gates?" she demanded.

"No," Tito replied, feeling paralyzed before her gaze. He summoned every ounce of self-control to maintain a mask of simple surprise.

"And are you certain he is not departing?" she pressed.

"I am certain he is not going."

"That is sufficient," Romola declared. Turning upward, she ascended the steps to seek refuge within the Duomo, needing solitude to compose herself.

Tito watched her retreat, harboring a sensation of hatred more acute than any he had ever felt. Among the crowd gathered in the square were not only devoted followers of the Friar but also Ser Ceccone, the notary. Like Tito, Ceccone was secretly an agent of the Medici. Unlearned, unattractive, unsuccessful, and devoid of generosity, he was a traitor lacking any redeeming charm. Naturally, he held little affection for Tito Melema.

The afternoon sun was beginning to fail as Tito returned home. In her small chamber, Romola sat opposite the cabinet, her fingers moving over parchment to copy documents. Seeing the light dimming, she prepared to cease her work when her husband entered. He did not go to the hall or the garden; he came straight to her room with a singular, defined purpose. As he stepped inside, Romola noted a profound shift in his demeanor. Without removing his cap or mantle, he leaned an elbow on the cabinet and stood directly before her, gazing at her in silence.

Throughout the day, Romola had been reassured regarding the Friar's safety, yet she now felt the heavy recoil of her own earlier actions. A pang of penitence struck her for the distrust and indignation that had driven her to confront her husband publicly on a matter he deemed private. She convinced herself she had been wrong. The calculated duplicity inherent in Florentine factionalism—a trait even her godfather acknowledged—could easily explain Tito's connection with Spini without implying that Tito intended to advance the conspiracy. She sought to atone for her impulsiveness, hoping that a confession of her haste might break the two-year silence that had locked their hearts away. That silence was absolute; Tito did not know she had fled and returned, nor had they ever dared to speak of the youthful love that had once existed between them, or the violent shock that had shattered it. They treated that past like a banquet hall where death had abruptly ended the feast.

She looked up at him with the submissive gaze born of self-reproach, ready to speak, but a subtle transformation in his face halted her words. For several moments, they stared at one another in a charged quiet. Tito felt, acutely, that a crisis had arrived in his marriage. The husband's latent determination to dominate, usually buried beneath layers of blandness and pleasantry, had surged to the surface. It altered his visage, giving him the appearance of a man secretly strangling something weak yet dangerous within his grip.

"Romola," he began, his voice a cool, liquid tone that sent a shiver through her, "it is time that we should understand each other."

"That is what I most desire, Tito," she replied faintly. Her pale, sweet face, stripped of anger and filled only with the timidity of self-doubt, seemed to highlight the overwhelming dominance of his dark strength.

"You took a step this morning," Tito continued, "which you must now perceive to have been futile—one that exposes you to scrutiny and may entangle me in grave practical difficulties."

"I acknowledge that I was too hasty," Romola said, finding a fuller, firmer tone in her voice. She hoped that expressing her regret would soften his glare, allowing her to say what truly mattered. "I wish you to understand once and for all," Tito added, his voice unchanged, "that such clashes are incompatible with our station as husband and wife. Reflect on how you were led to that step, lest it happen again."

"That depends chiefly on you, Tito," Romola retorted, catching a spark of defiance. It was not exactly what she intended to say, but the distance between them left little room for careful navigation.

"You would say, I suppose," Tito answered smoothly, "that nothing in the future should provoke your unreasonable suspicions. You were frank enough last night to declare you hold no faith in me. I am not surprised by the exaggerated conclusions drawn from slight evidence, but I must warn you of the consequences of using such distortions to interfere in affairs you know nothing of. Are you paying close attention?"

"Yes," Romola flushed, resenting the cold superiority in his tone.

"Well, then, sooner or later, some stray word or incident may set your imagination racing to devise crimes against me. You might rush to the Palazzo Vecchio to alarm the Signoria and stir the entire city. Shall I tell you the outcome? Not merely the disgrace of your husband—which you anticipate with such courage—but the arrest and ruin of many leading men in Florence, including Messer Bernardo del Nero."

Tito had meditated a decisive blow, and he had delivered it. The flush drained from Romola's face until her lips were pale—a rare sight, for she was seldom afraid. Tito sensed his victory.

"You might flatter yourself," he pressed on, "that you are performing a heroic act of deliverance. You might as well attempt to pick locks with flowery language as apply such notions to the politics of Florence. The question is no longer whether you can believe in me, but whether, now that you are warned, you will dare to rush, like a blind man brandishing a torch, into intricate matters beyond your comprehension."

Romola felt her mind gripped in a vice by Tito's logic; the dangers he outlined rose before her with terrifying clarity.

"I am too rash," she admitted. "I will strive not to be rash."

"Remember," Tito insisted relentlessly, "that your act of distrust this morning could have had far more fatal consequences than the sacrifice of your husband, a fate you have learned to contemplate without flinching."

"Tito, it is not so!" Romola burst out, rising and approaching him with a desperate resolve to speak her truth. "It is false that I would willingly sacrifice you. It has been the greatest struggle of my life to remain by your side. I fled in anger two years ago, yet I returned because I was bound to you more than to anything on earth. But it is useless. You shut me out from your thoughts. You treat me as too irrational to share in your affairs. You will reveal nothing."

She appeared like a guardian angel pleading with him, bending toward him with wide, imploring eyes and laying her hand upon his arm. Yet, Romola's touch and gaze no longer stirred a single fiber of tenderness in her husband. The good-natured, tolerant Tito, capable of little hatred or impatience, accustomed to gentleness toward the world, felt himself growing strangely hard toward this wife who had once been his strongest influence. Beneath his soft disposition lay a masculine intellectual power and purpose, sharp-edged and energetic, working steadily without needing great momentum. Romola possessed her own energy, one that thwarted his, and no man of ordinary weakness endures being thwarted by his wife. Marriage, he seemed to feel, must be either a union of sympathy or a contest of conquest.

No emotion crossed Tito's face as he heard her mention her departure two years prior. Only his lips tightened slightly as he offered a faint smile.

"My Romola, when certain conditions are established, we must accept them. No amount of wishing can fill the Arno river, as your people say, nor turn a plum into an orange. I have observed that prayer holds little efficacy in such matters. You are constituted to possess strong impressions inaccessible to reason; I cannot share these impressions, and consequently, you have withdrawn your trust from me. You have changed toward me, and thus I have changed toward you. There is no use looking backward. We must simply adapt ourselves to these altered conditions."

"Tito, it would not be useless for us to speak openly," Romola said, her voice trembling with the frustration of pushing living flesh against a lifeless, immovable wall. "It was the sense of deception in you that drove me away, keeping us apart. And it is untrue that I changed first. You changed toward me the night you first donned that chain-mail armor. You had a secret from me—concerning that old man—and I saw him again yesterday."

In a tone of agonized entreaty, she pleaded, "Tito, if you would once tell me everything, whatever it may be, I would not mind the pain. Let there be no wall between us! Is it not possible that we could begin a new life?"

For the first time, a flash of emotion crossed Tito's face. He stood utterly still, the flash seeming to whiten him. Ignoring Romola's appeal, he paused briefly before speaking quietly.

"Your impetuosity regarding trifles, Romola, possesses a freezing influence that would chill the baths of Nero."

At these stinging words, Romola recoiled, drawing herself up into her customary pose of self-possession. Tito continued.

"If by 'that old man' you mean the mad Jacopo di Nola, who attempted my life and made a strange accusation against me—which I withheld from you because it would have alarmed you to no avail—he, poor wretch, has died in prison. I saw his name listed among the dead."

"I know nothing of his accusation," Romola stated. "But I know he is the man I saw with a rope around his neck in the Duomo—the man whose portrait Piero di Cosimo painted, clutching your arm just as he saw him seize it the day the French entered, the day you first wore the armor."

"And where is he now, pray?" Tito asked, remaining pale but controlling himself.

"He was lying lifeless in the street from starvation," Romola replied. "I revived him with bread and wine. I brought him to our door, but he refused entry. I gave him some money, and he departed without telling me anything. However, he discovered that I was your wife. Who is he?"

"A man, half-mad and half-imbecile, who was once my father's servant in Greece. He harbors a rancorous hatred against me because I dismissed him for theft. Now you possess the whole mystery, along with the further satisfaction of knowing I am once again in peril of assassination. The fact that I wear armor, which seems to trouble you greatly, must have led you to infer I am in danger from this man

The winter days drifted past for Romola with the same silent, relentless rhythm as the white ships passing a lone watcher on the shore. Each vessel carried a hidden cargo of impending change, though the movement was marked only by a uniform quietude. Tito's earlier warning had infused her curiosity regarding public affairs with a deepening dread, leading her to prefer ignorance over knowledge. While the menacing German Emperor had vanished and Florence's immediate political situation had eased, a profound distress lingered. Consequently, Romola's active duties showed little diminution, serving once again as a sanctuary for her troubled mind.

She hesitated to celebrate the relief that had arrived at the brink of disaster—a relief that seemingly validated the policies of the *Piagnoni*. Indeed, Francesco Valori, the fiery leader of the faction, had been elected Gonfaloniere at the start of the year and was eager to implement his brand of liberty within his brief two-month tenure. On the surface, this appeared to strengthen the party most devoted to freedom and to bolster the protection afforded to Savonarola. Yet, Romola remained acutely sensitive to any sign that suggested the present stability was merely an unconscious incubation of mixed seeds destined to sprout tragedy. By late February, near the height of Carnival, her premonition was confirmed: the Medici family had ceased their passive stance and were actively maneuvering to secure the election of Bernardo del Nero as the new Gonfaloniere.

On the final day of Carnival, between ten and eleven in the morning, Romola departed toward the Corso degli Albizzi, fulfilling a promise to meet her cousin Brigida. Together, they planned to leave the Via de' Bardi early in the afternoon to occupy a window reserved by Tito in the Piazza della Signoria. They awaited a spectacle of unprecedented nature, one that demanded the attention of every Florentine eye. The *Piagnoni* were enforcing their vision of Carnival with absolute rigor. Despite the efforts of Dolfo Spini and his associates to revive traditional masques and bawdy jests seasoned with indecency, such frivolities were deemed incompatible with a city that had proclaimed Christ its King.

Romola set forth with a languid demeanor, typical of anyone undertaking a long day of sightseeing solely to please a cherished child or friend. The day undoubtedly marked an epoch in the history of Carnival observance, yet this phase of reform failed to ignite her enthusiasm. Unaware of it, she stood on the precipice of another epoch in her own life—one where a different destiny would become visibly intertwined with hers. Choosing to traverse the vast Piazza alone, she sought a preliminary survey of the extraordinary sight unfolding before her.

Entering from the south, her gaze fell upon something monstrous and kaleidoscopic: a structure resembling a pyramid, or more accurately, a colossal fir-tree standing sixty feet high. Its branches widened into shelves as they descended, expanding until the base circled eighty yards. The square teemed with life. Slender youths clad in white garments, olive wreaths adorning their heads, moved to and fro around the pyramid's foundation, carrying baskets brimming with vibrant colors. Beside them, mature figures—some in monastic robes, others in the loose tunics and dark-red caps of artists—assisted, examined, or retreated to vantage points to take in the wondrous totality. Among a distant group standing on the marble steps of Orgagna's Loggia, Romola recognized Piero di Cosimo, whose posture betrayed a mixture of discontent and scorn.

As she approached, she paused to inspect the myriad objects arranged in graded rows from the base to the summit. There were tapestries and brocades of immodest design; paintings and sculptures suspected of inciting vice. Boards and tables for every conceivable game lay alongside playing cards, printing blocks, dice, and other gambling paraphernalia. Worldly music books and instruments of all kinds—lutes, drums, cymbals, trumpets—were displayed prominently. Masks and costumes from the old Carnival traditions hung nearby. Handbound copies of Ovid, Boccaccio, Petrarch, Pulci, and other works deemed vain or impure lined the shelves. Then came the implements of feminine vanity: pots of rouge, false hairpieces, mirrors, perfumes, powders, and sheer veils designed to invite lecherous glances. Finally, crowning the entire edifice, sat the unflattering effigy of a mythical Venetian merchant, rumored to have paid a heavy price for this collection of "marketable abominations." Soaring above him in grotesque ugliness was the symbolic figure of the ancient, debauched Carnival.

This arrangement served as the preparation for a novel form of bonfire: the Burning of Vanities. Concealed within the pyramid's hollow core was a substantial stockpile of dry fuel and gunpowder. At dusk, on this final day of the festival, the pile was to be ignited to the blare of trumpets, sending the detested remnants of the old Carnival tumbling into the flames amidst songs of reformist triumph.

Such a grand gesture could scarcely have been orchestrated without a unique organization initiated by Savonarola two years prior. The masses of Florentine boys and youths were no longer left to their natural inclinations toward street mischief and crude dissoluteness. Guided by Fra Domenico, Savonarola's lieutenant, these lads and striplings—the hope of Florence—were instructed to speak only pure words. Their zeal was directed toward an "Unseen Good" that would shame the lukewarmness of their elders, restricting their pleasures to those of an angelic nature: singing divine praises and walking in white robes. High benches erected against the walls of the Duomo were reserved for them, where they had learned to listen to Savonarola, who addressed them as the future glory of a city divinely appointed to execute God's work.

These fresh-faced troops were the primary agents of the regenerated merriment of the new Carnival, a sacred parody of the old. Where once there had been bonfires, there would now be a fire consuming impurity from the earth. Where symbolic processions had existed, there were now processions featuring white robes, red crosses, and olive wreaths—emblems of peace and innocent joy—with banners proclaiming the triumphs of goodness. Instead of dancing in rings beneath the open sky to the sound of choral voices singing licentious songs, there would be dancing in a circle involving monks and laity united in fraternal love and divine joy, accompanied by hymns.

Collections from street passersby were expected to exceed previous totals, not to fund lavish feasts, but to aid the hungry and needy. Furthermore, there was the collection of the *Anathema*, or the Vanities, destined for the great pyramidal bonfire. Regiments of youthful inquisitors patrolled the streets, urging households to surrender their forbidden items. Perhaps, after yielding the obvious vanities, the mistress of the house still possessed small, reddened balls imported from the Levant, meant to create a deceptive flush of artificial beauty on a pale cheek? If so, she was urged to bring them down and cast them into the basket of doom. Or perhaps she kept ringlets and coils of "dead hair"? Then she was to bring them to the street door, holding them in her hands rather than wearing them, publicly renouncing the *Anathema* that masked the signs of age with a ghastly mockery of youth. In return, she would hear fresh, young voices pronounce a blessing upon her and her home.

The beardless inquisitors, organized into little battalions, undoubtedly embraced this task with eagerness. Coercing people through shame or spiritual pressure into relinquishing possessions they might resent losing proved to be a form of piety easily adopted by the boyish mind. Even when obstinate sinners threatened whips or cudgels, the excitement persisted. Savonarola himself seemed aware of the difficulties inherent in training these children, balancing noble aspirations with an imperfect grasp of means, often resorting to supernatural constraints as the only sure hope. The Florentine youth had harbored vicious habits and foul tongues; initially, it seemed an unmitigated blessing when they shouted "*Viva Gesù!*" However, Savonarola eventually confessed from the pulpit that there was "too much shouting." He warned that the constant repetition of sacred words risked bringing them into contempt, ordering a cessation of the clamor until the next feast.

Nevertheless, the sight of the long stream of robed youthfulness, adorned with little red crosses and olive wreaths, proceeding to the Duomo at dawn to receive communion from Savonarola's hands was undeniably beautiful. Doubtless, many of these young souls stored away memories of hope and awe that would protect them from viewing their civic duties through a merely vulgar lens. No conscious obedience represents a step backward from lawlessness, and these boys evolved into the generation of men who fought bravely and endured greatly in the Republic's final struggle.

In the intervening hours between early communion and dinner, the youths engaged in their final rounds to collect alms and vanities. This was the reason Romola observed the slender white figures moving about the base of the great pyramid.

"What think you of this folly, Madonna Romola?" a brusque voice asked close to her ear.

"Your *Piagnoni* will make Hell a pleasant prospect for us if they continue to carry matters their own way on earth," the speaker continued. "It takes a cudgel to haul over the mountains to see painters like Lorenzo di Credi and young Baccio assisting in the burning of color out of life in this manner."

"My good Piero," Romola replied, looking up and smiling at the grim man, "even you must find solace in seeing some of these things

Another figure, easily recognizable to us, approached the Piazza that morning. Unlike the mourners draped in black, she wore the vibrant hues of the old days—red, green, and white—as she prepared to witness the Carnival. She hailed from a different direction entirely; Tessa no longer resided on the Hill of San Giorgio. Following the tragic events involving Baldassarre there, Tito had deemed it prudent to relocate her, citing various reasons, yet he ensured her new home remained within a quiet, airy quarter. She now lived in a house bordering the expansive gardens north of the Porta Santa Croce.

Tessa had not ventured out sightseeing without explicit permission. The evening prior, Tito had accompanied her. She had held back the plea swelling in her heart and throat until she witnessed him in a state of radiant ease. With one arm around the sturdy Lillo and the other resting gently on her shoulder, he helped steady the tiny Ninna on her legs. Tessa observed that the weariness which had previously caused him to collapse into his chair upon entering had completely vanished from his brow and lips.

Tessa was quick to learn the subtle stratagems required to avoid vexing Naldo while securing a measure of her own desires. Though she could read nothing else, she had mastered the art of reading her husband's face. Indeed, the charm of that bright, gentle-humored Tito—who had awakened beneath the Loggia de' Cerchi on a Lenten morning five years ago, offering no hostages to deceit—had never returned so fully as it did in the person of Naldo, seated in the straight-backed, carved armchair provided for his comfort during visits to Tessa and the children.

Tito himself was surprised by the growing sense of relief he felt in these moments. No guile was necessary toward Tessa; she was too ignorant and too innocent to suspect him of anything. Furthermore, the little voices calling him "Babbo" were sweet in his ears for the brief duration he heard them. When contemplating leaving Florence, he never imagined abandoning Tessa and the little ones. He cherished these round-cheeked, wide-eyed creatures who clung to him, knowing no evil. Wherever affection springs, it resembles the green leaf and the blossom—pure and breathing purity, regardless of the soil in which it grows.

Poor Romola, despite her self-sacrificing efforts, was inadvertently hardening Tito's nature, chilling it with a positive dislike that had previously seemed impossible. Yet, Tessa kept the fountains of kindness flowing.

"Ninna is very good without me now," Tessa began, feeling her request rise high in her throat. She allowed Ninna to sit on the floor. "I can leave her with Monna Lisa anytime. If she cries in the cradle, Lillo is as sensible as can be; he goes and thumps Monna Lisa."

Lillo, whose great dark eyes appeared even darker against his light-brown curls (inherited from his mother), jumped off Babbo's knee. He immediately demonstrated his intelligence by thumping Monna Lisa, who sat shaking her head slowly over her spinning wheel at the other end of the room.

"A wonderful boy!" Tito laughed.

"Isn't he?" Tessa asked eagerly, moving slightly closer. "And might I go to see the Carnival tomorrow, just for an hour or two? Mightn't I?"

"Oh, you wicked pigeon!" Tito replied, pinching her cheek. "Those are your longings, are they? What have you to do with carnivals now that you are an old woman with two children?"

"But old women like to see things," Tessa said, her lower lip trembling slightly. "Monna Lisa said she would like to go, too, but she is so deaf she cannot hear what is behind her, and she fears we cannot manage both children."

"No, indeed, Tessa," Tito said, his expression turning grave. "You must not think of taking the children into the crowded streets, or I shall be angry."

"But I have never been into the Piazza without leave since Holy Saturday," Tessa pleaded in a frightened tone. "I think Nofri is dead; you know the poor *madre* died. I shall never forget the Carnival I saw once—it was so pretty, all roses and a king and queen beneath them, with singing. I liked it better than San Giovanni."

"There is nothing like that now, my Tessa," Tito replied. "They intend to make a bonfire in the Piazza—that is all. But I cannot allow you to go out alone in the evening."

"Oh no, no! I do not wish to go in the evening. I only want to see the procession by daylight. There *will* be a procession, is it not true?"

"Yes, after a sort," Tito conceded. "As lively as a flight of cranes. Do not expect roses and glittering kings and queens, my Tessa. However, I suppose any string of people to be called a procession will please your blue eyes. There is a structure they have raised in the Piazza de' Signori for the bonfire; you may like to see that. But come home early, and look like a grave little old woman. If you see any men with feathers and swords, keep out of their way; they are very fierce and like to cut old women's heads off."

"Santa Madonna! Where do they come from?" Tessa exclaimed, realizing he was joking. "Ah! You are laughing; it is not so bad. But I will keep away from them. Only..." She leaned forward in a whisper, placing her lips near Naldo's ear. "If I might take Lillo with me! He is very sensible."

"But who will thump Monna Lisa then, if she does not hear?" Tito asked, struggling to maintain a serious demeanor.

"No, Tessa, you could not take care of Lillo in a crowd, and he is too heavy for you to carry," Tito insisted.

"It is true," Tessa said, somewhat sadly. "He likes to run away. I forgot that. Then I will go alone. But now look at Ninna—you have not looked at her enough."

Ninna was a blue-eyed creature of the tottering, tumbling age—a fair solid object that, like a loaded die, found its base with a constancy warranting prediction. Tessa snatched her up. While Babbo paid due attention to Ninna's recent teeth and other marvels, Tessa whispered, "And shall I buy some confetti for the children?"

Tito drew small coins from his *scarsella* and poured them into her palm.

"That will buy no end," Tessa said, delighted by the abundance. "I shall not mind going without Lillo so much if I bring him something."

Thus, Tessa set out in the morning toward the great Piazza where the bonfire was to be erected. She did not consider the February breeze cold enough to require more than her green woolen dress. A mantle would have been oppressive, hiding a new necklace and a new clasp mounted with silver—the only ornamental gifts Tito had ever bestowed upon her. Tessa did not think of displaying her figure, for no one had ever told her it was pretty; however, she was certain her necklace and clasp were among the finest ever worn by the wealthiest peasant woman. She arranged her white hood so that the front of her necklace might be prominently displayed. In her mind, these ornaments must inspire respect, signaling that she was the wife of someone capable of affording such luxuries.

She tripped along cheerfully in the February sunshine, thinking mostly of the treats for the little ones to fill her small basket, oblivious to anyone watching her. Yet, her descent from her upper story into the street had been observed. She was being kept in sight by a person who had often waited in vain to confirm if Tessa indeed lived in the house to which he had once dogged Tito.

Baldassarre carried a package of yarn. Constantly employed in this manner to earn his scanty bread and keep the sacred fire of vengeance alive, he had come out of his way this morning, as he often did, merely to pass the house where he had followed Tito in the evenings. His long imprisonment had intensified his timid suspicion and his belief in a diabolic fortune favoring Tito. He dared not pursue him openly, fearing that if Tito's eyes fell upon him, he would again be subjected to obloquy, dragged away, stripped of his weapon, and cast helpless into a prison cell. His fierce purpose had become as stealthy as a serpent, depending on a single dart of the fang for its prey. Justice was weak and unfriended; he could no longer hear the voice promising vengeance echoing in the Duomo. He had visited it repeatedly, but that voice seemed stifled by cunning, strong-armed wickedness.

For a long while, Baldassarre's sole objective was to ascertain whether Tito still wore armor, for now his fainting hope would have been contented with a successful stab before the grave. Yet, he would never risk his precious knife again. The knowledge that the sharp steel was useless, and that he had no hope but in some new device, weighed heavily on his enfeebled mind. A dim vision of winning one of the two wives to aid him continually formed and dissolved before him. The wife who had lived on the hill was no longer there. If he could find her again, he might grasp a thread of a project and work his way toward clarity. This morning, he had succeeded. He was certain of her location. As he walked, bent slightly under his burden of yarn yet keeping the green-and-white figure in sight, his mind dwelt upon her and

When Romola mentioned that someone awaited her, she referred to her cousin, Brigida. Yet, she had no inkling of the profound crisis in which that kindred spirit found herself. As they walked back toward the Piazza, they spotted a gathering of youths halting before Tessa. Upon seeing the simple peasant girl's distress, Romola stepped closer and said, "Wait a moment, cousin."

Monna Brigida, however, reacted with haste. "Ah, I cannot proceed," she exclaimed breathlessly. "Come with me to Boni's shop; I shall return there."

The truth was, Monna Brigida was torn between two forces. On one hand, she clung to the "vanities" adorning her person; on the other, a growing terror seized her that the Piagnoni—the penitent friars—were correct in their assertion that rouge, false hair, and pearl embroidery damaged the soul. Their stern worldview hung in the air like a pervasive odor; nothing tasted or felt as it once had. Here stood her dear child, Romola, youthful and beautiful, living a life that demanded rigorous self-denial. For a widow of fifty-five whose happiness had long been derived from her appearance and the admiration of others, maintaining buoyancy required immense imagination. Monna Brigida had recently begun to struggle violently with her toilette. Was it truly worthwhile to apply rouge and braid her hair if her soul needed to flourish without them?

But when she raised her hand-mirror, she saw a sallow face with sagging cheeks and crow's-feet that no amount of lip-smirking could conceal. When she parted her grey hair to wear it simply in the Piagnone style, her courage evaporated. Monna Berta, though only fifty-two, possessed a sharp tongue and a superior demeanor that would surely reduce her to laughter, calling her an old hag. Furthermore, every non-Piagnone woman would sneer, and men would address her as if she were their grandmother. At fifty-five, she reasoned, she was not ancient; she merely needed a touch of makeup. Thus, the rouge, the braids, and the embroidered *berretta* remained, and she was pleased with the familiar effect. Her neck, usually hidden because she feared it looked too aged, appeared finer against the backdrop of necklaces, surpassing even Monna Berta's.

That very day, while preparing for the Piagnone Carnival, these internal conflicts had become unbearable. Driven by fear and longing, she had turned back to seek refuge in the druggist's shop rather than face the collectors of the Anathema without Romola's support. However, her retreat was not swift enough. Even before she turned, she had been spotted by the white-robed boys trailing behind the crowd that had circled Tessa. Perhaps their eagerness to seize Tessa stemmed partly from the fact that they had already cornered another figure bearing the visible weight of the Anathema.

Several of these youths were among the youngest recruits, trained in peculiar fervor. A small boy of ten, wearing an olive wreath over cherubic cheeks and wide brown eyes, possessed an imagination overwhelmed by the looming consequences of morality. Despite his desire for mischief, he was the first to intercept Monna Brigida, placing himself squarely in her path. She felt anger rising, searching for an exit, but none was available; fleeing now would only worsen her plight.

Yet, it was not the cherubic boy who spoke first. It was a youth of fifteen gripping the handles of a wide basket.

"Venerable mother!" he declared. "The Blessed Jesus commands you to surrender the Anathema you carry. That cap embroidered with pearls, those jewels securing your false hair—give them up and sell them for the poor. Cast the hair itself away, for it is a lie fit only for the fire. Doubtless, beneath your silk mantle, you harbor other treasures."

"Yes, lady," agreed the youth at the opposite handle, reciting Fra Girolamo's phrases with practiced ease. "They are too heavy for you. Heavier than a millstone, weighing you down toward perdition. Will you decorate yourself with the hunger of the poor and boast of carrying God's curse upon your brow?"

"In truth, you are old, *buona madre*," chimed in the little boy, his voice a sweet soprano. "You appear frightfully ugly with the red on your cheeks, that glossy black hair, and those fine trinkets. Only Satan delights in your sight. Your Angel grieves; He wishes you to wipe away the red."

Snatching a soft silk scarf from the basket, the boy held it out for her to use as her guardian angel commanded. Monna Brigida's anger and humiliation rapidly gave way to spiritual panic. Monna Berta and the cloud of fashionable onlookers were nowhere to be seen. Instead, she was surrounded by young monitors in white robes, wreaths, and red crosses, their terrifying candor radiating an awful solemnity. Her Franciscan confessor, Fra Cristoforo, was absent to bolster her skepticism of the Dominicans, leaving her helpless before a vague, supreme warning. With no other option presenting itself, she accepted the offered scarf and trembled as she wiped her cheeks in submissive obedience.

"It is well, madonna," said the second youth. "A holy beginning. Once you remove these vanities from your head, the dew of heavenly grace shall descend upon it."

As the spirit of mischief grew within him, the boy reached for one of the jeweled pins fastening her braids to the *berretta*. He pulled it free. The heavy black plait fell forward, dragging the rest of her headgear down over Monna Brigida's face. This became the final catalyst; she raised her hands frantically, undone the remaining fastenings, and tossed her beloved crimson-velvet *berretta*, with its unparalleled embroidery of seed-pearls, into the basket of doom. She stood revealed as an unrouged woman, grey hair swept back from a face where the deep lines of age had triumphed over her former fullness.

However, the *berretta* was not permitted to rest in the basket. With impish zeal, the youngsters lifted it and held it up mercilessly, letting the false hair dangle.

"Behold, venerable mother," proclaimed the taller youth, "the ugly lies from which you have freed yourselves! Now you resemble the Blessed Saint Anna, mother of the Holy Virgin."

Thoughts of immediate entry into a convent, never again to show her face to the world, raced through Monna Brigida's mind. Nothing remained but the care of her soul. Of course, spectators laughed; she did not need to glance around to confirm it. Perhaps it would be better to be forced to contemplate Paradise. Yet, at the realization that her cherished, accustomed world was no longer an option, hard tears gathered in her elderly eyes, blurring her vision. She saw only vaguely a large, rough hand clutching a red cross, thrust suddenly before her over the boys' shoulders, accompanied by a strong, guttural voice:

"Only four *quattrini*, madonna, blessing included! Purchase it. You will find comfort in it now that your wig is gone. *Deh!* What do we sinners do all our lives? Make soup in a basket and receive only the scum for our bellies. Better buy a blessing, madonna! Only four *quattrini*; the profit is less than the scent of a single coin, and it goes to the poor."

Dim-eyed and confused, Monna Brigida moved toward the further submission of extracting money from her embroidered purse, currently concealed by her silk mantle. Just then, the circle around her—which she had not yet considered escaping—parted before a figure as welcoming as an angel releasing prison bolts.

"Romola, look at me!" cried Monna Brigida in a piteous tone, extending both hands.

The white-robed troop was already drifting away, aware that their excessive zeal regarding the headgear warranted a dispensation from demanding further penitential offerings.

"My dear cousin, do not be distressed," said Romola, struck with pity yet unable to suppress a smile at the sudden transformation of her kinswoman into a genuine, natural guise, so strikingly different from her usual appearance. She removed the black drapery from her own head and draped it over Monna Brigida's.

"There," she soothed. "No one will notice you now. Let us turn down the Via del Palagio and go straight home."

They hurried away, Monna Brigida clutching Romola's hand tightly, seeking a firmer assurance of her presence.

"Oh, my Romola, my dear child!" called the short, stout woman, hurrying with frequent steps to match the majestic stride of her relative. "What an old scarecrow I am! I must be good—I mean to be good!"

"Yes, yes; buy a cross!" echoed the guttural voice. The rough hand thrust the red cross forward once more, for Bratti was not daunted by Romola's presence into abandoning a potential customer. He had quietly trailed their retreat.

"Only four *quattrini*, blessing and all—and if there were any profit, it would all go to the poor."

Even in a less submissive mood, Monna Brigida would have been compelled to pause. Raising one hand deprecatingly to stop Romola's protest, she reached out with the other, offering a *grosso* worth many white *quattrini* in an entreating tone.

"Take it, good man, and depart."

"You

To Romola, the events of that Carnival day offered little more than a renewed duty: the tender, exhausting task of supporting her poor cousin Brigida, who was resigning herself to the gray hairs and frailty of age. Yet, these domestic cares merely set the stage for a Lenten season defined by a feverish intensity of mental exertion. With the election of Bernardo del Nero as Gonfaloniere, the Medicean faction had secured a significant, albeit precarious, victory. This triumph, however, did not bring relief; rather, it deepened Romola's foreboding. She sensed a shadowy scheme brewing beneath the surface, one poised to mature within the next two months of her godfather's authority into either glorious success or catastrophic betrayal.

Every morning, as the pale dawn pierced her curtains, that haunting fear returned to haunt her. It accompanied her through the silent streets on her way to the early sermon in the Duomo. Yet, once inside the cathedral, amidst the throng of worshippers, the chill of dread evaporated, much as the terror of death fades in the heat of battle. Here, in the vast nave, she felt herself embroiled in a conflict of passion that transcended the walls of Florence. Savonarola was preaching—the final series of Lenten sermons permitted before the threat of exclamation became reality. He spoke with a terrifying clarity, holding up the rotting state of the Church in a mirror of unflinching truth, refusing polite euphemisms. With growing confidence, he proclaimed the advent of a great renovation, a moment when humanity would rise in revolt against corruption.

His own fate seemed to dance between two visions: sometimes he saw himself as the trumpet of a glorious revolution, shaking the very foundations of the Church until it rose anew like Lazarus; at other times, he foresaw only persecution and martyrdom, viewing this earthly life as a mere vigil preceding the true dawn. Such a stance possessed a magnetic gravity for all but the dull-minded, even for Machiavelli, who might have dismissed the Friar's character as merely ambitious. But for Romola, whose own fervor convinced her of Savonarola's genuine nobility, the crisis felt intimately personal. It blended with her daily labours as an exalting memory, demanding not just perseverance, but a new, fierce courage.

Famine still stalked Florence, and the prolonged suffering had hardened the hearts of the populace. Disease festered in the crowded streets, and the plague loomed on the horizon. Walking among the sick, the starving, and the muttering crowds, Romola found solace not merely in pity, but in the belief that she served a heroic struggle toward sublime ends. Her acts of kindness were but dew falling on weedy ground, preparing for a harvest she could not yet see.

Yet, the mighty music that swelled in the Duomo was not without dissonance. Since the early days of glowing hope—when the Friar preached peace, charity, and the coming of a French savior—conditions had shifted violently. Political intrigue refused to yield; the dream of French deliverance had collapsed; and hostility, both petty and grand, attacked the Prophet with renewed ferocity. Consequently, the spirit of contention and self-defense seeped into his sermons. Urged by popular demand, he began to rely increasingly on visions and private revelations, adopting a tone of defiant arrogance. Having once denied the necessity of miracles, he now claimed that Divine power would soon validate his prophecy through a sign.

In these rapid shifts of excited emotion, as the vision of triumph faded behind the grim reality of evil, threats of vengeance against tyrants and corrupt priests gained momentum, fueled by personal exasperation as much as righteous zeal. In the career of a great orator yielding to the moment, the hidden conflict of selfish and unselfish emotions erupts into open flame. These jarring notes disturbed Romola, though they thrilled others.

Among them was Baldassarre. Having discovered that the "wonderful Frate" was preaching again, he attended every sermon, drinking in the threats of a voice that sounded like justice incarnate. His motivation was partly admiration, but largely surveillance; he watched for the precise moment when external circumstances and his own mental state aligned to permit an interview with Romola. Twice he had caught her gaze in the Duomo—once when his dark stare locked onto hers. She wished to avoid him, yet she awaited his return like a portent, though any revelation regarding this old man now seemed secondary to her pressing anxieties.

As the stirring Lent drew to a close and April neared its end, nothing had materialized to fulfill her fears. Rumors of Piero de' Medici's machinations from Rome had circulated, but Bernardo's tenure was nearing expiration. Just as Romola tried to steel herself against these futile terrors, a messenger arrived on the twenty-seventh, bearing a summons from Camilla Rucellai, one of Florence's leading female seers. The matter was described as of the "highest moment."

Romola recoiled instinctively. She despised the shrill volubility of these illuminated women, and her aversion to Camilla was heightened by reports that the prophetess had recently announced revelations hostile to Bernardo del Nero. Initially, she intended to refuse outright, fearing that further knowledge would only weigh upon her spirit. However, her habitual discipline quickly overrode her impulse. Blaming her hesitation on cowardice and selfishness, she complied and proceeded directly to Camilla's residence.

She found the nervous, gray-haired woman in a chamber stripped bare, resembling a convent cell. When Camilla seized her thin hands and addressed her in a loud whisper, Romola physically shrank, finding it difficult to remain seated. Camilla delivered a vision: her Angel had revealed that Romola possessed dangerous secrets concerning her godfather, Bernardo del Nero. If disclosed, these secrets could save the Republic.
