As Camilla narrated, her voice climbed in pitch, urging Romola to obey her Angel and sever ties with the "enemy of God."

Romola's nature was massive and grounded; usually calm and self-controlled, she harbored a constitutional disgust for the shallow excitability of women like Camilla, whose faculties seemed entirely consumed by fantasy. Before the exhortation concluded, Romola sprang up, attempting to wrench her arm free. It was useless. Clutching her like a crab, Camilla only grew more frantic, launching into a cascade of corroborating visions. Christ Himself had appeared, commanding citizens to cast Bernardo del Nero from the window of the Palazzo Vecchio. Even Fra Girolamo, she claimed, knew of this divine mandate and dared not deny its authenticity.

"And since then," Camilla cried, her treble voice straining upward toward Romola's face with wild eyes, "the Blessed Infant has placed a wafer of sweetness on my tongue, confirming that I have done His will!"

"Let me go!" Romola roared, her voice deep with anger. "God grant you are mad! Else you are detestably wicked!"

The violence of her struggle finally broke Camilla's grip. The prophetess released her, and Romola fled the room, rushing out into the street without pausing until she reached the church of the Badia. She slipped behind the curtain under the ancient stone arch, entering a sanctuary shielded from the city's clamor, where every object whispered of eternal peace amidst turmoil. Sinking to her knees before Filippino Lippi's serene depiction of the Virgin visiting Saint Bernard, she hoped the inward tumult would subside.

The thought that plagued her most acutely was that Camilla might accuse Savonarola of sanctioning this wicked folly. Romola did not believe the Friar had approved casting Bernardo from the window; she was certain there was falsehood or error in that claim. While Savonarola had grown severe in his condemnation of malcontents, the principles of strict law and order were foundational to his political teaching. So why, knowing the potentially fatal impact of such visions, did he not publicly denounce them? Why did he not expose these pretenders who brought darkness instead of light?

The answer struck her with painful clarity: he was fettered internally by the realization that such revelations were inseparable from his own visionary experiences, and externally by the fear that condemning them would alienate his own followers, painting him as a suppressor of Divine inspiration—or worse, implying that his own confidant, Fra Salvestro, was merely a lesser vessel of the same power.

Kneeling with her face buried in the altar steps, Romola endured a sickening moment. The enthusiasm that had previously been her sole source of strength now seemed inextricably linked to vain dreams and willful blindness. Her mind drifted back to the sturdy worldly sense, the dignified prudence, and the untheoretic virtues of her godfather. He was being treated as a sacrificial lamb (Agag) for holding a restricted form of government and refusing to sever ties with the banished Medici family. Yet, this loyalty sparked a fresh presentiment: perhaps a plot existed to restore the Medici. Was the popular party's suspicion unjustified?

Again, she affirmed that keeping Florence's government pure was a sacred cause; the Friar was right here, and she had carried her understanding irrevocably with him. But at this moment, her intellectual assent was given unwillingly. Her heart recoiled from a righteousness allied with such narrowness, a system that judged men by superficial symbols rather than their inner humanity. Her affection and respect clung tenaciously to her godfather, linking him to the cherished memories of her father, who also opposed the crude division of mankind into sheep and goats based on political or religious labels.

Ideas require the solvent of feeling to move

"I would speak with you," Baldassarre said, breaking the silence as Romola regarded him with quiet anticipation. It was evident he had trailed her, waiting patiently for this moment. Finally, she intended to uncover the secret that weighed upon him.

"Yes," she replied, offering a submission akin to one accepting a penance imposed by authority. "Yet you wish to go where no ear can catch our words?"

"Where *he* will not stumble upon us," Baldassarre corrected, turning his head to glance nervously over his shoulder. "Out—into the open air—far from the crowded streets."

"I occasionally visit San Miniato at this hour," Romola offered. "If you wish, I shall go now, and you may follow. The journey is long, yet it offers solitude."

He nodded his agreement. Romola set off immediately. To lesser minds, venturing to a secluded spot with a man displaying the outward signs of the madness Tito had described might seem terrifying. But Romola possessed no such personal fears; instead, she welcomed the distance, viewing it as a necessary buffer before the next blow struck her spirit.

By late afternoon, the sun dipping low in the western sky, Romola paused on rough terrain beneath the shadows of towering cypress trunks, scanning for Baldassarre. He was close, yet when he finally joined her, he collapsed onto the edge of a stony bank, utterly spent. The thickset frame that had once displayed sturdy vigor during his initial appearance in the Duomo, bound in ropes, now bore the marks of decay. Even the brief exertion of climbing the hill had left his eyes clouded with a helpless vagueness.

"The ascent is steep," Romola observed, her tone soft with compassion as she sat beside him. "Do you fear you have been weakened by hunger?"

He turned his gaze toward her, silent and unable to formulate the words he sought. She remained perfectly still, terrified that any sign of impatience might shatter his fragile composure. In this light, he resembled nothing more than a neglected, ordinary old man. Yet, Romola was accustomed to the proximity of the suffering and often pondered their hidden burdens. Slowly, a clarity returned to his stare.

"Aha!" he exclaimed abruptly, his voice gaining weight. "You would have been my daughter!"

A swift crimson flush swept across Romola's cheeks and vanished instantly, leaving her lips parted in a pale, marble-like expression of horror. The revelation crashed over her mind. From that single phrase, she deduced the truth lurking behind the years of silence. Driven by her acute understanding of Tito's character, her impulse was immediate and unguarded. Her emotional reaction served as fuel for Baldassarre; for the first time, his words struck their intended mark.

With growing eagerness and resolve, he placed a heavy hand upon her arm. "You possess noble blood, do you not? You attend the sermons of the preacher; you despise baseness—the kind that smiles and triumphs. Do you hate your husband?"

"Oh, God! Were you truly his father?" Romola whispered, her mind consumed by visions of the past, oblivious to his direct inquiry. "Or did he lie? Did you take him as an infant?"

"You believe me! You understand what he is!" Baldassarre cried triumphantly, tightening his grip as if physical contact restored his fading strength. "Will you aid me?"

"Yes," Romola answered, though she did not interpret his request as he intended. She laid her palm gently over his rough, grasping hand, tears welling in her eyes as she gazed at him. "Oh, it is pitiful! Speak—were you a great scholar? Did you teach him? What became of it?"

She hesitated. Tito's accusation of Baldassarre's insanity echoed in her mind, and where were the traces of former refinement? Still, she maintained her composure, keeping her hand steady while listening with newfound caution.

"It is lost! All of it is gone!" Baldassarre lamented. "They refused to believe me because he lied, claiming I was mad, and had me dragged to prison. I am old; my mind will not return. The world conspires against me."

He paused, his eyes sinking under a wave of despair, before lifting them again with renewed fervor. "But *you* stand with me. He manipulated you into loving him, yet he has betrayed you; you hate him. Indeed, he made *me* love him too. He was beautiful and gentle, while I was a lonely man. I took him when they beat him. He slept in my bosom as a child, and I watched him grow, pouring all my knowledge into him, intending to give him everything I owned. I possessed much—money, books, jewels. He took my jewels and sold them; he left me in servitude. He never sought me out. When I returned, broken and wretched, he denied me. He called me a madman."

"He told us his father died—drowned," Romola murmured faintly. "Surely he must have believed that then. Oh! He could not have been so vile *then*!"

A vision rose before her of Tito in those early days, innocent in her eyes, regarding no evil as a child regards poison in flowers. The yearning regret of that memory offered a fleeting respite from the horror. With a great sob, tears burst forth.

"You are young," Baldassarre chided, showing a hint of impatience. "Tears flow easily, but they serve no purpose. They extinguish the inner fire, and it is the fire that matters. Tears will only hinder us. Listen to me."

Romola shifted slightly, startled. Once again, the specter of his madness pierced her thoughts, checking the flood of belief. Was this merely a deranged assassin? Yet, her profound conviction in his story remained, outweighing her fear. She approached him not with dread, but with sympathy, avoiding any display of doubt that might wound him.

"Tell me," she asked as softly as possible, "how did you lose your memory, your scholarship?"

"I fell ill. I cannot say how long—a total void. I recall nothing until I found myself sitting in the sun among the stones, surrounded by darkness. Slowly, gradually, I felt a longing for something unknown that never arrived. Later, aboard a ship upon the waters, I began to comprehend what I desired: the return of the Boy, the restoration of my thoughts. I felt locked out of my own mind. Now, I exist outside it. I perceive only walls and darkness."

Baldassarre drifted into a reverie, resting his head in his hands, until Romola's pity submerged her remaining cautions. The sorrow in his words resonated with an ancient pain of her own. Had she not witnessed daily how her father mourned for Dino and the future he had envisioned for that son?

"It all returned once," Baldassarre continued. "I mastered everything. I saw the world anew, my gems, my books. I believed I held him in my power, so I went to expose him where the lights shone and the trees stood tall. He lied again, calling me mad, and they dragged me away to prison... Wickedness is mighty, and he wears armor."

Fierceness flared within him once more. Speaking with his former intensity, he seized Romola's arm again. "But you will assist me? He has betrayed you as well. He has taken another wife, bearing children. He convinces her he is her husband, rendering her a foolish, helpless creature. I will show you where she resides."

The first shock that coursed through Romola was pure anger. The woman's sense of indignation surged to the forefront. Instinctively, Baldassarre sensed her alignment with his cause.

"You hate him," he pressed on. "Is it not true? No love exists between you. I know women can hate; you possess proud blood. You abhor falsehood, and you crave revenge."

Romola sat paralyzed by the collision of conflicting emotions, unaware that his grip was bruising her tender arm.

"You shall devise a plan," Baldassarre whispered eagerly. "I have memorized that you are his rightful wife. You are a noble woman. You frequent the preacher of vengeance; you will uphold justice. But you must think for me. My mind fails—sometimes everything vanishes except the fire. That fire is God; it is justice; it cannot die. Do you believe this? If they refuse to hang him for stealing from me, you must strip him of his armor. You will make him defenseless, and I shall strike him. I carry a knife, and my arm remains strong enough."

He slid his hand beneath his tunic, drawing out the concealed blade, tracing its edge absently as if needing the tactile sensation to sustain his delusions. To Romola, every passing hour of her life seemed destined to grow harder than the last. Her judgment was too sharp and rapid to waste breath on futile protests against a man in Baldassarre's state. Instead of answering his final plea, she chose to buy time, hoping to let his agitation subside by asking a different question. Trembling slightly, she spoke:

"You claim she is foolish and helpless—that other wife—who believes him to be her true husband. Perhaps he is; perhaps he wed her before marrying me."

"I cannot say," Baldassarre replied, pausing in his examination of the knife, his expression bewildered. "My memories end there. I only know where she lives. You shall see her. I will escort you, but not today,"

A singular purpose drove Romola forward, a resolve that had coalesced throughout the afternoon like a tributary swelling into the main stream of her existence. It felt less like a choice than an imperative of her soul. Ignoring the encroaching twilight and refusing Maso's offer of his cautious escort, she crossed the bridge where the river churned black beneath the fading crimson of the sunset. Taking the shortest path, she rushed toward the Old Palace. Perhaps she would find her husband there; it mattered not. Probability offered no comfort; she needed only to release the weight of her heart.

She moved blindly through the pillared courtyard and up the broad staircase, unaware of the frescoes lining the walls or the flicker of newly lit candles until the door swung open. Bernardo del Nero entered, his white hair held high above his silk *lucco*.

"Romola, my child, what is this?" he exclaimed, his voice tight with anxious surprise as he shut the door behind them.

She had removed her veil and stepped toward him in silence. He placed a hand on her shoulder, pulling her slightly closer to examine her features. Her face was gaunt, carved by exhaustion and prolonged turmoil; her hair hung loose and disordered. Yet, her eyes burned with a fierce excitement that seemed to have conquered her physical weariness.

"What has he done?" Bernardo demanded abruptly. "Speak, child. Tell me everything. Cast aside your pride; I am your father."

"It concerns not myself—not at all," Romola replied quickly. "Dearest godfather, it concerns you. I have heard whispers—some I dare not speak aloud. But you are in peril within these walls, in peril everywhere. Fanatics seek to harm you, and there are traitors among you. Trust no one. To trust is to invite betrayal."

Bernardo smiled faintly. "Have you driven yourself to such a feverish state, my poor child, to repeat such ancient truths to an old man like me?"

"Oh, no! These are not old truths," she insisted, clutching her hands together as if to stifle the confession trapped within them. "These are fresh revelations I possess but cannot fully articulate. Dearest godfather, you know I am not foolish. Why should I come to you without cause? Is it too late to warn you against every shadow that pretends to stand by your side? Is it too late to urge you to flee to your villa, to hide in the countryside once these final three days of office end? Oh, God! Perhaps it is too late! And if harm befalls you, it will feel as though I have caused it!"

The final words escaped her involuntarily, a sudden rupture of a long-suppressed dread. Startled by her own vehemence, she faltered.

"I mean," she added, her voice trembling, "that I know nothing with certainty. Only fear fills me."

"Poor child!" Bernardo said, regarding her with a gaze of quiet depth for a moment. Then he spoke softly, "Go, Romola. Return home and rest. Your fears may merely be monstrous shadows cast by something trivial and harmless. Even traitors require motive; the rats scurry only where they smell the cheese, and we do not yet know which direction the wind blows."

He paused, turning his eyes away with an air of abstract resignation, then shrugged slowly. "Warnings avail me nothing, child. I enter no conspiracies, nor do I ever abandon my colors. If I march alongside stubborn men rushing to meet guns and pikes, I must share their fate. Let us say no more. I have few years left in my sack for them to steal. Go, child; return home and rest."

He touched her head again, affectionately. She could not resist leaning into his arm, pressing her forehead against his shoulder. This caress felt like the final remnant of that youthful filial bond, which now appeared so radiant to her even amidst its sorrows, for those sorrows remained pure, untouched by malice.

"Is silence best, my Romola?" the old man asked.

"Yes, for now; but I cannot promise it will remain so," she answered hesitantly, lifting her head with a pleading expression.

"Well, while I draw breath, you have a father's ear and a father's home," he declared, lifting the heavy black drapery to wrap around her head. "Remember that." Opening the door, he urged, "Hasten away. You look like a specter; you will find safety enough outside."

That night, when sleep finally claimed Romola, it was deep and profound. Her agitation had reached its zenith; she required strength before she could endure further suffering. Against her usual rigidity, she slept well past sunrise.

When dawn broke, it shattered with the roar of cannon fire. From Maso, accompanied by numerous dubious details, Romola learned that Piero de' Medici stood before the gate facing Rome with thirteen hundred men. A countryman had alerted the Signoria just before daylight; otherwise, the city would have fallen unawares. The master of the informant was absent, having been summoned to the Palazzo long ago.

Romola dispatched the old servant to gather intelligence while she ascended to the loggia, scanning the horizon for signs of the dreaded entry or evidence of a successful defense. Maso returned with grim tidings: the great Piazza teemed with armed men, and many prominent citizens suspected of sympathizing with the Medici had been summoned and detained. Some of the populace seemed indifferent to Piero's arrival, even suggesting the Signoria itself had invited him; yet, regardless of intent, the soldiers from Pisa were arrayed against him.

In the chaotic memory of those morning hours, few external events stood out clearly above the surging tide of retrospection and anticipation. She recalled walking to the Badia precisely as scheduled, despite the street alarms, and waiting there in vain. The scene that haunted her most vividly occurred as she emerged from the church, standing on the steps while the doors were barred for the afternoon recess.

There was a palpable shift in the expressions and tones of both armed and unarmed passersby. The cannons fired again, yet this time the sound elicited only laughter. She soon understood the cause. Piero de' Medici and his cavalry had turned their backs on Florence, galloping furiously down the road to Siena.

She learned this from a sturdy shopkeeper-Piagnone who had not yet lowered his pike.

"It is true," he concluded, his voice tinged with bitter emphasis. "Piero is gone, but those who conspired with his arrival remain here—we all know that. If the new Signoria fulfills its duty, we shall soon learn who they are."

The words struck Romola like a sharp spasm. Yet, the evil they foretold was not yet immediate. As she returned to her home, her most urgent anxiety shifted to the terrifying possibility that she had lost sight of Baldassarre for a long time.

The sun-drenched days stretched on, offering neither the answer Romola craved nor the catastrophe she feared. There remained no word from Baldassarre, and despite the government's heightened vigilance, no evidence surfaced regarding the suspected conspiracy. Yet, fate delivered other trials that pressed close upon her spirit, bridging the chasm of anxious speculation with the tangible urgency of present suffering. These were the Plague and the Excommunication of Savonarola. Both events served to halt her drifting away from the Friar, anchoring her once more to the man who had unveiled a life of duty for her, and who appeared now to be losing a fierce battle between principle and corruption.

Romola found it impossible to navigate the streets of pestilence and woe carrying within her the exhilarating glow of a joy born solely from her own existence, without acknowledging that she owed this elevated moral life entirely to Fra Girolamo. Furthermore, she could not bear to witness the silencing and banishment of a figure distinguished not by heresy or superstition, but by the sheer energy with which he strove to make Christianity a living reality, without feeling pulled irresistibly toward his cause.

Deep into the scorching heat of June, the Excommunication decree, having traveled from Rome over several weeks, was solemnly proclaimed within the Duomo. Romola attended, hoping that the fervent resistance it sparked would fortify her sympathy for Savonarola, a wellspring of her own strength. The scene stood in stark, memorable contrast to the gatherings she had known before. Gone were the upturned faces of citizens filling the vast nave under the morning light—the young forming an amphitheater toward the walls, weaving a garland of hope around the elders' memories. Gone was the mighty voice that thrilled every heart with visions of grand struggles, both visible and unseen.

In their place stood the bare, somber walls illuminated only by the flicker of taper-light; a black-and-grey congregation of monks and secular priests with bowed, expectant faces; the occasional, faint chiming of small bells interrupting a monotone recitation of sentences long posted in church doors. Finally, the lights extinguished, leaving behind the slow, shuffling retreat of monkish feet into a dim silence.

Romola's devotion to the Frate was doubled by the gleeful triumph etched onto hardened, coarse faces, and by the terrified confusion evident in the expressions and speech of many of his staunchest allies. The boundary between the duty of obedience and the right of resistance was rarely clear, yet here it became overwhelmingly difficult due to the prevailing belief that the Church was not merely a political compromise, but a living organism infused with divine power to bless and curse. For most devout Florentines, this faith was not a chosen opinion but an inalienable certainty, akin to the curve of the sky. Consequently, Savonarola's bold arguments—that the Excommunication was unjust and therefore void—only caused them greater trembling, viewing his defiance as a challenge thrown at a mystical image against whose immense power no defense existed.

But Romola, whose mind had not been nourished by the traditional currents of the Christian community where her father lived apart, viewed her relationship with the Church solely through Savonarola. His moral force was the only authority she had ever bowed to. Thus, his excommunication appeared to her not as a tragedy, but as a threat from hostile vice. On one side stood a man dedicated to public virtue and spiritual purity; on the other, the assault of alarmed selfishness led by a lustful, greedy, lying, and murderous old man—once Rodrigo Borgia, now elevated to infamy as Pope Alexander VI.

Nuanced facts often soften such sharp contrasts, visible only to neutral observers unburdened by distress at seeing folly in martyrs or wisdom in their executioners. Yet Romola needed a strength neutrality could not provide. This Excommunication, simplifying and nobling Savonarola's stance by highlighting its broader implications, felt to her like a rescue from the isolating pressure of criticism and doubt. The Friar was no longer trapped in petty conflicts with Florentine foes arising from his pulpit's unchecked fervor; instead, he stood simply as a standard-bearer leaping into the breach, appealing to the entire Christian world against a vicious abuse of ecclesiastical power. Life seemed clearest and easiest when the heart raced at witnessing such generous self-sacrifice. In those moments, the soul's highest prize becomes undeniable, and one almost believes in the power to achieve it.

A fresh current of enthusiasm carried Romola through these arduous summer days. She had said nothing to Tito concerning her recent meeting with Baldassarre or the revelations he shared. What good could such agitated words accomplish? Only a cold sarcasm regarding her sympathy for his alleged assassin. Baldassarre was clearly helpless; the true danger was not that he would harm Tito, but that Tito, tracking him down, might devise a new scheme to eliminate the man who haunted his conscience.

Romola felt she could take no decisive action until she saw Baldassarre again and learned the full truth about the "other wife"—whether she was indeed the woman to whom Tito was originally bound. Possibilities surrounding this mystery, involving the deepest wound to her family's pride, mingled with her memories of an illusory love. These suspicions ate away at the tender remnants of her past marriage, while her absolute trust in Baldassarre's account filled her with a horror that drove her to avoid Tito. Had he been present, this fear might have compelled a passionate confrontation, but he was unusually absent from home.

Like many wealthy citizens during the plague, Tito spent his leisure hours in the countryside. The pleasant villa of Melema welcomed guests, and since Romola had refused to flee the city, he had no need to maintain a separate rural residence. However, by late July, as the public turmoil began to subside, Romola was left to face the quieter, heavier weight of her personal destiny. The Plague had nearly vanished, and Savonarola's position looked brighter thanks to a favorable magistrates who wrote urgent appeals to Rome requesting the revocation of the Excommunication.

Though her robust body succumbed to the natural lethargy following weeks of intense exertion, her restless mind refused idleness, demanding purpose save during the stifling midday heat. In the cool mornings and evenings, she walked constantly, varying her route with a vague hope: if Baldassarre still lived, she might meet him. Perhaps some illness had paralyzed his memory, causing him to forget her address—or even her existence. This was her most optimistic explanation for his silence. The probability she leaned toward, however, was far sadder: that he had died of the Plague.

The morning sun had grown oppressively warm by the time Romola, having walked along the city walls from San Marco, turned toward the intersecting streets at the gate of Santa Croce. The Borgo La Croce lay in a profound stillness. In the silent sunshine, she listened to the rhythm of her own footsteps until, rounding a bend, she spotted a small child, barely three years old, pausing mid-waddle. Clad only in a white shirt, the boy stopped and looked around.

At first, Romola saw only his back—square, sturdy, topped with a cloud of reddish-brown curls. Then he turned. Dark eyes, wide with tears, met hers; his lower lip trembled as he pushed it upward, and his fat brown fists clutched his shirt in helpless distress. A tall black figure casting a shadow over him seemed to trigger a climax of bewildered fear, and a loud, sobbing cry sent big tears rolling down his cheeks.

Acting on a maternal instinct born of her own passionate tenderness, Romola immediately uncovered her head. Stooping onto the pavement, she wrapped her arms around the child, pressing her cheek against his while speaking in soothing tones. Initially, his sobs grew louder, yet he made no attempt to flee. Soon, the outburst ceased with that strange abruptness characteristic of childhood emotions; his distorted face smoothed out, replaced by an open-mouthed stare fixed upon Romola.

"You have lost yourself, little one," she said, kissing him. "Never mind! We will find the house again. Perhaps Mamma will meet us."

She sensed he had escaped while his mother's attention was elsewhere and believed he would soon be pursued.

"Oh, what a heavy, heavy boy!" she remarked, attempting to lift him. Finding she could not carry him, she added, "Come, then, you must toddle back by my side."

The parted lips remained motionless in awed silence, one brown fist still gripping the shirt with tenacious force, while the other yielded willingly to her wonderful white hand—strong yet soft.

"Do you *have* a Mamma?" Romola asked, setting out and looking down at the boy with a yearning gaze. He offered no reply. A girl might have chattered, but this square-shouldered little man with his large cloud of curls remained mute.

However, he was alert regarding his destination. At the turn near San Ambrogio, he dragged Romola toward it, looking up. "Ah, that is the way home, is it?" she smiled. He merely thrust his head forward and pulled, urging her to hurry. After another turn he insisted upon, Romola found herself in a short street opening onto garden grounds. Here, the little fellow paused, pulling her toward some stone stairs. Evidently unwilling to lose her hand, and equally unwilling for her to leave without ensuring she reached her friends, they ascended. Emerging from the sunlight into dimness, they climbed until a final landing revealed an open doorway spilling extra light. Passing through a small lobby and another open door, Romola halted. No sound greeted her arrival.

In the far corner of the room, opposite the light, sat Tessa, one hand resting on the edge of a cradle, her head drooping slightly as she slept. Near a window, with her back to the door, Monna Lisa worked silently, preparing salad in a deaf, unconscious state. Romola caught only an instant of this still scene before Lillo snatched his hand away and ran to his mother's side. He leaned his head back against her arm, surveying Romola seriously from a distance, making no direct effort to wake her.

When Lillo nudged her, Tessa opened her eyes in bewilderment. But her glance had not yet settled on the figure at the doorway before she sprang up, blushed deeply, and began to tremble, unable to speak or move forward.

"Ah! We have seen each other before," Romola said, smiling and stepping closer. "I am glad it was *your* little boy. He was crying in the street; I suppose he had run away. So we walked together a little way, and then he knew where he was, and brought me here. But you hadn't missed him? That is well, else you would have been frightened."

The shock of discovering Lillo had wandered overwhelmed Tessa's other feelings. Her color drained and returned, and seizing the boy's arm, she ran to Monna Lisa, whispering loudly in the old woman's ear with a half-sob:

"Oh, Lisa, you are wicked! Why stand with your back to the door? Lillo ran away ever so far into the street."

"Holy Mother!" Monna Lisa exclaimed in her meek, thick tone, dropping the spoon from her hands. "Where were *you*, then? I thought you were there, watching him."

"But you *know* I go to sleep when I rock," Tessa retorted pettishly.

"Well, well, we must keep the outer door shut, or tie him up," Monna Lisa advised. "For he'll be as cunning as Satan before long, and that's the holy truth. But how did he come back?"

This question reminded Tessa of Romola's presence. Without answering, she turned toward the stranger, blushing and timid once more, while Monna Lisa's eyes followed her movement. The old woman made a low reverence and declared, "Doubtless the most noble lady brought him back." Advancing slightly, she added, "It is my shame that he was found with only his shirt on; but he kicked and refused his other clothes this morning. The mother, poor thing, will never allow him to be beaten. But what can an old woman do without a stick when the lad's legs grow so strong? Let your nobleness look at his legs."

Conscious of the scrutiny, Lillo pulled his shirt up higher, gazing down at his olive-round legs with a dispassionate curiosity. Romola laughed, stooping to give him a caressing shake and a kiss. This gesture aided the reassurance Tessa had already gleaned from Monna Lisa's respectful address. Earlier, when Naldo had recounted the Carnival incident and Tessa asked who the heavenly lady—who appeared just when needed and vanished so quickly—might be, Naldo had replied, "Not exactly, my Tessa; only one of the saints," refusing to elaborate. Thus, in Tessa's dreamlike synthesis of small experiences, Romola became vaguely associated with church paintings. Upon reappearing, the memory of her protection was tinged with religious awe, though not deeply, for Tessa feared mostly ugly and evil spirits. It seemed unlikely that good beings would anger or punish her, unlike Nofri and the devil. Now that Monna Lisa spoke freely of Lillo's legs and Romola laughed, Tessa felt at ease.

"Ninna is in the cradle," she said. "*She's* pretty too."

Romola approached the sleeping infant, and Monna Lisa, one of the exceptionally meek deaf women who rarely expected to be addressed, returned to her salad.

"Ah! She is waking; she has opened her blue eyes," Romola noted. "You must take her up, and I will sit in this chair—may I?—and nurse Lillo. Come, Lillo!"

Taking Tito's chair, Romola extended her arms toward the boy, whose eyes had tracked her. He hesitated, pointing small fingers at her with a mix of puzzlement and anger. "That's Babbo's chair," he said, unaware of how to explain the difficulty if Babbo arrived and found Romola occupying his seat.

"But Babbo is not here, and I shall go soon. Come, let me nurse you as he does," Romola said, wondering internally for the first time what kind of father this was—whose wife dressed in peasant fashion yet possessed a certain daintiness indicating idleness and plenty. Lillo consented to be lifted. Finding the lap comfortable, he began exploring her dress and hands, searching for ornaments besides the rosary.

Tessa, previously occupied coaxing the waking Ninna out of her peevishness, sat down in her low chair near Romola's knee, arranging the baby's tiny form. Jealous that the strange lady seemed to favor the boy as much as Naldo did, she murmured, "Lillo was going to be angry with me because I sat in Babbo's chair. Will he come soon and want it?"

"Ah, no!" Tessa replied. "You can sit in it a long while. I shall be sorry when you go. When you first came to take care of me at the Carnival, I thought it wonderful; you came and went away so fast. And Naldo said, perhaps you were a saint, and that made me tremble a little, though the saints are very good, I know; and you were good to me, and now you have taken care of Lillo. Perhaps you will always come and take care of me. That was how Naldo did a long while ago; he came and took care of me when I was frightened, one San Giovanni. I couldn't think where he came from—he was so beautiful and good. And so are you," Tessa concluded, looking up at Romola with devout admiration.

"Naldo is your husband. His eyes are like Lillo's," Romola observed, noting the boy's unusually dark eyebrows at his young age. She spoke not interrogatively, but with a quiet certainty of inference that was necessarily mysterious to Tessa.

"Ah! You know him!" Tessa said, pausing in wonder. "Perhaps

Tito possessed ample justification for claiming his safety. Over the preceding three months, anticipating the likely exposure of the Medicean conspirators, he had meticulously prepared his defenses. He had fortified his standing in both Rome and Milan, positioning himself as the conduit for clandestine intelligence and indirect maneuvers against the Friar and the popular faction. Simultaneously, he had cultivated the favor of that very popular party with unprecedented diligence, projecting a subtle alignment with their political convictions. Yet, throughout this double-dealing, he had not withdrawn his active service from the Medici; rather, he had sought greater employment and exclusive trust from them. Maintaining this triple game proved effortless. The Medici, admitting duplicity as a principle of statecraft, lacked the moral compass to judge a colleague who, unlike themselves, possessed no hereditary ties, alliances, or prejudices deeply rooted in their cause. To them, deceiving the enemy was a fair strategy; deceiving their own party was a baseness they felt no urge to commit. In employing Tito's agile talents, they remained oblivious to the fact that his lack of traditional attachment—which made him a convenient tool—was also the absence of the primary guarantee of mutual honor among themselves.

Furthermore, the *Arrabbiati* of Rome and Milan, the aristocratic opponents of Savonarola, operated a network of espionage and covert correspondence where profound hypocrisy was the highest currency and the largest payment the greatest merit. Suspecting an agent merely because he played a role convincingly would have been a logical absurdity. Conversely, the *Piagnoni*, driven by the direct energy of fervent conviction, were prone to believe Tito's professed loyalty to their cause, largely because he feigned no religious sympathy. Under these conditions, the last quarter had been a period of dazzling success for Tito. His primary ambition was to secure a permanent post in either Rome or Milan. He harbored a growing resolve to leave Florence once the opportune moment arrived, seeking the ease of capital cities where talent and learning were lavishly rewarded. Currently, the scales tipped toward Milan; if he could deliver decisive services to Duke Ludovico Sforza within the year, the prospects at the Milanese court outweighed those of Rome.

The revelation of the Medicean conspiracy had indeed been within Tito's foresight, though not the specific manner of its occurrence. The arrest of Lamberto dell'Antella, found carrying incriminating letters and fueled by bitter hatred for the Medici, was an unforeseen variable. Despite the careful pretexts guarding his agency, Tito could not evade implication in a broad disclosure of Medicean plots. However, his sharp mind quickly charted a path to safety with minimal collateral damage. While preserving one's integrity is pleasant, the skin remains sensitive to the air; Tito's calculations had not failed. That evening, before returning home, he secured the three outcomes he desired: exemption from prosecution regarding his alleged complicity with the Medici, retention of his secretaryship for another year (unless he chose to resign), and the strict confidentiality of the price exacted for these guarantees.

To most, the cost seemed exorbitant, and Tito himself would have preferred not to pay it. His first move was to win over Francesco Valori, one of the Ten holding immediate authority over him and a member of the special council investigating the plot. As the leader of the *Piagnoni*, Valori possessed noble qualities tempered by violent partisanship, an arrogant temperament that estranged friends, and deep-seated personal animosities—not least against Bernardo del Nero. In a brief, private interview, sealed by a promise of secrecy, Tito confessed his agency for the Medici. He spoke frankly of his motives, declared his belief in the futility of their efforts, affirmed his sincere preference for the popular government, and feigned a personal dislike for Bernardo del Nero. He then delivered his crucial proposal: there existed another Medicean plot, and if granted certain conditions by the government, he could travel to Siena and Romagna, where Piero de' Medici was amassing forces, to retrieve documentary evidence for the council. This mission required that his reputation as a Medicean agent remain intact among the Medici; thus, his role as a source of information for the authorities had to be buried in absolute secrecy.

Yet, even with precautions, rumors might leak. Before facing the consequences of turning against his former allies, Tito needed assurance of immunity from prosecution and protection from dismissal for the coming year. These terms did not resonate with Valori as they do today. Valori was not concerned with weighing Tito's moral ledger; his mind was singularly focused on securing the harshest possible sentence for the five prisoners. Given the inevitable efforts to save them, Valori believed the evidence against them must be overwhelming to terrify the well-meaning populace into fearing clemency. In the legal culture of the age, evidence was something to be acquired by foul means. Torturing suspects until they confessed everyone's guilt was a step toward justice, and a traitor's testimony was the final piece of the puzzle. Lamberto dell'Antella had been tortured to extract knowledge he barely possessed, yet stronger facts were needed, particularly against Bernardo del Nero, who had thus far refused to betray the plot despite earlier attempts to dissuade it. For the sake of Florence, Valori zealously believed Bernardo's guilt must be illuminated in the starkest light. Perhaps he was unaware that his zeal was fueled by hatred.

Valori accepted Tito's proposition, presented it to his colleagues anonymously, and swayed them to his view. Late that afternoon, Tito was summoned before the Special Council. He caused a stir by revealing a second plot designed to install Piero de' Medici as master of Florence, scheduled for mid-August. He argued that documentary proof was essential to clarify the situation. He was commissioned to depart for Siena at dawn and left the council chamber with a written guarantee of his immunity and continued office.

Among the twenty Florentines who regarded Tito with grave eyes as he stood before them, speaking of shocking matters with elegant circumlocution and an apparent lack of higher motives—a mask often worn by the most intense affectations—they began to reassess him. They silently concluded that this ingenious and serviceable Greek was now better suited for public utility than private friendship. Unprincipled men were assets, allowing those with scruples to keep their hands relatively clean in a world demanding dirty work. Indeed, it was unclear to the respectable minds of Florence, unless one shared the Friar's delusion of earthly purity, how any sphere of life could function without instruments deemed unworthy of respect, yet necessary for payment. Some of these very judges, soon to be involved in transactions requiring unscrupulousness as resolute as Tito's, reflected on the paradox: "Treachery pleases many, but no one loves the traitor." Society maintained gibbets for murderers and martyrs alike, hisses for cowardice and for generous truths—a mixed state signaling not chaos, but a struggling order.

For Tito, he was acutely aware that he had slightly lowered his standing in the eyes of those who had accepted his services. Possessing the self-reflection inherent in calculated action, he regretted being disapproved by worldly men. He had never intended to be judged poorly; he aimed to act so competently that opposition to others' standards went unnoticed. The barrier between himself and Romola had risen due to the impossibility of such concealment with her. He recoiled from condemnatory judgments as one recoils from an inhospitable climate. Yet, reality was less malleable than cleverness suggested, and events had turned inconveniently.

He bore no grudge against Messer Bernardo del Nero; he liked Lorenzo Tornabuoni and Giannozzo Pucci. He had served them brilliantly, deserving high reward had their party prevailed. Would he sacrifice all the agreeable fruits of life because their faction had failed? His offer of additional proof against them would likely alter nothing; he was convinced they would escape extreme punishment. Had he withheld it, however, his promising career would have been destroyed. What motive exists but self-interest? Florentines driven by petty politics might confuse family pride with self-interest; a naive philosopher might mistake ego for divine virtue; fanatics might find purpose in a future palm branch. But a man of clear intellect does not let his course be dictated by such childish impulses or dubious internal vapors. Did not Pontanus, the celebrated poet and philosopher, welcome the French king at Naples—who had come to dethrone his royal patron—and yet hold his head high and prosper? People rarely cared about such things unless their personal spleen was provoked. Weakness alone was despised; power commanded immunity. No man ascends high without incurring unpleasant necessities that invite enmity and, when needed, a little hissing.

It was a faint warning of this impending hissing, sensed by Tito during his audience, that gave his current conduct the weight of an epoch, prompting him to defend it with argumentative vindication. He was not taking a deeper step into wrongdoing, for his bond to his father remained stronger than any tie to the Medici. Yet, his treatment of his father had been hidden behind successful lies; his present act admitted no total concealment—it was inherently a revelation. Tito winced under this new vulnerability to disapproval. Very well, he reasoned. A little patience, and in six months or a year, he could turn his back on these hard, eager Florentines with their futile quarrels and declining fortunes. His brilliant success in Florence had ugly flaws: he had loved the wrong woman, and Baldassarre had returned under unpredictable circumstances. But as Tito rode loosely toward Siena, he envisioned a future free from these haunting mistakes. He had substantial funds safely outside Florence, was in the prime of his twenty-eighth year, and possessed proven skill

Tito returned swiftly from Siena, only to embark immediately upon another journey, absent until the seventeenth of August. Nearly two weeks had elapsed since the arrest of the accused, yet they remained incarcerated, their destiny hanging in a precarious balance. During this interlude, Romola felt as though all worldly cares had ceased to exist save for the singular, consuming task of monitoring the shifting probabilities surrounding their fate. At times, the odds appeared heavily weighted in their favor; the passage of time itself acted as a shield, heightening the stakes for those seeking to intervene, while the authorities' reluctance to bear the stigma of a hasty execution fostered an indefinite horizon of delay.

On one side, a clamorous voice declared the Republic perilously near collapse, warning that clemency toward the prisoners would serve as a beacon inviting attack from all enemies. Conversely, there existed a grim certainty: a verdict of death and confiscation against five citizens of such distinguished standing would inevitably breed a rancorous hatred from their kin against anyone deemed complicit in such a sentence. Ultimately, the power of judgment rested with the Eight, the body overseeing criminal justice, where a majority of six votes—the *set fave*, or "six beans," the political pulse of Florence—was required. Yet, the Eight recoiled from this heavy burden, requesting in this extraordinary instance that the Signoria share the responsibility. The Signoria, in turn, shrugged off the weight, proposing an appeal to the Great Council. According to a law championed by Savonarola three years prior, any citizen condemned to death by the fatal six votes possessed the right to appeal to the Great Council. However, at this juncture, the friends of the accused resisted the formal appeal, driven primarily by a desperate need for further delay; strictly speaking, a sentence must be pronounced before an appeal can be lodged. Prevailing in their strategy, a compromise was struck: the case was referred to a grand assembly convening on the seventeenth, comprising all higher magistrates, the Senate of Eighty, and a select group of citizens.

On this fateful day, Romola, her anxiety sharpened by the fear that her godfather's doom might be sealed before the proceedings concluded, secured permission to visit him for the second time, albeit only in the presence of witnesses. She returned to the Via de' Bardi accompanied by her cousin, Monna Brigida, still unaware of whether the council had reached a definitive conclusion. Brigida departed once more to await news at the residence of a friend within one of the magistracies, promising to return with authentic tidings the moment they became available. Exhausted and shattered, Romola collapsed onto the first seat in the sunlit salon, too overwhelmed by grief to mind her surroundings or the dissonance of the room's decor. With her back to the door, she rested her head in her hands, waiting. Time stretched endlessly until the door swung open, revealing not Brigida, but someone else.

Since her departure from Tito on that unforgettable night, Romola had possessed no tangible proof that he had secured his safety through treachery. To the contrary, evidence suggested he remained trusted by the Medici, ostensibly carrying out missions in Romagna under the guise of official government commissions. The fog surrounding the conspirators allowed for the interpretation that Tito had escaped all implication. Yet, Romola's suspicion refused to dissipate. Her horror at his treatment of Baldassarre cast a shadow over every perception of his actions; it was as if she witnessed him commit murder, leaving her with a permanent, diseased conviction that his hands were stained with fresh blood.

As his footsteps echoed on the stone floor, a cold shiver seized her. Unable to turn, unable to rise to greet him, she remained frozen. He spoke not a word initially, pausing briefly before sitting opposite her across the table. When she finally lifted her eyes to meet his gaze, she found herself mute. He showed no irritation, merely stating calmly, "This meeting corresponds with our parting, Romola. Yet I understand it is a moment of terrible suspense. I have come, if you will listen, to bring you the relief of hope."

She startled, adjusting her posture but regarding him with deep doubt. "It will not be unwelcome to hear—even though it is I who tell you—that the council has been prorogued until the twenty-first," he continued smoothly. "The Eight have finally succumbed to fear and passed a sentence of condemnation, but a demand has now been made on behalf of the condemned for an Appeal to the Great Council."

Romola's doubtful expression vanished, replaced by eager inquiry. "And when is it to be made?"

"It has not yet been granted, but it *may* be," Tito replied. "The Special Council reconvenes on the twenty-first to decide whether the appeal shall be allowed. In the interim, there is a three-day window wherein favorable chances may arise, wherein interest may be mobilized on their behalf."

Romola sprang from her seat. Color flooded her cheeks, and her hands began to tremble. In that instant, her feelings toward Tito receded completely. "Possibly," Tito observed, rising as well, "your own intuition anticipated what I was about to say. You are thinking of the Frate."

"I am," Romola admitted, surprised. "Has he done something? Is there anything to tell me?"

"Only this," Tito explained. "Messer Francesco Valori's bitterness and violence largely dictated the council's course today. Half the members who voted against the prisoners were frightened into compliance. Furthermore, there are numerous friends of Fra Girolamo within and outside this Special Council who strongly oppose the death sentence—such as Piero Guicciardini, a member of the Signoria who offered stout resistance, and Giovan Battista Ridolfi, a *Piagnone* who will not easily forgive the death of his brother Niccolò."

"But how can the appeal be denied?" Romola exclaimed indignantly. "When it is the law! Was it not one of the chief glories of our popular government to enact this very law?"

"They label this an exceptional case," Tito countered. "Of course, clever arguments abound, but there is much louder posturing about the danger to the Republic. Still, no opposition could prevent the assembly from adjourning. A powerful influence, rightly applied over these next three days, might sway the wavering courage of those wishing to grant the appeal, or even check the headlong fury of Francesco Valori. I have learned that the Frate has already intervened, sending a message to Valori in favor of Lorenzo Tornabuoni. Given that you occasionally have access to the Frate, it would surely be worthwhile to exercise your privilege now."

"It is true," Romola murmured, her gaze distant. "I cannot believe the Frate would approve of denying the appeal."

"I heard it whispered by several people in the Palazzo courts before I left," Tito said. "To allow a government composed almost entirely of his own party to deny the appeal without protest, especially after boasting in his books and sermons that he secured the law, would be to the everlasting discredit of Fra Girolamo. Moreover, between ourselves—and with all due respect for your Frate's abilities—he has fallen into the habit of preaching a form of human sacrifice known as 'killing tyrants.' Some of his followers might find this inconsistent with the leniency required in this specific case."

*[Footnote: The most recent and esteemed biographer of Savonarola, Signor Villari, argues that the Law of Appeal, ultimately enacted, was wider than Savonarola originally intended, serving as a source of bitter annoyance to him as a tool of the aristocracy to attach the injurious results of license to popular measures. However, this view overlooks the fact that Savonarola himself, in his deliberately prepared work, the Compendium Revelationum, written long after the law became statute, enumerated among the benefits secured to Florence 'the Appeal from the Six Votes, advocated by me, for the greater security of the citizens.']*

"I know, I know," Romola said, her voice and countenance betraying profound pain. "But he is driven into those excesses of speech. Things used to be different. I *will* request an audience. I cannot rest without seeing him. I trust in the greatness of his heart."

Her eyes were fixed on the floor, vacant and unfocused, lacking clear awareness that the words issuing from her mouth were being spoken by her husband.

"Do not waste any time, then," Tito urged, his tone smooth and untroubled, twirling his cap as if preparing to depart. "Now, Romola, you will perhaps be able to see, despite your prejudices, that my wishes align with yours in this matter. You will not regard the misfortune of my safety as an offense."

A sensation akin to an electric shock coursed through Romola; it was the sudden, full realization of her husband's presence returning to her. She stared at him, silent.

"At least," he added, his tone hardening slightly, "you will endeavor to base our interactions on reasoning other than the notion that because an evil deed is possible, *I* have committed it. Am I the sole individual excluded from the reach of your extensive charity?"

The emotion that had been suppressed from Romola's lips two weeks prior surged forth again with the gathering force of a tidal wave. She spoke with a decisiveness that signaled her disregard for consequences. "It is too late, Tito. No amount of denial can kill the suspicion that deceit has once been born. And now I know everything. I know who that old man was: he was your father, to whom you owe everything—to whom you owe more than if you had been his own biological son. Compared

The morning broke with a clarity that seemed to demand scrutiny. Romola set out once more for San Marco, having secured through Fra Salvestro the previous evening a rare audience with Fra Girolamo in the chapter-house. Such meetings were exceptional; Savonarola guarded his solitude with such rigorous discipline that he allowed no pretext for slander to take root. Consequently, when permission was granted, it came stripped of all mystery, scheduled during hours when the outer cloisters bustled with ordinary visitors. Romola chose this public path intentionally, seeking to gauge the pulse of the city.

Every loggia, every shadowed corner of the piazza, and every shop serving as a gossip's rendezvous buzzed with the feverish energy of unearned debate. Commerce lagged, fueling the vigor of political discourse. It was evident that factions supporting and opposing the execution of the conspirators were racing to exploit the three-day interim, desperate to define the popular mood. Handbills fluttered through the air like black birds: some boldly proclaimed the choice between justice for the conspirators or ruin for the Republic; others, in equally stark letters, urged adherence to the law and the granting of an appeal. Surrounding these islands of bold capital were vast lakes of smaller script detailing arguments deemed unnecessary to read aloud. Yet, at that moment, in the fresh enthusiasm for printing, many believed that no persuasion was more potent than a phrase in giant type. Romola, however, craved the nuance found in the smaller print. Though pressed for time, she scanned the streets anxiously, hoping to snatch a copy before the *sbirri* tore them from the walls or before eager readers consumed them.

For a long stretch, she found only bills clutched by avid buyers or pinned high above reach. Finally, slipping behind San Giovanni with a hurried stride to evade the crowd, she spotted Bratti. He was exchanging pamphlets for small coins, a familiar sight to Romola, who knew the humble rhythms of Florentine life.

"Bratti," she called out, "do you have both kinds of handbills? Give them to me quickly."

"Two sorts," Bratti replied, slowly separating damp sheets, a slowness that tested Romola's patience. "'Law,' and 'Justice.'"

"Which sells faster?"

"'Justice.' It flies off the shelves, so I raised the price to two danari. But then I realized 'Law' is just as valuable merchandise. People don't value cheap things; selling it for one danaro would be a wrong. I'm a fair trader. Whether 'Law' or 'Justice,' they are both good wares. I got them for nothing and sell them for a fair profit. Need more of either?"

"No, no," Romola interrupted, pressing a white quattrino into his hand. "Here for both." She folded the papers and fled toward the convent.

She arrived swiftly in the outer cloisters of San Marco. Fra Salvestro awaited her beneath the arcade, oblivious to her approach as he chatted with lay visitors. Thanks to a government favorable to the friars, the timid hesitation that had gripped San Marco following the initial shock of excommunication was fading. Among the listeners was Andrea Cambini, a known associate of Francesco Valori. He gestured emphatically, expounding some point while Fra Salvestro listened with an air of trivial curiosity—a demeanor Romola found repulsive, signaling a listener who cared deeply for news but little for its substance.

From disjointed fragments reaching her ears, Romola deduced that Cambini was discussing the fate of the conspirators. She avoided approaching the group. As soon as she captured Fra Salvestro's attention, she turned toward the chapter-house door. He nodded approvingly and vanished into the inner cloister.

A lay brother opened the heavy doors for her, closing them softly behind her. Inside, the wall frescoes seemed to mourn anew with her for the death of her brother Dino. The memory surged, not as a clear recollection, but as a blend of impressions merging with her current terror, as if her present anxiety were a resurrection of the fierce longing she had once brought to this spot—only to be met by cold marble indifference. She suppressed the thought immediately, unfolding the handbills, hoping to read them before Fra Girolamo's arrival. By the time she finished the tract advocating for the law, the door swung open.

Doubling the papers, she stood expectant. When the Friar entered, she knelt, adhering to custom. But upon receiving his blessing, she rose and stood opposite him at a respectful distance. Time had altered him; his seclusion since excommunication had etched deeper lines of mental strain and physical austerity onto his face. Yet, Romola sensed a shift more profound than mere aging. Was the serene elevation and pure humanity that once moved her absent? Or was it the growing rift regarding her godfather that clouded her vision? Perhaps both. Human relationships often fracture under the weight of conflicting loyalties, where the final blow rarely strikes until affection has already been eroded by repeated compromises.

Savonarola's gaze held a hardness born of self-absorption. He intuitively knew her purpose: the fate of the conspirators. A subject he had already wrestled with internally. Seated alone, correcting proofs for his *Triumph of the Cross*, he could rest in a posture of neutral resolve.

"It is a matter of grave importance, doubtless, that brings you to me, my daughter," he began, his tone gentle, though controlled rather than inclined. "I know you do not usually dwell on trifles."

"Father, you know the cause before I speak," Romola replied, pouring out her plea instantly. "You know what I cherish—it is the life of the old man I love most in the world. The thought of him has accompanied the thought of my father since I can remember daylight. That is my warrant for coming, even if my visit seems superfluous. Perhaps you have already decided that your influence over men's hearts should be used to deny Florentines a right you yourselves helped secure."

"I do not interfere with state functions, my daughter," Fra Girolamo stated firmly, disinclined to reignite a debate he had settled within. "I have preached and labored for a good government, essential to the perfection of Christian life; but I keep my hands from specific affairs entrusted to experienced citizens."

"Sire—" Romola started, then stopped. The word slipped out impulsively, only to be choked back by the realization that she was remonstrating with her guide and strength. In rebelling, she bruised her own reverence. Savonarola was too perceptive not to sense her internal conflict, yet too noble to adopt the calm, self-justifying evasion he often employed publicly.

"Speak your heart, my daughter," he commanded, arms crossed, waiting quietly.

"I was going to say, Father, that this matter surpasses in importance many topics you have fervently exhorted us upon. If it belongs to you to urge that men condemned for offenses against the state retain the right to appeal to the Great Council—and if counting it a glory to have won that right for them makes you proud—can it not also belong to you to oppose the denial of that right to those who need it most? Surely this touches the Christian life closer than predicting the death of the Dauphin or the conquest of Pisa."

A subtle tightening of his lips, a sign of suppressed pain, preceded his response.

"My daughter, I speak only as I am permitted. I am not master of the timing of revelations beyond common understanding. Here, I possess no illumination greater than the wisdom due to those charged with state safety. Regarding the Law of Appeal against the Six Votes, I strove to enact it so no Florentine might lose life or goods through the private hatred of a few in power. But these five men, who sought to overthrow a free government and restore a corrupt tyrant, were condemned with the assent of a large assembly. They initially refused to bring their cause before the Great Council. Thus, they forfeited the right to appeal."

"How can they have lost it?" Romola cried. "They have never been condemned until now! And forgive me, Father, but isn't it private hatred that denies them the appeal? Isn't it the violence of the few that frightens others? Why did the assembly split again immediately after seeming to agree? If anything argues against the law, let this weigh heavier: that you once preached more earnestly than anything else that there should be no room for hatred and bloodshed arising from party strife, so private ill-will finds no outlet in public acts. Father, you know private hatred is involved here. Will it not dishonor you not to intervene on the side of mercy, when many see it as the side of law and justice?"

"My daughter," Savonarola said, with visible emotion, "there is a mercy that is weakness, and even treason against the common good. The safety of Florence—which means far more than the welfare of individual Florentines—now demands severity, as it once demanded mercy. These men are condemned not only for a past plot but for one not yet executed. The devices leading to its execution remain active; the tyrant gathers forces in Romagna, and Florence's enemies, seated in the highest Italian thrones, await only a stone to crush her."

"What plot?" Romola asked, trembling with alarm.

"You carry papers in your hand," Savonarola noted, pointing to the handbills. "One of them confirms the government's new intelligence."

Romola hastily opened the unread sheet. It detailed positive evidence of a second plot intended for execution in August. To her mind, it read like proof that Tito had secured

Three days later, the moon, just cresting the rooftops of the Piazza della Signora before midnight, cast no familiar pattern of broad light and deep shadow upon the cobblestones. There was not a single square foot of pavement visible; the ground was entirely swallowed by the heads of a frantic, surging multitude. Gone was the hushed backdrop of the city's nocturnal wanderers—their soft footsteps, murmured conversations, the strumming of lutes, and the quick scurry of cats—against which such sounds usually stood out in sharp relief. In its place rose a deafening roar of mixed shouts, curses, trampling feet, pushing bodies, and the occasional clash of steel. Through this cacophony, nothing could be distinguished save a sudden, piercing shriek or the heavy, rhythmic tolling of a bell.

Almost everyone in Florence who considered themselves part of the public was awake at this hour, either trapped within the walls of the Piazza or fighting desperately to get inside. Inside the Old Palace, the Council Chamber remained filled with the city's highest magistrates, the eighty senators, and the select citizens who had debated furiously through long days and nights by torchlight. They argued whether the appeal for clemency should be granted or if the sentence of death should be carried out immediately to prevent the dangers inherent in any delay. Their debate had become so fierce, so much like a violent quarrel, that the noise leaked out to the crowd below.

Only in the last hour had the decision been reached. The Signoria had remained deeply divided; four members stood immovably for the appeal, despite the terrifying warning that if they refused to yield, their homes would be sacked. Finally, Francesco Valori, in a brief and furious speech, clarified his party's resolve ominously: if the Signoria would not defend the liberties of the Florentine people by executing those five "perfidious" citizens, there would be others willing to seize that cause, risking the lives of all who opposed them. The Florentine Cato had triumphed.

When the votes were recounted, the four stubborn "white beans" vanished; the entire panel voted "black"—nine to zero—deciding the immediate death of the five prisoners. This verdict also decided, though tacitly and with greater delay, the death of Francesco Valori himself. As the Judicial Eight departed for the Bargello to prepare the execution site, the five condemned men were led barefoot and shackled through the center of the Council Chamber. It was a deliberate act by their friends: they hoped that seeing such cruel ignominy visited upon two venerable elders, Bernardo del Nero and Niccolò Ridolfi—who had supported the new order before the Medici influence waned—and two brilliant young leaders, Tornabuoni and Pucci, would move the populace. Yet, it was futile. Any pity stirred now was of a hopeless kind, leading not to rescue, but to the slower, darker fuel of revenge.

While this scene unfolded above, Romola stood below, leaning against a massive pillar in the palace courtyard. She waited for the moment her godfather would emerge on his way to execution. Through powerful intervention, she had secured permission to see him that evening and stay until the Council's fate was sealed. Now, she waited alongside his confessor, ready to follow the guard leading him to the Bargello. Her heart was set on holding onto the presence of this childless old man until the very last second, just as her father would have wished. She had silenced all objections.

Giovan Battista Ridolfi, a devoted follower of Savonarola who went forward in bitter sorrow to witness the death of his elder brother Niccolò, had promised to guard her. He stood beside her now. Tito was also in the palace, though Romola had not seen him. Since the evening of the seventeenth, they had avoided one another. Tito knew only by inference from the friar's neutral reports that her pleas had failed. Surrounded by officials and dignitaries, both Florentine and foreign, who awaited the Council's conclusion, Tito maintained a subdued gravity, caught in a painful conflict between public duty and private feeling. When anyone mentioned his wife in relation to these events, he claimed that her violent mental agitation meant that merely holding office under a government condemning her godfather aroused a "diseased hostility" in her toward him. Thus, for her sake, he deemed it best not to approach her.

"The old Bardi blood!" scoffed Cennini, shaking his head. "I shall not be surprised if this affair shakes *her* loose from the Friar, as surely as it will loosen several others I could name."

"It is excusable in a woman," remarked a young French envoy, smiling and bowing to Tito, "who is doubtless beautiful, since she is the wife of Messer Tito. To believe that her affections must override the good of the State, and that no one should be beheaded unless they are someone's cousin... but such a view cannot be encouraged in the male population. It seems to me your Florentine polity is greatly weakened by it."

"That is true," replied Niccolò Machiavelli. "But where personal ties are strong, the hostilities they generate must be accounted for. Many of these half-measures are merely hot-headed blunders. The only safe blows to strike against men and factions are those too heavy to be avenged."

"Niccolò," said Cennini, "there is a clever wickedness in your talk sometimes that makes me distrust your pleasant young face as if it were a mask of Satan."

"Not at all, my good Domenico," Machiavelli smiled, placing a hand on the elder's shoulder. "Satan was a blunderer, an introducer of novelty who met with a stupendous failure. Had he succeeded, we would all be worshipping him, and his portrait would be far more flattering."

"Well, well," Cennini conceded. "I do not say your doctrine is not too clever for Satan; I only say it is wicked enough for him."

"I tell you," Machiavelli continued, "my doctrine is the philosophy of all men who seek an end slightly farther away than their own noses. Ask our Friar, our prophet, how his universal renewal is to be achieved. He will tell you: first, establish a free and pure government. Since this cannot be done by making all Florentines love one another, it must be accomplished by cutting off every head that stubbornly stands in the way. Only if a man incurs hatred by sanctioning a severity that is not thorough enough to be final does he commit a blunder. And I suspect the Friar has committed exactly that blunder. On this occasion, he might have gained lustre by exerting himself to maintain the appeal. Instead, he has lost lustre and gained no strength."

Before anyone else could speak, the announcement came that the prisoners were leaving the Council Chamber. Most present hurried toward the door, eager to secure the clearest path to the Bargello behind the prisoner's guard, for the sight of the execution drew both the deeply passionate and the coldly curious. Tito, however, remained behind. He possessed a natural repugnance to scenes of death and pain. Five days prior, whenever he had imagined this execution as a possibility, he had hoped it would never happen, believing the maximum sentence would be exile—a outcome sufficient for his own safety. But now, he felt that confirming Bernardo del Nero's death would serve as a welcome guarantee of his security.

The new knowledge and attitude revealed to him by Romola on the day of his return had instilled in him a fresh dread of the power she held to render his position precarious. If any act of hers succeeded in making him an object of suspicion and loathing, he foresaw not only frustration but frustration under unpleasant circumstances. Her belief in Baldassarre had clearly hardened her wavering feelings against further submission; if her godfather lived, she would easily win him to share her beliefs. Romola seemed increasingly an unmanageable fact in his destiny. But if Bernardo del Nero were dead, the difficulties that would beset her in opposing her husband would likely be insurmountable to her shrinking pride. Therefore, Tito felt relieved knowing the Eight had gone to the Bargello to order the immediate erection of the scaffold. Four other men—his intimates and confederates—were to die alongside Bernardo del Nero. Yet, a man's own safety is a god that sometimes makes very grim demands. Tito felt them to be grim; even in pursuing what was agreeable, this paradoxical life forced upon him the desire for what was disagreeable. Having experienced this before, as he heard through the open doorway the shuffle of many feet and the clanking of metal on the stairs, he answered the young French envoy's questions without betraying any emotion other than a sad resignation to the necessities of the State.

Those sounds struck Romola as if her power of hearing had been heightened along with every other sense. She needed no arm to support her; she shed no tears. She felt an intensity of life that transcended both grief and joy—an awareness that the mind becomes akin to ancient forces that shaped existence before the birth of pleasure and pain. Since her godfather's fate was decided, the previous turmoil within her had subsided into an identification of self with him during these supreme moments. She was inwardly asserting for him that, if he suffered the punishment of treason, he did not deserve the name of traitor; he was the victim of a collision between two forms of faithfulness. It was not given to him to die for the noblest cause, yet he died because of his nobility. A meaner man might have found it easier to avoid this guilt. Rom

On the eighth day following that unforgettable night, Romola stood upon the precipice of the Mediterranean Sea. Before her stretched the gentle, rhythmic pulse of the summer waves, just beyond the modest cluster of houses that constituted the fishing village of Viareggio. Once more, she had fled from Florence. This time, however, no commanding voice had summoned her return. Clad once again in the grey habit of a religious sister, she felt no compunction regarding the disguise; indeed, in the depths of her heart-sickness, she scarcely cared for appearances.

A fresh rebellion had ignited within her spirit, accompanied by a profound despair. *Why* should she concern herself with bearing one symbol of identity over another, or with being addressed by her own name? She had lost faith in the possibility of finding any consistent duty attached to that name. Where was the force capable of creating that sacred motive men call "duty"—a power that possesses no internal binding strength unless rooted in some form of believing love? Every tie of strong affection had been severed. In her marriage, the highest of all bonds, she could no longer perceive the mystical union that guarantees indissolubility, nor even the obligation of a voluntary pledge. Had she not demonstrated that the very things to which she had bound herself were impossible?

The impulse to liberate herself surged forth with overwhelming force, yet she recognized that this freedom offered only an exchange of calamity. There is no recompense for a woman who perceives the chief relationship of her life as nothing more than a grievous error. She had lost her crown. The deepest secret of human blessedness had half-whispered its truth to her, only to slip past her forever. Now, her greatest support amidst such supreme sorrow had vanished. The vision of any grand purpose, any end of existence capable of ennobling endurance and elevating the mundane labors of daily life with divine ardor, was completely eclipsed. All effort now seemed merely a futile dragging at tangled threads; every fellowship, whether for resistance or advocacy, appeared as mere unfairness and exclusion.

What, after all, was the man who had embodied for her the highest form of heroism—not the stoic endurance of the isolated warrior, but the willing, self-offering love of the saint? What was the cause for which he struggled? Romola had lost her trust in Savonarola. She had lost that fervent admiration that had previously rendered her blind to his aberrations, allowing her to focus solely on the majestic arc of his destiny. Now, as her deep affection for her godfather cast her into direct opposition with the Friar, she viewed the repulsive and contradictory details of his teachings with a painful clarity that magnified their flaws.

In the bitterness of her disillusionment, she declared that his struggle to renovate the Church and the world was a pursuit of a mere name—a title that signified nothing more than the practical measures designed to strengthen his own position in Florence. Indeed, these included questionable deeds and words undertaken merely to shield his influence from the consequences of his own mistakes. That political reform, which had once sparked a new passion in her soul, now reduced itself to narrow schemes for the safety of Florence, standing in contemptible contradiction to his alternating professions of blind trust in Divine providence.

It was inevitable that she should judge the Friar unjustly on matters of individual suffering, viewing them through the lens of personal tenderness, while he regarded them through the cold eyes of theoretical conviction. When he proclaimed that the cause of his party was the cause of God's Kingdom, she heard only the hollow ring of egoism. Perhaps such words are rarely spoken without that baser tone; yet, they remain the implicit formula of all energetic belief. If such belief, pursuing a grand and distant goal, frequently risks descending into demon-worship—where the devotee allows his children to pass through the fire with a readiness that barely resembles sacrifice—then tender compassion for the near and dear holds its own dangers, tending toward timidity and skepticism regarding the larger aims without which life cannot ascend to true religion. Thus, poor Romola was being blinded by her tears.

No one who has ever experienced the loss of faith in a fellow-man whom they have profoundly loved and revered will lightly claim that such a shock can leave the faith in the Invisible Goodness unshaken. As the high trust in humanity sinks, the dignity of life diminishes accordingly. We cease to believe in our own better selves, for that too is part of the common nature degraded in our thoughts; consequently, all the finer impulses of the soul grow dull. Romola felt even the springs of her once-active pity drying up, leaving her to barren, egoistic complaint. *Had not she suffered her own sorrows?* Few had cared for her while she had cared for many. She had done enough; she had striven for the impossible and was weary of this stifling, crowded existence.
