She longed for that repose in mere sensation she had occasionally dreamed of during the sultry afternoons of her early girlhood, when she imagined herself floating naively in the waters like a nymph.

The clear waves seemed to invite her. She wished she could lie down upon them and drift from sleep into death. Yet, Romola could not directly seek death; the fullness of young life within her forbade such a direct act. She could only wish that death would come to her. At the spot where she had paused, the shoreline curved deeply, and a small boat with a sail was moored there. In her longing to glide across the waters turning golden under the level rays of the setting sun, she recalled a tale from Boccaccio, one she had loved to linger over when her father slept and she crept from her stool to the floor to read the *Decameron*. It was the story of the beautiful Gostanza, who, in her lovesick despair, desired no further life but lacked the courage to end her youth. She had placed herself in a boat, pushed off to sea, and lain down, wrapping her mantle around her head, hoping to be wrecked, so that her fear would be powerless to flee from death.

That memory had remained a mere thought in Romola's mind, never blossoming into a distinct wish. But now, as she paused again in her pacing to and fro, she saw another boat gliding black against the red-gold horizon, carrying a single man toward the bend where the first, smaller vessel was moored. Walking forward, she eventually saw the man land, haul his boat ashore, and begin to unload cargo. He was likely the owner of the smaller boat as well; he would be departing soon, and her opportunity would vanish with him—her chance to purchase that small craft. She had not yet admitted to herself that she intended to use it, but she felt a sudden eagerness to secure the possibility of doing so, revealing the half-unconscious growth of a thought into a desire.

"Is that little boat yours also?" she asked the fisherman, who had looked up, slightly startled by the tall grey figure, and made a reverent bow to this holy Sister wandering mysteriously in the evening solitude.

"It is my boat," he replied. It was an old vessel, hardly seaworthy, yet valuable to anyone willing to repair it. Through the blessing of Saint Anthony, whose chapel stood in the village nearby, his fishing had prospered, and he now possessed a better boat, formerly owned by Gianni, who had died. However, he had not yet sold the old one.

Romola asked him its value. While he was occupied, she thrust the sum into a small satchel lying on the ground, which contained the remnants of his dinner. Afterwards, she watched him furled his sail and asked him how to set it if he wished to venture out to sea. Pacing up and down again, she waited until he departed.

The imagination of herself gliding away in that boat upon the darkening waters grew increasingly into a longing, much as the thought of a cool stream in oppressive heat becomes a painful thirst. To be freed from the burden of choice when all motives were bruised; to commit herself, in a state of sleep, to a destiny that would either bring death or new necessities capable of awakening a new life within her—it was a thought that beckoned her the more because the soft evening air made her yearn to rest in the still solitude, rather than returning to the noise and heat of the village.

Finally, the slow-moving fisherman gathered his belongings and walked away. Soon, the gold of the sunset shrank and faded into dusk in the sea and sky. There was no living creature in sight, no sound but the lulling monotony of the lapping waves. In this sea, there was no tide strong enough to carry her away if she waited for its ebb; yet Romola believed the breeze from the land was rising slightly.

She entered the boat, unfurled the sail, and fastened it as she had learned in that first brief lesson. She saw that it caught the light breeze, and that was all she required. Loosening the boat from its moorings, she urged it forward with an oar until she was far out from the land, until the sea turned dark even to the west, and the stars began to disclose themselves like a palpitating life over the vast heavens.

Resting at last, she threw back her cowl and removed the kerchief beneath, which had confined her hair. She doubled both under her head for a pillow against one of the boat's ribs. Her fair head was still very young and could endure a hard cushion. Thus she lay, with the soft night air breathing upon her as she drifted on the water, watching the deepening quiet of the sky.

She was alone now. She had freed herself from all claims

About ten o'clock on the morning of February 27th, the flow of pedestrians in the Florentine streets shifted decisively toward San Marco. It was the final day of the Carnival, and everyone knew that a second Bonfire of Vanities was being prepared before the Old Palace; yet, at this hour, it was plain that the center of public attention lay elsewhere. The Piazza di San Marco teemed with a multitude exhibiting no movement save that caused by newcomers pressing forward from every opening, crowding against the tightly packed front ranks. These ranks formed a dense semicircle before the church, enclosing the Dominican friars of San Marco as they assembled. However, the temporary wooden pulpit erected above the church door remained empty.

Soon, it would be occupied by the man whom the Pope's decree had banished from the Duomo's pulpit, whom fellow clergy were forbidden to associate with, and whom the citizens dared not listen to without fear of excommunication. This man had declared, "A wicked, unbelieving Pope, who seized the pontifical chair through bribery, is not Christ's Vicar. His curses are broken swords; he grasps a hilt without a blade. His commands contradict the Christian life: it is lawful to disobey them—nay, *it is unlawful to obey them*." Despite the Pope hanging terrible threats over Florence, demanding the city renounce this "pestilential schismatic" and send him to Rome to be "converted," the people still flocked to hear him preach in his own church of San Marco. On this very morning, they accepted communion from his excommunicated hands.

For what if this Brother truly held sway over divine lightnings greater than that official successor of Saint Peter? It was a momentous question that the masses could never resolve by the Friar's ultimate test: determining what aligned with the highest spiritual law. No. In such a crisis, if God had chosen the Friar as His prophet to rebuke the High Priest wearing the mystic raiment unworthily, He would attest His choice with an unmistakable sign. So long as belief in the Prophet offered no threat of external calamity, but rather the confident hope of exceptional safety, no sign was required; his preaching was merely music to which the people marched willingly. But now, since belief threatened their commerce, shook their standing among the Italian states, and risked an interdict on their city, the inevitable question arose: *"What miracle showest thou?"*

Slowly at first, then faster, this fatal demand swelled in Savonarola's ear, provoking a response. Outwardly, he declared that the miracle would come at the fitting time; inwardly, he clung to a faith—not entirely unwavering, for what faith ever is?—that if the need for a miracle became urgent, the magnitude of his task was too great for Divine power to abandon. His faith wavered, but not his speech. It is the lot of every speaker addressing a crowd to often speak in virtue of yesterday's faith, hoping it returns tomorrow.

Prepared for a scene designed to answer the popular impatience for a supernatural guarantee of his mission, the wooden pulpit stood ready. Yet, while ordinary friars in black mantles entered and arranged themselves, the multitude's faces were not yet eagerly fixed on the pulpit. There was a sense that Savonarola would not appear immediately, and curiosity lingered in identifying the various monks—some belonging to high Florentine families, others connected to the artisans and shopkeepers forming the bulk of the crowd. Only when the procession of monks concluded, fluttering their books and beginning to chant, did people whisper, "Fra Girolamo must be coming now."

That expectation, rather than any spell cast by the familiar wail of psalmody, spread a silence and anticipation over the multitude like a pale, solemn light. All faces turned upward toward the empty pulpit. Instantly, the space was no longer empty. A figure cloaked head-to-foot in black cowl and mantle stepped inside, kneeling with bowed head and face averted. To the eager crowd, the time seemed endless as the black figure knelt and the monks chanted. Yet the stillness held; the Friar's private audiences with Heaven were charged with an electric awe that unnerved even those who might have stoned him.

Finally, a vibration rippled through the crowd, each person seemingly touching their neighbor with an aspen-leaf tremor, as watchers in the sky perceive the silent arrival of the awaited presence. The Friar rose, turned toward the people, and partially pushed back his cowl. The monotonous chanting ceased. To those nearest the pulpit, it seemed as if the sounds that had filled their ears moments before suddenly merged into the force of Savonarola's flashing glance as he surveyed the silence.

He stretched out his hands. In their exquisite delicacy, they appeared transfigured—from mere organs for grasping into vessels of sensitivity too acute to require rough contact. They emerged like an appealing speech from the part of his soul masked by his strong, passionate face, now etched with deeper lines around the mouth and brow than forty-four years of ordinary life would warrant. At the first extension of these hands, some in the front ranks fell to their knees, and scattered devout disciples further back followed suit. But the great majority stood firm. Some resisted the impulse to kneel before this excommunicated man (fearing that a great judgment might fall upon them in this act of blessing); others bristled with scorn and hatred for the ambitious deceiver staging this new comedy, before which they nonetheless felt powerless, as before the triumph of a fleeting fashion.

Then came the voice, clear and low at first, uttering the words of absolution—"*Misereatur vestri*"—and more fell to their knees. As it rose higher and clearer, the upright heads grew fewer and fewer. By the time he spoke "*Benedicat vos omnipotens Deus*," it erupted into a masculine cry, as if protesting its power to bless under the grip of a demon seeking to stifle it. It rang like a trumpet to the farthest reaches of the Piazza, and beneath its sound, every head bowed.

After the benediction, Savonarola himself fell to his knees, hiding his face in temporary exhaustion. Such great jets of emotion were a necessary part of his existence; he had told the people long ago, "Without preaching I cannot live." But it was a life that shattered him. Minutes later, some rose to their feet, though a larger number remained kneeling, all faces intent on watching him. He took a crystal vessel containing the consecrated Host into his hands and addressed the crowd.

"You remember, my children, three days ago I besought you," he said, holding the Sacrament openly before them all, "to pray fervently to the Most High that if this work of mine does not come from Him, He will send a fire to consume me, that I may vanish into eternal darkness, away from His light which I have hidden with my falsity. Again I beseech you to make that prayer, and to make it *now*."

It was a breathless moment. Perhaps no one truly prayed, unless some, in a spirit of devout obedience, made the effort. Every consciousness was dominated by the sense that Savonarola was praying—in a voice not loud, but distinctly audible in the wide stillness. "Lord, if I have not wrought in sincerity of soul, if my word cometh not from Thee, strike me in this moment with Thy thunder, and let the fires of Thy wrath enclose me."

He ceased speaking and stood motionless, holding the sacred Mystery, eyes uplifted, his entire aspect quivering with excitement. Everyone else was equally motionless and silent. Sunlight, which for the last quarter-hour had pierced the greyness in fitful streaks, struck the convent wall, causing some awe-stricken spectators to startle timidly. Soon, however, a wider parting occurred. With a gentle quickness, like a smile, a stream of brightness poured onto the crystal vase and then spread over Savonarola's face with mild glorification.

An instantaneous shout rang through the Piazza: "Behold the answer!" The warm radiance thrilled through Savonarola's frame, as did the shout. It was his last moment of untroubled triumph. In his rapturous confidence, he felt carried to a grander scene yet to come, before an audience representing all Christendom, where he would once again be sealed as the messenger of supreme righteousness, feeling himself fully charged with Divine strength. It was but a moment that expanded into a vast vision.

While the shout still echoed in his ears, he turned away within the church, feeling the strain too great to endure longer. When the Friar had disappeared and the sunlight seemed to lose its special illumination, spreading impartially over both clean and unclean alike, a confusion of voices began alongside the crowd's general movement. Certain strong discords and varying scales of laughter revealed that during the previous silence and universal kneeling, hostility and scorn had only submitted unwillingly to a momentary spell.

"It seems to me the plaudits are giving way to criticism," observed Tito, who had watched the scene attentively from an upper loggia in a house opposite the church. "Nevertheless, it was a striking moment, was it not, Messer Pietro? Fra Girolamo is a man to make one understand that there was a time when the monk's frock was a symbol of power over men's minds rather than over the keys of women's cupboards."

"Assuredly," replied Pietro Cennini. "And until I have seen proof that Fra Girolamo possesses much less faith in God

A month after the Carnival, toward the end of March, Tito descended the marble steps of the Old Palace. He carried a weighty purpose in his heart, directing him toward San Marco. Yet, driven by some unspoken instinct, he refused the direct route—a simple, slightly curved path from the palace. Instead, he chose a circuitous journey through the Piazza di Santa Croce, anticipating a surge of humanity spilling forth from the great church following the early Lenten sermon.

Indeed, the Grand Church of Santa Croce had recently become the theater of the largest crowds in Florence. Fra Girolamo's voice, once thunderous in his own sanctuary of San Marco, had been silenced by a hostile Signoria. Obedient to a fresh decree from the Pope, who threatened an immediate interdict upon the city unless this "wretched worm" and "monstrous idol" ceased preaching and sought pardon in Rome, the friar was forbidden to speak. Consequently, the most electrifying occupation of the Lenten season had shifted: it was no longer listening to Fra Girolamo, but watching him dismantled, argued against, and vilified. This spectacle was available at Santa Croce, where the Franciscan assigned to deliver the Quaresimal sermons had proposed a dramatic climax: a trial by fire alongside Fra Girolamo.

Had not the schismatical Dominican claimed that his prophetic doctrine would be proven by a miracle at the proper time? Here, apparently, was that time. The Franciscan proposed that Savonarola walk through the flames; if emerged unscathed, the divine origin of his teachings would be irrefutable. If consumed, his falsehood would be exposed. To ensure the Franciscan could not evade this test, he declared his willingness to share the pyre, offering himself as a sacrifice to secure the necessary premise for the logic of the trial.

True to his nature, Savonarola had ignored these pulpit assaults. However, the zealous preacher of Santa Croce was none other than Fra Francesco di Puglia. The previous year, at Prato, he had faced a similar challenge from Savonarola's fervent follower, Fra Domenico, but had been recalled by his superiors before the rhetoric heated into physical confrontation. When honest Fra Domenico, currently preaching to women in the Via del Cocomero, heard of this renewed threat, he instantly seized the gauntlet on behalf of his master. He proclaimed his readiness to walk through the fire with Fra Francesco.

The populace, eager for what seemed a swift and decisive method of argumentation, began to swell with anticipation. Yet, Savonarola, acutely aware of the perils inherent in mere debate, commanded Fra Domenico to withdraw his acceptance and sever ties with the affair. The Franciscan acquiesced, noting that his challenge had been directed solely at Fra Girolamo himself, not his subordinate.

Following this, the fervor surrounding the Lenten sermons waned slightly. But that morning, as Tito entered the Piazza di Santa Croce, he found exactly what he anticipated: a throng pouring from the church. Rather than dispersing, many gathered near the entrance of the Franciscan monastery. Tito moved through the crowd with a careless, leisurely gait, yet his eyes remained fixed on the monastic doors, scanning for any sign of interest.

His expectations were misplaced. The crowd's focus was not on the monks emerging from the gate, but on a large placard affixed by order of the Signoria. Covered in legible official handwriting, it promised answers to their questions. Curiosity, however, was dampened by the fact that the document was primarily in Latin. While nearly everyone knew roughly what the notice contained, a gnawing hunger for precise interpretation lingered, creating an irritating sense of superiority regarding their neighbors' inability to decipher the learned tongue. Familiarity with Latin phrases picked up from sermons offered little aid when confronted with written script. Furthermore, the orthography of the vernacular was in a chaotic state for the literate masses, leaving the majority of those closest to the board unable to read it at all.

"It is the Frate's doctrines that he must prove by being burned," observed Goro, a prominent figure among the onlookers. "The Signoria has intervened, and the writing confirms it. It matches what the Padre preached."

"Nay, Goro," replied a slick shopkeeper with compassionate correction. "Thou hast twisted thy legs into hose. The Frate must prove his doctrines by *not* being burned. He is to walk through the fire and emerge sound and whole on the other side."

"Yes, yes," chimed in a young sculptor, wearing his white-streaked cap and tunic with jaunty pride. "But Fra Girolamo objects to walking through the fire. Being sound and whole already, he sees no need to undergo the ordeal to remain in the same condition. He leaves such odd jobs to Fra Domenico."

"Then I say he flinches like a coward!" Goro wheezed. "Suffocation! That was his trick at the Carnival. He had us all in the Piazza to watch lightning strike him, and nothing happened."

"Hold your tongue," interjected a tall shoemaker, balancing bunches of slippers over his shoulders. "Friend, you are as wise as a calf with water on its brain. The Frate will flinch from nothing. Perhaps he says nothing beforehand, but when the moment arrives, he will walk through the fire without asking a grey-frock to accompany him. Though, I'd give a shoestring to know what this Latin actually says."

"There is so much of it," sighed the shopkeeper. "Unless I'm good at guessing, I'm lost. Is there no scholar nearby?"

A collective turn of heads revealed Tito approaching from behind.

"Here is one," smiled the young sculptor, raising his cap.

"It is the Secretary of the Ten," announced the shopkeeper, bowing respectfully. "He is surely heading to the convent; make way." Such deference was rare among Florentines, reserved usually for the highest officials. The exceptional reverence was demanded by the splendor and grace of Tito's appearance; his black mantle, adorned with a gold fibula, resembled a royal robe, and his ordinary black velvet cap looked like a unique crown. The hardening of his cheeks and mouth, the primary change in his visage since arriving in Florence, seemed to superficial observers merely to add a masculine edge to his beauty. Tito acknowledged the greeting with a polite lift of his own cap.

"Thank you, my friend," he said smoothly. "I merely wished, as you did, to inspect the contents beneath this placard. Ah, as I suspected. I was informed that the government permits anyone to subscribe their name as a candidate to enter the fire—an act of liberality worthy of the magnificent Signoria—while reserving, of course, the right to select the participants. Doubtless many believers will be eager to sign. What is it to enter the fire for one whose faith is firm? A man fears fire because he believes it burns him; but if he believes otherwise?"

Tito lifted his shoulders, pausing oratorically. "For this reason, I have never hesitated to believe the Frate when he stated he would enter the fire to prove his doctrine. In his place, if you believed the fire would not burn you, which of you, my friends, would not step into it as readily as you would walk along the dry bed of the Mugnone?"

As Tito surveyed his audience, a transformation rippled through them, akin to an eager dog sensing a pungent scent. With the matter of burning becoming practical, few were rash enough to commit to a general theory on faith and flame. The scene might have overwhelmed someone less composed than Tito.

"Then, Messer Segretario," the young sculptor remarked, "Fra Francesco appears the greater hero, offering to enter the fire for the truth despite knowing it will burn him."

"I do not deny it," Tito replied blandly. "But if it transpires that Fra Francesco is mistaken, he will have been burned for the wrong cause, and the Church has never counted such victims as martyrs. We must suspend judgment until the trial truly takes place."

"It is true, Messer Segretario," the shopkeeper murmured with subdued impatience. "But will you favor us by translating the Latin?"

"Assuredly," Tito smiled. "It expresses the specific conclusions or doctrines the Frate teaches, which the trial by fire aims to verify. They are doubtless familiar to you. First, that Florence—"

"Let us have the Latin phrase by phrase, then explain the meaning," requested the shoemaker, a frequent listener to Fra Girolamo.

"Willingly," Tito agreed. "You may then judge if I render the correct meaning."

"Yes, yes; that is fair," nodded Goro.

"_Ecclesia Dei indiget renovatione_," Tito recited. "That is, the Church of God needs purification or regeneration."

"It is true," several voices chorused.

"That means the priests ought to live better lives; no miracle is needed to prove that. That is what the Frate has always said," noted the shoemaker.

"_Flagellabitur_," Tito continued. "It will be scourged. _Renovabitur_: it will be purified. _Florentia quoque post flagellam renovabitur et prosperabitur_: Florence also, after the scourging, shall be purified and shall prosper."

"That means we shall regain Pisa," the shopkeeper declared.

"And recover the wool from England as we once did, I should hope," added an elderly man in an old-fashioned berretta, speaking for the first

Tito's arrival at San Marco had been anticipated. Fra Niccolò, the secretary of the friar known as the Prophet, ushered him up the spiraling stone stairs and into the long, echoing corridors lined with cells. Here, amidst the silence of the monastery, the delicate frescoes painted by Fra Angelico caught the eye. Like rainbows shimmering on melting clouds, these paintings seemed to reflect an ethereal world where the Madonna sat crowned in radiance and the Divine Infant gazed forth with eternal promise.

It was a quiet hour; most cells stood empty, their narrow windows casting light onto bare walls, hard straw pallets, and wooden crucifixes. Even in the innermost chamber, accessible only through an antechamber where the Prior typically worked, the scene remained stark. A single beam of light illuminated the back of a figure clad in the long white tunic and scapular of the Dominicans. He knelt with his head bowed before a cross. To the casual observer, he appeared to be any ordinary Brother Girolamo—a man concerned only with avoiding sin during choir practice or suppressing petty jealousies while illuminating manuscripts, striving merely to climb the narrow ladder of prayer toward Paradise.

Yet, beneath that simple white garment beat a heart pulsing with a consciousness unknown to the average monk. Perhaps no man outside of those who have traversed a tumultuous inner journey could fully grasp it. Within this soul, irredeemable errors and noble aspirations were tangled together; self-serving expedients were woven into the fabric of a monumental mission that the entire being refused to abandon. It was a state where conscience found no perfect rest, regardless of the path chosen.

Savonarola was not merely in the posture of prayer. Latin words of supplication escaped his lips—"Create in me a clean heart"—yet his spirit was not truly praying. He had collapsed to his knees seeking a divine calm that would validate his resolve against the storm of conflicting thoughts and passions raging within him. However, the visions and impulses driving him were too imperative to be silenced. While his hands pressed against his face and his voice murmured the ancient plea, his mind was consumed by the traps his enemies had set. He argued internally, constructing defenses against their taunts and accusations.

His struggle extended beyond his adversaries. That morning, he had received fresh evidence that his own followers and friends were as eager to push for the "Trial by Fire" as his detractors. They desired, silently and openly, that he finally accept the challenge to evoke the miracle that would dispel doubt and crush malice. Had he not proclaimed that God would reveal Himself at the appointed time? To the straightforward minds of the Florentines, anxious to settle political disputes, no moment seemed more fitting than this. If Brother Domenico emerged from the flames unscathed, it would indeed be a miracle, and the fervent faith of the *Piagnoni* viewed it as a promising omen.

But Savonarola was painfully aware that his explanations had failed to quell their secret longing to see him step forward. Yet, satisfying them was impossible. With bitter anguish, he realized he could no longer resist the pressure to allow the trial to proceed regarding Brother Domenico.

He had not lied when he claimed belief in a future supernatural confirmation of his work. His nature allowed him easily to believe in distant, undefined miracles supported by logical reasoning, and even more readily to trust in internal miracles like his own prophetic inspirations. Conversely, it was insurmountably difficult for him to believe in a specific, tangible miracle—one requiring detailed visualization and demanding extraordinary action. For a man of his constitution, the prospect of walking through fire was terrifying. His acute physical sensitivity made the concept of pain and destruction vivid and overwhelming. Just as a man might theoretically accept that hot iron burns, yet practically dare to touch it with a wet finger based on immediate sensation, Savonarola found that when the Trial by Fire was presented as an immediate demand, his imagination recoiled. He could not believe anyone could pass through flames unharmed, nor could he believe that even if he resolved to offer himself, he would not shrink at the final moment.

The common people, however, lacked such nuance. To the general mind, finding moral answers easy and judging conduct by clear-cut choices was a mark of vigor. Nothing seemed clearer than that a man who promised a miracle yet hesitated when tested had spoken falsehoods. How could Brother Domenico and Brother Mariano, along with hundreds of others, possess such courage unless their faith was superior? Savonarola could not reconcile his actions with his friends' expectations, nor could he fully explain them to himself. Human emotions rush to cloak themselves in convenient phrases, but articulating the truth of one's inner turmoil requires more than mere sincerity.

In these very moments, as Savonarola knelt and prayed aloud, he stopped hearing his own words. They were drowned by the clamor of argumentative voices shaping reasons for an external audience. *"To appeal to Heaven via a reckless acceptance of a trap set by ignoble foes is tempting God... Let the Pope's legate come, let the ambassadors of all powers arrive, promising that a General Council and church reform depend on this miracle—I will enter the flames, trusting God will not withhold His seal until then. Until that time, I reserve myself for higher duties..."*

But no! When Savonarola focused his mind on the Piazza, imagining a human body stepping into the fire, his belief shattered. It was not a scene his imagination could view calmly; he felt it with shuddering tremors running through his sensitive fingertips. The miracle could not happen. Indeed, the trial itself should not occur; he was justified in using every means to prevent it. Fuel might be gathered, crowds assembled, formalities completed—all of this might become inevitable, forcing him to act lest he bring dishonor upon himself and, consequently, upon the cause of God.

However, it was not truly intended that the Franciscan should enter the fire. While Savonarola hesitated, there would be ways to stop Brother Domenico. At the absolute worst, if Domenico were forced to proceed, he could carry the consecrated Host, offering a mystery that might suspend the fire's effects. More likely, resistance would halt the attempt entirely, serving as a final barrier to the ordeal. These strategies could not be voiced; he must appear to wait frankly for the trial and trust in its outcome.

This dissonance between his inner reality and outer appearance betrayed the Christian simplicity he had preached throughout his youth and maturity. In the heat of the day, with burning cheeks and shouting crowds ringing in his ears, how blessed is one who remembers the yearnings of the cool, silent morning and knows they have not betrayed them?

*"O God, it is for the sake of the people—they are blind, their faith rests on me. If I don the sackcloth and throw myself among the ashes, who will raise the banner and lead the battle? Was I not led by a path I did not know to the work lying ahead?"*

The conflict was endless. Amidst his prayers, his troubled mind washed its wounds in thoughts of the immense task awaiting him, a task no other man could fulfill if he abandoned it. It was rare for a man to be inspired with the vision and daring required to become a sacred rebel. Soon, the words of prayer faded completely. Savonarola continued to kneel, though his mind drifted to the consequences rippling across Europe, losing the immediacy of his difficulties in the glow of that vast vision.

Then, a knock at the door announced the expected visitor. Savonarola drew his mantle around him, adhering to his custom for receiving guests. Possessing a nature accustomed to exerting influence through speech, he met Tito with a gaze as composed and strong as if he had risen from a moment of resolution rather than internal strife.

Tito did not kneel. Instead, he offered a greeting of profound deference, which Savonarola accepted quietly, omitting sacerdotal formulas. After inviting him to sit, Savonarola asked immediately, "Is your business of sufficient weight to bypass intermediaries?"

"Assuredly, Father," Tito replied. "Otherwise, I would not presume. I will not waste your time with preliminaries. From a remark by Messer Domenico Mazzinghi, I understood you might wish to utilize the next special courier bound for France, carrying dispatches from the Ten. I beg your pardon for my officiousness, but since Messer Domenico is currently at his villa, I wished to inform you that a courier bearing urgent letters departs for Lyons at daybreak tomorrow."

The muscles of Fra Girolamo's face remained perfectly controlled, a hallmark of powerful personalities who habitually caution their speech and share intentions only when necessary. Yet, under strong mental stimuli, his eyes were prone to dilating and brightening—a reaction no willpower could suppress. He stared steadily at Tito, pausing to consider whether this news served any of his hidden purposes. Tito, whose gaze was rarely observant yet missed nothing, had expected exactly this dilation and flash of intensity. Seeing it, Tito immediately began adjusting his gold fibula, seemingly implying patient expectation.

In truth, Savonarola had hoped to receive this intelligence from Domenico Mazzinghi himself, an ardent disciple he had already tasked with writing a private letter to the Florentine ambassador in France. This letter was to pave the way for a missive from Savonarola to the King of France, drafted and ready in his desk beside him. It called upon the monarch to aid in convening a General Council to reform the Church's abuses, beginning with the deposition of Pope Alexander VI, whom Savonarola deemed a corrupt usurper elected through simony and governed by vice. Tito did not know this content, but his sharp intuition, guided by subtle

Little more than a week later, on the seventh of April, the great Piazza della Signoria staged a spectacle stranger even than the infamous Bonfire of the Vanities. A larger multitude had gathered than had ever attempted to fit themselves into the vast square, surpassing even the crowds of San Giovanni Day. By midday, the scene was teeming with humanity. Since dawn, people had swarmed every available perch: men sat precariously on iron rods jutting sharply from walls, clung to slender pillars with arms and legs, balanced astride the necks of rough statues crowning grand entrances, and squeezed onto mere palm-widths of architraves or footholds on rustic stonework, gripping the heavy iron rings and staples driven into the masonry. They endured cramped limbs and scraped flesh as minor inconveniences compared to the promise of a miracle unfolding before them. While mankind often hears of miracles and believes in them vaguely, the Florentines were about to witness one—or at least half of one. If the monk emerged unscathed, it would be a true miracle; if he entered the flames and vanished, the inference would be equally damning: he was burned in the middle.

There could be no doubt the fire would be lit and the monks summoned. Before their eyes lay a long platform, eight feet wide and twenty yards long, backed by a terrifying grove of fuel. Great branches of dry oak formed the base, topped by crackling thorns, well-anointed tow, and rags chosen specifically to create fierce flames in Florentine illuminations. The platform began at the corner of the marble terrace before the Old Palace, near the Marzocco, the stone lion whose ancient face frowned upon the fuel stretching obliquely across the square. Flanking the scene were three massive bodies of armed men: five hundred hired soldiers of the Signoria before the palace; five hundred *Compagnacci* under Dolfo Spini on the opposite side; and three hundred armed citizens led by Marco Salviati, Savonarola's ally, guarding the loggia where the Franciscans and Dominicans were to stand. Much money and labor had been expended, involving high dignitaries. The stakes were clear: if the two monks were simply burned, God would speak plainly that Fra Girolamo was not His prophet.

Time was short. Near midday, half the monks were already in position. The half of the loggia facing the palace was filled with grey habits, while the section divided by boards remained empty save for a small altar. The Franciscans had entered in silence. Then, from the other side of the piazza, loud chanting rose from two hundred voices, signaling the arrival of the Dominicans. The repetition of "Let God arise, and let His enemies be scattered" struck some impartial ears as a display of arrogant confidence designed to incite dismay. Leading the procession was Fra Domenico, dressed in a flame-colored velvet cope, holding a cross. His simple mind was genuinely exalted by faith, intending truly to enter the flames for the glory of God and Fra Girolamo. Behind him walked Savonarola in white priestly vestments, carrying a vessel with the consecrated Host. Both chanted loudly, appearing firm and confident. Under the scrutiny of eager, anxious, and malignant eyes, Savonarola's countenance flashed with an intensified energy.

Human nature dictates that the false persona we dread in private solitude becomes effortless under the stimulus of expectant audiences. Few possess the innate need to dominate that Savonarola held; this need grows imperious as life complicates the separation of Self from Purpose. Consequently, on the day of the Trial by Fire, the duplicity inherent in public careers was sharper in Savonarola's consciousness than ever before. He struggled not against impending martyrdom, but against impending ruin. Thus, he projected thorough confidence while a leaden weight of foreboding pressed his heart, caused not only by the trial's probable outcome but by a recent event spreading satisfaction through the mind of a man watching him from a window of the Old Palace.

Two evenings prior, news arrived that the Florentine courier of the Ten had been arrested and robbed of his dispatches. Savonarola's letter was now in the hands of the Duke of Milan and soon the Pope's—a development heightening rage and justifying extreme measures. Tito Melema felt no malice toward the friar; it was merely the mild self-gratification of a skilled player winning a game. To Tito, the bundle of desires and contrivances known as human nature, molded into a plain-faced preacher, was not a pathetic figure but a puzzle solved. Fra Girolamo was immensely clever, mixing absurd superstitions with remarkable political notions, capable of keeping secrets. Tito harbored no spite; rather, Fra Girolamo's existence had been conveniently useful, providing Tito with a rung on the ladder from which he intended to leap to a smoother footing. With preparations complete, Tito awaited the setting sun to depart Florence. He had amused himself with today's comedy, a feat the thick-headed Dolfo Spini could never have achieved without his guidance.

The chanting swelled to a deafening roar, taken up by the *Piagnoni*, who carried red crosses and sang prayers for the confusion of God's enemies, expecting a signal response from Fra Domenico. Now kneeling before the altar where the Sacrament rested, the flame-clad friar waited for his summons. Across the loggia, the Franciscans offered only silence and grey robes. However, they held a counterbalance: two champions. Fra Giuliano was paired with Fra Domenico, while the original challenger, Fra Francesco, reserved his challenge solely for Savonarola.

Spectators perched on rods and pillars wondered aloud if all were ready, hoping the chanting would cease so the friars might move toward the platform. But the Dominicans did not advance. Pale Franciscan faces peered anxiously over the boards at the flamboyant cope, suspecting enchantment or magic. A monk might emerge whole, yet it could be the Devil's work. Movement began between the loggia and the palace terrace; the chanting quieted as everyone watched more intently. Yet, this movement proved an obstacle, not a beginning. Dignitaries appointed as moderators entered and exited the palace, debating fiercely with the Franciscans. Finally, it became clear that Fra Domenico was being fetched toward the palace. The fire likely burned unseen, and the miracle was imminent.

No. The flame-colored cope disappeared inside the palace, followed shortly by another Dominican. For a long time, the tedious chanting continued, and Fra Girolamo stood motionless in his white vestment. Eventually, something shifted. Fra Domenico emerged, returned to his brethren, and had changed clothes with a fellow monk, flanked by Franciscans to prevent any further enchantment.

"Aha!" thought the distant spectators, forgetting their hunger and cramps. "Fra Domenico does not enter the fire. It is Fra Girolamo after all. We shall see him move, and if he emerges, we shall have a fine view!"

But Fra Girolamo did not move, save for the gestures accompanying speech. His words were bold, firm, and perhaps ironically remonstrant, like Elijah challenging the priests of Baal. Yet, speech is the most irritating argument for those cramped in limbs and empty in stomachs. Why argue? If the miracle failed, it was Fra Girolamo's fault. He could end the delay by offering himself now that the fuel was ready, just as he had been eager when none existed.

More movement, more debate, and the afternoon slipped away as clouds gathered, casting a chill through the hungry crowd. Now, Fra Domenico insisted on carrying the crucifix into the fire, fearing its profanation. Reluctantly, Savonarola conceded, gaining a concession but immediately placing the vessel with the Host into Fra Domenico's hands. An idea flickered in his mind: perhaps the sacred Mystery could avert the fire's effects in extremis. But his real calculation was different. Taking up the Host, he spoke quietly, as if fulfilling a preordained role: "Since they refuse you entry with the crucifix, my brother, enter simply with the Sacrament."

New horror seized the Franciscans; new resolve hardened in Savonarola. "It was impious presumption to carry the Sacrament into the fire," they protested. "If burned, the scandal among the weak and ignorant would be immense."

"Not at all," Savonarola replied. "Even if burned, only the accidents would be consumed; the substance remains."

Here lay a theological quagmire, elastic and endless, unsuitable for resolution by trial. The only path was for both sides to remain firm: the Franciscans refusing the Host's entry, and Fra Domenico refusing to enter without it. Meanwhile, the darkened clouds and chilling air deepened the gloom. The chanting faded into inaudible argument, replaced by confused murmurs from the piazza. Expectation relaxed, replaced by an irritating sense that nothing decisive would occur. Scattered shouts broke out, then grew frequent and scornful: "Light the fire and drive them in!" "Let us have a smell of roast—we want our dinner!" "Come, Prophet, tell us if anything happens before the twenty-four hours are over!" "What is your last vision?" "He has a dozen visions in his belly; they're the small change for a miracle!" "Where are you, Brother? Stop wasting the fuel!"

Yet, the same futile shuffling continued between the loggia and the palace, a debate slow and unintelligible to the masses, like insects touching antennae. Soon, an interpretation arose for the unheard arguments: Fra Girolamo was the obstruction. Everyone appealed to him, and he hung back. The

The following day was Palm Sunday, known locally in the olive-rich Valdarno simply as "Olive Sunday." The morning sun broke forth with a clarity and sweetness born of the previous night's rain. Once again, Savonarola ascended the pulpit in San Marco. Before him gathered a flock whose faith remained unshaken, and in a tone of calm, sorrowful sincerity, he proclaimed his readiness to die. Amidst all visions, he beheld only his own impending doom. He offered the benediction, watching the faces of men and women rise toward him in veneration and love. Then, he descended the steps of the pulpit and turned forever away from that scene.

Before the sun had set, Florence erupted into chaos. The passions ignited the day prior had smoldered through the quiet morning, now bursting forth with a fury aided by design and tacitly encouraged by official complicity. The disturbance began at the Duomo, where certain members of the *Compagnacci* attempted to disrupt the evening sermon that the *Piagnoni* had assembled to hear. But once blood rose in the veins of the crowd and the gathering swelled into an affray, the cry rang out: "To San Marco! Fire to San Marco!"

Long before darkness fully claimed the sky, both the church and the convent were besieged by an enraged, swelling multitude. Resistance was fierce. The monks, long aware of the mounting hostility outside, kept arms within their walls. Some fought with such vigor in their long white tunics that they resembled Knights Templar, defying even Savonarola's commands to stand down. Their muscles, still strong beneath the rough Dominican serge, refused to yield to the impulse of surrender. Laymen who had chosen to remain also fought desperately; gunfire echoed near the high altar beside the great crucifix, stones and burning embers rained from the convent roofs, and swords clashed violently within the cloisters.

Despite the overwhelming force of the attackers, the assault raged until deep night. Meanwhile, the government's demonstrations had targeted the convent directly. Early in the conflict, guards were summoned—not to disperse the rioters, but to order everyone inside the convent to lay down their arms, to command all laymen to depart, and to demand that Savonarola himself leave Florentine territory within twelve hours. Had Savonarola obeyed then, he likely would have been torn apart immediately; he was willing to go, but his friends obstructed him. Many considered it a grave risk even for prominent laymen to exit via the garden wall, yet among those who chose to flee was Francesco Valori, hoping to rally aid from without.

By the time the struggle reached its deepest hour, the fight could scarcely last much longer. Soon, the *Compagnacci* might carry their swords into the library, where Savonarola prayed with the brethren who had either not taken up arms or had laid them down at his command. Just then, a second body of guards arrived, commissioned by the Signoria to seize Fra Girolamo and his two coadjutors, Fra Domenico and Fra Salvestro.

When the lantern-light revealed the Friar emerging from the convent door accompanied by a guard offering no protection save that of the prison, a roar of triumphant hatred swept through the crowd. The struggle became a frantic race: who would reach the prophet first in the rushing stream ascending the narrow street? Who would secure the best position to hurl insults, or perhaps strike and kick him? This was easy for many armed *Compagnacci*, who mixed freely with the guards. As Savonarola felt himself dragged and shoved through the jeering multitude, as lanterns were raised to reveal mocking faces, and as he endured spittle, blows, and kicks accompanied by grossest insults, it seemed to him that life's bitterest cup was finally drained. If men deemed him guilty and thirsted for his blood, death alone awaited him. Yet, the deepest bitterness cannot always be forced from without; the lowest depth of resignation is not found in martyrdom, but only when one covers one's head in silence and feels, "I am not worthy to be a martyr; the Truth shall prosper, but not by me."

That brief, imperfect triumph of humiliating the Friar, who soon vanished beneath the doorway of the Old Palace, proved merely like the taste of blood to a tiger. Why stop there? Were not the houses of the hypocrites' friends waiting to be sacked? Already, half the armed multitude, positioned too far in the rear to participate significantly in the siege of the convent, engaged in the more profitable task of attacking wealthy homes. They did not plunder with mindless frenzy, but with discriminating precision, targeting residences belonging to leading *Piagnoni*. This selectivity indicated that the riot was guided, the rabble with clubs and staves well-commanded by sword-girt *Compagnacci*.

Next in line for criminality stood the ambitious Francesco Valori, suspected of plotting with the Friar to establish himself as a lifelong Doge or Gonfaloniere. Then came the grey-haired man who, eight months prior, had raised his arm and voice in a ferocious demand for justice against five fellow citizens. Having narrowly escaped San Marco to experience what others termed "justice," he now watched his house surrounded by an angry, greedy mob. His wife was slain by an arrow, and he himself was murdered on his way to answer a summons to the Palazzo, struck down by the swords of men named Ridolfi and Tornabuoni.

Thus, this "Masque of the Furies," labeled Riot, was enacted throughout Florence during the hours of night and early dawn. However, the chief director remained invisible, issuing orders from a private retreat due to his high standing, unwilling to let his red plume wave amidst the work before sunrise. This sanctuary was the same house and room in a quiet street between Santa Croce and San Marco where we previously witnessed Tito's secret visit to Dolfo Spini.

Here, the Captain of the *Compagnacci* sat through this memorable night, receiving a revolving cast of visitors—some disguised as armed *Compagnacci*, others dressed obscurely without visible weapons. Abundant wine flowed on the table, cups available for any chance comer. Though Spini guarded against excess, he drank enough to heighten the excitement generated by the ceaseless flow of bad news. Among the shadowy guests, Ser Ceccone was a frequent presence. As the hours advanced toward the morning twilight, he remained Spini's constant companion alongside Francesco Cei, who hid carelessly in Florence, awaiting the revocation of his banishment once the Friar fell.

The tapers burned down into low, shapeless masses, and holes in the shutters glowed with a somber outer light when Spini suddenly sprang from his seat. Walking up and down with an angry flush on his face, reacting to recent conversation with his two unmilitary associates, he exploded: "The devil spit him! He shall pay for it, though. Ha, ha! The claws shall come down on him when he least expects it. So he was to be the great man after all! He pretended to throw everything into my cap, as if I were a blind beggar, all the while winking and filling his own purse. I'd like to hang skins about him and set my hounds on him! And he stole that fine ruby of mine—I was a fool to give it to him yesterday. Malediction! He laughed at me in his sleeve two years ago and spoiled the finest plan ever laid. I was a fool to trust myself with a rascal whose twisted contrivances no one could see to the end except himself."

"A Greek, too," interjected Francesco Cei, a slight smile of amusement playing on his face at Spini's rage, "who dropped into Florence with gems packed about him."

"You did not choose your confidant very wisely, my Dolfo," Cei remarked.

"He is a cursed deal cleverer than you, Francesco, and handsomer too," Spini retorted, turning on his associate with a general desire to torment whatever presented itself.

"I humbly conceive," said Ser Ceccone, "that Messer Francesco's poetic genius will outweigh—"

"Yes, yes, rub your hands! I hate that notary's trick of yours," Spini interrupted, noting that his patronage relied largely on such frankness. "But here comes Taddeo, or someone else: now is the time! What news, eh?" he demanded as two *Compagnacci* entered with heated expressions.

"Bad!" replied one. "The people have decided they want to sack Soderini's house. Now that we have blocked them, they will turn on us unless we act carefully. I suspect some Mediceans are buzzing among them; we may see them attacking your palace over the bridge shortly unless we find another bait."

"I have it!" cried Spini. Seizing Taddeo by the belt, he drew him aside to give instructions while the other continued informing Cei of the Signoria's interference regarding Soderini's house. Presently, Spini exclaimed, pushing Taddeo slightly toward the door, "Go, and make quick work."

On the opposite bank of the Arno, amidst the chill grey twilight, another figure emerged from the shadows. While the Compagnacci marched toward their appointed violence, this solitary man sought the river's edge at a secluded spot within the city walls. Here, high warehouses and granaries turned their backs to the water, leaving a stretch of damp, sloping grass and rushes, crisscrossed by gutters that fed the swift current below. This desolation was his sanctuary. Drawn by the memory of kindness—a crust of bread, a raw carrot, apple parings offered by a friendly "runlet"—Baldassarre returned to this hidden place to wait. It was worth the vigil in a world unseen by others, preferable to the silent, humiliating begging on the steps beyond Porta San Frediano. Baldassarre loathed the act of begging; he would sooner choose death than make that appeal, yet one desperate possibility anchored him to life.

It was no longer hope, but a haunting probability that seized his mind entirely. Like a woman watching a headland for a ship long deemed lost, he clung to the faint chance that fate might intervene. Having escaped the convent hospital of San Miniato only to find the wife who was to aid him dead to the plague, he had realized his own impotence. His knife was gone, his body broken, his mind too weary to devise a plan for revenge. A shattered, bewildered old man, he nonetheless waited for something formless and dim that stirred violent pulses within him, much like the instinctual dread we feel waking from sleep. Because he desired to live, he crept out into the grey light, sat in the long grass, and watched the waters, seeking a faint promise in their flow.

Meanwhile, the Compagnacci executed their orders. Left largely unchecked by a distracted Signoria, the armed bands split into two streams converging on the Arno—one heading for the Ponte Rubaconte, the other for the Ponte Vecchio. Their destination was unanimous: the Via de' Bardi, specifically a house near its eastern end where the master was expected to be asleep. But Tito was not sleeping. The events of the previous day had spurred his flight; investigations loomed, and delay bred danger. Though the specific fear of Baldassarre had died with the man's apparent demise, the *habit* of fear remained undying. He felt unsafe in Florence's turbulent air and prepared to depart. His household was packed: Maso handed over the keys, horses awaited in San Gallo, and Tessa and the children rested in the Borgo, ready to join him.

Tito descended the stone steps and crossed the threshold, no longer the man who had once loved Romola, but a figure casting off his old life. He intended to take the private route via the Ponte Rubaconte, but sounds approaching from the opposite direction forced a change of course. Was the mob coming into Oltrarno? Unpleasant sensations tightened his mantle as he hurried toward the Ponte Vecchio. Soon, louder noises confirmed his dread: the crowd was crossing the bridge. Driven by an impulse stronger than reason, he turned to flee, only to realize the mob was already flooding the street from the other end. Refusing to return to his house, fearing the chaos, he pushed onward toward the bridge. The roar of trampling feet and confused shouts grew deafening until he reached the end of the street, where the crowd poured out to hem him in.

Before he could process the sudden shout rising around him, he found himself surrounded—not by unarmed rabble, but by armed Compagnacci. His cap was knocked off, and he was shoved violently into the throng along the narrow bridge passage. Shouts erupted: *"Piagnone! Medicean! Throw him over!"* Strong hands tore at his mantle, threatening to throttle him, while others lunged for his money bag (*scarsella*). Amidst the hustle and drag, the bag slipped free, swinging at his side. The cacophony of execrations stunned him. Tito's worst nightmare was being trampled before reaching the central arches. His only salvation lay in being thrown overboard before he was beaten senseless.

In that critical moment, Tito summoned a desperate reserve of strength. Mastering the clasp of his belt, he flung it and the *scarsella* toward a patch of clear space against the parapet, screaming in a ringing voice, **"There are diamonds! There is gold!"**

The hold on him loosened instantly as the mob rushed for the valuables. Seizing the opening, Tito threw himself onto the parapet and leaped. He plunged with a great splash into the dark river below. It was his chance of survival. Rising to the surface, he felt a surge of deliverance. Striking out with the energy of his youth, aided by the current, he aimed to swim past the Ponte alla Carrara to a remote landing near San Gallo. Life still beckoned. Above, the idiot mob continued to shout, convinced he was drowned. Peering over the parapet, they saw only the drifting blackness of his hair and velvet sleeves. Only from the downstream perspective could a pale olive face be seen, white against the dark water—a face marked by a square forehead, low arched brows, and lustrous, agate-like eyes.

Driven by terror of his fellow men more than the uncertainty of the river, Tito swam on, panting and straining. He passed the bridge of Santa Trinita, then the final bridge. Exhaustion began to blur his senses, dragging him toward unconsciousness, but suddenly, distant eyes caught his movement.

Downriver, Baldassarre sat motionless among the rank green stems, searching the runlet for scraps. When a white object drifted into view, he stared, his eyes filling with a new light. As the form gathered clarity, he leaned forward, a start of recognition seizing him. Suddenly, a body was cast violently onto the grass mere yards from him. Baldassarre sprang forward like a panther, clutching the velvet tunic as he fell atop the stranger.

"Dead?" he wondered, staring into rigid eyes. But no—justice often brings a semblance of death. Hope surged in his heart, mixed with trembling tremors. All the slow-burning embers of his life seemed to leap into flame. The eyes remained locked, unblinking. Could he truly be dead? Time seemed to stretch, freezing hope into despair. Then, a vibration broke the stillness; the eyelids quivered, and the eyes opened wide.

Tito recognized him, yet the nature of their reunion terrified him. Was this life or the chill gloom of the past forever hanging over him? Now, Baldassarre's sole dread was losing his grip on the young limbs. Pressing his knuckles against the round throat, he knelt upon the chest with the full force of his aged frame. *Let death come now!* He watched the face, refusing to trust the fleeting signs of life. When the eyes stiffened again, he held fast, vowing never to release his hold until witnesses arrived. He declared internally that he had killed this traitor, his former son-in-law, and hoped justice would validate his claim. He wished to die clutching the body, following the traitor to hell to seize him there.

Thus he knelt, pressing his knuckles against the throat, ignoring the passing hours and the lack of witnesses. Florence busied itself with greater tragedies nearby; shortly after, Savonarola cried out under torture, "I will confess!" It was not until late afternoon that a wagon drawn by a mild grey ox approached the grassy margin. The driver, gathering stones, noticed the startling sight: the aged man had collapsed forward, his dead hand clutching the garment of the younger. Unable to separate them, the driver loaded both bodies into the wagon to transport them to the Piazza for the Eight.

As the wagon rolled into the crowded streets, a growing escort followed the strange burden. At first, the identities were obscured, the old man's face hidden beneath the younger. But recognition spread quickly.

"I know that old man," testified Piero di Cosimo. "I painted his likeness once. He is the prisoner who clutched Melema on the steps of the Duomo."

"He is perhaps the same old man who appeared at supper in my gardens," added Bernardo Rucellai, one of the Eight. "I had forgotten him. I thought he had died in prison. But the truth is unknowable now."

Who can point to the work of justice and declare, "It is done"? Justice resembles the Kingdom of God: it exists not merely as an external fact, but as a profound yearning within us.

Romola's consciousness drifted from the depths of sleep into a restless, vivid dreaming, until finally, she found herself stretching her arms wide within the courtyard of the Bargello. The flickering flames of the tapers there seemed to swell in intensity, blotting out the darkness with a sudden, overwhelming light. Her eyes snapped open, and the reality of morning flooded her senses.

Her boat lay motionless in a secluded cove. To her right stretched the flawless, sapphire blue of the Mediterranean; to her left unfolded a landscape of recurring beauty, a rhythmic scene beloved by the shores of that loveliest sea. Deep curves of mountains framed a swath of verdant land, draped in the gentle shadows of trees sloping toward rocky heights. Winding upward through the foliage, a path led to a ragged cluster of buildings that seemed to have scrambled hastily up the mountainside, securing a precarious perch solely to display its tall belfry as a beacon to the scattered village below. The rising sun cast oblique rays upon the western edge of this crescent-shaped bay, leaving the rest bathed in dewy shadow. Silence reigned absolute; the waters themselves seemed to have curled inward for rest.

The warm sunlight touched Romola, thrilling her gently like a caress. She lay still, scarcely watching the view, merely absorbing the profound peace and beauty surrounding her. In the freshness of youth, there often comes, upon waking, a moment where mere passive existence becomes a *Lethe*—a bliss so exquisite and indefinite that it erases memory and desire alike. As the soft warmth seeped into her young limbs and her eyes rested on this secluded luxury, the agitating past seemed to glide away like the dark vision of the Bargello. The afternoon dreams of her girlhood returned to her. For a minute or two, the oblivion was undisturbed; she did not even conceive that she could rest forever, she simply felt that she rested.

Then, distinct awareness returned: she was lying in a boat that had borne her across the waters throughout the night. Instead of delivering her to death, it had served as the gently lulling cradle of a new life. Despite her evening despair, she was grateful that morning had come again, glad to rest in the familiar sunlight rather than in the unknown realms of death. *Could* she not rest here? No sound from Florence could reach her. Yet, the oblivion was already troubled. Through the golden haze, domes, towers, and walls pierced the air, separated by a river and enclosed by green hills.

Rising from her reclining position, she sat up in the boat, willing herself to resist the surge of thoughts questioning how far the boat had carried her. Why worry? This was a sheltered nook inhabited by simple villagers who would not harm her. For a little while, at least, she could rest and make no resolutions. Soon she would procure bread and milk, then nestle in the green quiet, finding a pause in her life. Turning to watch the crescent valley, she sought to reclaim the soothing sense of peace she had felt upon waking.

She had not maintained this contemplative pose for more than a few minutes when a piercing cry cut through the stillness. It was not a brief shout but a continuous, intensifying wail. Romola was certain it was a child in distress, crying for help that never arrived. She sprang up, placing one foot on the boat's side, ready to leap onto the beach, but paused to listen. Surely the mother was nearby; the cry must soon cease. But it continued, drawing her irresistibly, seeming all the more pitiful against the backdrop of the preceding peace. Compelled, she leaped onto the sand and walked many paces before realizing which direction to take.

She believed the cry originated from a rough garden growth several yards to her right, where a half-ruined hovel stood. Climbing a low, broken stone fence, she traversed patches of weedy greens and neglected, ripe corn. The cry grew clearer, confirming her guess, yet as she hurried toward the hovel, she felt an oppressive shift in the air as she left the sea behind. Was there some hidden taint lurking among the green luxuriance that had seemed such an inviting refuge from the coming heat?

Now seeing the entrance to the hovel, the cry darted through her like physical pain. The next instant, her foot crossed the threshold, but the sight within the sombre light arrested her with a shock of awe and horror. Upon the straw-strewn floor lay three dead bodies: a tall man, a girl of about eight years old, and a young woman whose long black hair was being clawed and pulled by a living child—the very source of the screaming.

Romola's experience with death and disease rendered her thought and action prompt. She lifted the little living child, soothing it against her bosom, while simultaneously checking the bodies to confirm they were truly dead. Their strongly marked racial features and peculiar garb suggested they were Spanish or Portuguese Jews, perhaps put ashore and abandoned by rapacious sailors who coveted their property. Such tragedies befell Jews forced to flee their homes by the Inquisition; the cruelty of greed threw them from the sea, and the cruelty of superstition cast them back.

"But surely," Romola thought, "I shall find a woman in the village whose maternal heart will not refuse to tend this helpless child—if the real mother is indeed dead." This doubt lingered because while the man and girl appeared emaciated and long deceased, the woman seemed harder, retaining some robustness. Kneeling, Romola was about to check the pulse beneath a piece of yellow woolen drapery when she saw purple spots marking the familiar pestilence.

It struck her then: if the villagers knew of this, she might face resistance. They might shrink from her with the child in her arms. However, she possessed money and could offer payment for goat's milk. Setting out immediately toward the village, her mind focused on soothing the dark-haired infant and figuring out how to secure a caregiver. She hoped, with a touch of awe, that her unknown arrival in religious dress would inspire trust.

As she crossed cultivated fields, she noted with wonder that patches of corn had been left to over-ripen, untouched by the sickle, and that golden apples and dark figs rotted on the weedy earth. Grassy spaces existed, yet no cows, sheep, or goats were visible. The stillness now held something fearful. Hurrying toward the densest cluster of houses, seeking the most life to plead for the helpless life she carried, she stopped occasionally to bite pieces of sweet fig pulp to quiet the child.

Entering between two rows of dwellings, she found it was time for villagers to stir, yet no soul was in sight. The air grew increasingly oppressive, laden with a horrible impurity. One door stood open revealing grim emptiness; another showed a man lying dead, dressed in his clothes, his head resting across a spade handle, an earthenware cruse in his hand as if he had collapsed suddenly. Horror seized Romola. Was she in a village of the unburied dead? Wanting to listen for any faint sound, the child cried out anew when she ceased feeding it, filling her ears.

Finally, she spotted a figure crawling slowly from a house, sinking back against the wall in a sitting posture. Hastening forward, she found a young woman in fevered anguish, holding a pitcher. As Romola approached, the woman did not start; the single need was too absorbing for distraction.

"Water! Get me water!" she moaned.

Romola stooped to take the pitcher and whispered gently, "You shall have water; can you point toward the well?" The hand lifted toward the distant end of the narrow street. Romola set off with all the speed possible, balancing the pitcher and feeding the child. The morsels of fruit calmed the infant, allowing Romola to observe her surroundings more clearly. The well lay twenty yards beyond the street's end. Approaching it, her eyes caught a glimpse of a cow and two goats grazing high on the green slope below the church. She tried to trace a path to this cheering sight once her errand was done.

So engrossed was she that she failed to notice someone approaching the well from the opposite side, fixing astonished eyes upon her. Romola presented a startling figure: her gaze fixed intently on the distant slope, her thick grey garment flowing rapidly, her hair rolling backward and illuminated by the sun, and the little olive-skinned baby on her right arm gazing out with jet-black eyes.

"She carries a pitcher to fetch water for the sick. It is the Holy Mother, come to care for those afflicted by the pestilence."

It was a vision of awe. Perhaps she would be angry with those who fetched water only for themselves. The youth dropped his vessel in terror, and Romola, noticing the black-and-white figure fleeing toward the slope she had admired, realized she was being watched. Remembering the parched sufferer, she quickly filled her pitcher and hastened back.

Inside a house searching for a small cup, she found salt meat and meal—no signs of want. Seating the baby on the ground, she lifted a cup of water to the sufferer, who drank eagerly before closing her eyes and leaning back in relief. Moments later, she opened her eyes and looked at Romola.

"Who are you?"

"I came over the sea," Romola replied. "I arrived this morning. Are all the people dead in these houses?"

"I think they are all ill now—all that are not dead. My father and sister lie dead upstairs, and there is no one to bury them. Soon I shall die too."

"Not so, I

In the hushed silence of those winter hours, as Romola rested from her exhaustion, her mind drifted backward through the past and gazed across the foggy horizon of the future. Everything appeared shifted, viewed from a vantage point entirely new. Since awakening in the boat, her recent experiences had struck her with the force of a fresh seal pressing upon dissolving wax. She had felt untethered, devoid of purpose, sinking into a selfish lament that life offered her nothing but emptiness. A desperate cry had risen within her—"I am weary of life; I wish to die"—as she succumbed to sleep. Yet, from the moment she woke to the sound of that cry, reflection ceased. Unlike in Florence, where she had often rationalized her survival by thinking how she might alleviate sorrow, she now simply *lived*. With an urgent, driving impulse, she sought to share the existence around her, answering every call of need and performing the labor that screamed to be done. The reasons for enduring, working, and breathing no longer required logical argument; they were instinctive.

This transformation felt like a rebirth. In Florence, the simple connections between human beings had become tangled for her with the heavy knots of marriage, state duty, and religious devotion. When these structures failed her, the blow had severed her from life and numbed her compassion. Now, however, she declared, "It was sheer baseness in me to crave death. If all else is uncertain, this suffering I can ease is absolute truth; if the glory of the cross is a phantom, then sorrow is merely the reality." As long as strength remained in her arms, she would extend them to the fainting; as long as light visited her eyes, she would seek out the forsaken.

Then, the past surged forward with renewed urgency. Her work in this verdant valley was finished, and the emotions previously held captive by those around her flooded back into the deep channels of utility and love. This rare opportunity for self-reflection, born of total separation from her accustomed life, allowed her to judge herself as never before. The guilt inherent in a sensitive nature, acutely aware of others' potential pain, stirred within her with growing intensity. She questioned the justice of her own judgments and actions: she had been rash, arrogant, perpetually dissatisfied that others were not virtuous enough, while failing to honor the highest ideals her soul once recognized. She condemned her departure as a cowardly act of self-preservation. After all, the reasons Savonarola had given for bringing her back were truer, deeper than the excuses for her second flight. How could she claim to feel the needs of strangers while ignoring the needs of those closest to her?

Yet, resistance rose against this harsh self-reproach. Memories of her life with Tito surfaced—the circumstances that rendered their true union impossible, while their outward marriage imposed false duties that concealed and sanctioned what her mind rebelled against. These memories told her that flight had indeed been her only recourse. Every mind, save perhaps those dulled by insensibility, must endure this recurring conflict where life's complex twists forbid the fulfillment of sacred bonds. Strictly speaking, relationships cannot be replaced; the arrival of the new does not erase the failure or breach of the old. Life has lost its perfection; it is maimed. Until the wounds fully scar, conscience constantly casts backward, casting doubtful glances.

Romola shrank in terror at the prospect of renewing her proximity to Tito, yet unease gnawed at her regarding his fate. She feared the moment might arrive when he suffered and needed her. A thread of pain remained within her, echoing Fra Girolamo's warning: she could not cease to be a wife. Could anything truly end for her that had once mingled with the very current of her heart's blood? Florence, and all her life there, returned to her like a sudden hunger. Her feelings refused to wander toward the abstract and vague; their living fiber was sustained by the memory of familiar things. The thought that she had severed herself from them forever grew increasingly insistent during these idle hours.

*What if Fra Girolamo was wrong?* *What if the life of Florence is a tapestry woven from contradictions?* Was she, then, something superior, entitled to shake the dust from her feet and declare, "This world is not good enough for me"? If she were truly higher, she would not have so readily lost all her trust. Her indignant grief for her godfather no longer held complete sway; her sense of debt to Savonarola was regaining dominance. Nothing that had occurred or would occur could negate the fact that he had inspired a new life within her. Who, in all her experience, could demand equal gratitude? His errors—could they not bring calamity? She could not rest. She barely knew whether her renewed vigor stemmed from the budding spring or an eager longing to draw nearer to Florence. Though she did not dare imagine entering the city, the desire to be close enough to learn what transpired there pressed upon her with a strength that excluded all other aims.

One March morning, the villagers gathered in the valley to witness the Blessed Lady's departure. Jacopo had procured a mule for her and accompanied her over the mountains. The Padre also traveled with her to the nearest town, intending to assist her in finding the safest route to Pistoja. Her hoard of trinkets and coin, untouched since arriving in the valley, was ample for her journey. Had Romola been less consumed by the longing pulling her away, leaving the village for the final time would have been a difficult moment. She stood on the village street, while the Padre and Jacopo waited near the well with the mule. Her progress was slowed by the weeping crowd, who knelt to kiss her hands, then clung to her skirts, kissing the grey fabric as they cried, "Ah, why will you leave, when the good season begins and the crops promise plenty?"

"Do not mourn," Romola replied gently. "You are well now, and I shall remember you. I must go to see if my own people require me."

"Ah, yes—if they have the plague!" someone called out.

"Look upon us again, Madonna!" another pleaded.

"Yes, yes, we will be good to little Benedetto!"

Finally, Romola mounted the mule. However, a vigorous scream from Benedetto, seeing her turn away in this new capacity, gave everyone an excuse to follow her, insisting that the child must ride on the mule's neck down to the bottom of the slope. The parting was inevitable, yet as Romola kept turning before disappearing from view, she watched the small flock linger, striving to catch the last wave of her hand.

On the fourteenth of April, Romola returned to the walls of Florence. Her journey had been fraught with anxiety; unable to find peace in Pistoia due to conflicting rumors regarding the Trial by Fire, she had pressed onward to Prato. There, amidst a growing dread, she began to feel the inevitable pull toward the city she feared most. Yet, fate intervened sooner than she anticipated. As she traveled, she encountered a monk from San Spirito, the same priest who had served as her late husband's confessor.

From this Augustinian friar, Romola learned the grim totality of the recent upheaval: the arrest of Savonarola and the tragic end of her husband, Tito. The monk recounted how he had witnessed the procession carrying the condemned man into the Piazza, burdened by the weight of his crimes. He spoke of the general sentiment in Florence, noting that Tito had managed to escape a frenzied mob only by leaping into the Arno River, yet he had perished shortly after, struck down on the riverbank by an elderly man harboring a deep-seated grudge against him. Regarding Savonarola, the monk delivered his verdict with the characteristic bias of the Black Brothers (*Frati Neri*) toward a soul that had shown signs of purity beneath its dark habit: he had confessed himself a deceiver of the people.

Romola heard these words, but they registered differently in her heart than in anyone else's. To her, the tragedy was not merely political or religious; it was personal and absolute. That evening, she sat in Florence, enveloped in a heavy, agitated silence. Around her, the city erupted in a cacophony of joyous exclamations and mournful wails, interwoven with the gossipy narratives of neighbors. Among them was Monna Brigida, whose hair, previously dyed artificially during Romola's absence, had now been stripped back to its natural gray—a sacrifice she claimed she would gladly make if only her "dear child" would remain with her.

However, Romola remained unmoved by Brigida's dramatic declarations or the trivial gossip surrounding her. The crushing reality of Tito's death and Fra Girolamo's coerced confession of duplicity left her with little capacity to process minor circumstantial details. Under the weight of her awe-stricken grief, her entire mental energy focused on two singular, overriding imperatives: to locate Savonarola, and to discover the fate of Tessa and the children.

When Monna Brigida's rambling speech finally veered from current troubles into plans for Romola to reside permanently with her, Romola interrupted her abruptly.

"Cousin," she asked sharply, "since Tito's passing, has anything been seen or said concerning a young woman accompanied by two small children?"

Monna Brigida recoiled, her eyes widening in shock, her hands rising in a gesture of disbelief. "Cristo! No! What manner of man was he to treat you so poorly, my poor child? Ah, then, that explains your departure! You left me believing you had chosen to go of your own free will. If I had known the truth—that there was a young woman and children involved—I would not have judged you so strangely or called you flighty.
