In my heart, I often wondered, 'Why would she abandon her husband to his misfortunes just because they severed poor Bernardo's head? She possesses her father's fiery temper,' I thought. 'That is the root of it.' Never scold me, child; Bardo *was* fierce, you cannot deny it. But had you spoken the truth about the mother and the babes, I would have understood immediately. Has anything been seen or heard of her? Nothing, and the less the better. Enough evil is spoken of him already without adding to it. Since that was your reason for leaving—"

"No, dear cousin," Romola interjected earnestly, cutting her off. "Please, cease speaking thus. Above all else, I wish to find that young woman and her children, and to protect them. They are utterly helpless. Do not argue against it; that is my first and foremost duty."

"Well," Monna Brigida muttered, shrugging her shoulders while lowering her voice to a tone of confused discomfort, "if seeking them makes one a *Piagnone*, then I have been counting peas instead of reciting prayers. Fra Girolamo himself declared that widows should not remarry. Stepping through the front door is a sin and a shame, apparently, yet crawling down the chimney invites welcome. *Two* children—Santiddio!"

"Cousin," Romola replied gently but firmly, "the poor girl has committed no conscious wrong; she is ignorant of everything. I will explain later—not now."

Early the following morning, Romola proceeded to the house beyond San Ambrogio where she had once discovered Tessa. As she had feared, Tessa was gone. Romola reasoned that Tito must have dispatched her ahead to a location where he intended to meet her, for he would never willingly separate her from the children. This possibility brought fresh pain; if Tessa had fled Florence, locating her seemed nearly impossible. Romola imagined the frightened child waiting in vain at some lonely roadside, consumed by helpless misery.

Those who lived nearby offered no more than the fact that the old deaf woman, Lisa, had departed a week prior with her belongings, though no one knew her destination. With no concrete leads to follow, and hindered by both her natural reserve and a profound sensitivity that made her reluctant to publicly reveal Tessa's connection to Tito alongside her own quest, Romola found herself paralyzed by inaction.

Days stretched into weeks of anxious stillness. Even when urged by other concerns, her heart would race if she spotted a pair of round brown legs or a woman dressed in the attire of a country laborer (*contadina*). She never convinced herself that this search was an act of heroic charity or elevated virtue. Rather, she possessed a specific bond she felt compelled to honor; she yearned to embrace the children and earn their affection. Such a reunion would bring solace not only to herself but also serve as a redemptive fruit from her past sorrows.

She learned that Tito had left behind considerable property to which she held a claim. Yet, distrustful of the cleanliness of such tainted wealth, she resolved to surrender it all to the State, retaining only enough to purchase her father's library. This sum would suffice for the modest sustenance of Tessa and the children.

However, Monna Brigida consistently overshadowed these prudent plans. She clamored insistently that Romola must live with her, never abandoning her until she was safely ushered into Paradise, questioning why she had ever encouraged the conversion to the *Piagnoni*. Furthermore, Brigida argued that if Romola wished to raise other people's children, she should do so alongside her own household. Only, she insisted, must the children be found first. Romola sensed the full weight of this implication. Yet, strong feelings unfulfilled inevitably breed superstition, whether of hope or despair. Romola's was the superstition of hope: somehow, she would find the mother and the children.

Finally, a new avenue for inquiry presented itself. She learned that Tito had arranged for horses and mules to await him at San Gallo; consequently, he planned to depart Florence via the Gate of San Gallo. Determined to verify this, she decided to inquire with the gatekeepers whether anyone matching Tessa's description, accompanied by children, had passed through before the morning of the ninth of April.

Walking along the Via San Gallo, her face concealed beneath her long widow's veil, she scanned the surroundings vigilantly. Suddenly, she spotted Bratti haggling with a customer. That wandering merchant, she surmised, might prove useful. She need not fear discussing Tessa with him. Just as she removed her veil and stepped across the street toward him, her gaze fell upon something dangling from the corner of his basket. Her heart leaped with a surge of hope far greater than before.

"Bratti, my friend," she said suddenly, "where did you acquire that necklace?"

"Your servant, madonna," Bratti replied, glancing around with deliberate caution, his mind seemingly impervious to surprise. "It is a necklace of value, yet I shall gain little from selling it, for my heart is too tender for trade; I have pledged it."

"Pray, tell me where you obtained it—from a little woman named Tessa, is it not true?"

"Oh! If you know her," Bratti said, "and would redeem it from me at a small profit to return it to her, you would be performing a great charity. She wept bitterly when parting with it—you would have thought she was plunging into a brook. A small fee is all I ask. One florin suffices, for I dislike being hard-hearted."

"Where is she?" Romola demanded, handing over the coin and unclasping the necklace from the basket with trembling, joyful hands.

"Just outside the gate there, at the far end of the Borgo, at the house of old Sibilla Manetti. Anyone will show you the way."

With wings of anticipation beating at her heels, Romola thanked heaven for the Carnival incident that had allowed her to memorize the necklace's unique appearance. Soon, she stood before the house she sought. Inside the inner room, the young woman and the children had been languishing for over two weeks, possessing neither money nor means to pay the poor widow who housed them, save for their few garments. But since the lady recognized them...

Romola waited for no further explanation. She pushed the door open.

Tessa sat upon the low

When Romola returned home with Tessa and the children, late April was drawing to a close. Yet another weight pressed upon her heart, heavier than before: the publication of Fra Girolamo's trial. More accurately, it was the release of the confessions extracted from him by the sixteen Florentine citizens commissioned to interrogate him. Issued by the Signoria, this document had ignited a storm of public suspicion and outrage so fierce that immediate orders were given to recall it. Copies, however, had slipped away; a second edition, unauthorized but eagerly circulated, found its way into the hands of the populace.

Despairing of ever seeing Fra Girolamo again, Romola read the evidence repeatedly, seeking clarity amidst the conflicting narratives swirling through the city. Among Savonarola's most devoted followers, the friar's lack of constancy under torture and his retraction of prophetic claims caused a profound consternation. Soon, this fear hardened into certainty: any words contradicting their faith were deemed fabrications inserted by Ser Ceccone, the disgraced notary who compiled the transcript.

Yet, glaring inconsistencies undermined the official record. Half the panel of sixteen examiners consisted of the prophet's fiercest enemies. Notorious Dolfo Spini sat among the newly elected "Eight," seemingly appointed solely to ensure a verdict against a man whose destruction had already been decided by the ruling faction. To many, the proceedings in the Old Palace resembled a slow, formalized murder. The Signoria aimed to strike a deal with the Pope and the Duke of Milan by eliminating a threat to both the corrupt clergy and greedy foreign powers. The Frate was doomed; the debate was merely whether Florence would surrender him alive to the Papacy or, in exchange for taxing rights on church property, execute him publicly on their own piazza. Under such circumstances, who could trust the so-called confession?

If the Frate had denied his gift, it was likely wrung from him by the agony of torture—a pain that, in his sensitive constitution, would surely induce delirium. What if the examiners claimed he suffered only three times over one day, confessing freely thereafter? Could anyone believe that? Romola knew better. He had been tormented far more intensely, proportional to the distress his words caused those who loved him.

Other friends of Savonarola, less ardent in their devotion, accepted the confession's substance despite the notary's manipulations. They argued indignantly that no crime warranted death or even severe punishment. To impartial observers, the evidence suggested the Frate died not for sin, but because he had become inconvenient to the Pope, to rapacious states eyeing Tuscany, and to ambitious citizens undermining the common good. No political crime was proven; no stain marred his private conduct. Fellow monks, including a former secretary renowned for his critical intellect, testified to his unimpeachable purity, even after the shock of his retraction. The Pope himself could charge him only with disobedience to mandates and ignoring excommunication. While difficult to justify legally, many felt a moral uprising against the Roman Court, blurring the line between the institution of the Church and its flawed members. Ordinary citizens sensed that the victory of the Frate's enemies was truly the triumph of gross license.

Shrewd Florentines like Soderini and Piero Guicciardini wore an angry smile at the government's clumsy pretense of judicial fairness for a predetermined act of policy. They noted that if the Frate had mixed deeper frauds with zeal less threatening to powerful figures, his deceit might have been hailed as essential lubricant for the state machine. Still, they admitted the Pope's actions were acts of self-defense. A rebel who sought to rally Europe to depose the Pontiff posed an existential threat. Florence had heard him declare he would not obey the devil; thus, a life-and-death struggle was inevitable. Yet, it was less certain that Florence should serve as the Pope's executioner.

Romola's mind was torn between the fervent faith wounded by betrayal and the sober judgment of worldly prudence. Neither offered satisfaction. Her long meditation over the printed document revealed painful truths gathered during her years of discipleship: Savonarola's retraction was not merely a spasmodic attempt to survive torture. Conversely, her soul desperately needed an explanation that preserved the purity of his life's mission. The bitterness of losing her trust in an embodiment of high motive had sparked a strange resilience, a faith rising from despair.

She concluded that the negative thoughts plaguing her soul could not reflect reality. The spirit that had once breathed through the Frate's words was real, not a hollow pretence. Any falsehood in him was a tragic fall, a gradual entanglement he struggled against, not a calculated scheme nurtured by success. Examining the text, she spotted sentences bearing the mark of clumsy fabrication—the emphatic, repetitive self-accusation typical of low hypocrisy. Yet, these contradictions stood in stark opposition to Savonarola's character and the overall tone of the document, suggesting the remainder reflected his actual words.

Hardly anything was dishonorable except regarding his prophetic claims. He remained consistent in stating his goals for Florence, the Church, and the world. Apart from the false claim of special inspiration used to sway minds, he admitted no unworthy means. Even here, stripped of the notary's malice, Fra Girolamo appeared as a man seeking glory, but through laboring for humanity's moral welfare, transforming beliefs into active forces in daily life.

One memorable passage, though possibly altered, resonated deeply: *"Everything that I have done... I have done with the design of being for ever famous... to win credit in Florence... to establish my position... to do great things in Italy and beyond... to rouse the princes of Christendom... to subdue the infidels. It was not much in my thoughts to get myself made a Cardinal or Pope... to be the head of that work was a greater thing than to be Pope, because a man without virtue may be Pope; but such a work as I contemplated demanded a man of excellent virtues."*

This blend of ambition and belief in supreme goodness was familiar to Romola, echoing the voice that once rang through the Duomo. Savonarola always conceived great tasks and believed himself capable of achieving them. Justice, purity, and love must triumph, ideally through his voice and work. In moments of ecstasy, doubtless, his sense of self dissolved into the Divine, fostering genuine humility. But toward his fellow-men, pre-eminence seemed a necessity. Perhaps this confession, acknowledging a conscious doubleness, simply reflected the wavering of belief common to humans facing drastic shifts—from adoration to hatred, from triumphant progress to the hooting crowds, the hard-faced judges, and the horrific torture.

As Romola imagined the anguish following the confession—the solitude of the prison, the battle between conscience and coerced words—it pressed upon her own heart, drawing slow, bitter tears. Vulgar voices in Florence glibly condemned him, unaware of the depth of sorrow he knew, reserved for souls that have loved perfection and beheld themselves fallen. She later witnessed the evidence of his mental state after laying his mouth in the dust.

Days passed until reports of new examinations ceased. Left alone in his cell, Savonarola was permitted pen and ink. He wrote, but not to defend his innocence or protest his treatment. His writings became a dialogue with divine purity, an outpouring of self-abasement, a continuous cry for inner renewal. The old, vehement assertion—"Look at my work, for it is good!"—had vanished. Now, the Voice of Sadness spoke: *"God placed thee in the midst of the people... Thou hast cured others: and thou thyself hast been still diseased. Thy heart was lifted up at the beauty of thy own deeds... thou art fallen into the depths of the sea."* Hope, too, softened its tone, offering not promises of future works, but comfort in his current penitence: *"Thou art not forsaken, else why is thy heart bowed?"*

There is no evidence that from imprisonment to his final hour, Savonarola viewed himself as a martyr. Martyrdom had been a dream of future triumph, a passion dividing his hope. Now, replaced by both, came a resignation he named with no glorifying title. *But therefore he may the more fitly be called a martyr by his fellow-men to all time.* Power rose against him not due to sin, but because of his greatness; not because he sought to deceive, but because he sought to elevate the world. Through that greatness, he endured a double agony: the reviling, the torture, the death-throes, and the crushing pain of sinking from a vision of glorious achievement into a deep shadow where he could only whisper, *"I count as nothing: darkness encompasses me: yet the light I saw was the true light."*

The words echoed in Romola's mind, repeated by countless voices until they sounded like a single, piercing cry. They were the final utterances of Savonarola, spoken as he signed his confession before the brethren of San Marco: *"Pray for me, for God has withdrawn from me the spirit of prophecy."* These words struck her anew, casting deep shadows of doubt over the man she had revered. How could he look back upon the past with such absolute self-possession if he truly believed his divine inspiration had fled? Her uncertainty grew heavier when more pitiful reports reached her ears.

May nineteenth had arrived, and with its bright sun, Florence welcomed the two Papal Commissaries, sent to finalize the friar's trial. They entered the city amid thunderous acclamations demanding the death of the *Frate*. The populace cried out, "It is the Frate's deception that has brought on all our misfortunes! Burn him, and all shall be right; our evils will cease."

The following day, the tortured frame of the broken man endured fresh agony. Upon seeing the horrific instruments prepared for him, Savonarola, seized by convulsive agitation, fell to his knees. In brief, passionate words, he **retracted his confession**, declaring that he had lied when he denied his prophetic gift. "If I suffer," he proclaimed, "I suffer for the truth. The things I spoke, I received from God."

Yet the torture continued. When questioned why he had uttered those retracting words, the answer came, torn from his lips: "I said it to appear good. Tear me no more; I will tell you the truth."

Florentine assessors witnessed this second retraction, and the news spread quickly through the city, filling Romola with dismayed uncertainty. A sudden flash of insight pierced her gloom: *There will come a moment when he speaks freely.* When fear of falsehood vanishes, replaced only by the dread of death itself; when he is lifted above the mob, gazing upon them for the last time, they cannot stop him from speaking a final, decisive word. *I must be there,* she resolved.

Three days later, on May twenty-third, 1498, a long, narrow platform stretched across the great Piazza della Signoria, extending from the Palazzo Vecchio toward the Tetta de' Pisani. Unlike previous gatherings, there was no grove of wood ready to burn; instead, a single great heap of fuel sat on the circular area at the platform's end. Rising above this pyre was a gibbet with three halters. Though it possessed two arms, making it resemble a cross enough to unsettle some observers, one arm had been deliberately truncated to avoid the likeness.

On the marble terrace of the palace stood three tribunals. Near the main door waited the Bishop, tasked with performing the ceremony of degradation on Fra Girolamo and his two fellow brothers, who were to suffer as his accomplices. Another tribunal housed the Papal Commissaries, who would declare them heretics and schismatics, handing them over to the secular arm. Closest to the Marzocco statue, at the corner where the platform began, sat the third tribunal for the Gonfaloniere and the Eight, who would pronounce the sentence of death.

Once again, the Piazza teemed with expectant faces. A great fire was destined to be kindled. Among the crowd pressing around the gibbet, most harbored either ferocious hatred or a morbid curiosity to witness a barbarous spectacle. Yet, scattered across the wide pavement, on rooftops, and at windows, lingered many who, amidst bitter grief and the humiliation of enduring insults from the hypocritical *Piagnoni*, clung to a faint hope. Even at this eleventh hour, they prayed that God might intervene with a miraculous sign to vindicate their beloved prophet. Others, however, trembled with eagerness, just as Romola did, awaiting that final instant when Savonarola might cry out, *"O people, I was innocent of deceit!"*

Romola stood at a window on the north side of the Piazza, far removed from the marble terraces where the judges sat. Beside her watched Jacopo Nardi, a young Florentine of twenty-two, who would later earn honor as one of the few to write about Fra Girolamo with simple veracity. Seeing the struggle in Romola's face—a conflict between shuddering horror and a desperate yearning to witness the end—Jacopo had gently urged her earlier: *"Madonna, there is no need for you to watch these cruel things. I will tell you when he emerges from the Palazzo. Trust me; I know what you wish to see."*

Romola had covered her face, but the jeers and hoots seemed to render the hideous scene visible regardless. Finally, her arm was touched, and she heard the whisper, *"He comes."*

She turned toward the Palace. There, clad in his Dominican garb, Savonarola was led forth. Before the Bishop, he was stripped of his black mantle, his white scapular, and his long tunic, leaving him in a coarse woolen undergarment that revealed no sacred office, no rank. He had been degraded, severed from the Church Militant. The baser elements of the multitude delighted in such degradations, understanding them as pure satire. A fresh hoot of triumph rang out as the three degraded brothers marched to the tribunal of the Papal Commissaries, who pronounced them schismatics and heretics. Could the prophet now pass for anything other than a heretic? It was easy to believe in the damnation of a man standing stripped and humiliated.

They proceeded to the third tribunal, where the Florentine officials awaited. From her distance, Romola could distinguish the odious figure of Dolfino Spini, dressed in his grave black *lucco* as one of the Eight. The three figures, robed in tight white garments, tramped along the platform amidst yells and grating insults.

"Cover your eyes, Madonna," Jacopo warned softly. "Fra Girolamo will be the last."

Soon, she was forced to uncover her face again. Savonarola was there. He had mounted the steps, and she could see him looking out upon the multitude. But in that same moment, her high hopes died. All she saw was what he saw: torches waving to ignite the fuel beneath his corpse, faces glowing with a glare worse than hellfire. All she heard was what he heard: gross jests, taunts, and curses. The moment had passed. She covered her face once more, knowing only that Savonarola's voice had faded into eternal silence.

***

On the evening of May twenty-second, 1509, five individuals, whose histories we have partially traced, sat in a handsome upper room opening onto a loggia. This loggia overlooked the Borgo Pinti and, at its right-hand corner, gazed down upon the city gate toward Fiesole and the solemn heights beyond.

At one end of the room lay an archway leading to a narrow inner recess, barely larger than a niche, where light filtered from above onto a small altar draped in fine white linen. Above the altar hung a picture, discernible only as a small, full-length portrait of a Dominican Brother from this distance. It was shaded from the overhead light by overhanging branches and wreaths of flowers, while fresh tapers below remained unlit. Yet, the decoration seemed incomplete. Part of the floor was strewn with a chaotic array of flowers and green boughs.

Seated among them was a delicate, blue-eyed girl of thirteen, tossing her long, light-brown hair aside as she selected blooms for the wreath she wove. Occasionally, she looked up at her mother's work with an air of instruction, offering guidance. For the mother was not particularly adept at flower-weaving or any craft. Tessa's fingers had grown less dexterous with the passing years—indeed, much fatter—and she moved slowly, turning her head frequently to ask Ninna's opinion with great deference. Tessa never ceased to marvel at the wisdom of her children. She still wore her countrywoman's gown, merely broader than the old one, adorned with a silver pin in her rough, curly brown hair and the memorable necklace with its red cord ending mysteriously in her bosom. Her rounded face bore an expression of childish contentment more perfect than in her youth; everyone was so good in the world, Tessa thought. Even Monna Brigida never found fault with her anymore, preferring instead to sleep—an amiable habit shared by all, one that Tessa enjoyed for herself.

Monna Brigida slept soundly in a straight-backed armchair a couple of yards away. Her hair, parted backward beneath her black hood, possessed a soft whiteness unlike snow, simply the lovely white of aged locks. Her chin rested on her bosom, her hands lying on the chair's elbow. She had not been working; she had merely been looking on, as she usually did, before succumbing to sleep.

Two other figures sat farther off, near the wide doorway opening to the loggia. Lillo reclined on the ground, his back against the door-post angle, his long legs stretched out. He held a large book open on his knee, occasionally swatting an inquisitive fly with his hand, displaying an interest stronger than that stirred by the finely printed copy of Petrarch resting nearby, which he kept open as if memorizing lines.
